Lorenzo was dead; Pope Innocent was dying; a troublesome Neapolitan succession loomed alongside an intriguing, ambitious Milan, threatening to tear Italy apart soon. The times were likely to be difficult. Nevertheless, there was all the greater reason for the Republic to maintain its religious festivals.

Midsummer morning in 1492 was no less bright than usual. Early in the morning, the symbolic offerings destined for the grand procession were assembled at their starting point in the Piazza della Signoria. This famous square housed the massive, turreted Palace of the People, the Palazzo Vecchio, and the spacious Loggia built by Orcagna—the stage for all grand state ceremony. The sky formed the fairest blue tent, beneath which the bells swung so vigorously that every evil spirit possessing enough sense to be formidable must long since have fled. Windows and terraced roofs teemed with human faces; sombre stone houses glowed with hanging draperies. The boldly soaring palace tower, the yet older square tower of the Bargello, and the spire of the neighboring Badia seemed to keep watch overhead. Below, on the broad polygonal flags of the piazza, unfolded the glorious display of banners, horses with rich trappings, and gigantic *ceri*, or tapers, aptly called towers—strangely aggrandized descendants of the torches by whose faint light the Church worshipped in the Catacombs. Early in the morning, all processions needed to move under the Midsummer sky of Florence, where the shelter of narrow streets had to be periodically exchanged for the glare of wide spaces. The sun would be high in the heavens before the long pomp concluded its pilgrimage in the Piazza di San Giovanni.

At the point where the procession was to pause, the magnificent city, aided by its ingenious Cecca, had provided another canopy than the sky. The entirety of the Piazza del Duomo—from the octagonal baptistery in the center to the façade of the cathedral and the walls of the houses lining the quadrangle—was covered, at a height of forty feet or more, with blue drapery adorned with well-stitched yellow lilies and familiar coats of arms. Sheaves of multi-colored banners drooped at fitting angles beneath this superincumbent blue, creating a gorgeous, rainbow-lit shelter for the waiting spectators. These observers leaned from windows, formed a narrow border on the pavement, and waited for the show to commence.

Among these spectators was Tito Melema. Surrounded by brightness, he sat at the window of the room above Nello's shop. His right elbow rested on the red drapery hanging from the windowsill, his head supported in a backward position

"Five hundred florins," the printer declared, tapping the heavy stack of coins on the table. "A fortune, *giovane mio*. Take my word for it: if you seek wisdom, deposit these florins with me today. Let them grow in a vault rather than slip through your fingers on the banquet tables of our Florentine youth. Scholars, especially those who scent their books with vanity like Pietro Crinito, often squander with one hand until they beg with the other. I bring the capital; you choose the path. Put them to usury, or save them for a *morgen-cap*—a dowry for a wife you may yet wed. The choice is yours, but the temptation is the test of every man's art."

Cennini locked the door, leaving Tito alone with the silence and the glint of gold. The smile died instantly from his lips. He stood rigid, thumbs hooked in his belt, staring down at the florins as if they were not currency but a verdict.

"A man's ransom!" The phrase echoed in the hollow of his mind. Who had spoken it? Five hundred florins was the price of a life, yet it was also the cost of a conscience.

If, under this relentless midday sun, a man of advanced years—stricken, yet possessing a heart of fire and a mind of high thought—was currently toiling as a slave in the heat, hewing wood and hauling water, buffeted by overseers for his sluggishness... if that man were Baldassarre Calvo, the father who had once pulled a starving boy from the gutter and raised him with a tenderness that bordered on divinity...

Then the math changed. If Baldassarre were alive, trapped somewhere in the Archipelago, waiting for the manuscripts and gems that Tito carried within him, then this pile of gold was not wealth; it was a ticket to redemption. Tito could raise funds, sail to Venice, track the survivor, and break the chains.

But the certainty required to justify such a leap was missing. The galley carrying the cargo had been intercepted by a Turkish vessel en route to Delos. That was the report from the companion ship that had escaped. Resistance had occurred; blood had likely been spilled. A man had been seen plunging into the sea. Was he the sole survivor? Or had the storm claimed them all?

Tito had faced shipwreck himself, clinging to wreckage while death hovered inches away. He knew the ocean's indifference. Rumors of pirate settlements on Delos were unreliable whispers, useful only to frighten the superstitious. If he abandoned Florence now, burning his bridges to the city's elite, selling his reputation, and rushing to Venice only to find the Archipelago empty or his father already dead... he would be left with nothing but debt and shame. He would be a wanderer again, poorer than when he started, having gambled his future on a ghost.

The vision of failure was sharper, more tangible, than the faint possibility of reunion. To turn his back on the soft airs of Florentine promise, on the adoration of Bartolommeo Scala, on the intellectual ascent awaiting him as a professor of Greek... it felt like a betrayal of his own potential. Yet, the alternative was a lifetime of wandering, searching for a man who might not exist.

"Do I not owe something to myself?" Tito whispered, the words scraping against his throat. He shifted his weight, the first movement he'd made since the door clicked shut. "Before I abandon everything to a desperate gamble, I require a reasonable hope. Do I possess it? No. I believe he is dead. Cennini is right; I will entrust these florins to him tomorrow."

By the next morning, the decision hardened into steel. In choosing to bury the truth, Tito had inadvertently chosen his fate. He had painted himself into a corner where the only logical exit was to pretend the past never happened. He had made it impossible for himself to accept anything less than the conclusion that Baldassarre was lost. The temptation to lie, to protect his own comfort and status, became stronger than the memory of the father who had once called him his own. The facts of his conduct remained sealed, forever concealed, as the weight of the gold settled firmly onto his shoulders.

"Three weeks past the Feast of St. John," Tito noted, his stride light and unhurried as he navigated the sun-drenched thoroughfares toward the Via de'Bardi. The weight of his florins, recently transferred to Cennini, rested easy in his mind; he carried no shadow of anxiety, for his spirit was too buoyant to harbor dread. As he stepped from the glare into the cool recess of a narrow alley, removing his black *berretta* to toss his brown curls back against the breeze, his countenance remained unreadable—not deceitful, yet not entirely candid either. It was simply the honest, smooth profile of a young man unburdened by pretense. His gaze, drifting lazily across the upper windows of the surrounding dwellings, possessed a pellucid clarity, devoid of guile or naive simplicity alike. Whether his presence drew others in or pushed them away depended entirely on the observer's disposition, though the prevailing sentiment was one of admiration for his striking, negative void of vanity.

By now, the fervor of the *Festa* had spilled even into the tightest side streets. The massive crowds, previously funneling through the procession routes, now fractured into chaotic streams chasing fleeting diversions. These interludes were perilous moments for the herd mentality, where the collective animal spirits grew petulant and prone to sacrificing the solitary individual for the group's amusement. Entering the district of San Martino, Tito encountered a thickening throng. Near the *Bertucce*, or Baboon, inn, a knot of spectators formed around a specific disturbance. Withdrawing from the main flow required no grand cause, yet Tito hesitated before turning aside when a high-pitched, distressed cry pierced the laughter: "Loose me! Holy Virgin, help me!"

Driven by instinct, he surged into the cluster. The source emerged instantly: a young peasant girl, her white hood discarded in the struggle, fighting to escape the grip of a *cerretano*—a conjurer dressed in garish, multi-colored attire. The performer attempted to soothe her with practiced charm, earning the amused sympathy of the onlookers who mocked the girl with a vocabulary of Florentine synonyms for 'simpleton.' Initially, Tito saw only the back of her head, adorned with light-brown braids pinned by a silver filigree. But the struggle twisted her face toward him, revealing Tessa. Her infantile features were flushed with tears, her lower lip trembling violently. Yet, in the mist of her distress, a spark of recognition ignited. Like a child suddenly recognizing a familiar hand amidst strangers, Tessa's eyes locked onto Tito's, and a fragile hope bloomed within them.

Without hesitation, Tito breached the circle of indifferent bystanders. Seizing Tessa's waist, he demanded, "Release this child! By what right do you detain her against her will?"

The conjurer, a man with facial angles—eyebrows, nose, jaw—all tilting upward in a perpetual sneer, grinned impishly. He released the girl's hands, stretching his arms back in a theatrical shrug. "I harbor no ill intent, Messere. Ask this respectable assembly. I sought merely to demonstrate my artistry, aided by this damsel's kittenish visage, promising her a handful of confetti as recompense. But surely, Messere, you possess superior confections at hand?"

A ripple of laughter erupted among the crowd, fueled by the visible relief in Tessa's posture as she clutched Tito's arm. She regarded the jeers with the indifference of prey fleeing predators, caring only for the noise of the beasts' retreat. Tito, however, sensed the precariousness of the situation. Having secured her on his arm, he moved swiftly to distance them from spectators deprived of their entertainment, eager to restore equilibrium before further mockery ensued.

"Behold, little one! Your hood," the conjurer called out, flinging the white drapery over Tessa's head. "*Orsù*, bear me no malice; return when Messere permits."

"Aha! Maestro Vaiano, she'll return shortly, just as the toad returned to the harrow," a spectator quipped, noting Tessa's recoil.

Vexed by the delay, Tito propelled himself toward a side street, intending to shed the burden of the peasant girl. Yet, the next thoroughfare offered no respite; holiday-goers continued to remark on the unusual pairing. Before they could proceed, Tito adopted a brisk, paternal tone. "Where were you bound, little one? Did you arrive alone for the *Festa*?"

"No!" Tessa whispered, her fear returning. "I've lost my mother... and my father-in-law. They will be furious—he will strike me. In the crowd at San Pulinari, someone shoved me, and I couldn't stop. I escaped them. Oh, I don't know where they've gone! Please, don't abandon me!"

Fresh tears welled, culminating in a sob. Tito accelerated his pace. The Church of the Badia loomed nearby; they could seek refuge in its cloister. Though the church doors were closed, the covered arcade provided temporary shelter. "Do you have kin in Florence, my little Tessa, where you might seek asylum? Or are you paralyzed by fear following that conjurer's trick?" he asked, glancing ahead. "I am pressed for time to reach Oltrarno, but if I can escort you partway—"

"I am terrified," she choked out. "He was the Devil—I knew it. I have nowhere to go. My mother intended to dine elsewhere, and I know not where. Holy Madonna! I shall be beaten."

Her mouth curled downward in piteous despair, her small bosom, marked by rosary beads atop a green serge gown, heaving with suppressed anguish. A loud sob threatened to break loose, tears cascading as if to compensate for lost time. The dilemma was acute: cruelty was unthinkable given Tito's gentle nature, yet his mission awaited. Regretting his deviation from the Via de'Bardi, he watched her lift her holiday apron to wipe her streaming eyes. Instinctively, he mirrored the gesture, pressing his hand to her cheek and kissing the soft roundness.

"My poor little Tessa," he murmured. "Cease your weeping. Let us ascertain a solution. Where is your dwelling?"

Silence answered, save for the diminishing sobs.

"Very well. I shall accompany you a short distance, provided you reveal your destination."

The apron dropped, and Tessa's countenance transformed, blossoming with the serene contentment of a cherub emerging from clouds. The conjurer, the threat of violence, receded into memory.

"I believe I shall return home if you will guide me," she whispered, gazing up with wide, azure eyes that held a sweetness beyond a mere smile—a profound, childlike tranquility.

"Then come, little one," Tito cooed, reclaiming her arm. "Which direction lies your path?"

"Beyond Peretola—to the great pear-tree."

"Peretola? Through which gate, *pazzarella*? Remember, I am a stranger."

"The Por del Prato," Tessa replied, gripping his arm tightly as they walked.

Unfamiliar with every winding lane, and aware that crowded areas offered the best chance to intercept Monna Ghita and conclude his quest, Tito steered them directly toward Porta Rossa and onward to Ognissanti. He maintained his radiant, appeasing demeanor, absorbing the jeers of the mixed throng—frolicking apprentices jealous of his luck, bold-eyed women veiled in yellow, beggars mocking his haste, sharpers, and rowdy boys accustomed to brutal street games. The streets of Florence were rarely a moral tableau in those eras, and Tessa's terror of getting lost was entirely justified.

Upon reaching the Piazza d'Ognissanti, Tito slowed. Both were breathless from the hurried trek. The expansive square allowed them to pause on a stone bench affixed to the ancient wall.

"Holy Virgin!" Tessa exclaimed. "We have escaped those women and boys. Yet, I was not truly frightened, for you protect me."

"Pretty little Tessa," Tito smiled. "What grants you such security with me?"

"Because you are so beautiful—like those entering Paradise. They are all good."

Noticing stalls laden with fruit and sweets nearby, Tito observed her hunger. "You haven't eaten since morning, Tessa. Are you famished?"

"Yes, I suppose so—if you care to share."

Tito purchased apricots, cakes, and comfits, depositing them into her apron. "Let us continue to the Prato; perhaps then you will feel brave enough to finish the journey alone."

"But you will miss your treats," Tessa protested, rising obediently to gather her apron as a makeshift bag.

"We shall see," Tito declared aloud, though inwardly he reflected: *Here is a peasant girl capable of inspiring an idyll surpassing Lorenzo de'Medici's 'Nencia da Barberino,' a tale his friends currently laud. Were I a Theocritus, or possessed of the leisure to cultivate such pastoral experiences through these untimely excursions!* He sighed internally. *However, the damage is done. I am already late; another half-hour makes no difference. Pretty little pigeon.*

"We possess a garden abundant with pears," Tessa chimed in. "Two cows, plus mules. I adore them. Yet my father-in-law is a cross man. I wish my mother hadn't wed him. He is wicked, terribly ugly."

"And does your mother permit him

"Monk is a Florentine," Tito muttered to himself as he turned from the church of Santa Felicita, the weight of the decision settling heavily upon him. "If he stays in Florence, everything must be disclosed."

A new crisis had seized him, yet he moved with a calculated composure, suppressing the agitation that threatened to betray his inner turmoil. He proceeded along the Via de' Bardi toward the house of Bardo, intending to apologize for his absence. His talent for concealment was rapidly evolving from a passive trait into a weaponized skill. While it remained theoretically possible—and perhaps inevitable—that he should openly acknowledge the altered circumstances and confess Baldassarre's existence, such an admission would cast a damning retroactive light on his previous silence. It would frame his earlier reticence not as prudence, but as studied equivocation designed to evade a recognized claim, compounded by the fact that he had already settled his own affairs and invested his florins before his benefactor's fate was confirmed.

For now, the tactically superior move was to feign ignorance. He would suspend decisive thought until the darkness of the night allowed for private meditation, ensuring no one could pinpoint the exact hour he received the incriminating letter. Entering the second-story apartment where Romola and her father sat amidst parchment and marble, aloof from the street's chaos, Tito masked his anxiety with a brightness that barely concealed his regret at arriving late—a regret born of sacrificing the spectacle of the Corso to prioritize this meeting. He projected extra animation onto the evening conversation, though his mind operated like a complex engine, depositing thoughts distinct from the flow of polite discourse. By the time he descended the stone stairs and emerged into the starlight, his psyche had crystallized into a new strategic phase.

The following day, freed from his professorial duties, Tito turned up the Via del Cocomero toward the convent of San Marco. His purpose was now fully formed: he intended to interrogate Fra Luca regarding the extent of his suspicions and the evidence supporting them, alongside the duration of the friar's stay. Armed with this intelligence, he planned to construct a statement that would absolve him of the necessity of fleeing Florence.

Tito had never before fabricated an intricate deception, yet the occasion demanded it. Circumstance invariably breeds opportunity for tacit falsity, and his ingenuity was primed. He had convinced himself that he was under no obligation to hunt for Baldassarre. He had previously stated that a fair assurance of his father's location would compel him to pursue him, but he now questioned the absolute imperative. Why was he bound to go? Ultimately, the goal of life was to extract the maximum sum of pleasure. Was not his own blooming vitality a promise of incomparable joy—not only for himself but for others—far surpassing the withered, rigid existence of a man past the prime of enjoyment? These ideas, sown in the fertile soil of his young mind, were vibrant germs aligned with the natural order, which treated maturity merely as a nursery for youth.

Baldassarre had completed his cycle; he had drawn his draft of life. It was Tito's turn. The prospect was vague—"I think they are taking me to Antioch"—yet it represented a vast horizon. After a long voyage, he could spend months or years searching for a prize with no guarantee of success, abandoning a life of distinction and love behind him. Even if he found something, he risked returning to the old, exacting companionship he knew by rote. Technically, the gems and the florins belonged to Baldassarre in the narrow legal sense of possession. However, in the broader, radical view of nature where the world belongs to youth and strength, they rightfully belonged to whoever could derive the greatest pleasure from them.

Tito was acutely aware that societal norms expected him to immediately apply those funds to his benefactor's rescue. But what was the sentiment of society? A tangled web of anomalous traditions and opinions, useful only as a guide for personal comfort. He did not cherish the florins solely for their own sake; he would relinquish them readily for Romola. What he craved was the joy hovering close to his lips, the sweetness of existence he felt no moral compulsion to deny himself. Maxims demanding the rejection of necessary goods for the sake of abstract duty were merely the lining of human selfishness turned outward, crafted by those wishing others to sacrifice for their benefit.

Ideally, he wished Baldassarre to suffer no harm; he disliked pain in anyone. Yet, could any philosophy prove he was obligated to value another's suffering more than his own? Only if he loved Baldassarre devotedly. He did not. Was that his fault? Gratitude, scrutinized closely, held no valid claim. Baldassarre's life would have been dreary without Tito; were humans truly indebted for the pleasures others generate for themselves?

Once initiated, Tito's reasoning acted as a virulent acid, dissolving the tissues of sentiment. His mind lacked the dread often dismissed as mere animal self-preservation—the awe of Divine Nemesis felt by religious pagans or Christians alike. That terror of the unseen, which serves as a guardian of the soul, forcing wisdom upon men even under the full sunshine, was absent. Without it, the hard scrutiny of imperfect thought could probe obligations lacking sanctity. As he entered the outer cloister of San Marco and inquired for Fra Luca, there was no shadowy presentiment in his mind; he considered himself too cultured and skeptical for such superstition, nurtured in contempt for the impudent lives of priests and erudite familiarity with disputes on the Chief Good, which he deemed a matter of mere taste.

Yet, fear remained a potent element in his nature: specifically, the fear of losing pleasure. He harbored a definite dread that Fra Luca might be the instrument of his expulsion from Florence.

"Fra Luca?" a *fra converso* answered, his accent marking him as a raw peasant (*contadino*), his dull gaze offering no curiosity. "Ah, he is gone to Fiesole—to the Dominican monastery there. He was taken on a litter in the cool of the morning. The poor Brother is very ill. Could you leave a message for him?"

"Thanks; my business can wait."

Tito turned away with a profound sense of relief. "This friar is unlikely to survive," he reasoned internally. "I saw he was worn to a shadow. At Fiesole, there will be nothing to recall me to his mind. Besides, if he returns, my current explanation will suffice. Still, I wonder what it was about his face that struck me."

"Prize nearly grasped," the title declared, yet for Tito, the victory felt less like a conquest and more like a precarious balance. The paralyzing dread that had gripped him earlier had evaporated, replaced by the buoyant rhythm of his natural disposition. He was moving toward Romola, drawn by a magnetic pull he could no longer resist. Yet, as he visualized her, he saw only the epitome of dutiful endurance—a woman defined by patient suffering and the acceptance of irksome burdens. This was precisely the path he had fled, the very terrain from which he had constructed his excuses.

But Tito was not a man devoid of virtue, nor was he prepared to surrender entirely to vice. He was still in the flush of youth, his pulse quickened by every charm and loveliness he encountered. He retained a robust appetite for the ordinary joys of humanity. The poison of corruption worked slowly, seeping in drop by drop. He had sold himself to evil, but currently, life appeared unchanged to him; he remained oblivious to the invisible chains binding him. His intention was to maintain the status quo, both internally and externally. He planned to win the admiration of others through meritorious effort, through ingenious scholarship, and through compliant amiability. He sought no disruption of the harmony he shared with those he cherished. Above all, he loved Romola; he envisioned her as his beautiful, loving wife. While a wealthier alliance might theoretically await someone of his eventual success, no woman in Florence possessed the radiance of Romola. When she stood near him, regarding him with her sincere hazel eyes, he was overwhelmed by a delicious influence as potent and inevitable as a musical vibration that commands a rhythmic empire, demanding repetition the moment it ceases.

As he ascended the stone stairs, still outside the threshold, accompanied only by Maso, this anticipatory influence began its work simply through proximity.

"Welcome, Tito mio," the old man's voice called out before Tito could speak. There was a renewed vigor in the tone, a fresh cheerfulness illuminating the blind face, a transformation evident since their first meeting over two months prior. "You have surely brought fresh manuscripts; but since we spoke last night, I have conceived new ideas. We must broaden our scope—we must retrace our steps."

Tito rendered his customary homage to Romola as he advanced, proceeding directly to Bardo's chair. As was his habit, he placed his hand into the palm extended to receive it, settling onto the cross-legged leather seat with its scrolled ends, positioned close to Bardo's elbow.

"Yes," Tito replied in his gentle manner. "I have brought the new manuscript, but that can wait until your pleasure dictates. I possess young limbs, you know, and can walk back up the hill without difficulty."

He avoided looking at Romola, yet he knew with absolute certainty that her gaze was fixed upon him with delight.

"That is well said, my son," Bardo remarked, having adopted this form of address for Tito on several recent occasions. "And I perceive with gladness that you do not shrink from labor. Without it, the poet has wisely observed, life offers nothing to mortals. Too often, young ambition is seduced by the 'palma sine pulvere,' the prize of glory without the dust of the race. But what does the Greek say? 'In the morning of life, work; in the mid-day, give counsel; in the evening, pray.' It is true, I might be thought to have reached that helpless evening; but I am not so, while I retain counsel within me that remains unspoken. For my mind, as I have often stated, was sealed shut like a dam; the plentiful waters lay dark and motionless behind it. But you, my Tito, have opened a duct for them, and they surge forward with a force that surprises even myself. And now, what I require is that we revisit our preliminary grounds with a wider scheme of commentary and illustration. Otherwise, I risk losing opportunities I now recognize retrospectively, chances that may never recur. Do you understand what I am saying, Tito?"

Bardo had just stooped to retrieve his manuscript, which had rolled away, and his jealous ear caught the slightest movement. Tito might have been excused for shrugging at the prospect ahead, but he was not naturally impatient. Furthermore, he had been raised in that laborious erudition, simultaneously minute and copious, which constituted the primary intellectual pursuit of the age. With Romola nearby, he was carried along by waves of agreeable sensation that rendered every task effortless.

"Assuredly," Tito responded. "You wish to expand your comments on certain passages we have cited."

"Not merely that," Bardo corrected. "I intend to introduce occasional *excursus* where we discuss authors to whom I have devoted special study. For I may die too soon to produce any separate work. Moreover, this is not a season for scholarly integrity and sifted learning to remain idle. We must fear not only rash ignorance, but men like Calderino. As Poliziano has demonstrated, they resort to impudent falsifications of citation to serve their vanity and secure triumphs for their own errors. Therefore, my Tito, I deem it ill-advised to let slip the opportunity lying beneath our hands. Let us return to the point where we cited the passage from Thucydides. First, I wish you to accompany me through all my notes on the Latin translation by Lorenzo Valla. The incomparable Pope Nicholas the Fifth—with whose personal notice I was honored while I was still young, when he was yet Thomas of Sarzana—paid him, I say not unduly, the sum of five hundred gold scudi. Yet, since Valla, though otherwise of dubious fame, is held in high honor for his severe scholarship, whence the epigrammatist jocosely noted that since he descended to the shades, Pluto himself dares not speak in the ancient languages, it becomes imperative that his name not serve as a stamp warranting false goods. Thus, I propose introducing an *excursus* on Thucydides, wherein my castigation of Valla's text may find its proper place. My Romola, you shall fetch the necessary volumes—you know them—on the fifth shelf of the cabinet."

Tito rose simultaneously with Romola, declaring, "I will fetch them, provided you indicate their location," and hurried into the adjoining small room. Here, the walls were lined with ranges of books arranged in perfect order.

"There they are," Romola pointed upward. "Every book rests exactly where it was when my father ceased to see them."

Tito stood beside her, delaying the act of reaching for the books. They had never occupied this space together before.

"I hope," she continued, turning her eyes fully upon Tito with a look of grave confidence—"I hope he will not weary you; this work brings him such happiness."

"And me too, Romola—if you will only allow me to say, I love you—if you will only consider me worthy of a little love."

His words emerged as the softest murmur. The dark, beautiful face, drawn closer to hers than ever before, regarded her with beseeching tenderness.

"I do love you," Romola whispered. She looked at him with the same simple majesty as always, yet her voice had never before sunk to such a murmur. It seemed to both of them that they gazed into each other's souls for a prolonged duration before her lips moved again; yet it was but a fleeting moment until she confessed, "I know *now* what it is to be happy."

Their faces met, and the dark curls mingled for an instant with the rippling gold. Swift as lightning thereafter, Tito stepped onto a projecting ledge of the bookshelf and retrieved the required volumes. Both were content to remain silent and separate, for that first blissful experience of mutual consciousness was all the more exquisite for being unperturbed by immediate sensation.

Everything had unfolded as rapidly as the irreversible mingling of waters; even the eager and jealous Bardo had not grown impatient.

"You have the volumes, my Romola?" the old man asked as they returned. "Now prepare your pen. Since Tito marks off the scholia we decide to extract, it is essential that you copy them without delay—mind numbering them carefully to correspond with the numbers in the text which he will write."

Romola always possessed some task granting her a share in this joint endeavor. Tito took his stand at the *leggio*, where he both wrote and read, while she positioned herself at a table just in front of him, ready to deliver anything her father might request or relieve him of a volume he had finished. They had always maintained this arrangement since the work commenced, yet today it felt novel. It was so different now for them to sit opposite one another; so different for Tito to take a book from her as she lifted it from her father's knee. Yet, there was no finesse employed to secure an additional glance or touch. Each woman creates in her own likeness the tokens of love offered to her; and Romola's deep, calm happiness enveloped Tito like the rich, quiet light of an evening that dissipates all unrest.

Two hours had passed in their work, and they were just pausing as the light began to fade, when the door opened and a figure entered, strikingly incongruous with the current of their thoughts and the suggestions of every object in the room. It was the silhouette of a short, stout woman with black eyes, approximately fifty years of age. She wore a black velvet *berretta*, or close cap, embroidered with pearls, beneath which surprisingly massive black braids surmounted a little bulging forehead and fell in rich, plaited curves over her ears. The afternoon of September seventh moved with a languid, oppressive heat, two months since the confession that bound Romola and Tito together. Inside Nello's shop, the atmosphere was thick with the dust of idle days. Tito stepped down from the threshold to find the barber sprawled across the bench, a cap pulled low over his eyes. One leg was drawn up, the other slipping toward the floor, dragging a manuscript volume of verse behind it; the pages lay crushed, a testament to the barber's surrender to sleep. In the corner, Sandro sat alone, engaged in a solitary game of *mora*, his left fingers responding to the arithmetical demands of his right with a solemn, rhythmic precision.

Tito moved with a silent tread, snatching up the lute. He bent over the sleeping man, plucking the strings softly as he sang Lorenzo de' Medici's carnival song: *"Quant' è bella giovinezza / Che si fugge tuttavia! / Chi vuol esser lieto sia, / Di doman non c'è certezza."*

*"Beauteous is life in blossom! And it fleeteth—fleeteth ever; Whoso would be joyful—let him! There's no surety for the morrow."*

Nello woke instantly, as easily startled as a bird from a branch. The cap flew from his eyes. "Ah, my Apollino!" he exclaimed, springing upright. "I seem to have overslept on this infernal heat. The fault lies in not sleeping naturally, but ingesting a potion of potent poetry. Listen to me matching my syllables to their initials, like a *Trovatore*. That is one of my afflictions: I fear the fine wine of my intellect is running off at the spigot of authorship, leaving me an empty cask smelling of dregs, like so many geniuses I know."

"What is it, my Orpheus?" Nello extended his arms fully, then curled them inward to grasp Tito's curls, pulling them out playfully. "What do you seek from your well-tamed Nello? I hear a coaxing strain in your music. Show me the needle's eye of your desire so I may thread it."

"That is merely a tailor's metaphor," Tito replied, his fingers drifting lightly over the strings. "Yet you have divined the source of my impatience. I require an extra measure of your artistry—not upon my chin, but upon the *zazzera*, which is as tangled as Florentine politics. Your comb works with adroitness, flattering the skin and stirring the animal spirits in that region. A touch of your delicate orange-scent would be fitting, for I am bound for the Scala palace. The young Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici attends, accompanied by Bernardo Dovizi of Bibbiena, whose wit moves so rapidly that I see no defense against it save the fragrance of orange blossoms."

Nello had already seized his comb, flourishing it before pushing Tito gently backward into the chair and wrapping a cloth around him. "Never speak of rivalry, *bel giovane mio*: Bernardo Dovizi is a keen youth who will never cast a net to catch the wind. Yet he bears the sharp-muzzled look of his brother, Ser Piero, the weasel Piero de' Medici employs to slip through small holes. No! You distance all rivals and may soon touch the sky with your forefinger. They claim you have carried enough honey to sweeten the sour Messer Angelo; he has pronounced you less of an ass than expected, given the good understanding between you and the Secretary."

"And between ourselves, Nello mio, Messer Angelo possesses more genius and erudition than all other Florentine scholars combined. It suits them to praise me now that Poliziano is bowed by grief or illness. I might attempt a flight with a sparrow-hawk like Pietro Crinito, but Poliziano is a large-beaked eagle who would swallow me, feathers and all, without noticing the loss."

"I will not contradict your modesty," Nello said smoothly. "We clever Florentines do not repeat the same truths daily; we cry down Dante and cry up Francesco Cei simply for variety. If we hail you as a new Poliziano, heaven ensures the lie remains manageable. Besides, are you not a pattern of virtue in this wicked city? With your ears double-waxed against siren invitations that would lure you from the Via de' Bardi and the great work destined to astonish posterity?"

"Posterity," Tito murmured, "whom it will probably astonish as the universe does, by the impossibility of discerning the plan."

"Yes, similar prophecies circulated recently. Cristoforo Landino remarked that the excellent Bardo is one of those scholars who lie overthrown in their learning, like cavaliers in heavy armor, only to rage because they are over-ruled. A pithy observation, though it seems Messer Cristoforo is the very pearl of such commentary, feeding men with an empty spoon and gagging them with vain expectation. *Ecco!* You are perfect now."

Nello withdrew the cloth. "Impossible to add further grace! But love is not always fed on learning, is it? I shall have to dress the *zazzera* for the betrothal soon, shall I not?"

"Perhaps," Tito smiled, "unless Messer Bernardo recommends that Bardo require me to yoke a lion and a wild boar to the car of the Zecca before I can win my Alcestis. Yet I confess he is right; I am unworthy of Romola. She is a Pleiad that may grow dim by marrying any mortal."

"*Gnaffè*," Nello scoffed. "Your modesty is misplaced. Fate seems to have measured and chiseled you for the niche left empty by the old man's son. By the way, Cronaca tells me he is now at San Marco. Did you know?"

A faint electric shock passed through Tito as he rose, though he concealed it by stooping to flatten the crushed leaves of the fallen book. "No; I thought he was at Fiesole. Are you certain he has returned to San Marco?"

"Cronaca is my authority," Nello shrugged. "I do not frequent that sanctuary, but he does. Ah," he added, taking the book from Tito's hands, "my poor Nencia da Barberino! It jars your scholarly sensibilities to see the pages dog-eared. I was lulled by the well-rhymed charms of that rustic maiden—'prettier than the turnip-flower,' 'with a cheek more savory than cheese.' But to acquire such a well-scented notion of the *contadina*, one must lie on velvet cushions in the Via Larga, not attend the *Fierucoloni* stampeding into the Piazza della Nunziata this evening after sundown."

"And pray, who are the *Fierucoloni*?" Tito asked indifferently, settling his cap.

"The country women from the mountains of Pistoia and the Casentino, and wherever else, keeping vigil in the church of the Nunziata to sell their yarn and dried mushrooms at the *Fierucola*, as we call it. They make a queer spectacle with their paper lanterns, howling hymns to the Virgin on her nativity eve. If you had the leisure..."

"No?" Nello interrupted. "Well, I have had enough. There is wild work in the Piazza today. One may receive stones about the ears or shins without asking for them, and I have never favored such pressing attention. *Addio*."

Tito departed carrying a subtle unease. His visit ended sooner than anticipated due to the absence of the boy-cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, the youngest of the red-hatted fathers who would later present his broad dark cheek to posterity as Pope Leo X. Detained at his favorite pursuit, the chase, the Cardinal had failed to appear.

It was nearly half an hour before sunset when Tito left the Scala palace, intending to proceed immediately to the Via de' Bardi. He had not traveled far when, to his astonishment, he saw Romola advancing along the Borgo Pinti. She wore a thick black veil and mantle, yet her figure and gait were unmistakable. Beside her walked a short, stout form: Monna Brigida, recognizable despite her unusually plain attire.

Romola had not been raised for devotional observances outside the loggia on her father's roof, occasions so rare and heavily premeditated due to Bardo's jealousy that Tito assumed there must be urgent grounds for her absence unheard of the previous day. She spotted him through her veil and quickened her pace.

"Romola, has anything happened?" Tito asked, turning to walk beside her.

She remained silent initially. Monna Brigida interjected. "Ah, Messer Tito, you do well to turn round; we are in haste. Is it not a misfortune? We are obliged to circumvent the walls via the Via del Maglio because of the Fair. The *contadine* blocking the road to the Nunziata would delay us to San Marco by half the time."

Tito's heart hammered violently. "Romola," he whispered, "are you going to San Marco?"

They had emerged from the Borgo Pinti and were now beneath the city walls, where wide gardens lay to their left and all was quiet. Romola lifted her veil slightly to breathe, revealing a face of subdued agitation.

"Yes, Tito mio," she said, meeting his gaze with sad eyes. "For the first time, I am doing something unknown to my father. It comforts me to meet you, for at least

The cacophony of the Piazza exploded against Tito's ears—a chaotic symphony of shouting, hooting, and clashing instruments that instantly triggered the memory of Nello's warning regarding the *Fierucoloni*. Instead of recoiling, Tito surged forward into the throng, deriving a grim satisfaction from the physical jostling. The collision of bodies served as a necessary distraction, numbing the paralyzing dread of solitude that threatened to consume him as he contemplated his future: a lonely wanderer chasing phantom fortunes, or perhaps, finally turning back to seek out Baldassarre.

From the opposing archways, fresh waves of humanity poured into the square, illuminated by crude paper lanterns bobbing precariously on wooden poles. A rhythmic, monotonous chant provided a structural backbone to the noise, overlaid by shrieks, whistles, mocking songs from boys' high voices, the thunder of drums, and the tinkling of bells. Periodically, a floating lantern would be shattered by a stray stone hurled in a fit of vandalism, eliciting a fresh wave of panic and renewed jeering. Yet, amidst this whirlwind of destruction, pockets of order remained. On the periphery, ragged men darted sharp, predatory glances around them while their tongues clicked with manic glee, offering games of chance to the countryside folk on terms of deceptive generosity. Two figures masked and elevated on stilts, having snatched lanterns from the crowd, swayed the lights in erratic, meteoric arcs as they patrolled the space. Nearby, a shrewd merchant conducted brisk trade at a covered stall, dispensing *berlingozzi*, a dense flour-based delicacy favored by the masses. Another vendor, perched atop a barrel with his back braced against a pillar of the Loggia before the Foundling Hospital (*Spedale degli Innocenti*), hawked miracle pills concocted by a physician from Salerno, guaranteed to cure toothaches and prevent drowning deaths. Further down, a juggler displayed his acrobatic feats on a makeshift platform, while a group of apprentices, bored by the guerrilla-style stone-throwing, engaged in a concentrated, private tournament of their favorite Florentine sport at the narrow entrance of Via de' Febbrai.

Driven by the current, Tito found himself momentarily hemmed in beside a procession of barefoot peasant women. Their feet, hardened by calluses, struck the pavement with a heavy rhythm. Their faces were sun-leathered and bronze-toned, framed by garments of faded, obsolete fabrics caked with generations of dust. To the city dwellers, they appeared as spectral ancestors returning from a pilgrimage undertaken a century prior. These were the hardy women from the mountain ranges of Pistoia, carrying the tangible fruits of a year's labor in modest bundles of yarn strapped to their backs. Behind their stoic expressions lay a fragile calculus of hope and terror, anxieties entrusted solely to the intercession of the Blessed Virgin. It was believed that on this Eve of her Nativity, the miraculous image, painted by angels, would have its veil lifted, allowing its divine potency to flow unimpeded.

Elsewhere, Tito was shoved toward the quieter edges of the square, where opportunists had strategically positioned themselves to maximize profit while minimizing risk. Among these was Bratti, a known associate, leaning casually against a pillar. His lips were pursed in a mask of disdainful silence, scanning approaching customers with a cold, superior gaze, his hand gripping the edge of a serge cloth that concealed the contents of his basket. Surprised by such unusual composure in a desperate trader, Tito stepped closer. Two women approached the basket with tentative curiosity, prompting Bratti to pull the cloth tighter and avert his eyes. Provoked, one woman demanded to know the nature of his wares.

"Before I answer, Monna," Bratti retorted, his voice dripping with feigned pity, "I must ascertain if you intend to purchase. I cannot expose such select merchandise to every idle fly hoping for free inspection. My goods are too refined for that. Furthermore, I possess only two items remaining, and I have no desire to soil them. Given the rumors of pestilence circulating among the wise, these pieces could prove worth their weight in gold. So, *andate con Dio*."

The women exchanged uneasy glances. "And what is the price?" asked the second.

"Not within the reach of your purse, *buona donna*," Bratti replied, adopting a tone of compassionate arrogance. "I advise you to place your faith in Messer Domeneddio and the saints; the poor can do little for themselves."

"Not so poor!" the second woman snapped indignantly, producing her money-bag. "Come, then! What do you say to a *grosso*?"

"I state you may acquire it for twenty-one *quattrini*," Bratti countered coolly, "but not from me, for I lack such small change."

"Then two?" the woman insisted, her temper flaring as her companion watched with envy. "It will barely exceed two, I suspect."

Following a brief negotiation marked by mercantile coquetry, Bratti adopted an air of reluctant concession. "Since you are determined," he said, slowly lifting the cover, "I would be loath to cause you harm. Maestro Gabbadeo often remarked that when a woman desires something and fails to obtain it, she falls into greater peril of the plague than before. *Ecco!* I have but two left. Let me assure you, the charm resides on the finger of Maestro Gabbadeo, who has departed for Bologna—a physician as sagacious as any in the land."

The artifacts revealed were two clumsy iron rings, hammered into the style of ancient Roman jewelry occasionally unearthed. The thick rust coating them and the completely obscured mechanism of their supposed power rendered them perfectly satisfactory. The *grossi* changed hands without complaint. The first woman, lacking such currency, managed to drive a bargain using some of her yarn after displaying considerable reluctance, eventually departing with the remaining ring in triumph. Bratti quickly resealed his basket, now filled with miscellaneous odds and ends likely acquired under similar duress. As he moved away from the pillar, he collided unexpectedly with Tito, who, given the choice, would have preferred anonymity.

"By the head of San Giovanni," Bratti exclaimed, pulling Tito back against the stone column, "what a stroke of luck! I was discussing you this morning, Messer Greco. I noted he has ascended among the *signori*—a development I welcome, for I was at the bottom of his fortune. However, securing an audience with him is nearly impossible now; he is no longer to be found lounging on the stones. But this is your luck, Messer Greco, minus a trifling fee to compensate me for my efforts in this transaction."

"You speak in riddles, Bratti," Tito replied sharply. "Recall that I do not hone my intellect by haggling over iron rings; you must be direct."

"By the Holy Gospel! It was a generous deal I extended. If a Hebrew accepts thirty-two percent interest, surely a Christian deserves a marginally higher return. Had I possessed a clearer conscience, I would have doubled both the coin and the yarn. But speaking of rings... it is your ring—the very one on your finger—for which I can find a buyer. Yes, a buyer with a substantial purse."

"Really?" Tito asked, examining his own ring and listening intently.

"A Genoese traveler heading directly to Hungary, as I understand. He visited my shop recently, scrutinizing every item to determine its value; I wager he thought I carried a pumpkin on my shoulders. He had been scouring the shops of Florence. He wore a ring—not identical to yours, yet of similar design. Discussing rings, I mentioned knowing a promising young man, a close friend of mine, who owned such a piece. He responded, 'Who is he? Tell him I will pay top dollar.' I considered pursuing you to Nello's tomorrow. In my estimation, Messer Greco, you are not the type to watch the Arno turn to broth and remain passive without dipping a finger in."

Tito absorbed every word, though his mind raced elsewhere. Why retain the ring? It had been a sentimental impulse preventing its sale alongside his other gems. Had he acted with prudence earlier, selling it, he might have avoided the identification by Fra Luca. True, the ring had originally belonged to Baldassarre and transferred to Tito as his hand grew, yet superstition regarding inanimate objects held no logical validity. The ring had facilitated his exposure; Tito had developed a distaste for recognition, a demand imposed by the past. This foreigner's offer, assuming a genuine valuation, represented a viable exit strategy, eliminating the burden of sourcing a purchaser.

"You speak with your customary wisdom, Bratti," Tito conceded. "I have no objection to hearing your Genoese's bid. When and where shall we meet?"

"Tomorrow, three hours after sunrise. He will await you at my shop. If your faculties possess the acuity I have always attributed to you, Messer Greco, you will demand a steep price; he cares little for coin. My suspicion is he purchases on behalf of someone else, perhaps a high-ranking noble."

"It is settled," Tito agreed. "I shall attend your shop, barring unforeseen hindrances."

"And you will undoubtedly treat me honorably, considering our long-standing acquaintance, Messer Greco. Thus, I need not linger to negotiate the token sum you will provide for my services. It feels as though a millennium has passed since I left this square; a fair becomes tedious, bordering on malevolent, once one has exhausted one's inventory."

The heavy oak doors of San Marco groaned shut, sealing the noise of the city outside. Inside the cloister, the air was still and cool, smelling of wax and old stone. Monna Brigida retreated into the adjacent church, leaving Romola alone with the Frati. She was ushered to the threshold of the chapter-house, the boundary line where the sanctity of the enclosure ended and the infirmary began. No woman was permitted to cross further.

The door swung open, revealing a dim chamber illuminated only by the grey twilight filtering through the lattice and the flickering glow of two tapers set behind a narrow bed. Lying there was Fra Luca. His face was a map of emaciation, the skin stretched tight over bone, crowned by the golden tonsure. His hazel eyes, sunken deep into hollow sockets, were fixed on a small crucifix held tremblingly in his hand. He was propped upright, a fragile silhouette against the gloom. As Romola stepped forward, casting aside her veil, she registered the presence of another monk standing silently beside the bed, his black cowl pulled low, retreating toward the exit as she entered. Behind them, high on the frescoed wall, loomed the painted agony of Christ, pale and staring, with sorrowful faces peering down from the haloed heads of the apostles.

Then, Romola's gaze locked with Fra Luca's. From the crucifix, the monk's eyes lifted to meet hers. A sudden, sharp pang of recognition struck her, binding the emaciated figure before her to the memory of her fair, vibrant brother.

"Dino!" The word escaped her throat as a low cry of anguish. Yet, she did not lean in. She remained rigid, holding herself erect at a distance of two yards. An unconquerable repulsion surged within her. This monastic guise felt like a brand of dastardly ingratitude, the mark of a groveling superstition that could twist such betrayal into piety. Her father, a man of proud sincerity and simple living, had been one of the few frank pagans of his age. He had raised Romola to ignore the Church's claim to regulate the minds of rational beings. In her view, the Church belonged to the mixed multitude of the ignorant, separate from the cultivated intellect. Consequently, she viewed her brother's path not with religious awe, but with incurious, indignant contempt. Still, the loving current of her soul clung to the image of the boy she had known. Even as she stood immovable, a yearning search haunted her eyes, seeking something too faint to grasp.

But there was no corresponding warmth in the monk's face. He regarded his younger sister, returned to him in her full womanly beauty, with the distant gaze of a spirit revisiting a lost world.

"My sister," he whispered, his voice feeble yet distinct, broken by labored breaths. "It is well thou hast not delayed longer. I have a message to deliver, and my time is short."

Romola took a tentative step closer. She expected a confession of affectionate penitence directed at their father. Her heart began to soften; nothing could erase the long years of abandonment, but a guilty conscience looking back on irreparable harm often summoned pity. Perhaps, at the very end, there would be understanding. Dino would pour out natural filial feelings, asking about his father's failing sight, the darkness of his days, the life in the home where he had been fed. The final words from dying lips would surely be ones of tenderness and regret.

"Romola," Fra Luca began, "I have had a vision concerning thee. Thrice in the last two months, and each time it has grown clearer. Therefore, I came from Fiesole, convinced this was a mandate from heaven. And I gather a promise of mercy in this, that my breath is preserved..."

The difficult breathing cut off his sentence.

Romola felt her heart chill anew. A vision? One of those spectral intrusions her father had cited with bitter disdain? Indignation flared instantly.

"Dino, I thought you had words for my father. You deserted him when his sight failed; you made his life desolate. Do you care for that? Never repent? What is this religion of yours that places visions above natural duty?"

The deep-sunken eyes turned slowly toward her, resting in silence for several moments, as if weighing the cost of an answer.

"No," he said finally, his tone low and passionless, sounding less like a dying man and more like a spirit speaking through mortal instruments. "No. I have never repented fleeing the stifling poison-breath of sin that choked me, threatening to drown my senses like besotting wine. My father could not hear the voice calling me night and day; he knew nothing of the demon-tempters dragging me back. He lived amidst human sin and misery without believing in them, like one busy picking shining stones in a mine while a world died of plague above him. I spoke, but he listened with scorn. He told me the studies I desired were either childish trifles or must be warmed by worldly ambition and fleshly lusts—the very substances of the poetry and history he demanded I consume."

"Has my father not led a pure and noble life?" Romola burst out, unable to endure the implied accusation. "He sought no honors; he was truthful; he denied himself all luxuries. He lived like an ancient sage. He never wished you to serve worldly ambitions; he wished you to live as he did, according to the purest maxims of philosophy, which he instilled in you."

She spoke partly by rote, as ardent youth often do, but with intense conviction. A pink flush burned her cheeks, and she quivered from head to toe. Her brother was slow to respond, regarding her passionate face with strange, impassive eyes.

"What were the maxims of philosophy to me? They commanded strength when I felt weakness; when I was ready, like Saint Benedict, to roll among thorns and court wounds as deliverance. For the Divine Love had sought me, penetrating me, creating a vast need within me, like a seed demanding space to grow. Brought up in careless ignorance of the true faith, I had not studied its doctrines, yet it seized me like a rising flood. I perceived a life of perfect love and purity for the soul—a existence free of hungry craving, tormenting doubt, or fear of suffering. Before knowing the history of the saints, I foresaw their ecstasy. The same truth permeated pagan philosophy: that it is bliss to die to mortal needs and live in the life of God as the Unseen Perfection. But to attain it, I must forsake the world. No affection, no hope could wed me to that which passes away. I must relate to my fellow-beings only as souls connected to the eternal. This need urged me constantly. It overwhelmed me in visions when my mind grew weary of the vain words recording dead men's passions; it seized me after I was tempted into sin and turned away with loathing from the scent of the emptied cup. In these visions, I saw the meaning of the Crucifix."

He paused, breathing heavily for a minute or two. Romola did not prompt him. Contact with this unearthly brother's mind was futile, as useless as trying to grasp a shadow. When he spoke again, his heaving chest was calmer.

"I felt whom I must follow. But I saw that even among the servants of the Cross who claimed to have renounced the world, my soul would suffocate in the fumes of hypocrisy, lust, and pride. God did not choose me, as He chose Saint Dominic and Saint Francis, to wrestle with evil within the Church and the world. He called upon me to flee. I took the sacred vows and I fled—to lands where danger, scorn, and want bore me continually, like angels, to repose on the bosom of God. I lived as a hermit, ministering to pilgrims. But my task was short: the veil dividing me from my everlasting rest has worn very thin. I came back to Florence that—"

"Dino, you *did* want to know if my father was alive," Romola interrupted, the image of his suffering life stirring a desperate desire for union and forgiveness.

"—That before I die, I might urge our brethren to study the Eastern tongues, as I failed to do, and pursue greater ends. I find them already bent on this work. And since I arrived, Romola, I feel I was sent partly to thee—not to renew earthly bonds, but to deliver the heavenly warning conveyed in this vision. I have seen it thrice. Through all the years since the Divine Voice first called me, while I was still in the world, I have been guided by visions. For in the painful linking of our waking thoughts, we cannot be sure we have not mingled our own errors with the light we pray for; but in visions and dreams, we are passive, our souls mere instruments in the Divine hand. Therefore, listen, and speak not again—for the time is short."

Romola's mind recoiled from listening. Her indignation had subsided, only because she sensed the widening chasm between them. Just as Fra Luca spoke, the figure of the other monk entered, standing on the opposite side of the bed, his cowl drawn low.

"Kneel, my daughter," a strong, rich voice declared, startlingly contrasting with Fra Luca's frailty. "For the Angel of Death is present, waiting while the message of heaven is delivered. Bend thy pride before it is bent for thee by a yoke of iron."

The tone was not imperious, but carried a quiet self-possession and an assurance of right,

"Full up. No room."

The door slammed with the dull, decisive thud of bureaucratic finality.

Tito stood in the chill of the early morning, calculating the precise cost of his failure. To have walked the streets of Florence seeking employment, then begging for a night's rest in the casual ward, was a tactical error already committed. To be denied entry to that sanctuary of the destitute was a strategic defeat that threatened his entire campaign. He had lost his confidence in Fortune; the feverish excitement of his earlier alarms had curdled into a dull, regretful lassitude. He realized now that he had valued the fragile approval of his peers above the harsh necessities of survival, risking ignominy to avoid what they called obligation.

But actions, once taken, possess an indestructible vitality. Like children born to a parent, they live and act apart from the will of their creator. Deeds cannot be strangled; they persist in consciousness and beyond, pressing upon the actor with relentless force. For the first time, this dreadful vitality of his past deeds was crushing Tito.

He turned toward his lodgings in the Piazza di San Giovanni, deliberately avoiding the Mercato Vecchio—the shortest route—to evade the possibility of seeing Tessa. He was not in the mood to seek anything; he could only await the first sign of his altered lot.

The piazza was bathed in the warm sunlight of a festival morning, where autumn dew still lingered, inviting an idleness undulled by fatigue. Around the octagonal Baptistery, stalls were being spread with fruit and flowers, while laden mules stood quietly absorbed in their nose-bags, their drivers perhaps already kneeling before the Blessed Virgin within the sacred doors. On the broad marble steps of the Duomo, scattered groups of beggars and gossipers formed a chaotic tableau: an old crone with white hair encouraged a round-capped baby to test its tiny bare feet on the warmed stone, while a nearby dog sniffed the performance with suspicion; shaggy-headed boys watched a small, pale cripple carving a face onto a cherry-stone; and above them, on the wide platform, men practiced changing knots in desultory laughter or gesticulated eagerly in close pairs.

Yet the largest and most significant assembly of loungers awaited Tito. It was peak business hours for Nello, and in this warm season, clients preferred the shade of the pretty red-and-white awning outside the shop to the cramped interior. Sitting with a lathered chin thrown backward, allowing one's nose to serve as a handle, was not a noble posture, yet it was a fashion of Florentine respectability. Men looked grave and serious while undergoing this ritual, united by the shared absurdity of the moment. It was also the hour when yesterday's gossip was freshest, and the barber's tongue found its greatest glory alongside the razor. The deft activity of those two instruments seemed driven by a common spring. Tito foresaw that escape was impossible; he would have to smile, retort, and appear perfectly at ease. It was merely the ordeal of swallowing bread and cheese pills after all. The man who let the mere anticipation of discovery choke him possessed weak nerves.

Just then, a hand landed on his shoulder. No amount of prior resolution could prevent the unpleasant jolt of that sudden contact. As Tito turned, his face betrayed an inward shock, but the owner of the hand broke into a light laugh. He was a young man of Tito's own age, with keen features, a small, close-clipped head, and a closely shaven lip and chin, suggesting a mind stripped of unnecessary material. His eyes were bright with hope and friendliness, qualities that often vanished later in bitterness and disappointment. At this moment, however, there were only pleasant predictions surrounding Niccolò Machiavelli, a man of promise expected to restore the fortunes of his ancient family.

"Why, Melema," Machiavelli said, "what evil dream did you have last night that you mistook my light grasp for that of an *sbirro* or something worse?"

"Ah, Messer Niccolò!" Tito recovered instantly. "It must have been an excess of dulness in my veins this morning that shuddered at the approach of your wit. But the fact is, I have had a bad night."

"That is unfortunate, for you are expected to shine today in the Rucellai Gardens without any obstructing fog. I assume you are attending."

"Messer Bernardo invited me," Tito replied, "but I shall be engaged elsewhere."

"Ah! I remember—you are in love," Machiavelli shrugged. "Otherwise, you would never have such inconvenient engagements. We are to feast on peacock and ortolans under the loggia among Bernardo Rucellai's rare trees. There will be the finest spirits and wines in Florence. Only, since Piero de' Medici will be present, the choice spirits may be drowned in the capricious verses of impromptu poetry. I detest that game; it is a device for the triumph of small wits, who are most inspired by the smallest occasions."

"What are you saying about Piero de' Medici and small wits, Messer Niccolò?" asked Nello, whose slender figure currently dominated the Herculean frame of Niccolò Caparra.

The famous ironworker, seen earlier in the Mercato Vecchio with bared muscles and a leather apron, wore a holiday suit this morning. As he sat submissively while Nello skipped around him, applying lather, seizing his nose, and scraping with magical quickness, he resembled a lion donned in linen and tunic, preparing to enter society.

"A private secretary will never rise in the world if he couples great and small in that manner," Nello continued. "When great men are not permitted to marry their sons and daughters as they wish, small men must not expect to marry their words as they please. Have you heard the news Domenico Cennini was sharing? That Pagolantonio Soderini struck Ser Piero da Bibbiena across the ear for instigating Piero de' Medici to interfere with the marriage between young Tommaso Soderini and Fiammetta Strozzi, and is to be exiled as ambassador to Venice as punishment?"

"I do not know which I envy him most," Machiavelli remarked. "The offense or the punishment. The offense will make him the most popular man in all Florence, while the punishment will send him among the few in Italy who truly understand how to manage their own affairs."

"Yes, provided Soderini remains in Venice long enough," Cennini added. "He may adopt the Venetian style and bring it home. The Soderinis have long been allies of the Medici, but this event may finally open Pagolantonio's eyes to the utility of our old Florentine trick of swapping harnesses when the old one galls us—if we have not entirely lost that trick in the last fifty years."

"Not we," declared Niccolò Caparra, rejoicing in the free movement of his lips again. "Eat eggs during Lent and the snow will melt. That is what I tell our people when they grow noisy over their cups at San Gallo, discussing insurrection. I say: never plan a *romor*. You might as well try to fill the Arno with buckets. When there is sufficient water, the river will be full; but that will not occur until the torrent is ready."

"Caparra, that oracular speech is due to my excellent shaving," Nello quipped. "You could never have produced it with that dark rust on your chin. *Ecco*, Messer Domenico, I am ready for you now. By the way, my learned friend," he continued as he noticed Tito moving toward the door, "old Maso has been seeking you, but your nest was empty. He will return shortly. The old man looked mournful and seemed in haste. I trust there is no trouble in the Via de' Bardi."

"Doubtless Messer Tito knows that Bardo's son is dead," said Cronaca, who had just joined them.

Tito's heart leapt—had the death occurred before Romola saw him?

"No, I had not heard," he replied, maintaining composure equal to the occasion. He turned and leaned against the doorpost, feigning reluctance to depart. "I knew his sister went to see him. Did he die before her arrival?"

"No," Cronaca stated. "I was in San Marco at the time and saw her emerge from the chapter-house with Fra Girolamo. He told us the dying man's breath was preserved by a miracle, solely so he might disclose his secrets to his sister."

Tito felt his fate sealed. His mind raced over the circumstances of his departure from Florence, and he formulated a plan to retrieve his money from Cennini before the disclosure became public knowledge. If he secured his funds, he need not endure the scorching stares and biting words any longer. He would wait, accompany Cennini, and extract the payment immediately. Holding this project in his mind, he stood motionless, hands in his belt, eyes fixed absently on the ground.

Nello glanced at him, convinced Tito was consumed by anxiety regarding Romola. Finding such a pretty image of self-forgetful sadness, he subtly pointed his razor at Tito and shot a challenging look at Piero di Cosimo, whom he had never forgiven for refusing to acknowledge any prognostications of character in his favorite's handsome face. Piero, leaning against the opposite doorpost close to Tito, shrugged his shoulders. The frequent recurrence of such challenges from Nello had shifted the painter's initial neutrality into a positive inclination to believe ill of the much-praised Greek.

The loggia atop Bardo's house rose like a crown above the surrounding structures, forming a gallery that encircled the quadrangular walls. On the street-facing side, the roof was upheld by sturdy columns; on the others, a solid wall was pierced by arched openings. Through these arches, Romola could survey the chaotic sprawl of irregular, poorly built dwellings stretching toward the hill of Bogoli, enjoying a walk entirely shielded from prying eyes.

Near one of these arches, close to the entry point, Tito waited. Sunlight slanted before him, mingling with the ruin of his hopes, casting a sickening pallor over his anticipation. He had never truly relied on Romola's passion to withstand the repulsion born of his secret; he lacked the presumptuous vanity that might have blinded him to the fact that her love grew from the same soil as her belief in him. Yet, as he imagined her approaching in her radiant beauty, softened by the mortal tenderness of her soft hazel eyes, a desperate wish seized him: that she were something lower, merely flesh and blood, so he might clasp her and kiss her before they parted. He had received no real caress from her save for occasional glances, brief touches, and fleeting pressures of the hand. He had longed endlessly for solitude with her. Now, they were finally alone, yet she stood inexorably aloof.

His heart hammered a frantic rhythm as he sensed movement behind the door. Romola appeared. It struck him with the sudden, blinding intensity of lightning—not visually, but viscerally—he felt the glory haloing her head, the tearful appeal in her gaze. He felt, rather than heard, the cry of love erupting from her lips: "Tito!" In the same instant, she was in his arms, sobbing, her face pressed against his chest.

How desperately Romola had yearned through the long watches of the night to behold that bright face! The new specter of death, the bewildering doubt injected into her psyche by the story of a life alien to her understanding, the haunting vision she seemed not only to hear in the low, gasping voice of her brother but to inhabit as her own dream—it had rendered her more conscious than ever that Tito was the source of the warm stream of hope and gladness that had flooded her existence, turning away the keen edge of pain regarding her brother. She would confide in Tito; there was no other ear capable of hearing it. She had restrained herself in her father's presence all morning, but now, the dam of pent-up grief burst. Proud and self-possessed before the world, Romola became as simple and unreserved as a child in her devotion to Tito. Content with days spent merely gazing at one another, she now felt the urgent need to cling, with no inhibitions hindering her.

"My Romola! My goddess!" Tito murmured, clutching her with passionate fondness, kissing the thick golden ripples of her neck. He was in paradise; disgrace, shame, separation—none of it mattered anymore. This happiness was too potent to be tarnished by the knowledge that Romola was deceived; indeed, he rejoiced in her delusion. After all, concealment had been wisdom. The only regret he harbored was his needless dread, though perhaps that fear had been worth enduring for the sake of this sudden rapture.

The sob subsided, and Romola lifted her head. Silence stretched between them, filled only with the sweet wonder characteristic of young love. Her long white hands rested on his dark-brown curls; his dark fingers were bathed in the streaming gold of her hair. Each was the epitome of beauty to the other, experiencing that undisturbed mutual consciousness for the first time. A cold pressure of fresh sadness settled on Romola's heart, making her linger in that silent, soothing proximity. Tito dared not press his lips to hers, for that would alter the sacred nature of the moment.

"Tito," she said finally, her voice trembling slightly, "it has been altogether painful, but I must tell you everything. Your strength will help me resist the impressions that reason cannot shake off."

"I know, Romola—I know he is dead," Tito replied. The long, lustrous eyes betrayed nothing of the myriad wishes that would have summoned that death long ago if mere desire held such potency. Romola read her own pure thoughts reflected in their dark depths, much as one reads letters in a lucid dream.

"So changed, Tito! It pierced me to think it was Dino. And so strangely hard: not a word to my father, nothing but a vision he wanted to convey. Yet it was so pitiful—the struggling breath, the eyes that seemed to look toward the crucifix, yet failed to see it. I shall never forget it; it feels as though it stands between me and everything I shall ever look at."

Romola's heart swelled again, forcing her to pause. Yet the imperative need to unload her mind onto Tito urged her to suppress the rising anguish. When she resumed speaking, her thoughts had drifted.

"It was strange, Tito. The vision concerned our marriage, yet he knew nothing of you."

"What was it, my Romola? Sit down and tell me," Tito said, guiding her to the nearby bench. A fear crossed him lest the vision somehow implicated Baldassarre; this sudden shift prompted him to change positions.

Romola recounted every detail from her entrance into San Marco, omitting not a single word of her brother's speech, which had burned itself into her memory. But as she reached the climax of the vision, she faltered. The remainder remained too vivid to utter. She sat staring into the distance, momentarily unaware of Tito's presence.

*His* mind, however, was at ease. The vague vision had passed over him like white mist, leaving no trace. He remained silent, awaiting her continuation.

"I took it," she continued, as if Tito had read her thoughts aloud. "I took the crucifix; it is down below in my bedroom."

"And now, my Romola," Tito entreated, "banish these ghastly thoughts. The vision was an ordinary monkish hallucination, bred of fasting and fanatical ideology. Surely it holds no weight for you."

"No, Tito; no. But poor Dino... *he* believed it was a divine message. It is strange," she mused, "this life of men possessed by fervid beliefs that appear madness to their peers. Dino was not a vulgar fanatic; and that Fra Girolamo—his very voice seems to penetrate me with a sense that there is some truth in what moves them: some truth of which I know nothing."

"It was only because your feelings were highly wrought, my Romola. Your brother's state of mind was no more than a form of that theosophy, the common disease of excitable, dreamy minds throughout all ages; the same ideas your father's old antagonist, Marsilio Ficino, pores over in the New Platonists. Only your brother's passionate nature drove him to act out what others merely write and discuss. As for Fra Girolamo, he is simply a narrow-minded monk with a gift for preaching and instilling terror in the multitude. Any words, any voice, would have shaken you at that moment. When your mind has found repose, you will judge such matters as you have always done."

"Not about poor Dino," Romola insisted. "I was angry with him; my heart seemed to close against him while he spoke. But since then, I have thought less of my own mind and more of his. Oh, Tito! It was terribly pitiful to witness his young life ending thus. That yearning look at the crucifix as he gasped for breath—I can never forget it. Last night, I stared at the crucifix for a long time, trying to imagine how it could help him, until, by the lamplight, it seemed to me as if the suffering face shed pity."

"My Romola, promise me to resist such thoughts. They are fit for sickly nuns, not for my golden-tressed Aurora, who appears made to scatter all such twilight fantasies. Try not to dwell on them now; we shall not remain alone for long."

The final words were delivered in a tone of tender beseeching. He turned her face toward him with a gentle touch of his right hand. Romola had been gazing absently at the arched opening, failing to notice the distant hill. She had been trapped within the chapter house, observing the pale images of sorrow and death. Tito's touch and pleading voice recalled her to the present. Now, bathed in warm sunlight, she perceived that rich, dark beauty that seemed to gather around it images of joy: purple vines festooned between elms, robust corn maturing under the vibrating heat, bright-winged creatures hurrying and resting among the flowers, round limbs beating the earth in gladness with cymbals held aloft, light melodies chanted to the thrilling rhythm of strings—all objects and sounds testifying to Nature reveling in her force.

A strange, bewildering transition occurred in her mind, shifting from those pale specters of sorrow and death to this vibrant youthfulness, akin to a sun-god ignorant of night. What thought could reconcile the worn anguish on her brother's face, that straining reach for the invisible, with this satisfied strength and beauty, rendering them intelligible parts of the same world? Or was there no reconciliation, only a blind worship of clashing deities, first in mad joy, then in wailing? For the first time, Romola felt this questioning need like a sudden, uneasy dizziness, a void demanding something to grasp. It was an experience shorter than a sigh, for the eager theor

When Tito departed the Via de' Bardi that afternoon, his stride carried the confident rhythm of a man who had successfully evaded imminent catastrophe. The threat was gone, yet his mind, now unburdened by the immediate presence of Romola and her father, circled back to the paralyzing hours of terror he had endured. He realized with a jarring clarity that his actions during those moments had been driven by panic rather than strategy. Had he possessed true foresight, he would never have parted with his ring. To surrender a gem he had professed to cherish was a move that would strike Romola and her circle as sordid, unless couched in the elaborate fiction of purchasing a special gift. At that critical juncture, Tito had harbored a profound nausea toward the art of simulation. Fortunately, fate had intervened, granting him immunity from the dire consequences of that initial deception. The weight of potential ruin had vanished, leaving him convinced that Romola deserved this reprieve. Who truly suffered? The repercussions for Baldassarre remained too murky to calculate, but Tito felt a desperate need to sever every invisible tether binding him to Romola, however slight the constriction. He failed to recognize that the intoxication of this newfound freedom was simultaneously numbing the very sensibilities required to navigate future entanglements safely.

Ultimately, the sale of the ring was a trivial transaction. More pressing was the dilemma posed by little Tessa, whose mind was currently clouded by a delusion destined for inevitable disillusionment. Should he seek her out alone to dispel the illusion immediately, or allow time and circumstance to erode it naturally?
