Happy dreams were fleeting, dissolving under the harsh glare of daylight and the clamor of daily life. The image of her—sweet, pouting, innocent, and round—was impossible to ignore. Tito contemplated bringing her a gift to assuage her, perhaps using the occasion to gauge whether her stepfather had intensified his cruelty following her mother's death. Alternatively, he could expose the truth to her instantly, then inform Romola, arranging a better destiny for the child. Yet, the memory of the *cerretano* incident and his own negligence in allowing Tessa to depart under false pretenses created a rigid boundary: this must never reach Romola's knowledge. Since no amount of explanation could purge Tessa's mind of her belief, maintaining the status quo carried a perpetual risk of exposure. Furthermore, even a child like Tessa might harbor a bitter gall upon discovering her love had been misplaced. Yes, she undoubtedly loved him, and this affection necessitated his continued absence. Still, it was a minor episode, akin to a rural girl's infatuation that would eventually yield to the advances of a sturdy peasant, prompting her to abandon her secret and accept the truth of her liberty. *Dunque*—farewell, Tessa! May your wishes be fulfilled. Tito resolved that the affair of the *cerretano* would bear no further fruit for him; humans often mistake personal resolve for ironclad certainty. Regarding the fifty-five florins used to purchase the ring, he had formulated a plan to account for the missing gem while simultaneously inoculating Romola's mind against the resurgence of monastic fantasies, which repelled him intensely. With this objective fixed, he proceeded to the Via Gualfonda to locate Piero di Cosimo, the preeminent artist of the era, renowned for his fantastical mythological designs.

Tito entered the courtyard where Piero's residence opened, noting immediately that the heavy iron knocker was wrapped in thick wool and secured with intricate cordage. Recalling the painter's habit of isolating himself from auditory intrusion, Tito was unsurprised by these defensive measures. He began with tentative taps, escalating to vigorous shaking of the door, but received no response. Just as he prepared to retreat, chastising himself for squandering time on a futile errand when he could await the painter's appearance at Nello's, a small girl emerged carrying a basket of eggs. She approached the door, rose on her toes, and slid up a small iron plate set in grooves. Pressing her face to the aperture, she called out in a high, piping voice, "Messer Piero!" Moments later, the sound of bolts retracting echoed through the air. The door swung open, and Piero appeared clad in a red nightcap and a loose brown serge tunic, his sleeves rolled to the elbows. He cast a startled glance at Tito before extending a hand to claim the basket from the child. Re-entering the house briefly, he returned with the empty container and asked, "How much to pay?" "Two grossoni, Messer Piero," the girl replied, adding, "They are all freshly boiled; my mother insists." Piero extracted coins from a leather pouch at his belt, and the maid departed, casting upward glances of awed admiration at the imposing young gentleman.

Piero's gaze, however, lacked such courtesy. "What brings you to my door, Messer Greco?" he asked. "I saw you this morning at Nello's; had you inquired then, I would have informed you that I do not entertain strangers without prior consultation regarding their business." "Pardon, Messer Piero," Tito responded, his demeanor unflappable. "I acted without due deliberation. My mind was occupied solely by your unparalleled skill in crafting whimsical inventions, and a sudden urge to commission something similar drove me here." The painter's eccentricities were notorious throughout Florence, rendering this reception a non-event rather than an affront. Even if Tito had sensed hostility, his instinct to cultivate goodwill outweighed any resentment. Piero made a habitual grimace, stretching the corners of his mouth downward and pressing his brows together to obscure his true sentiments behind a mask of defiance. "And what is this requirement?" he asked after a brief pause. Internally, he felt a surge of temptation at the prospect of applying his inventive talents.

"I require a delicate miniature device drawn from poetic fables, which you possess the expertise to synthesize," Tito stated. "It must be painted on a wooden case—I will demonstrate the scale—as a triptych. The interior requires simple gilding; my interest lies entirely with the exterior. This is a favored motif among Florentines: the Triumph of Bacchus and Ariadne, yet I demand a novel interpretation. A passage from Ovid will provide the necessary framework. Young Bacchus must be seated in a vessel, his head wreathed in grape clusters, wielding a spear entwined with vine leaves. Dark-berried ivy must coil around the masts and sails; the oars must be thyrsi, and flowers must adorn the stern. Leopards and tigers must crouch before him, while dolphins play nearby. However, I insist upon including the fair-haired Ariadne beside him, rendered immortal with a golden crown—an addition not found in Ovid, but one you will surely envision. Above them, youthful Loves, masters of your craft, must shoot roses from the tips of their arrows—"

"Speak no further!" Piero interjected sharply. "I possess Ovid in the vernacular. Locate the passage. I detest being suffocated by the musings of others. You may enter."

Piero guided Tito through the first chamber, where a basket of eggs rested on the open hearth amidst a chaotic pile of shattered shells and ash. Jarringly incongruous with this sordid clutter stood a low bedframe of carved ebony, draped carelessly with a rich Oriental carpet that seemed stolen from the steps of a Madonna's throne. Beside it lay a carved *cassone*, a large chest adorned with painted motifs. Beyond this sparse arrangement, the room contained little else: plaster casts, wooden scaffolding, easels, and rough crates, all festooned with cobwebs. The adjacent space was even more expansive yet densely packed. Clearly, Piero was hosting a gathering, for the double doors beneath the high window—which admitted the artist's primary illumination—stood ajar, revealing a garden or rather a dense thicket. Fig trees and vines sprawled in tangled wildness among nettles and hemlock, while a towering cypress pierced a stifling canopy of yellowish mulberry leaves. It appeared this dank luxuriance had breached the interior walls; in one corner, amidst a confused heap of carved marble fragments and rusted armor, tufts of long grass and dark, feathery fennel had taken root. A large stone vase, tilted precariously, seemed to pour forth ivy streaming across the floor.

Pen-and-oil sketches of fantastical sea monsters, dances of satyrs and maenads, Saint Margaret's resurrection from the dragon's maw, Madonnas bathed in celestial light, botanical studies, and grotesque heads hung everywhere. On irregular, rough shelves, a few books lay scattered among drooping bunches of corn, bull horns, pieces of dried honeycomb, stones encrusted with rare lichens, skulls, bones, peacock feathers, and massive bird wings. Rising from the filth-covered floor were life-sized figures: one dressed in the frock of a Vallombrosan monk, bizarrely topped with a helmet featuring a barred visor; another smothered in brocade and skins hastily thrown over it. Amidst this heterogeneous still life, several speckled and white pigeons strutted or perched, too accustomed to humanity to flee at the approach of men. Three plump toads crawled in intimate proximity near the threshold, while a white rabbit, seemingly the model for the creature frightening Cupid in the central easel's depiction of Mars and Venus, twitched its nose with evident contentment atop a box filled with bran.

"And now, Messer Greco," Piero instructed, gesturing for Tito to sit on a low stool near the door, while he loomed over him with folded

Messer Bernardo del Nero remained as immovable as Romola anticipated regarding the postponement of the marriage until Easter. In this strategic maneuver, Bardo was entirely subordinate to the counsel of his sagacious and pragmatic ally. Yet, even Bernardo, though immune to the personal magnetism Tito exerted upon others, could not wholly dismiss the seductive logic of success that invariably captivates men of consequence. Tito was ascending rapidly through the highest echelons of power, particularly gaining favor with the youthful Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, who had already proposed incorporating Tito into his scholarly entourage for an imminent pilgrimage to Rome. The brilliant Greek, possessed of a tongue that flowed effortlessly without ever descending into contention, became increasingly indispensable at the lavish suppers along the Via Larga and at Florentine spectacles where he held no claim to expertise yet admired the unparalleled prowess of Piero de' Medici with effortless grace. Through an unbroken chain of cause and effect, Tito's reputation as a congenital companion in "magnificent" society rendered his intellect and talents more radiant; he possessed genuine accomplishment sufficient to render any exaggerated estimation perilous.

For Messer Bernardo, entrenched in old prejudices and attachments, these factors began to dismantle the newer, weaker bias against the supple young Greek stranger. To the seasoned Florentine, it was illogical to scorn the endorsement of standing well with the city's premier families. As Tito secured thorough acceptance into that exclusive circle whose opinions constituted the undisputed metric of social worth, it appeared irrational to deny that the path to satisfaction for Bardo as a father-in-law and for Romola as a wife was now unobstructed. Undeniably, Tito's arrival marked the dawn of a renewed existence for both father and daughter, surpassing even the initial promise. The blind old scholar—whose rigid honesty precluded participation in the deceitful flattery and its reciprocal excesses that formed the fabric of learned discourse—had fallen into obscurity among his peers. When mentioned, the consensus was that while his blindness and the loss of his son were tragic, he grew tedious in defending the merit of his own labors; his discontent seemed incongruous in a man who had previously spurned ecclesiastical orders, thereby complicating patronage arrangements. However, since Tito's emergence, the monotonous shadow cast by Bardo's name had lifted. The old man ceased his lamentations, and the fair daughter was no longer confined to a dowerless pride, awaiting a *parentado*. The winning demeanor and rising stature of the handsome Greek, poised to assume the dual roles of son and husband, transformed the new interest into a thoroughly cordial alliance. Visitors now frequently enlivened the quiet library, drawn by the indefatigable urge to renew contact whenever an old acquaintance becomes the subject of fresh conversation. Young men, invited by Tito, found themselves returning readily after overtaking him on his route to the Via de' Bardi, resting hands on his shoulder to engage in easy discourse. It was delightful to behold Romola's beauty; seated with the grandeur described by Firenzuola, she spoke with gravity, smiled with modesty, and radiated an aura of queenship. Under this genial breath of admiration and homage, she unfolded like a robust white lily, indifferent to the specifics of her new vibrant life anchored in Tito's devotion.

Tito had further fortified Bardo's hope that, before his death, he might secure the long-awaited stability for his library: ensuring it would not be absorbed into another collection, nor transferred to a monastic order to bear a religious name, but would perpetually remain the Bardi Library for the use of Florentines. The ancient habit of trusting in the Medici could not perish while their influence remained the state's primary lever; and Tito, now holding the ear of Cardinal Giovanni de' Medici, could achieve more than Messer Bernardo alone by demonstrating to a learned assembly the unique value of Bardi's holdings. Tito spoke sanguinely of this outcome, eager to buoy the old man's spirits, aware that Romola repaid these tender words with an adoration that no direct tribute to herself could elicit. This matter of the library dominated discussions between Bernardo del Nero and Tito as Christmas passed and the wedding horizon neared—all conducted strictly outside Bardo's hearing. Bardo clung to a vague conviction, which they dared not disrupt, that his assets, excluding the library, sufficed to meet all obligations. He refused, unless driven by a fleeting surge of despair, to acknowledge that the will disinheriting Dino left Romola heir only to debts, or that he required patronage beyond securing a distinct location for his library in exchange for a deed gifting it to the Florentine Republic.

"My assessment," Bernardo told Romola during a consultation beneath the loggia, "is that since you are to marry and Messer Tito possesses a competent income, we must commence winding up affairs to ascertain precisely the sum required to shield the library from encroachment, rather than allowing debts to accumulate further. Your father requires nothing but his daily portion of mutton and macaroni; I believe Messer Tito may undertake to provide this for the remainder of his years, substituting it for the *morgen-cap*."

"Tito has always understood that my life is inextricably bound to my father's," Romola replied. "He is kinder to my father than I am; he delights in making him happy."

"Ah, he is not molded from the same clay as other men, is he?" Bernardo observed, smiling. "Your father attempted to exclude feminine folly by stuffing you with Greek and Latin, yet you proved as susceptible to the first pair of bright eyes and soft words as any other Christian girl, unable to recite anything but Paternosters."

"Now, godfather," Romola countered, shaking her head with playful defiance, "as if mere brightness of eye and softness of word accounted for my love of Tito! You know better. You know I love my father and you because you are both good, and I love Tito equally because he is so good. I perceive it, I feel it, in every utterance and action. And if he is handsome as well, why should I not love him the more? Beauty appears to me an integral component of the perfected language through which goodness speaks. You must have been quite handsome yourself, godfather"—she gazed upward with a radiant, affectionate smile at the stately elder—"tall as Tito, with very fine eyes; only you carried a steerner, prouder countenance, and..."

"And Romola reserves all the pride for herself?" Bernardo remarked, not impervious to this charming coaxery. "Nevertheless, it is fortunate that in one regard Tito's demands prove more modest than those of any Florentine husband of suitable rank we might otherwise have encountered for you; he seeks no dowry."

Thus, the arrangement was finalized between Messer Bernardo del Nero, Romola, and Tito. Bardo assented with a sweeping gesture of the hand when Bernardo informed him that it was prudent to begin liquidating property and extinguishing debts, accustomed as he was to view financial liabilities and assets as a dense forest that his imagination never penetrated, let alone traversed. Meanwhile, Tito embarked upon winning Messer Bernardo's respect by interrogating Florentine monetary matters with his agile intellect: probing the secrets of the *Monti*, the valuation of real estate, and the profits of banking.

"You will soon forget that Tito is not a Florentine, godfather," Romola noted. "Observe how he absorbs everything pertaining to Florence."

"It seems to me he belongs to the *demoni*, children of no specific nation," Bernardo replied, smiling. "His mind is too nimble to be weighed down by the burdens we men carry in our hearts."

Romola smiled in turn, bathed in happy confidence.

The final week of the Carnival transformed Florence into a chaotic theater of noise and violence. Under the pale, frosty sky, the streets teemed with masked processions singing the mandatory hymns introduced by Lorenzo the Magnificent, while the *rigoletto*—a frenzied round dance—stamped out in the piazzas. But the true spectacle was the mob itself, particularly the boys and striplings who formed its most volatile element. At Carnival's peak, they became as loud and uncontrollable as cicadas, wielding poles to block every passerby until a tribute was extorted for supper and bonfire. This culminated in the standing entertainment of stone-throwing, a practice far from monotonous given the variety of injuries inflicted; often, a single victim was not enough, and death claimed multiple targets. The pleasures of the season were thus checkered with grossness and barbarity. A painter attempting to depict them truthfully would create a canvas so repulsive it required mounting face-down, save for the rare historical justification needed to condemn the narrow-minded zealots ignorant of such facts. Yet, amidst this, persisted a more innocent, picturesque merriment inherent to a people of quick animal spirits and sensitive nerves. It lacked the heavy stupor of the northern blood and the stealthy ferocity of the south that turns a brawl into a dagger-stroke.

High morning found the carnival spirit still lingering in lazy recollection of the previous night's jests. Tito Melema moved at a brisk, purposeful pace toward the Via de' Bardi, accompanied by Young Bernardo Dovizi—the keen-eyed Cardinal da Bibbiena of later portraits. Their conversation was animated, seemingly unrelated to the sights and sounds rushing past them along the Por' Santa Maria. Yet, as they gestured and smiled, both men periodically scanned their surroundings with sharp, tactical awareness. Near the turn onto the Lung' Arno, leading to the Ponte Rubaconte, Tito registered a presence he desperately wished to avoid recognition from at that specific moment. His mind was consumed by a singular, high-stakes objective: his betrothal, scheduled for the evening. The decision had arrived abruptly. While preparations for the marriage had been underway—primarily Tito pouring his florins into refurbishing Bardo's sparsely furnished dwelling, excluding the library—the union was originally slated to occur post-Easter, after Tito's year of probation mandated by Bernardo del Nero concluded. However, an urgent summons from Cardinal Giovanni to Rome, offering Tito a chance to assist Bernardo Dovizi in organizing a library using his superior Greek, presented an undeniable path to advancement. Tito had seized the opportunity, urgently petitioning that the betrothal proceed immediately. Delaying the marriage until his return would minimize the separation; being outwardly and inwardly pledged to Romola would render his claim irrefutable against Messer Bernardo or any rival. In the legal framework of the time, the exchange of rings and signing of mutual contracts constituted more than half the validity of marriage, with the nuptial benediction completing the ritual later. Romola's feelings aligned perfectly with his, and the elders' consent was secured.

Now, Tito hastened to snatch a morning visit from Romola, needing to exchange necessary final words before their evening commitment. Recognition was undesirable, especially from Tessa. Unmistakably, he spotted her moving with a timid, forlorn expression toward the very bend of the Lung' Arno he was rounding. As he maintained his discourse with the young Dovizi, an uncomfortable undercurrent confirmed Tessa had seen him and would undoubtedly pursue him. Escape along the direct route by the Arno and over the Ponte Rubaconte was impossible. She dared not approach while he was accompanied, so Tito kept Dovizi close, quickening his stride and weaving new topics into their conversation. Yet, the phantom sensation of Tessa trailing him intensified, becoming increasingly irritating. Perhaps it was exacerbated by a flicker of tenderness and pity for the poor creature, making the decision to evade her notice without acknowledgment a distasteful necessity. Tito persevered, guiding his companion to the door, skillfully executing his farewell without turning his face toward the potential line of sight for a pair of importunate blue eyes. Ascending the stone steps, he attempted to drown unpleasant thoughts in self-deception, convincing himself Tessa might not have seen him, or if she had, might not have followed.

However, reliance on such probabilities was flawed. Upon exiting the doorway, Tito adopted a brisk, oblivious demeanor, unaware of figures to his right or left. Human vision, however, operates beyond voluntary control, capturing a wide field of view. Tito knew instantly—a small figure in a white hood stood near the entrance. Before the physical contact occurred, his mind registered the presence. Then, a hand grasped his arm—not a tentative tap, but a desperate, forceful clutch. Poor Tessa, startled by his rapid exit, had surged forward with a final, frantic effort. When Tito halted and turned, her face betrayed a terrified dread of her own audacity.

"Tessa!" Tito's voice cut through her with a sharpness she had never known. "Why are you here? You must not follow me—you must not loiter at doorways waiting for me."

Her blue eyes widened, filling with tears, yet she remained silent. Tito feared the worst: being seen in the Via de' Bardi with a peasant girl staring pathetically at him. It was a street of high, silent residences, devoid of traffic, yet Bernardo del Nero—or someone equally dangerous—could appear at any moment. Even absent the day of his betrothal, the incident would be awkward and damaging. Yet, driving Tessa away with harsh words was brutality; it was impossible. That cursed folly regarding the *cerretano*, buried quietly for months, had erupted now as an unseasonable crop of vexation. Unable to speak harshly, he spoke hurriedly instead.

"Tessa, I cannot—must not talk to you here. I will go to the bridge and wait for you. Follow me slowly."

He turned, walking rapidly to the Ponte Rubaconte, leaning against the wall of a quaint house spaced evenly along the span, facing the approach Tessa would take. Seeing the little figure advance, her round face pale and saddened since their parting at the Nunziata, would have softened a harder heart than Tito possessed. Fortunately, it was the least-frequented bridge, nearly empty at that hour. He wasted no time addressing her as she drew near.

"Now, Tessa, I have very little time. Do not cry. Why did you follow me this morning? Never do so again."

"I thought," Tessa whispered, struggling against a sob rising at the timbre of his voice, "I thought you wouldn't be so long before coming to take care of me again. And the *patrigno* beats me, and I can't bear it anymore. Every holiday I walk to find you, but I can't. Oh, please don't send me away again! It has been so long, and I cry because you never come. I can't help it; the days are endless, and I don't mind the goats or kids... I can't—"

Sobs tore through her quickly, great tears streaming. Tito felt compelled to comfort her. Sending her away was a necessity, yes; he *had* to act immediately. But telling her anything that would plunge her into hopeless grief was impossible. He sensed deeper troubles looming, yet the immediacy of the moment demanded action over foresight.

"Tessa, my little one," he said, adopting his old, caressing tone, "do not cry. Endure the cruel *patrigno* a little longer. I will return to you. But I am leaving now for Rome—a very long journey. I shall return in a few weeks, and then I promise to come see you. Promise me to be good and wait."

It was the familiar voice; the sound alone was half sufficient to soothe Tessa. She looked up with trusting eyes, still glittering with tears, sobbing despite her utmost efforts to comply. He repeated gently, "Promise me, my Tessa."

"Yes," she whispered. "But you won't be long?"

"No, not long. But I must *leave now*. Remember what I told you, Tessa. No one must know you see me, or you will lose me forever. Go straight home now, never follow me again. Wait for me to come. Good-bye, my little Tessa: I *will* come."

There was no alternative; he had to turn and depart without looking back to gauge her reaction, for he had no time to spare. When he glanced around, he found himself in the Via de' Benci, where the bridge was invisible. Tessa, too trusting and obedient to disobey, would do exactly as instructed.

For that day, the difficulty was resolved. Yet, this resurgence of Tessa's presence, occurring when he could not deceive her into believing the truth, was an unpleasant burden to carry. Tito's mind, however, was currently saturated with the hopeful fervor of his first love, intertwined with ambitious prospects. The future necessity of grieving Tessa was akin to hearing the faint cry of a suffering animal hidden in a thicket while riding a merry cavalcade across a sunlit plain. On his second crossing of the Ponte Rubaconte that day, the thought of Tessa caused no perceptible dimming of his happiness. Well-muffled in his mantle, perhaps less for protection against the chill than to avoid drawing attention to his dainty attire, he leaped up the stone steps two at a time. Meeting Maso, he asked hurriedly, "Where is the damigella?"

"In the library; she is quite ready. Monna Brigida and Messer Bernardo are already there with Ser Braccio

November 17, 1494. Eighteen months had passed since Tito and Romola were wedded in the spring, a union marked by the ancient Greek custom of raining comfits from the sky to signify celestial blessing upon their dual existence. Yet, the landscape of Florence had shifted violently since that Easter. Just as a tree's bounty depends entirely on the flow of its sap, the private fortunes of the couple were now tethered to the grand, turbulent currents of Italian politics.

Only a week prior, the spirit of antiquity had surged back into the Florentine populace. The great bell of the palace tower had shattered the afternoon silence with a hammer-stroke of alarm, summoning the citizenry to arm themselves with rusted weapons, farming tools, and improvised clubs. They drove out the Medici. The gates of San Gallo slammed shut on Piero de' Medici, fleeing toward Bologna with his mercenaries in panic, and barred the path of his younger brother, the Cardinal, who escaped disguised as a Franciscan friar. A price was placed on their heads. Following the expulsion, the customary sackings occurred; the effigies of former conspirators were scrubbed from public walls, and the exiled enemies of the Medici were welcomed home. The fledgling tyrants were cast from their nests in the Via Larga, and the Republic regained its autonomy.

Now, however, the great palace in the Via Larga was being transformed for a different occupant. Drapery draped the streets in unfamiliar hues; banners spilled from windows; carpets and tapestries covered every step where exceptional feet might tread. This was undeniable evidence of civic joy, born of anticipation for a new guest. The stream of color flowed from the palace, winding past the Cathedral, sweeping the Piazza della Signoria, crossing the Ponte Vecchio, and reaching the Porta San Frediano, the gateway facing Pisa. Near this gate, a platform and canopy had been erected for the Signoria. Messer Luca Corsini, a doctor of law, felt his heart race with the weight of a Latin oration awaiting delivery. Every chief elder prepared himself, smoothing their chins and adjusting their silk *luccos* for the procession. Well-born youths inspected their new tunics, modeled after the French style, designed to impress the stranger with a peculiar grace. Meanwhile, a vast assembly of the clergy—from the Archbishop in his radiance to the trains of black, white, and grey monks—conferred early in the morning on the marshaling of relics, sacred banners, and consecrated jewels, ensuring their movements aligned perfectly with the anticipated arrival of the illustrious visitor at three in the afternoon.

This was an unprecedented guest. He had traversed the Alpine passes with an army Italy had never witnessed: thousands of formidable Swiss mercenaries, hardened to fight for love, hate, or coin; a host of gallant cavaliers proud of their lineage; and an infantry unit where one in every hundred carried an arquebus. Worse still, he brought bronze cannons that fired iron balls instead of stone, drawn by horses rather than oxen, capable of firing a second shot before a city could repair the breach caused by the first. Some compared this newcomer to Charlemagne, the legendary restorer of Florence and conqueror of degenerate kings; others likened him to Cyrus, the liberator of the chosen people and restorer of the Temple. He arrived with glorious designs: to march through Italy amid the jubilation of a grateful populace, to settle conflicting disputes at Rome, to seize the Kingdom of Naples by hereditary right and minimal bloodshed, and from that strategic foothold, to launch a crusade against the Turks, intending to slaughter or convert them to the Christian faith.

Such a scheme seemed fitting for the Most Christian King, head of a nation strengthened by the cunning of Louis XI, who had died in terror of his own prospects a decade earlier. This antitype of Cyrus and Charlemagne was none other than the son of that subtle king—the young Charles VIII of France.

On a general level, nothing could appear more grandiose or more capable of stirring memories of divine dispensations that reshaped human history. A widespread conviction held that the arrival of the French king and his army was an event beyond the ordinary order of Providence—one where marble statues might sweat, phantom warriors fought in the air, and beasts birthed monsters. This belief rested less on the sheer magnitude of a foreign invasion than on the moral emotions of the age, crystallized into presentiments. These sentiments found their most remarkable expression in the voice of a single man.

That man was Fra Girolamo Savonarola, Prior of the Dominican convent of San Marco in Florence. On a September morning, as news of the French army's entry rang in men's ears, he preached in the Cathedral from the text, "Behold I, even I, do bring a flood of waters upon the earth." He believed this was supreme guidance, marking the exact point in his exposition of Genesis reached during the previous Lent. He interpreted the "flood"—a symbol of both avenging wrath and purifying mercy—as the divinely indicated manifestation of the French army. His audience, comprising some of the finest spirits of the age, the most cultivated intellects in the most cultured Italian city, accepted this interpretation with shuddering awe. Savonarola possessed a rare power to imprint his beliefs upon others and sway varied minds. Four years prior, he had proclaimed from Florence's chief pulpit that a scourge was descending upon Italy to purge the Church. Savonarola appeared to believe, and his followers waveringly agreed, that he held a mission akin to the Hebrew prophets, positioning the Florentines as a second chosen people.

While prophetic gifts were not unknown in that era—seers and heralds of the future existed within and without the cloisters—Savonarola stood out as a grand exception. In others, prophecy was like a farthing candle illuminating small corners of destiny with gossip; in Savonarola, it was a mighty beacon casting a warning light far ahead for humanity. To the soberest minds, the supernatural quality of his foresight gained credence from the peculiar conditions of the age.

By the close of 1492, the year Lorenzo de' Medici died and Tito Melema wandered into Florence, Italy enjoyed a peace and prosperity unthreatened by immediate danger. There was no fear of famine, for the harvests of corn, wine, and oil had been abundant. New palaces rose in fair cities, and villas dotted pleasant slopes. Those who possessed more than their share feared neither poverty nor the larger masses who possessed less. Citizens' armor grew rusty, and populations seemed tamed, licking the hands of masters who purchased ready-made armies as easily as they bought goods from Smyrna. Even the fear of the Turk had faded; the Pope found it more profitable to accept bribes for potential poisoning than to plan conquest or conversion.

This world, with its partitioned empire and spacious universal Church, appeared to be a handsome establishment for the fortunate and the wise—a realm where lust, obscenity, lying, treachery, oppression, and murder were pleasant, useful, and manageable. Adorning this substantial delight of tyranny and avarice was the patronage of polite learning and the fine arts, allowing flattery to be expressed in the choicest Latin and sublime artists to paint the holy and the unclean with equal skill. The Church, though reportedly disgraced in its leadership and lacking signs of renewal in its lower ranks, was more prosperous than in past eras. The heavens smiled above, and the earth remained still.

Yet, amidst this splendid masquerade, certain forces worked to disturb the general festivity. Ludovico Sforza, the Duke of Milan, a patron of Leonardo da Vinci, coveted the ducal crown for himself rather than letting it rest on a feeble nephew susceptible to poison. He feared the Spanish-born King Ferdinand of Naples and his Crown Prince Alfonso, who despised the cruelty required to eliminate a near relative for a Lombard usurper. The Neapolitan royals, in turn, feared their suzerain, Pope Alexander Borgia. All three watched Florence anxiously, fearing its central position might tip the balance through underhanded support. Furthermore, all states, including Venice—the cautious, stable, and powerful republic seeking dominion across the Adriatic and onto the western coast—feared one another.

It was thought that Lorenzo de' Medici prevented the fatal outbreak of these jealousies by maintaining alliances with Naples and the Pope while persuading Milan of their mutual benefit. However, the rash vanity of young Piero de' Medici quickly nullified his father's wary policy. Ludovico Sforza, suspecting a league against him, devised a counter-move: inviting the French king to march into Italy and claim Naples as heir to the House of Anjou. Ambassadors, termed "orators" in those harangue-filled times, traveled constantly. A recalcitrant cardinal, refusing to acknowledge a Pope elected by bribery and viewing him as a personal enemy, also journeyed back and forth, seconding the invitation with fiery rhetoric. The young king lent a willing ear. By 1493, rumors spread that Charles VIII of France was preparing to cross the Alps with a mighty army. The Italian populations, accustomed since the fall of the Roman Empire to seeking arbiters from afar, began to view his coming as a means of avenging wrongs and redressing grievances.

Within these rumors, Savonarola heard confirmation that his prophecy was being verified. What filled the ears of ancient prophets but the distant tread of foreign armies arriving to execute justice? He no longer gazed vaguely at the horizon for a storm; he pointed directly to the rising cloud. The French army was the new deluge destined to purify the earth from iniquity

The Piazza del Duomo was draped in a festive sky of azure, studded with the golden constellations of lilies and coats of arms, yet the celebration felt brittle, a veneer stretched over a powder keg. Banners snapped taut at the corners of the Baptistery, but the marble steps remained bare, trodden by the heavy, indifferent boots of citizens who cared little for the Advent sermons echoing from within. The atmosphere was charged not with piety, but with the restless kinetic energy of a populace on edge, moving with the brisk, purposeful gait of men who had urgent business elsewhere.

At the periphery, where the artificial carnival light of the square gave way to the somber gray of the morning, a cluster of workers stood apart. Their attire bore the stains of daily labor, their arms bare and brawny, many clutching tools that doubled as weapons. Among them stood Ser Cioni, a notary whose voice cut through the murmur with the precision of a blade. He addressed the crowd with the complacent arrogance of a man whose political faction currently held the reins of power.

"Never speak to me," Cioni commanded, his tone incisive, dismissing the specter of Swiss mercenaries or French infantry as irrelevant threats. "Peasants have shattered the finest armies of our condottieri when trapped between precipices. Tell this to the Florentines: no army can hold our streets."

A dyer, his forearms stained a permanent crimson that mimicked fresh blood, stepped forward, a small hatchet protruding from his belt. "True enough, Ser Cioni. Those French cavaliers who paraded in their tight doublets recently tasted our hospitality. When I was carrying cloth through Ognissanti, I saw my masters looking disdainfully at the Vespucci and Agli palaces as if they were pigsties. They eyed us like strutting roosters, pitying our inability to strut properly. 'Stick out your stomachs,' I told them, 'for I carry a meat-axe that will find its way inside you.' Then the great bell tolled—the *vacca muglia*—and chaos erupted. We raised a barricade, trapping them. Our Ciompi surged forward, and one wields a scythe that severed the plumes from a nobleman's helmet. True enough! The women rained stones until the horses screamed. A pity Piero de' Medici hadn't returned then; we would have stripped him limb from limb."

"Aye," agreed a young butcher, knife at his hip. "Piero looks like a hunted chicken now. He'll be a long time returning. Swallow no Medici again, whatever the French King demands."

"But those French cannons!" protested Goro, a man swollen with two years of grievance. "San Giovanni defend us! If Messer Domeneddio truly favors us, Ser Cioni, why not route the French through Naples?"

"Fools," Cioni began, but his rebuke died as a collective shout tore from the crowd: "Look there!"

All heads turned toward the Via de' Cerretani. Advancing was a party of distinction: Lorenzo Tornabuoni, radiant in his status; a French nobleman encased in elaborate white linen and embroidery; two other Florentines of high birth; and, trailing slightly behind, a figure that commanded attention solely through sheer force of character. This man wore only black, a garment destined soon to be overlaid by a red mantle as he prepared to walk in procession as Latin Secretary to the Ten. Tito Melema.

Tito had become indispensable in the dealings with the French, leveraging his fluency in the language and his knowledge of southern Italy. Fortune smiled upon him; he moved with a graceful, unpretentious ease, smiling and explaining, radiating the confidence of a man who knows exactly where he stands. Yet, a sharp observer might note a subtle shift, the shedding of moral youthfulness replaced by the hardened self-possession of a professional actor. His features remained soft, his eyes pellucid, but something intangible had vanished, much like the mist at dawn.

The Frenchman halted to receive instructions regarding ceremonial protocol before returning to Signa, using the opportunity for a final survey of the Piazza. As the group entered, the gaze of the crowd was mixed. While some offered delicate flattery regarding the Frenchman's hoof-shaped shoes, others muttered venomous curses against the Mediceans, carefully modulating their volume to avoid detection. Lorenzo Tornabuoni, master of dissembling annoyance, smoothed over the friction, while Tito, possessing an alien detachment from local passions, absorbed the hostility with impassive grace.

Pausing to observe the Duomo, the party noted the festoons adorning the central doorway. Tornabuoni signaled to Piero di Cosimo, who was idling before Nello's shop. What followed was a heated exchange, rendered hilarious by Tito's literal translation of Piero's pungent wit. The Frenchman's astonishment rippled through the group, turning hostile onlookers into a circle of suppressed, amused spectators. For Tito, this was a triumph; he reveled in the approving glances, basking in the role of the indispensable interpreter.

Yet, while the elite celebrated in the sunlit square, the gray streets nearby churned with a darker current. Here, the French dress and absurd footwear drew not admiration, but fury. Pressing the advance of the dignitaries was a swelling tide of non-admiring Florentines. Leading this mob were three men in ragged raiment, their hands bound by cord, a rope looped tightly around their bodies and necks, allowing their captors to throttle them instantly should rebellion occur. Their guards were French soldiers, prodding the prisoners with knotted ends of rope and broken Italian commands to beg for ransom.

Two of the prisoners complied, holding out their bound hands with piteous appeals: "For the love of God and the Holy Madonna, give us something for our ransom! We are Tuscans, taken in Lunigiana!"

The third, however, remained stubbornly silent. He was a study in contradiction. Unlike his youthful, sturdy companions, he was ancient, likely fifty or sixty years past, his beard long and neglected, his hair thick and white framing a bald crown. Though emaciated, his thickset frame remained firm and upright, projecting a strange vitality that belied his age. His dark eyes and heavy brows burned with an isolated intensity against his yellow, bloodless, deeply wrinkled face. Yet, there was a fitfulness in his gaze; after flashing with fierce scrutiny at windows and passersby, they would drift into a lost, wandering stare. His lips were sealed, his hands held rigidly down. He would not beg.

This refusal ignited the crowd's exasperation. Initially, many had dropped coins in automatic response to the plea for God or out of terror of the French reputation for cruelty. But as the procession moved deeper into the city, that compliance evaporated. The crowd transformed into a hostile escort, chanting unintelligible jeers that required no translator. The soldiers, inclined to violence but hamstrung by the need to control their charges, hurried toward a nearby inn seeking refuge.

"French dogs!"
"Bullock-feet!"
"Seize their pikes!"
"Cut the cords! Let them run! They're web-footed geese!"

These cries were vague threats, met with equally vague enthusiasm. Then, the dyer, seeing the meaning of the tumult, snatched his axe from his belt. "Come along!" he roared, joining the fray, followed by the butcher and the rest of the workers, save for Goro, who fled up a narrow alley.

The sight of the dyer, red-armed and axe-high, electrified the crowd. He did not attack directly but thrust himself among the citizens, a living banner of resistance. From this ferment emerged Lollo, a conjuror's impish boy wielding a sharp knife. With no compassion for the prisoners but a desire for dexterity, he saw the perfect moment. As the crowd surged, Lollo darted forward, sliced the cord binding the eldest prisoner, and leaped back with the agility of a startled bird.

"Run, old one!" he whispered urgently.

The prisoner seized the chance. Escape had been a constant, quiet obsession, and the crowd's anger provided the fuel. He bolted, but his speed alone would not suffice; the Florentines instantly formed a wall between him and his captor. He surged into the Piazza, only to hear the thunder of pursuit behind him. The other two prisoners had also escaped, forcing the soldiers into a clumsy, tardigrade chase, their hoof-shoes impeding their progress. One of the youths diverted the crowd up the Borgo di San Lorenzo, but the main focus remained on the Piazza.

Lorenzo Tornabuoni and his party turned just as a prisoner dashed past them.

"Prisoners escaping," Tornabuoni observed, his voice cool. "The people are not content with having emptied the Bargello yesterday. Without visible authority, they will fall upon the police to liberate thieves. Ah! A French soldier. This becomes serious."

"You are easily frightened," Piero observed, his laughter cutting through the tension with a sharp, mocking edge. "My portrait lacks the ferocity of the original. Yet, that old man possessed a tiger's gaze. I intend to return to the Duomo and study him anew."

"It is unpleasant to be seized by a madman, provided he remains mad," Lorenzo Tornabuoni interjected, offering a polite defense for Tito's earlier panic. "Though perhaps he is merely a ruffian. We shall ascertain the truth. Furthermore, we must determine if we possess sufficient authority to halt this disturbance between our citizens and these foreigners," he added, turning his attention to the French officer.

They moved forward, swords drawn, flanked by a silent procession of onlookers who parted respectfully. Tito followed suit; the initial paralysis of terror had already yielded to the rapid calculation born of mortal peril. The mob, prone to jeering at the soldier rather than inflicting lethal harm, dispersed instantly upon seeing the nobles advance. The interrogation began. The Frenchman revealed that his party had entered the city solely to ransom captives: two Tuscan soldiers captured in Lunigiana and an elderly Genoese man, whose group had clashed with French foragers near Fivizzano. While the old man appeared deranged, he posed no threat. The soldier admitted ignorance of the elder's speech, unable to comprehend a single word.

Tito remained deaf to the proceedings until Tornabuoni addressed him directly. "Will you accompany us, Melema? Or, since Messere departs for Signa, will you wisely adopt the current custom and attend the Frate? The orator will be a torrent in full flood this morning—a necessity for anyone wishing to preserve their Medicean integrity. I would attend myself if leisure permitted."

Tito's complexion had regained its hue, and he managed a display of cheerful composure. "Naturally, I rank among the admirers of the inspired speaker," he smiled. "Unfortunately, I am detained by the Segretario until the procession commences."

"I shall enter the Duomo to observe that savage old man once more," Piero declared.

"Then, Piero mio, exercise the charity to present him to one of the hospitals for travelers," Tornabuoni commanded. "The monks may discern whether he requires confinement within a cage."

The group dispersed. Tito proceeded toward the Palazzo Vecchio to locate Bartolommeo Scala. Though the distance was short, the journey felt elongated, stretching into the agonizing duration of a waking nightmare. Every step across the street and piazza resurrected memories and precipitated anxieties that could have defined months of existence. A serpent seemed to coil tightly around his limbs.

Baldassarre alive and residing in Florence represented a living vendetta—an entity that would not rest, much like a coiling snake, until it had crushed its prey. Such a man did not allow injuries to pass unavenged; his love and hatred burned with a passionate fervor that subjugated all other faculties, driving a man to sacrifice himself to his obsession as if worshipping a destructive deity. Baldassarre had vanished, yet Tito understood the implication perfectly: the vengeance was being studied, refined to ensure absolute certainty.

If he had not uttered those decisive words—"He is a madman"—if he could have summoned the mental fortitude to acknowledge his recognition of Baldassarre openly, the risk would have been mitigated. He could have claimed positive evidence of the old man's death. The only witnesses capable of contradicting him were Fra Luca, who was deceased, and the crew of the companion galley that delivered news of the pirate encounter. The probability of Baldassarre having reunited with any member of that crew was infinitesimal. Tito harbored bitter resentment that a timely, well-constructed falsehood might have spared him fatal consequences. However, fabricating such a lie would have demanded perfect self-command during a moment of convulsive shock. He realized now that his words had erupted spontaneously, like a sudden birth nurtured in darkness, devoid of preconception.

This was the inexorable law governing human souls: we prepare for sudden acts through the reiterated choices of good or evil that gradually forge character. His only remaining recourse was Baldassarre's potential failure to execute his revenge. And then, a thought emerged, more actively cruel than anything Tito had previously entertained: might his own unpremeditated denial contain a kernel of truth? Sufficient truth, at minimum, to shield him against any declaration Baldassarre might attempt?

The old man appeared strange and wild; with his eager intellect and suffering heart, madness was a plausible outcome. If true, the vengeance seeking to inflict disgrace could be thwarted. Yet, there existed another form of vengeance that no amount of cunning deception could parry. Baldassarre belonged to a lineage where the thrust of the dagger was as instinctive as the tiger's leap. Tito recoiled with shuddering dread from disgrace, yet he shared a visceral terror inherent to a soft, pleasure-seeking nature—one that prevents a man from facing wounds and death as a welcome respite from shame.

His mind raced toward some hidden defensive armor capable of saving him from a vengeance no subtlety could deflect. He marveled at the potency of the passionate fear consuming him, transforming the joyful vitality of youth into acute pain. One resource remained theoretically open: he could have retreated, sought out Baldassarre again, and confessed everything—to Romola, to the world. But he never conceived of doing so. True repentance, severing all ties to evil, demands more than selfish fear. He possessed no conviction that truth offered strength or safety; he trusted only his ingenuity and his dissimulation.

Now that the initial shock, exposing the treacherous signs of fear, had subsided, he hoped to navigate all emergencies through cool deceit and defensive stratagems. It was a defining feature of Tito's experience at this critical juncture that no direct measure to eliminate Baldassarre ever crossed his mind. Every other possibility traversed his consciousness, including his own flight from Florence, yet no scheme arose to remove his enemy. His dread generated no active malignancy; he would still prefer not to cause pain to any mortal. He had simply chosen to make life easy for himself, carrying his human burden in a manner that pinched him nowhere. This choice had, at various intervals, deposited him in unforeseen predicaments.

The question now was not whether he should share the common pressure of destiny with his suffering fellow-men, but whether the entire arsenal of lying would suffice to prevent him from being crushed by the inevitable consequences of that habitual choice.

Baldassarre shoved aside the heavy curtain, his wrists raw where the bonds bit into the flesh, the rough hemp of the rope digging into his throat and torso. As the darkness of the sacristy gave way to the cavernous light of the Duomo, he froze, a statue of shock against the threshold. He had anticipated the usual hollow shell of a church: a vast nave stripped of life, save for flickering tapers and pale, rigid idols staring back from side altars. Perhaps a handful of worshippers in the distant choir, murmuring a monotonous chant to the ceiling. That was the expectation of a man who viewed sanctuaries merely as architectural shells. Instead, he found a sea of living heat. Hundreds of faces, upturned in breathless silence toward the pulpit, formed a mosaic ranging from the dignified robes of magistrates and gentlewomen to the coarse tunics of artisans and peasants.

At the rostrum stood a Dominican friar, features sharp and commanding, dark hair framing a face etched with intensity. He held the crucifix high, preaching with a fervor that seemed to vibrate through the stone floor. For the first few minutes, Baldassarre registered nothing of the sermon. His entry had been silent, yet the atmosphere shifted instantly; eyes near the doorway snapped toward him with suspicion. The ropes around his limbs screamed their verdict: *escaped prisoner*. Yet, in this place, the church was a sanctuary, a legal shield he was entitled to claim. His advanced age and the wild misery stamped upon his face were weapons designed to elicit pity, not alarm. As he stood motionless, his gaze drifting aimlessly from the throng to the patterned pavement at his feet, the observers lost their vigilance, drawn back into the magnetic pull of the preacher's voice.

Among those eyes that had locked onto him was Romola's. She had slipped in late through a side portal, positioning herself to command a full view of the main entrance. Her gaze lingered on Baldassarre with a piercing attention. Grey hairs, a rare sight in Florence, struck a chord in her heart, as did the unmistakable brand of suffering on his face, sealed by the cord around his neck. These details stirred the dormant sensibilities of her soul, honed by a lifetime of witnessing sorrow. She imagined his eyes meeting hers in that initial, startled sweep, but Baldassarre remained oblivious. He possessed only a fractured awareness of the scene—a fleeting spark that failed to pierce the dense, turbulent fog of emotion ignited by his confrontation with Tito. Memories of the past surged like delirious visions, tangled with thirst and anguish. No coherent thought of the future could form amidst this internal tempest; the closest approximation was a bitter recognition of his fading strength and a vague, corrosive resolve born of total distrust.

Suddenly, the ambient noise coalesced into a roar, echoing the thunder of his own inner turmoil. A voice, cutting through his marrow with an accent of triumphant certainty, declared: *"The day of vengeance is at hand!"*

Baldassarre quivered, forcing his eyes upward. Too distant to discern detail, he saw only the silhouette of the preacher, right arm outstretched, lifting the crucifix like a weapon. He gasped for the threatening cadence again, as if it were a promise of salvation. The preacher paused, slowly lowering his arm until the wooden cross rested on the pulpit's edge. He folded his arms across his chest, scanning the multitude as if challenging every single soul to meet his gaze.

"All ye in Florence are my witnesses," the voice boomed, resonating with terrifying clarity. "I spoke not in a corner. Ye are my witnesses that four years ago, ere signs of war or tribulation appeared, I proclaimed the coming scourge. I lifted my voice as a trumpet to the prelates, princes, and people of Italy, declaring: 'The cup of your iniquity is full.' Behold, the thunder of the Lord gathers; it shall fall and shatter the cup. Your iniquity, which tastes to you as pleasant wine, shall be poured out upon you, becoming molten lead."

"And you, O priests, who mock, saying, 'Ha, ha! There is no Presence in the sanctuary—the Shechinah is naught—the Mercy-seat is bare: we may sin behind the veil, and who shall punish us?' To you, I declare, the presence of God shall manifest in His temple as a consuming fire. Your sacred vestments shall become winding sheets of flame. Where there was sweet music, there shall be shrieks and hissing; where soft couches awaited, there shall be thorns; where the breath of wantons flowed, there shall come pestilence. Trust not in your gold and silver, nor in your high fortresses. Though walls be of iron and citadels of adamant, the Most High shall instill terror into your hearts and weakness into your councils, leaving you confounded and fleeing like women. He shall break in pieces mighty men without number, replacing them with others. For God will no longer endure the pollution of His sanctuary; He will thoroughly purge His Church."

"And since Scripture declares that God reveals His purposes only to His servants the prophets, He has chosen me, His unworthy servant, making His purpose present to my soul through the living Word and the deeds of Providence. By the ministry of angels, He has revealed it to me in visions. His word possesses me so completely that I am but a branch of the forest when the wind of heaven strikes it; I cannot remain silent, even if I am a derision to the scorner. For four years, in obedience to the Divine Will, I have preached three truths delivered by the Lord: that *in these times God will regenerate His Church*, that *before this regeneration comes the scourge over all Italy*, and that *these things will come quickly*."

"But hypocrites, cloaking their hatred of the truth with a show of love, have told me, 'Come now, Frate, leave your prophecies; it is enough to teach virtue.' To these I answer: 'Yes, you whisper in your hearts that God dwells afar off, that His word is a parchment written by dead men, and that He deals not as in the days of old, rebuking nations and punishing oppressors. But I cry again in your ears: God is near, not afar off. His judgments do not change. He is the God of Armies; the strong men who march to battle are His ministers, even as storm, fire, and pestilence. He drives them by the breath of His angels, and they descend upon the chosen land which has forsaken the covenant. And thou, O Italy, art the chosen land; has not God placed His sanctuary within thee, and hast thou polluted it? Behold, the ministers of His wrath are upon thee—they are at thy very doors!'"

Savonarola's voice had climbed to a peak of impassioned force before abruptly silencing. His hands fell, clasping quietly before him. This silence was not a lull; it was a spell as potent as his voice. Across the vast cathedral, men and women sat frozen, faces upturned like breathing statues, until the voice returned in clear, low tones.

"Yet there is a pause—even as in the days when Jerusalem was destroyed, there was a pause that the children of God might flee from it. There is a stillness before the storm: lo, there is blackness above, yet not a leaf quakes; the winds are stayed, that the voice of God's warning may be heard. Hear it now, O Florence, chosen city in the chosen land! Repent and forsake evil; do justice; love mercy; cast out all uncleanness from among you, that the spirit of truth and holiness may fill your souls and breathe through all your streets and habitations. Then the pestilence shall not enter, and the sword shall pass over you and leave you unhurt."

"For the sword hangs from the sky; it quivers; it is about to fall! *The sword of God upon the earth, swift and sudden!* Did I not tell you, years ago, that I beheld the vision and heard the voice? And behold, it is fulfilled! Is there not a king with his army at your gates? Does not the earth shake with the tread of horses and the wheels of swift cannon? Is there not a fierce multitude capable of laying bare the land as with a sharp razor? I tell you the French King with his army is the minister of God: God shall guide him as a hand guides a sharp sickle, and the joints of the wicked shall melt before him, mown down as stubble. He that flees shall not escape, and he that escapes shall not be delivered."

"And the tyrants who built their thrones from the vices of the multitude, and the unbelieving priests who traffic in souls and fill the sanctuary with fornication, shall be hurled from their soft couches into burning hell; and the pagans and those who sinned under the old covenant shall stand aloof and say: 'Lo, these men have brought the stench of a new wickedness into the everlasting fire.'"

"But thou, O Florence, take the offered mercy. See! The Cross is held out to you: come and be healed. Which nation in Italy has received a token like yours? The tyrant is driven out; the men holding bribes in their left hand and rods in their right have departed, and no blood has been spilled. Now, cast away every other abomination, and you shall be strong in the strength of the Living God. Wash yourselves from the black pitch of your vices, which have made you akin to heathens; cast out the envy and hatred that have

Outside the Duomo, while Baldassarre remained paralyzed by the psychic echo of Savonarola's voice, a third party had slipped through the rear archway. Piero di Cosimo stood in the periphery, his gaze fixed not on the pulpit but on the man who had just emerged—a former prisoner of the Frate's fervor. As the congregation succumbed to the aftershocks of the sermon, a collective, wordless catharsis washing over them, the newcomer moved forward. He placed a hand on Baldassarre's shoulder. The painter looked back, tears tracking slow paths through the dust on his cheeks, exhaling a ragged breath that signaled the end of his emotional collapse.

Piero lowered his voice. "Shall I sever your bonds? I've heard the account of your capture."

Baldassarre hesitated, his eyes narrowing with a defensive suspicion toward this intruder. Finally, he conceded. "If you wish."

"Let us move outside," Piero suggested.

Suspicion flickered again in Baldassarre's stare. Seizing the moment, Piero smiled, drew a blade, and sliced the ropes binding the man's wrists. He observed the prisoner's face, noting a peculiar intensity that hinted at madness rather than simple despair. *'If he causes trouble,'* Piero reasoned, *'he'll be re-caged quickly enough. Let him breathe for a moment.'*

"You fear me," Piero whispered, pressing closer. "You hesitate to share your story."

Baldassarre crossed his arms, relishing the久违 sensation of muscle engagement under his tattered sleeves. His demeanor shifted from wary to resolute. "No. I have nothing to say."

"As you prefer," Piero replied, shrugging. "Though consider this: Florentines are notoriously hospitable to travelers with torn coats and hollow bellies. There is a hospital for the destitute beyond the city gates. I could arrange passage. Your French guard has been dispatched; there is no threat."

Baldassarre nodded silently, accepting the lifeline, and the two men departed the church together.

"You would not wish to sit for a portrait?" Piero asked as they walked along the Via dell'Oriuolo toward the Gate of Santa Croce. "I am a painter. I would pay you handsomely."

The doubt returned to Baldassarre's gaze. "No."

"Ah!" Piero dismissed the request curtly. "Then proceed directly to the Porta Santa Croce. The hospital lies just outside. Do you refuse my further aid?"

"I thank you for your kindness thus far. I require nothing more."

"It is settled," Piero muttered, turning away.

*Mysterious old tiger,* the artist mused internally. *Truly a subject worthy of canvas. Ugly, carved deep by time, as if the plowshare and harrow had ravaged his very heart. A stark, brutal counterpoint to my own bland, smiling Messer Greco—the Baccus Triumphans who wedded the fair Antigone against all historical precedent. Ah! The scholar's blood curdles at the sight of that ancient claw.*

Upon returning to the Piazza del Duomo, the tide of humanity surged outward from every portal. The frantic pace of the dispersing crowd testified to the gravity of the sermon, which had stolen hours from their daily labors. Piero leaned against the sharp angle of the Baptistery, watching the exodus with an appreciative eye. He delighted in the kaleidoscope of attire and the piercing individuality of the faces—features reminiscent of Masaccio's work from half a century prior, or those of Ghirlandaio, who had not yet abandoned such vivid realism.

This morning marked a unique convergence. The assembly before Fra Girolamo represented the full spectrum of Florentine society and its fracturing politics. Among them were noblemen of high station, freshly emboldened as enemies of the Medici and champions of popular rule, devout Piagnoni who viewed San Marco as a modern-day sanctuary of Samuel. Figures like Francesco Valori stood out—men of commanding presence and fiery tempers, intoxicated by the immediate divine mandate to liberate Florence in their own image. Beside them stood figures like Soderini, possessing less of the zealous fanatic and more of the seasoned statesman.

Others included men of family, such as Piero Capponi, brave defenders of sober republicanism who preferred the sword to debate, rejecting any ideology that excluded the Medici or stifled public spirit. At their sides walked doctors of law, whose study of Accursius had not extinguished their spiritual fire; Messer Luca Corsini, for instance, a man destined soon to hurl stones in the streets for the cause of religion and the Frate.

Amidst the dignitaries draped in black luccio mantles or fur-lined cloaks, there were those with contemplative, sensitive visages: scholars bearing names like Strozzi and Acciajoli, already inclined toward the monastic habit; artists inflamed by Savonarola's teachings, including a young painter who had recently surpassed himself in a fresco of the Divine Child within the Frate's bare cell—unaware that he too would soon don the tonsure and become Fra Bartolommeo. There was the mystic poet Girolamo Benevieni, rushing perhaps to deliver news of the beloved Frate's imminent arrival to his friend Pico della Mirandola, a man who would never see another sunrise.

Well-born women appeared, clad in such rigorous simplicity that their refined grace served only to distinguish them from their lesser peers. Predominant among the ranks were the genuine popolani of the Major and Minor Arts, acutely aware of purses drained by war taxes. Most striking, however, was the long stream of poorer tradesmen and artisans. Their faith ranged from a crude, undifferentiated trust in the Frate as the champion of the poor against the oppressive rich, to an intense, sedentary absorption in biblical subtleties that illuminated the dim corners of their workshops with a pale, divine flame.

Scattered throughout this motley host were those who were neither disciples nor opponents. Some were Mediceans, displaying feigned deference born of fear and strategy. Others were sincere advocates of liberty who viewed Savonarola merely as an ambitious monk—a half-witted, half-fanatical tool wielded by the masses, a social fact to be endured. Even bitter enemies lingered: members of the old aristocratic anti-Medicean faction seeking to reclaim control for specific families, or licentious youths who despised the Frate as Florence's ultimate killjoy.

The sermons in the Duomo had evolved into political spectacles, drawing the ears of curiosity and malice alongside faith. Intellectuals like young Niccolò Machiavelli attended to observe and report to friends in the countryside; gluttons like Dolfo Spini came to hunt the Frate as a public nuisance depleting game supplies, lying in wait for accusations.

Perhaps no preacher ever exerted such massive influence as Savonarola, yet none ever worked with such heterogeneous raw material. The secret of his power lay in the complexity of his message. Baldassarre, driven to ecstatic self-martyring vengeance, represented only an extreme edge of the audience's fragmented sympathies. Savonarola's preaching contained strains that appealed to the highest susceptibilities of human nature, balanced by elements that gratified low egoism, piqued gossiping curiosity, and fascinated timorous superstition. His need for personal dominance, his labyrinthine allegorical readings of Scripture, his enigmatic visions, and his false certainty regarding Divine intent were, in his own vast soul, continually elevated by fervid piety, a passionate sense of the infinite, active sympathy, and a clear-eyed demand for subordinating selfish interests to the general good—traits shared by the greatest minds of mankind.

Yet for the mass of his listeners, the potency of his words rested solely on his bold assertions of supernatural claim, his denunciatory visions, and the false certainty that transformed his sermons into urgent political bulletins. Having mastered the audience, it became essential to his nature—and ostensibly to their welfare—to maintain that mastery. The result was inevitable: no leader struggles to retain power over a mixed multitude without corruption; his standards inevitably descend to meet their lowest needs rather than rising to his own highest insights.

The complexities of human character were rarely displayed with greater efficacy than in Girolamo Savonarola. Yet, if one regards his life as a drama marked by profound internal shifts accompanying external changes, a reverence emerges that requires no closing of the eyes to fact. Up to this juncture, when his direct intervention in political affairs had only just commenced, his imperious need for ascendancy likely burned undiscernibly within the fierce flame of his zeal for God and man.

It was customary in antiquity, when an ox was led to sacrifice for Jupiter, to chalk over its dark spots, presenting a false facade of unblemished whiteness. Let us discard the chalk. Boldly state it: the victim is spotted, yet it remains no less valid that his mighty heart is laid upon the altar of men's highest hopes.

At six o'clock, the spectacle of the new Charlemagne's arrival in Florence concluded, leaving the populace with a mixture of exhaustion and awe. From the turreted rooftops, where observers watched the winding column of troops against the backdrop of green hills, the event appeared as a triumphal tableau. Without the sun to glare upon the banners or the dust to obscure the steel, the army emerged with terrifying clarity: tall, rugged Scottish archers; Swiss halberdiers, massive and menacing; agile Gascon footmen poised for climbing; and cavalrymen who resembled knight-errants mounted on warhorses. It was a force designed solely to crush the enemies of God, a certainty that transformed the display of martial splendor into a source of genuine comfort. Nobles, knights, and pages of noble birth paraded in satin scarves embroidered with pious symbols, jeweled sword hilts, and brocaded mantles, all crowned by a transcendent canopy borne aloft by select youths above the Most Christian King. As an old diarist noted, simply to witness this *fù gran magnificenza* was a privilege.

For the Signoria, however, the ceremony had been a logistical nightmare. Stationed on platforms before the gates, they were forced to march out precisely timed to meet the guest, only to find their orator, Messer Luca Corsini, rendered speechless by the sudden onset of rain. The precipitation created a chaotic urgency among men and horses alike, breaking the rhythm of his prepared Latin address and reducing the scholarly city to offering a clumsy, impromptu welcome in French. This breakdown provided Tito Melema with a critical opening. Positioned behind the Signoria amidst the throng of officials, Tito seized the moment when Soderini demanded someone speak French. Hesitating dignitaries retreated, but Tito stepped forward instantly. With the natural grace of a man accustomed to command, he delivered the requisite few words in the name of the Signoria, allowing the King to pass without further delay. When later praised for his composure—a trait that had notably deserted him during the morning's crisis—he dismissed the accolade with characteristic modesty, deflecting credit to Francesco Gaddi. In the end, vanity proved the ultimate test of character; Tito's popularity stemmed from his ability to navigate chaos while others faltered.

Yet, the day's proceedings yielded other disappointments. The Florentine procession of clergy and laity had been bottlenecked, forced onto narrow backstreets to reach the Cathedral, far from the ceremonial route. Furthermore, the young monarch, though draped in the magnificent canopy, appeared less like the legendary Charlemagne and more like a grotesque caricature. His leg was too thin, resting awkwardly on cloth of gold and pearls; his mouth was a reptilian slit, and his head possessed an exorbitant, unnatural outline. Yet, amidst the blaze of rubies and the black velvet of the canopy, these flaws were negligible distractions. The crowd roared "Francia, Francia!" with fervor proportional to the sheer opulence of the canopy, which they subsequently tore apart as spoils of war, adhering to ancient custom. Royal lips touched the altar, and despite the mishaps, the King and his retinue were lodged safely in the Palace of the Via Larga, while the terrified soldiery were encamped in the open fields of Prato.

By eight o'clock, the business of the day was done, yet the streets of Florence presented a startling transformation under the November stars. Gone was the usual gloom, broken only by feeble lamps before saintly images. Now, every house displayed suspended lights, casting a uniform glow that allowed pedestrians to traverse the city with the security of daylight. Another instance of *fù gran magnificenza*.

Tito Melema walked these illuminated thoroughfares, heading home from the Piazza di Santa Croce via the Via dei Benci. Having endured a day of concealed anxiety, he had managed to slip away unnoticed from the post-dinner festivities. Alone now, he resolved to confront his circumstances with rational adjustment, hoping to shed the childish fears that plagued him. He particularly regretted his inability to recognize Baldassarre earlier; had he succeeded, the outcome might have been more favorable, sparing his father unnecessary distress. Though he preferred Baldassarre's prosperity, the path was now closed. Conflict was impossible; survival was the only imperative.

His thoughts were interrupted by a sound that cut through the evening air—not the muffled noise of revelry, but the sharp, rhythmic clang of an anvil. Startled, Tito quickened his pace. The sound signaled hope. He recognized the source: the workshop of Niccolò Caparra, a renowned destination for those seeking exquisite ironwork. "What drives the giant to work so late?" Tito wondered. "All the better," he thought. "I can attend to this small matter tonight rather than tomorrow."

Preoccupied, he paused briefly before the workshop, drawn by the sight within. The wide doorway, situated at the truncated angle of a large block of houses, was topped by a loggia with fluted tile roofing and supported by stone columns bearing rough capitals. Inside, bathed in the red glow of the forge, stood the imposing silhouette of Niccolò. His massive frame moved with rhythmic power, his huge arms rising and falling to shape metal, occasionally revealing the firm line of his mouth and powerful brow. Flanking him were two smaller, darker figures—one tending the anvil, the other pumping the bellows—emphasizing the master smith's superior bulk.

Tito entered the doorway, his form a stark contrast to the fiery interior. He waited in silence, knowing speech was futile until Niccolò acknowledged him. Only after the smith had hammered an axe head to its perfect edge and tossed it aside did Niccolò turn. He nodded with a blunt, good-natured gesture, resting his hammer on his hip.

"What is it, Messer Tito? Business?"

"Undoubtedly, Niccolò," Tito replied smoothly. "Otherwise, I would not dare interrupt your work outside of hours, which I interpret as a sign of urgent demand."

"I've been at it all day," Niccolò grunted, wiping sweat from his brow. "Making axes and spearheads. Every fool who passes my shop sticks his head in asking if I'll go see the King of France and his soldiers. I tell them no. I don't wish to see their faces; I'd rather see their backs."

"Are you forging arms for the citizens, then? Something better than rusty scythes and spits in case of trouble?"

"We shall see," Niccolò said, his tone shifting to a sharper edge. "Arms are good, and Florence will likely need them. The Frate predicts we regain Pisa, and I agree with him. But I'd like to know how that promise holds water if we aren't supplied with quality weapons. The Frate sees far ahead; he understands sense, not the nonsense of those who think they can catch birds by blinking at them."

"You're a bit of a Medicean, Messer Tito Melema," Niccolò added, his gaze piercing. "Ebbene! So was I, in my time, before the cask began to run sour. What brings you here?"

"Merely to inquire about the price of that fine coat of mail I noticed hanging here recently. I intend to purchase it for a specific individual who requires protection beneath his doublet."

"Let him come and buy it himself," Niccolò replied bluntly. "I am particular about my clientele. I require knowledge of who stands to wear my goods."

"I understand your scruples, Niccolò. But this is defensive armor; it cannot harm anyone."

"True," the smith conceded, though his expression remained guarded. "But it may embolden the wearer to inflict harm. Besides, this piece is not mine; it is the fine work of Maso of Brescia. I would be loath to see it protect the heart of a scoundrel. I must know the identity of the purchaser."

"Well, then, to be perfectly frank, Niccolò mio, I want it myself," Tito stated, sensing persuasion was futile. "I anticipate a journey soon, and you know the perils of travel in these times. Do you suspect me of treason against the Republic?"

"No, I know no ill of you," Niccolò said, returning to his straightforward manner. "But do you possess the funds? You frequent my shop enough to know my signs; you've seen the burning account books. I trust no one. The price is twenty florins, discounted for its second-hand condition. Unlikely you carry such a sum. Perhaps we can arrange payment for tomorrow."

"I happen to have the money," Tito countered, recalling his recent winnings at play. "I will transport the armor home with me."

Niccolò reached down and lifted the finely wrought coat, which collapsed into a bundle barely larger than two handfuls. After counting the florins across his palm, he handed over the armor.

"There," he said. "Take the coat. It is designed to foil swords, daggers, and arrows. Personally, I would never don such a garment. It is akin to carrying fear within one's bosom."

The words struck Tito with unsettling resonance, yet he masked his reaction with a smile. "Ah, Niccolò, we scholars are all cowards. Wielding a pen does not thicken the arm as your hammer does. Addio!"

Tito folded the armor beneath his mantle and hurried across the Ponte Rubaconte, the weight of the garment a silent testament to the dangers awaiting him.

"No room. Full up."

The door slammed with the dull, decisive thud of bureaucratic finality.

Tito paused, his grip tightening on the leather-wrapped bundle containing the newly acquired armor plates. He had traversed the bridge with purpose, driven by the weight of his commission and the urgency of the moment. Yet, the rejection echoed louder than the city's clamor. To have marched through the streets seeking employment, to have stooped to beg for sustenance, and to be denied even the most basic shelter—the casual ward—was a tactical failure of the highest order. It was not merely poverty; it was the systematic dismantling of dignity.

He stood frozen, staring at the barricaded entryway. The logic of the institution was opaque, designed to filter out the weak and the weary. A man's resolve could be eroded by a single refusal, turning a soldier into a supplicant.

"Full up," the gatekeeper had muttered, his voice devoid of empathy, treating the plight of a stranger as a clerical error to be corrected.

A figure detached itself from the gloom of the alley, stepping into the dim light. He was a veteran of the gutters, his posture hunched against the damp, hands buried deep in the pockets of ruined trousers. He smelled of wet wool and stale gin, a scent that spoke of survival rather than comfort.
