"He says it's at capacity," Tito observed, his voice steady despite the shivering that seized his chest. "Capacity implies a limit. Is this a calculated falsehood?"

"That's the standard deterrent at Fulham," the man replied, offering a jagged, cynical grin that revealed yellowed teeth. "They always say it's full. Keeps the ledger clean and the workload light. If there's square footage, they're legally bound to take you. If I were you, I'd ring the bell again and make him do his job."

"There is a certain dark logic to testing the defenses twice," Tito murmured, assessing the threat level. He approached the heavy door and yanked the iron bell-pull. The mechanism groaned, a sound of mechanical resistance, before the door swung open almost immediately.

The same grizzled pauper stood there, acting as gatekeeper with the unearned authority of a minor official. "Here again?" he sneered. "What's the tactical objective here? Think I've got nothing better to do than wait on the likes of you?"

"I am here to fulfill an obligation," Tito stated, his gaze locking onto the man's eyes. "My mission requires resources. Your facility provides them. Let us proceed."

The man blinked, caught off guard by the sheer intensity of the stranger's demeanor. The dynamic shifted instantly; the beggar was no longer a victim but a commander issuing orders. Reluctantly, the gatekeeper stepped aside, allowing passage.

Inside the courtyard, the atmosphere changed. The cold was a physical enemy, steadily liquefying the marrow in Tito's bones and draining the last of his cellular energy. But the open archway presented a tactical anomaly, and more importantly, a lifeline.

It was a ground-level opening, thrown wide to the deluge. He had only to breach the perimeter to secure dry cover. There was no watch, no patrol, no sign of life in the immediate vicinity. Out here, he was at the mercy of the elements, another anonymous casualty waiting to be swept into the Arno. The tramp at the casual ward had surrendered his freedom to the constabulary in exchange for a warm cell. Tito preferred to maintain his operational independence.

Perhaps the villa was empty. Civilian carelessness was a luxury of the secure and the comfortable. He considered knocking to alert the occupants of their vulnerability, perhaps angling for a meager reward. But experience in a harder school had taught him the futility of relying on the gratitude of the comfortable classes. More likely, he would be turned over to the authorities as a vagrant. Better to appropriate the shelter and vanish before dawn.

He leaned over the low brick wall, testing the air. The thermal differential was immediate; warmth bled from the dark aperture. Moving with deliberate silence, he vaulted the barrier. The ground beneath his boots felt cemented and stable. He pressed himself against the exterior masonry, listening at the crevice. Complete silence. It defied logic that any homeowner would leave a ground-floor blind fully raised and the sash open in this weather, but the battlefield is largely dictated by the blunders of the enemy.

Tito slipped inside, closing the door behind him. The silence of the room was absolute, a stark contrast to the chaotic noise of the street. He moved toward the fire, the heat radiating from the logs providing a temporary respite from the encroaching cold. He checked his supplies, ensuring the armor was secured, his mind already mapping the next phase of his journey. The uncertainty of the past hour had been resolved; the path ahead, however difficult, was now defined by action rather than despair.

Four days later, Romola traversed the winding lanes toward the workshop of Piero di Cosimo on the Via Gualfonda. The atmosphere along the route was thick with tension; the thoroughfares were clogged with French soldiers and Florentine citizens locked in a mutual, hostile scrutiny. On one side, the invaders, stripped of their homeland's context and confronted with a culture they deemed inferior, paraded with an arrogant air of pre-eminence. On the other, the Florentines, weary of performing hospitality for guests who treated them like subjects rather than hosts, simmered with resentment. The initial veneer of diplomatic festivity—the grand religious spectacles with their impossible flying machines, the royal courtship rituals, and the hollow discussions of commerce—had evaporated. In its place rose the harsh reality that the new Charlemagne viewed Florence not as a partner, but as a conquered prize, intending to install a viceroy and restore the exiled Medici. This logic was absurd, a grotesque inversion of divine justice, given that the sole grievance of the city was the unpopular policies of Piero de' Medici. Yet Florence refused submission. This defiance manifested in the frantic councils of the Old Palace and in the defiant banners fluttering above the piazzas, mocking every insolent Frenchman. Consequently, the streets became dangerous terrain for noblewomen. However, Romola, cloaked in her somber black veil and mantle, accompanied by the loyal old Maso, moved with a sense of armored security, immune to prying glances. Her destination was urgent: she needed to inspect the progress of her father's portrait.

Piero di Cosimo was a capricious artist, prone to abandoning canvases for months or burying them in coffers until they were lost to oblivion unless driven by strict deadlines. Though Romola enjoyed his favor, she knew that sentiment was no shield against his whims. Having promised completion weeks prior, he had failed to deliver, fueling her nervous anxiety. She possessed a desperate need to possess a likeness of her father during his blindness—a period of profound darkness in his life—and feared that without this visual anchor, his image would fade from her mind entirely. Love, she understood, demanded absolute fidelity; it required the preservation of the beloved's essence in memory. By special dispensation, she was permitted to enter his studio without announcement.

She slid the heavy iron bar and called out in a sharp, flute-like tone, mimicking the little maid who once summoned Tito. Piero responded instantly, though his explanation for his readiness was less flattering than intended. "Ah, Madonna Romola," he said, opening the door. "I thought my eggs had arrived; I was starving for them."

"I have brought you sustenance superior to hard-boiled eggs, Piero," Romola replied, offering a smile as she adjusted her veil. From Maso, she retrieved a small basket brimming with cakes and *confetti*. Stepping across the threshold, she continued, "I know you delight in such treats when they require no effort. Confess it."

"Yes, when they descend upon me as effortlessly as sunlight," Piero admitted, crossing his arms and staring down at the sweets as Romola unveiled them. He looked up then, his eyes tracing the line of her hair and the fall of her hood with the practiced appreciation of a master painter. "Yet I suspect these confections serve a different purpose: to silence your tongue while you lecture me."

"Proceed to the inner chamber," he instructed, gesturing toward the workspace. "You will find I have advanced the composition since your last visit, though it remains unfinished. Remember, I do not bind myself to promises. As the saying goes: 'Whoever promises and fails to keep, shall never have a soul at peace.'"

The door leading to the wild garden was shut tight, sealing the space. Piero was already at work, but not on Romola's commission. Her father's portrait stood propped against the wall, awaiting transport to the optimal light source. As he bent to lift it, he inadvertently revealed another canvas hidden behind it—an oil sketch of Tito, recently updated. The smaller work sat recessed within the larger frame, unnoticed by Piero until he shifted the heavy panel. While examining a detail in the piece he held, he began to move it toward an easel, unaware of the discovery.

Romola gasped, her cheeks flushing with shock. "That is Tito!"

Piero glanced around, offering a silent shrug of vexation at his own lapse in memory. He was annoyed by the oversight. Romola remained transfixed, her expression shifting from surprise to alarmed confusion. "What a peculiar image," she said, her voice trembling slightly. "When did you create this? What signifies it?"

"A mere whim," Piero dismissed, removing his skullcap and scratching his head, adopting his characteristic grimace to deflect emotional exposure. "I required a handsome youthful visage, and your husband's features suited the task perfectly."

He stepped forward, lifting the sketch with its back turned to Romola, feigning a cursory inspection before discarding it as unworthy of display. But Romola, recalling the distinctive chain-mail armor and pierced by the uncanny convergence of symbols linking Tito to fear, moved to his side. "Do not hide it; allow me to view it again. That man with the rope around his neck... I witnessed him. I saw you approach him in the Duomo. What compelled you to include him in a portrait alongside Tito?"

Seeing no viable escape, Piero chose partial honesty. "It was pure chance. The man was fleeing—climbing the steps and seized hold of your husband. Presumably, he stumbled. I happened to be present, observed the scene, and deemed the savage-looking elder a suitable study. But it is worthless; merely a reckless stroke."

With a gesture of finality, he moved the sketch away, placing it high on a shelf. "Come, examine the Oedipus."

His haste to conceal the drawing had achieved the exact opposite of his intent: it confirmed the unsettling nature of the incident. The implication lingered—that there was something inherently negative, something cowardly, about the event involving Tito. This suggestion struck a chord of pride and delicacy in Romola, silencing her further inquiry. She feared that pressing the matter might suggest she harbored even a whisper of doubt regarding her husband. Instead, she spoke softly, her voice steady. "He was a pitiful, strange creature, that prisoner. Know you anything else of him?"

"No further information," Piero replied smoothly. "I merely directed him to the hospital. Observe now; the face of Oedipus is nearly complete. Tell me your assessment."

Romola turned her full attention to the portrait, standing in reverent silence before it. Finally, she spoke. "Ah," she breathed, her eyes brightening with moisture. "You have achieved exactly what I desired. You have imbued it with that look of listening. My dear Piero..." She turned to him, gratitude evident in her expression.

"Now that is precisely what I cannot endure in women," Piero snapped, kicking aside debris cluttering the floor. "You pour forth emotions where none are warranted. Why should you offer thanks for a commissioned work, especially when I make you wait? If I paint, surely it is for my own satisfaction and reputation, yes? Should you thank a man for not being a rogue or a fool? It suffices if he thanks God for granting him such virtue. Women imagine walls are held together with honey."

"You crusty Piero!" Romola laughed through her tears, reaching into the basket to extract a particularly crisp sweetmeat. "I forgot how irritable you are. Here, consume this delicacy."

Piero accepted the treat with the grace of a bear dreaming of pears receiving a swan's egg—he genuinely savored the sweetness, yet remained accustomed to masking his vulnerabilities beneath a rough exterior. "It is excellent, Madonna Antigone," he murmured, reaching for another. He had subsisted on nothing but hard eggs for two weeks.

Romola stood opposite him, her fresh anxieties momentarily suspended by the sight of this naive joy. "Farewell, Piero," she said eventually, setting the basket down. "I promise not to express gratitude if you finalize the portrait promptly and skillfully. I shall remind you that you were obligated to complete it for your own sake."

"Agreed," Piero replied curtly, assisting her efficiently in folding her mantle and veil.

Once the door clicked shut behind her, he exhaled, thinking, "I am relieved she asked no further questions about that sketch. I dread the notion that she might deduce I considered her fine husband a fitting model for a coward. I minimized the matter; she will not dwell on it."

Piero was overly optimistic, a common trait among open-hearted men attempting subtle deception. Yet, as Romola walked home, the image of the sketch weighed heavily upon her mind. She could not reconcile the facts: the chain armor and the encounter described by Piero, both occurring on the morning the armor was acquired. Had the look of terror on Tito's face been genuine? What did it signify?

"It means nothing," she whispered to herself, trying to steady her resolve. "It was merely a coincidence. Shall I inquire of Tito?"

Her reason commanded, "No: I will not interrogate him about actions he did not voluntarily disclose. To do so would violate the trust I owe him."

But her heart countered, "I dare not ask him."

A terrible fracture existed within that trust. Like those who clutch a precious gemstone, fearing it may be cracked, she hesitated, paralyzed by the desire to believe the bond remained intact.

"The old fellow has vanished; he headed toward Arezzo the following morning, presumably unable to endure the scent of the French after serving as their captive. I visited the hospital to ascertain whether those monkish cooks had managed to determine if his faculties remained intact. Their verdict was that he exhibited no symptoms of insanity—merely a profound indifference to inquiry, appearing to cultivate a vineyard twenty miles distant. He was a cryptic, formidable tiger. I would have relished knowing more of his nature."

These words emerged from Nello's shop, spoken by Piero di Cosimo on the twenty-fourth of November, precisely one week following the French incursion. The atmosphere within the city was electric; a party of six or seven gathered at the unconventional hour of three in the afternoon, driven by the anticipation of a decisive political shift. Every public thoroughfare teemed with activity, and every shopkeeper lacking a spouse or deputy stationed himself at the threshold, thumbs hooked in his belt, surveying the chaos. The streets were littered with artisans drifting aimlessly like debris suspended in water, poised to surge forward with impetuous violence should any catalyst arise.

Nello strummed his lute while seated casually atop the counter facing the window, maintaining a vigilant gaze upon the piazza.

"Ah," Nello declared, setting down the instrument with deliberate weight, "for a gold florin, I would not have missed witnessing the spectacle of the French soldiers lumbering in their broad-soiled footwear after their fleeing captives! This is the inevitable consequence of abandoning my post to shave distinguished chins. History repeats itself: whenever I vacate this epicenter, calamity seizes my piazza."

"Piero, you should have been present," the painter retorted, his tone sharp as flint, "to observe your favored Greek trembling as though Satanasso himself had seized him. I derive pleasure from seeing your perpetually smiling patrons caught in a gale, forced to reveal their undersides against their will. Consider the hue of a man's liver when he resembles a bleached stag the moment a stranger grasps him."

"Nello, reserve your vinegar for seasoning your own omelets!" Nello countered. "What grievance exists regarding my *bel erudito* appearing startled when he sensed claws upon him and beheld an unchained lunatic at his side? Your scholar differs vastly from those brutish Swiss and Germans, whose skulls serve only as battering-rams and whose appetites justify consuming a cannonball before breakfast. We Florentines value attributes beyond this crude definition of bravery, acquired by hiring dim-witted mercenaries at a fixed rate. Let me clarify: once humanity realized they possessed superior intellect to oxen, they delegated the pulling to the beasts. Similarly, when we Florentines recognized our intellectual supremacy, we assigned the fighting to them."

"Treason, Nello!" a voice erupted from the interior chamber. "Such sentiments contradict state doctrine. Florence grinds its weapons; the latest authenticated vision from the Friar depicts Mars standing atop the Palazzo Vecchio, his arm resting upon the shoulder of Saint John the Baptist, who offers him a piece of honeycomb."

"It is acceptable, Francesco," Nello conceded smoothly. "Florence possesses several thick-skulled individuals suitable for bombarding Pisa; meanwhile, the refined spirits remain at home to conduct the reasoning and the shaving. Concerning our Piero here, if he insists on valor, let him wield his largest brush as a spear and his palette as a shield, challenging the widest-mouthed Swiss he encounters in the Prato to single combat."

"Go on, Nello," Piero growled, "your tongue operates with the relentless momentum of a mill during the Arno's flood—regardless of whether grain exists."

"Plentiful grain, I assure you. It would be as logical to expect a weathered painter to crave a javelin piercing his vitals as it is to anticipate a man whose intellect has been honed by classical texts enjoying having his visage torn by a savage beast."

"There you go, assuming people will insert their limbs into a sack merely because you term them 'hosen,'" Piero dismissed. "Who posited the existence of a wild beast, or an unarmed man charging into battle? Combat is a profession, not mine. I would be a fool to pursue danger, yet I could confront it if it sought me out."

"Why, then, do you tremble before thunder, my Piero?" Nello pressed, intent on dismantling the accusation. "One should comprehend why a specific individual is unnerved by trifles that affect others less—you who retreat with the rodents the instant a storm approaches."

"This stems from a heightened sensitivity to auditory stimuli; it bears no relation to my courage or conscience."

"And Tito Melema may possess a unique hypersensitivity to being ambushed by fugitives escaping French guards. Humans are born with aversions; I personally cannot tolerate the aroma of mint. Tito was born with an antipathy toward elderly prisoners stumbling and clutching. Behold!"

A collective laugh rippled through the assembly, confirming that Piero's reluctance toward Tito was not shared by the company. The painter, contorting his features into an unreadable grimace, stuffed tow into the crevices of his scarsella in indignant disdain, while Nello continued his triumphant monologue.

"No, my Piero, I cannot afford to disparage my *bel erudito*; nor can Florence, given that her scholars molt prematurely at the tender age of forty. Our Phoenix Pico has ascended directly to Paradise, as the Friar informs us; and the incomparable Poliziano departed mere months ago—to where, well, let us hope he has not joined the eminent scholars in the Malebolge."

"Incidentally," interjected Francesco Cei, "have you heard that Camilla Rucellai has surpassed the Friar in prophecy? Two years ago she foretold Pico's death during the season of lilies. He expired in November. 'Not the season of lilies,' scoffed the detractors. 'Go on!' retorts Camilla; 'I referred to the lilies of France, and they appear remarkably close to your nostrils.' I declare, 'Euge, Camilla!' Should the Friar demonstrate that any of his visions have been fulfilled with such precision, I shall proclaim myself a Piagnone tomorrow."

"You are excessively frivolous regarding the Friar, Francesco," observed Pietro Cennini, the scholar. "In these critical weeks, we owe him dearly for preaching peace, tranquility, and the cessation of factional strife. Those of limited discernment eagerly await the populace slipping the Friar's leash. Should the Most Christian King remain obstinate regarding the treaty today, refusing to sign terms equitable and honorable for Florence, Fra Girolamo is the sole figure capable of compelling him to reason."

"You speak truth, Messer Pietro," Nello affirmed. "The Friar represents one of Florence's most robust anchors—at least, according to the most reputable clients I honor with the blade. Yet, young Messer Niccolò remarked earlier this morning—and Francesco undoubtedly shares this sentiment—that the elasticity of interpreting visions rivals the stretching of Dido's bull-hide. A dream may signify whatever follows it. As Franco Sacchetti noted, a woman dreams of a serpent biting her, smashes a drinking vessel the next day, and cries, 'Behold, I anticipated an event; now the serpent's meaning is plain.'"

"But the Friar's visions differ fundamentally," corrected Cronaca. "He does not merely predict outcomes—the Church will be scourged and renewed, the heathens converted—but declares they shall occur swiftly. He is no slippery opportunist crafting loopholes; he is—"

"What is this? What is this?" Nello shouted, leaping from the counter and thrusting his head through the doorway. "People stream into the piazza, shouting. Some catastrophe must have unfolded in the Via Larga. Ah!" he burst forth with delighted astonishment, stepping outside laughing and waving his cap.

The entire company rushed to the door. News from the Via Larga was exactly what they awaited. Yet, the manner of its arrival left them somewhat bewildered. Carried aloft on the shoulders of the mob, seated upon a bench seemingly plucked from the street, sat Tito Melema, smiling with amused detachment at the coercion imposed upon him. His cap had slid from his head, dangling by the *becchetto* loosely wound around his neck. Upon spotting the group at Nello's door, he raised his fingers in a gesture of beckoning recognition. Within a heartbeat, he leaped from the bench onto a cart laden with bales positioned in the vast space between the Baptistery and the steps of the Duomo, while the populace swarmed around him with the noisy fervor of chickens awaiting feed. Silence fell instantly as he began to address them in his clear, mellifluous voice.

"Citizens of Florence! I possess no mandate to disseminate news save your collective will. However, the tidings are favorable and harmless to recount. The Most Christian King is signing a treaty honoring Florence. Yet, you owe this victory to one of your own citizens, a man who uttered words worthy of ancient Rome—you owe it to Piero Capponi!"

An immediate roar of voices erupted. "Capponi! Capponi! What did our Piero say?" "Ah! He refused to be transferred from Herod to Pilate!" "We knew Piero!" "_Orsù_! Inform us, what did he utter?"

Once the clamor of demand subsided slightly, Tito resumed.

"The Most Christian King demanded excessive concessions—proved obstinate—finally declaring, 'I shall order my trumpets to sound.' Then, Florentine citizens! Your Piero Capponi, speaking with the voice of a free city, proclaimed, '

This was the first time Baldassarre had returned to the Piazza del Duomo since his flight. The urge to hear the monk's sermon again burned within him, yet the memory of his previous appearance—a ragged specter with unkempt hair, a noose around his neck, labeled a lunatic by the very crowd he sought to haunt—held him back. The shame of that scene was too fresh, a raw wound that no amount of grooming could instantly cauterize. Even as he adjusted his new guise, the whisper of Tito's betrayal echoed: *"Some madman, surely."* It was not merely the cruelty of the insult that stung, but the terrifying realization that he lacked the proof to refute it. Beside the fire of vengeance that consumed him rose a cold, pragmatic dread: the suspicion that his ability to enact this retribution was illusory. It felt as though Tito had been coached by a devilish prompter, fed the darkest truths of Baldassarre's condition. Baldassarre was sane; he bore the tragic hallmark of a fractured mind, acutely aware of his own decay. Yet, the moment vindictive rage surged, a primal caution seized him, akin to a wounded beast or an insect trembling on shifting sand. This instinct to conceal his vulnerabilities had already led him to spurn Piero di Cosimo's overtures.

His caution extended to the hospital, where he claimed to be a French captive from Genoa. The monks, noting his age and the aristocratic cadence of his speech, offered him charity beyond the usual scraps: a coarse woolen tunic, peasant shoes, and a handful of *danari*. Early the next morning, he traveled toward Arezzo, stopping at the first town to shave off his beard and trim his hair to its former style. The barber produced a small steel hand-mirror. Years had passed since Baldassarre had gazed into glass, and as his eyes met the reflection, a sudden, jarring thought halted his momentum. *"Does Tito truly not recognize me?"* The question struck him like a physical blow; his hand trembled violently as he reached for the mirror. He needed to see himself before the cut was made. The barber, sensing the shake, held the glass steady.

No, the resemblance remained undeniable. The wrinkles were deeper, etched by three years of suffering, but the skin retained its rough, clumsy texture, marked by shallow bosses like cipher symbols. It was only yellower, resembling a dried rind. That shaggy white beard was no disguise; it was a signature known to eyes that had scrutinized him for sixteen years, eyes that should have recognized him amidst the debris of the sea. Something had shifted in his gaze, a difference that should have made identification more startling, for a known voice often strikes hardest when heard as a desperate cry. Doubt was a luxury he could not afford; he felt Tito knew him. Pushing the mirror away, he resumed his stride, the surging currents of hatred and vengeance driving him forward once more.

He retraced his steps toward Florence but avoided the city until dusk, turning instead to a secluded pool shaded by alder bushes still clinging to autumn gold. On this quiet November day, the still water tempted him as a potential mirror. He sat on the bank, bending low to study his reflection with an intensity he hadn't dared show the barber.

Was there madness in his face? Did his visage betray the chaos within?

Now, under the scrutiny of eager inquiry, his eyes burned with purpose. But in the long, dark hours of solitude, when whispers mocked him and phantom forms drifted away from grasping fingers, doubt undoubtedly clouded his expression. Worse still, when hope briefly pierced the fog only to collapse again, leaving him helpless, his face must have shown the blank confusion of sudden blindness.

Could he prove his identity? Could he construct a chain of logic that wouldn't snap? Who would believe he once possessed a mind brimming with rare knowledge, sharp with intricate thought and eloquent speech? That library of memories had slipped away, scattered like water from a broken urn. Had it vanished forever, or was it trapped behind a dam that might one day burst?

He clung to the latter hope. Since waking from his fever-stricken stupor on the straw mat, his mind had undergone a turbulent transformation. As his body regained strength, so did his will and the memory of his emotional life. But the sensation of lost knowledge gnawed at him constantly. Occasionally, during moments of intense excitement, flashes of his former self returned—he read Greek texts effortlessly, his mind dancing freely among familiar concepts. These were but sparks in a rising darkness, yet they fueled a dangerous ambition: if these flashes could lengthen into sustained periods, he might finally execute his revenge. He envisioned crafting a torment so exquisite that a mere mortal arm could never inflict it.

Rising from his crouch, Baldassarre folded his arms and focused every ounce of mental energy on his immediate strategy. He required knowledge and opportunity, and while waiting, he needed sustenance without begging. His greatest fear was being perceived as a senile, helpless relic. No one must know that half his mind was gone; the lost strength might return, however briefly, and that interval might suffice.

He knew how to uncover information regarding Tito. The name "Bratti Ferravecchi" had lodged in his mind with obsessive repetition. A man in Genoa, who had seen Tito's ring, had revealed that the gemstone was purchased in Florence from a young, well-dressed Greek in the shop of a dealer named Bratti Ferravecchi, located on the street of the same name. This revelation sent Baldassarre into a violent turmoil. Until now, he had clung desperately to his faith in Tito, refusing to accept that he had been abandoned. Initially, he reasoned, *"My message never reached him; he searches for me, tracing my path."* Later, in Corinth, he had convinced his captors to secure inquiries about his fate, believing, *"Here, at last, he must find me."* But illness had shattered him, rendering him worthless save for a ransom that never arrived. Sitting helpless in the sun, he had succumbed to despair, assuming Tito was dead or imprisoned. He appeared so much like a hopeless idiot that his owners gladly transferred him to a Genoese merchant. During months of arduous travel through the Archipelago and Asia Minor, Baldassarre had physically recovered, yet landed in Genoa with a weariness that made death preferable. Only one possibility kept him alive: Tito might live, perhaps in prison or poverty. If so, there remained a faint, deferred hope of reunion, of clasping hands with the one being who remembered him before the fracture. It was in this fragile state that he encountered the stranger wearing Tito's onyx ring. Though Baldassarre couldn't have described the ring beforehand, its sight ignited dormant fibers of recognition. The fact that Tito, nearly a year after their separation, thrived in Florence selling a gem he shouldn't have sold, was a truth Baldassarre preferred to remain stunned by rather than dissect logically. He tried to feel only joy at seeing Tito again. Perhaps Tito believed his father dead; the mystery could be explained. *"At least,"* he thought, *"I shall meet eyes that remember me. I am not alone."*

Once more, Baldassarre declared, *"I am not alone; my revenge walks beside me."*

Viewing himself in the water, he saw not a pitiful old man, but an instrument of retribution, separate from his decaying flesh. Human passion, whether noble or base, reaches a point where it transcends selfishness, becoming a fire that consumes everything else as mere fuel.

He stared at the pale, black-browed image until he merged with it.

*"I was a fool who worshipped a woman, believing she cared. Then I took a helpless child, fostering him, watching to see if he would care for me above all else. I would have bled to warm him if I'd seen him share my pain. I labored, straining to extract a single drop of unselfish love from this hard life. Fool! Men love their own pleasures; there is none in me. Yet I watched, convinced I saw what I sought. As a child, he lifted soft eyes to mine, holding my hand willingly. I thought, 'This boy will love me.' Because I gave my life to spare him sorrow, he would care when I thirsted... Curses on him! May I see him die with those red lips white and dry as ash. When he seeks pity, may he see my face rejoicing in his agony. It is all a lie—this world is a lie. Goodness exists only in hate. Fool! Not one drop of love came from your striving; life gave you nothing. But there are deep wells for hatred and revenge. I retain memory for that, and strength in my arm, strength in my will. If killing him is all I can do..."*

Baldassarre's mind recoiled from the brevity of such a punishment. His soul demanded an eternity of vengeance, where he, an undying hate, could clutch an undying traitor and hear that fair, smiling hardness cry in eternal anguish. Yet, his primary need was a slow, grinding revenge played out under the same sky, on the same earth where he had been forsaken and driven to fainting despair. As soon as he concentrated on the means, the weight of his weakness pressed down like a frostbite. This despised body, the vessel of his sublime quest, required nourishment and decency. If he had to wait, he must labor, and his labor must be humble, for he possessed no trade skills. He wondered if the sight of writing might spark

"Romola," Tito said on the second morning following his address in the Piazza del Duomo, "grand visitors arrive today. The Milanese Count returns, accompanied by the Seneschal de Beaucaire, the great favorite of the Cristianissimo. I perceive you harbor no desire to endure the hollow rituals required by such rustling magnates, figures we shall likely never encounter again. Since they intend to inspect the antiquities and the library, you ought to suspend your labors and accompany your cousin Brigida."

Romola detected the subtext—a request for respite—and immediately acquiesced. Yet, returning moments later, hooded and mantled, she declared, "Ah, what a prolonged exhalation Florence will undertake once the gates swing wide and the final Frenchman strides out! Even you, Tito, wear the signs of fatigue beneath your patience; confess it. Your brow burns."

Tito leaned over his desk, quill moving rhythmically across parchment. She reached out, intending a customary caress upon his head, but found her palm met resistance. Her hand rested there, yet he remained rigid, incapable of lifting his gaze as if her touch were a leaden cowl pressing down upon him. Instead, he maintained a light, perfunctory tone while his pen continued its work. "The French depart from Florence with the same indifference wasps show toward a ripe pear once they have extracted their fill."

Romola, acutely attuned to the vacuum where his usual engagement should have been, withdrew her hand. "I am leaving, Tito."

"Farewell, my sweet one. I remain here. Take Maso with you."

Still, Tito did not lift his eyes. Romola departed without further comment. In the annals of matrimony, trivialities often mark epochal shifts; this morning, for the first time, she acknowledged not merely that Tito had altered, but that his trajectory relative to her had shifted irrevocably. Was the fault hers? She might have conceded this point had not the armor and the painting suggested an external catalyst, a mystery entirely opaque to her understanding.

The moment Romola crossed the threshold, Tito lowered his pen and gazed upward, seeking refuge in the safety of parchment and fractured marble. A surge of disgust washed over him; he chided himself for failing to greet her as he invariably had. Had he possessed the capacity, he would have doubled his kindness, yet he found himself paralyzed by an involuntary recoil. This shrinking stemmed directly from the very traits that drove his desire to prove his affection—to succeed, to provide. He faced a precipice: a step he knew would ignite her profound indignation, requiring him to endure significant discomfort before securing her forgiveness. Tito's nature, devoid of defiance or audacity, leaned heavily toward preserving her tenderness. He was not shackled by sentimental scruples—his own rapid internal logic had dismantled such notions as irrelevant to utility—but his lack of scruple offered no shield against the visceral dread of pain. No device could insulate him from the agony of the impending collision; no bland persuasion could soften the impact of the desperate leap he was being driven to make, spurred by the terror of the tooth and the claw closing in.

A latent realization had crystallized earlier: his stubborn adherence to Bardo's directives regarding the library had devolved into sentimental folly, stripping both himself and Romola of tangible advantage. Only the events of the past week—the crushing pressure of a new imperative and the fleeting window of opportunity—had forged this resolution. Specifically, it was not until he beheld Baldassarre regaining his composure, nor until he sensed the compulsion to flee Florence, that he resolved to exercise his legal right to liquidate the library before the chance vanished amidst the bidding of French and Milanese interests. If exile became inevitable, he would not depart as a destitute wanderer. Accustomed to an agreeable existence, he sought to transport the means to sustain it. Among his priorities was carrying Romola with him, ensuring he did not drag any infamy into their flight. Success had ignited an appetite for the privileges of elevated social standing; the notion of decamping under dishonor, even laden with diamonds, appeared not as a temptation but as a grotesque alternative.

He could not achieve independence from the Florentines who treated him with mere polite regard, nor from Romola. She was the wife of his first love; he still loved her. She constituted essential furniture of his life, from which he recoiled at the thought of separation. He winced under her judgment, uncertain of the extent of her revulsion. The power a husband wields to risk betrayal—power a lover rarely possesses—proved insufficient to counteract Tito's acute unease. This was the leaden weight that held his head bowed, preventing him from meeting her eyes. Their pure light illuminated the prospect of struggle too vividly. Yet, helplessness was not an option. If they were to abandon Florence, capital was non-negotiable. Indeed, Tito could not construct a viable future without a substantial sum, and this necessity had been the root of all his transgressions. He would have embraced any sacrifice provided it lacked unpleasantness.

The rustling magnates arrived and departed; bargains were struck; Romola returned home. That night, however, nothing grave was spoken. Tito proved gay and chatty, recounting stories and descriptions of the French visit with a fervor unseen before. Romola perceived an effort in his vivacity, interpreting it as penitence born of his awareness that she had been wounded. She accepted this display, though an ache lingered within her at the widening chasm between them—an offense neither dared name.

The following day, Tito remained absent until late evening. It marked a significant date for Romola: Piero di Cosimo, spurred by the fear that he might have caused her pain in the preceding week, had dispatched her father's portrait. She propped it against the back of his old chair, studying it when the door opened behind her. Bernardo del Nero entered.

"It is you, godfather! How I wish you had arrived sooner; dusk is already falling," Romola said, approaching him.

"I stopped briefly to share good tidings, knowing Tito had not yet returned," Bernardo replied. "The French King departs tomorrow; delay is no longer permissible. Another clash occurred this morning between our citizens and his soldiers. Yet, there is now hope for the restoration of order and the resumption of trade."

"That is joyous," Romola responded. "Yet sudden. Yesterday, Tito seemed convinced the King's departure was distant."

"He has been well barked at, hence the retreat," Bernardo smiled. "His own generals opened their throats quite effectively, and finally, our Signoria unleashed the city's mastiff, Fra Girolamo. The Cristianissimo, frightened by that thunder, issued the order to move. I fear little consensus will emerge once he leaves, but at least all parties rejoice at the cessation of Florence being stifled by soldiery. The Friar barked successfully this time."

"Ah, what is this?" Bernardo asked, as Romola, grasping his arm, guided him before the portrait. "Let us examine."

He began unwinding his long scarf while she arranged a seat for him. "Do you require your spectacles, godfather?" Romola asked anxiously, wishing him to see precisely what she saw.

"No, child, none needed," Bernardo replied, uncovering his grey head as he sat with firm erectness. "At this distance, my aged eyes may surpass yours. Old men's vision resembles old men's memories; they retain their strength best for things seen long ago."

"It is preferable to having no portrait at all," Romola apologized after a brief silence. "It bears less resemblance to him than the image I hold in my mind, though that too may fade with the years."

She rested her arm upon his shoulder, drawn strongly toward him by their shared reverence for the dead. "I cannot say," Bernardo murmured. "Almost, I perceive Bardo as he was in youth more clearly than this picture depicts him in age. Your father possessed immense fire in his eyes when young. It remains a mystery to me how one with such an impatient spirit could dedicate his life to books and shadows. Nevertheless, he poured his heart into it."

Bernardo shrugged slightly at the end, yet Romola detected in his voice a sentiment resonating with her own. "And he died disappointed," she said involuntarily. Immediately fearing her words might be construed as an indictment of Tito, she hastened to continue, "If only we could witness his longest, dearest wish realized exactly as he envisioned!"

"Well, so we may," Bernardo said kindly, rising to don his cap. "Times are clouded, but fish are caught by waiting. Who knows? Should the wheel turn sufficiently, I may yet serve as Gonfaloniere before my death; no creditor can touch these assets." He glanced around, then turned to her, patting her cheek. "And need not fear my demise; my ghost claims nothing. I have ensured that in my will."

Romola seized the hand resting against her cheek and pressed it to her lips in silence. "Have you not been chastising your husband for his recent absences? I see him everywhere except here," Bernardo remarked, eager to shift the topic.

A flush spread rapidly over her neck and face. "He has been greatly missed," she replied. "You know he speaks so eloquently. I am pleased to know his worth is recognized."

"You are content, then, Madonna Orgogliosa?" Bernardo asked, smiling as he moved toward the door.

"Assuredly."

Poor Romola! There existed one element that would render the sting of disappointment in her husband more unbearable: the knowledge that others perceived

The following day, the entire city of Florence vibrated with the electric anticipation of the French departure. For Romola, amidst the general rejoicing, there lingered a faint, superstitious hope that the specific anxieties plaguing her regarding Tito—burdens that had arrived alongside the foreign guests—might evaporate with them. The French occupation had lasted barely eleven days, yet within that brief span, Romola had endured a depth of acute unhappiness unknown to her prior existence. Since their arrival, Tito had donned a hateful armor of defensiveness; while she could not logically discern why their exit should dismantle the source of his terror—though the visceral image of a prisoner seizing him, captured in Piero's sketch, haunted her thoughts—she knew that with the French gone, she would finally be rid of the tangible associations of her pain.

Clad in her mantle, Romola stationed herself beneath the loggia atop the house, scanning the horizon for glimpses of the retreating troops and the royal entourage crossing the bridges toward the Porta San Piero, the gateway leading to Siena and Rome. Even after the gates sealed shut, she remained, seeking to resonate with the thunderous toll of the bells. Dusk had fallen by the time she descended into the library. With steely resolve, she kindled her lamp, vowing to quell the agitation that had paralyzed her throughout the day and resume her transcription of the catalogue. Tito had departed early; she anticipated his return later. Her intention was to vacate the study once he arrived, moving to the adjacent salon adorned with dancing nymphs and birds—a space he had previously deemed too chilly and somber for her comfort.

To her profound astonishment, Tito appeared before she had even commenced her work. Her initial reaction was a pang of concern: how desolate he must feel, navigating the vast darkness of the room illuminated only by a solitary oil lamp at the far end, with the embers of the fire nearly extinguished. She moved toward him with urgency.

"Tito, dearest," she breathed, raising her pale arms to loosen the *becchetto* from his neck, "I had no idea you would return so soon."

"Am I unwelcome?" he countered, flashing one of his brightest smiles. He embraced her, yet playfully tilted his head back, creating a playful distance.

"Tito!" The word escaped her lips in a tone of affectionate rebuke. Immediately, he kissed her, stroking her hair in his customary manner, seemingly unconcerned with removing his cloak. Romola quivered with delight. The cumulative emotions of the day had sharpened her sensitivity to the restoration of his familiar demeanor. "It will return," she murmured to herself, "the old happiness. He is like himself again."

Yet, Tito was exerting immense effort to project this normalcy; his heart hammered with underlying anxiety.

"If I had anticipated your return so quickly," Romola said, assisting him in shedding his wrappings, "I would have staged a small celebration for this joyous ringing of the bells. I did not intend to remain in the library upon your arrival."

"Never mind, sweet," he dismissed casually. "Cease worrying about the fire. Come, sit."

A low stool stood beside his chair, Romola's usual perch during their conversations. She rested her arm upon his knee, mimicking the gesture she once shared with her father, gazing upward as he spoke. He had failed to notice the portrait looming nearby, and she held her tongue, contemplating it with renewed intensity.

"I have experienced the clamor of the bells for the first time, Tito," she began. "I relished being shaken and deafened by them; I felt akin to a Bacchante possessed by divine fury. Do the citizens appear truly joyful tonight?"

"Joyful in a sour, pious manner," Tito shrugged. "Truth be told, those remaining in Florence have scant cause for jubilation. In my view, the most rational grounds for gladness lie in fleeing Florence entirely."

Tito struck the desired note effortlessly, devoid of pretense. His delivery was unemphatic, yet his gravity prompted an anxious query from Romola.

"Why, Tito? Have fresh troubles arisen?"

"No need for new ones, my Romola. Three powerful factions currently exist in the city, poised to tear each other apart. Should the Frate's faction prove dominant enough to silence the others—as seems probable—life will resemble a funeral procession. They are already simmering with plans for a Great Council; should they succeed, the individual who chants sacred Lauds the loudest shall be deemed most fit for office. Furthermore, the city will be financially hemorrhaged by the subsidy paid to the French King and the war to reclaim Pisa. The outlook is dismal enough without the tyranny of fanatics. Florence will undoubtedly be a paradise for those who spend their evenings in crypts whipping themselves, but for everyone else, the exiles hold the advantage. Personally, I have concluded that wisdom dictates we depart Florence, my Romola."

She recoiled. "Tito, how could we abandon Florence? Surely you do not think I could leave—at least not yet, certainly not for some time." Her voice faltered, turning cold and trembling. Tito must perceive the depths of her hesitation.

"That is merely a fabrication of your own imagination, my sweet one. Your isolation has led you to place undue weight on trivialities. Recall what I told you before our marriage: I wished we resided elsewhere than in Florence. Had you traversed more lands and encountered diverse peoples, you would understand my sentiment. There is something in the Florentines that reminds me of their biting spring winds. I prefer individuals who approach life with less fervor. It would benefit your Romola greatly to witness a new existence. I wish to immerse her slightly in the soft waters of forgetfulness."

He leaned forward, kissing her forehead and resting his hand once more upon her fair hair. Yet, she felt his caress no more than if he had touched a mask. Agitated by the widening chasm between their minds, she was oblivious to the contact of his lips.

"Tito, it is not because I deem Florence the most pleasant place in the world that I refuse to depart. It is because—and because—we must fulfill my father's wish. My godfather is elderly; he is seventy-one. We cannot abandon him."

"It is precisely those superstitions clinging to your mind like obscuring clouds that constitute a primary reason for my desire to relocate us two hundred leagues from Florence. I am compelled to act contrary to your will: if those tender eyes perceive falsely, I must see for them and rescue my wife from squandering her life on impracticable dreams."

Romola sat rigid and silent. She could not ignore the trajectory of Tito's argument: he sought to convince her that the library could be deposited in a monastery or otherwise disposed of to sever their ties to Florence. She resolved never to submit her conscience to his judgment on matters of filial duty, preparing internally to endure any pain required for resistance. However, this determination remained dormant in these opening moments, crushed by the heart-wrenching realization that she and Tito were now openly divided in their aspirations. He welcomed her silence; despite fearing the strength of her feelings, he could not help but overestimate the persuasive power of his own logic, trapped as he was in the narrow confines of a merely clever, unimpassioned mind. His actions did not appear sinister to himself, nor did his imagination allow him to visualize their impact on Romola. Proceeding in the same gentle, admonishing tone, he continued.

"You know, dearest—you whose clear judgment has always guided you—that the concept of isolating a collection of books and antiquities, attaching a single name to them eternally, offers no valid, substantial benefit to its object. Moreover, it is susceptible to defeat in myriad ways. Witness the fate of the Medici collections! In my opinion, it is even blameworthy to harbor such petty notions of appropriation. Why should anyone reasonably rejoice that Florence possesses the fruits of scholarly research and taste more than any other city? I comprehend your reverence for the wishes of the deceased; however, wisdom imposes limits on such sentiments, lest lives be perpetually wasted in futile devotion—much like praising deaf gods forever. You dedicated your life to your father while he lived; why demand more of yourself?"

"Because it was a trust," Romola replied, her voice low but distinct. "He trusted me, he trusted you, Tito. I did not anticipate you would feel differently, but I did expect you to recognize that."

"Yes, dearest, naturally I would feel so regarding points concerning your father's actual welfare or happiness. But there is no question of that now. If we believed in purgatory, I would be as anxious as you to ensure masses were said. If I believed it would pain your father to see his library preserved and utilized in a manner differing from his intent, I would share your rigidity. Yet, a touch of philosophy should teach us to cast aside these air-woven fetters that mortals bind themselves with, spending their lives in misery under the mere imagination of burden. Your mind, which seizes ideas so readily, my Romola, is capable of distinguishing between substantial good and these brain-wrought fantasies. Consider, dearest, what possible utility these books and antiquities serve, stored together under your father's name in Florence, compared to if they were dispersed or transported elsewhere? Nay, is not the very scattering of such treasures among hands that know how to value them a means of extending their usefulness? This rivalry among Italian cities is petty and illiberal. The loss of Constantinople was the gain of the entire civilized world."

Romola remained paralyzed by the crushing weight of the new revelation Tito was unveiling about himself. Resistance found no outlet against this fluent

Baldassarre wandered the uneven terrain outside Florence's walls, seeking the cheapest shelter available. His path led him toward the hill of San Giorgio, a jagged scar of land where a landslide had shattered homes centuries prior, leaving behind rough ground strewn with great stones. Above this broken earth stood a peculiar, truncated tower capped with fluted tiles, and nestled against its ruins was a small outhouse. Here, under the skeletal branches of a dying mulberry tree, a shriveled old woman untied a goat with two kids. One section of the outbuilding housed livestock; the other, open to the air, contained only straw and tools—the perfect refuge Baldassarre sought.

He approached the woman, shouting his request for lodging and offering payment. Initially, she responded only with muffled denials, her head shaking in the rhythmic cadence of the deaf. Persistence eventually pierced her silence. He explained he was a destitute stranger from overseas, unable to afford hostels, needing only a place to sleep on straw for a few coins a week. She remained dubious, muttering to herself, until a pragmatic thought struck her. Fetching a hatchet, she pointed to a log half-buried in litter in the corner. "Chop that," she demanded. "If you do, you may sleep here tonight."

Baldassarre agreed. Monna Lisa watched him with arms akimbo, a smile of cunning satisfaction playing on her lips. *'It's lain there since my old man died,'* she muttered, observing his efficient strokes. *"What then? Might as well throw a stone on the fire. He chops well, speaks a foreign tongue, looks odd. Cheaper than anyone else. If he only needs straw, I'll make him run errands up and down the hill. Who cares? Hidden sins are half-forgiven. Peccato celato e mezzo perdonato. He's a stranger; he won't notice her. I'll tell her to keep quiet."*

Her thoughts drifted to a pair of bright blue eyes fixed on a four-inch hole in the upper window shutter. The shutter was closed not out of malice, but necessity; the opposite window held glass, while this one remained a crude aperture for ventilation and surveillance. The hole was too high, requiring the small observer to stand on a rickety stool, yet Tessa endured the discomfort willingly. Drawn by the strange voice, she had darted across her room repeatedly, finally settling on the stool to watch until the wood was split and Baldassarre entered the darkness, seating himself on the straw as dusk gathered.

A powerful urge seized Tessa. She wanted to share her supper, to converse with this silent man. Unlike Monna Lisa, he wasn't deaf, and she could whisper truths to him that would be wasted on the old woman. Strangers were transient figures, drifting through lives that mattered less to her than the safety of her current arrangement. Though obedience formed the bedrock of her duties, the temptation was overwhelming. She could confess this transgression to the Padre next Easter alongside her minor faults—falling asleep during prayers, her occasional temper with the deaf woman. With idle hands and no fear of fabrication, she turned from the shutter, her childish face flushed with excitement. Her attire was that of a peasant girl prepared for a festival: a clean dark-green serge gown cinched with a red girdle, a string of red glass beads at her throat, and brown hair knotted tight with a silver pin. A single new ornament, a fine gold ring, caught the fading light.

For a moment, she sat nursing her knees, her soul trembling on the precipice of forbidden joy. She had been strictly warned that forming acquaintances would cause her happiness to vanish like a treasure turning to lead upon exposure. Yet, her compliance had been driven partly by terror of her stepfather, Nofri, whose shadow loomed even here. This old man posed no threat; he was a stranger sleeping in the outhouse, likely unaware of Nofri's existence. Furthermore, if she fed him, he would be grateful and silent. Monna Lisa's prohibition would be irrelevant once the act was committed.

Downstairs, supper was being prepared—a mountain of cheesy macaroni, fragrant enough to tempt any wanderer. Tripping lightly, Tessa descended with a mind full of schemes. Pretending innocence, she asked what the noise was, then furrowed her brow with a peremptory air reminiscent of a kitten attempting intimidation. She ordered the old woman upstairs, declaring her intention to dine below. Within moments, Tessa emerged, lantern in one hand, a wooden bowl of macaroni in the other, kicking gently at the outhouse door.

Baldassarre, lost in somber reverie, blinked in confusion as the door opened to reveal a surprising little maid. Delight sparkled in her wide eyes, breaking his isolation. "I've brought you supper!" she shouted, leaning close as if addressing the deaf. "Eat while I stay with you."

Distrust warred with surprise, but Baldassarre found no impulse to reject her. He sank onto the straw passively as Tessa positioned herself beside him, placing the bowl on his lap and setting down the lantern. She crossed her hands, nodding significantly at the food, signaling permission to eat. In her fervor, she had momentarily forgotten his hearing ability, defaulting to her usual pantomime and volume.

The invitation was welcome; he had been gnawing dry bread, his appetite keen for warmth. Tessa watched silently as he consumed several mouthfuls, noting how his initial fierce gaze softened. Then, she leaned in again. "I like my supper, don't you?"

Baldassarre returned a look of gentle affection, akin to a dog touched by kindness but unable to smile. "Yes," he said softly. "But I hear well. I am not deaf."

"It is true; I forgot," Tessa admitted, clasping her hands. "But Monna Lisa is deaf. She's kind, and I'm not afraid. We live well; we have plenty. I can have nuts whenever I want. I used to work, but I hated it. I loved feeding the mules, especially poor Giannetta. We have a goat and two kids now. I used to talk to the goat because Monna Lisa was the only one there. But now I have something else—can you guess?"

She pulled back, challenging him with a mischievous smile.

"No," Baldassarre replied dreamily, setting aside his bowl. The girl seemed a fragment of his own distant past.

"You like me to talk to you, don't you?" Tessa pressed. "But you mustn't tell anyone. Should I fetch some cold sausage?"

He shook his head, appearing mild and harmless.

"Well, then, I have a little baby. Such a pretty *bambinetto*, with tiny fingers and nails! Not old yet; born at the Nativity, Monna Lisa says. I was married one Nativity, a long time ago, and nobody knew. Oh, Santa Madonna! I didn't mean to tell you that!"

Tessa stiffened, biting her lip, watching him as if expecting a dramatic reaction. But he seemed indifferent, perhaps due to the nature of strangers.

"Yes," she continued aloud, "you're a stranger. You don't live anywhere or know anyone, do you?"

"No," Baldassarre murmured, thinking aloud rather than answering. "I only know one man."

"His name isn't Nofri, is it?" Tessa asked, anxiety tightening her features.

"No," Baldassarre noticed her fear. "Is that your husband's name?"

The assumption amused Tessa. She laughed, clapping her hands. "No, indeed! But I can't tell you about my husband. You'd never guess what he's like—he's nothing like Nofri!"

She laughed again at the absurd contrast between the name Nofri, synonymous with a cross-grained stepfather, and the image of her actual spouse.

"But I don't see him often," she said more seriously. "Sometimes I pray to the Holy Madonna to send him more frequently, and once she did. But I must return to my *bimbo*. I'll bring it tomorrow to show you. You'd like to see it. Sometimes it cries and makes faces, but only when hungry, Monna Lisa says. You wouldn't believe it, but Monna Lisa had babies once, and they're all dead old men. My husband says she'll never die now because she's so well-dried. I'm glad, for I love her. Would you like to stay here tomorrow?"

"I'd like to have this place to rest in," Baldassarre said. "I'd pay for it and harm no one."

"No, indeed. You're not a bad old man. But you look troubled. Is there something you'll cry about when I leave you alone? *I* used to cry once."

"No, child. I think I shall cry no more."

"That's right. I'll bring breakfast and show you the *bimbo*. Goodnight."

Tessa collected her bowl and lantern, closing the door behind her. To Baldassarre, the girl was no more than a faint rainbow against the blackness of a man wrestling in deep waters. He barely thought of her again until his dreamless wakefulness gave way to disturbed dreams.

Yet Tessa dwelt heavily on him. Upon entering the house, she immediately recounted her actions to Monna Lisa, insisting the stranger be allowed to rest whenever he pleased. The old woman, hoping to cultivate a useful tenant, feigned reluctance, shaking her head

"Eleven days," Messer Naldo confirmed, his voice cutting through the twilight of the twenty-eighth of November. "Not a month's march. Just a short detour past the mountains."

The scene unfolding in the Via de' Bardi served as a grim epilogue to the morning's confrontation, illuminating the desperate calculus that drove Naldo back toward the hill of San Giorgio. It was a man cornered by his own momentum, returning to the source of his obsession before the dust of his departure had settled.

Inside the cottage, the atmosphere was thick with the scent of woodsmoke and unwashed linen, a stark contrast to the sterile grandeur of the city. Tito stood in the doorway, his silhouette framed by the gloom of the street. When he had first secured this haven for Tessa over a year prior, he had convinced himself it was an act of pure charity—a reflexive kindness toward a fragile, trusting creature who mistook him for a husband. Yet, the truth was more tangled. While he possessed a natural inclination to coddle the pretty, prattling girl, his heart belonged elsewhere. For the first time in his life, Tito loved Romola with a ferocity that rendered Tessa's charms irrelevant; her presence was merely a soothing balm, a vibration against his cheek, whereas Romola possessed him entirely.

His intellect was too sharp, his fiber too refined, to indulge in the gross pleasures of a hedonist. Instead, he had woven a gossamer web around himself and Tessa, a trap he felt powerless to dismantle. In the initial moments following their sham marriage, he had calculated the utility of keeping her under an illusion, banking on his future needs. But since that pivotal decision, the web had grown autonomously, spiraling outward regardless of his intent. The distinction between kindness and self-deception blurred in a soft nature like his. The irritation he felt during her relentless pursuit on the day of his betrothal, coupled with his solemn vow to reveal the truth, had paradoxically strengthened his resolve to maintain the deception. He had chosen to provide a home, to let the lie stand, driven by a twisted form of benevolence.

Now, in the throes of his first true devotion to Romola, Tito required a shield. He needed a narrative to justify his secret infidelity, a way to reconcile the guilt of betraying his wedded love with the overwhelming pull of his new passion. Every intense emotion constructs its own private morality, its own sacred piety, just as a son's love for a mother can endure amidst the foulest corruption. Tito could not yet bear the weight of committing a silent crime against his vows.

Consequently, he redoubled his efforts to preserve the secrecy of this offense. Monna Lisa, a woman of limited horizons who rarely ventured beyond specific shops or the confessional, knew nothing of his true identity or whereabouts. She believed only that he paid her handsomely to ensure her comfort, caring little else except for her growing affection for Tessa and the domestic duties she performed. A veil of mystery hung over the arrangement; Tessa was a peasant girl (*contadina*), while Messer Naldo was a gentleman. Yet, thanks to Tito's terrorizing of Tessa—who had been warned that silence was the price of his continued presence—and Monna Lisa's profound deafness, the truth remained buried. Tessa, unable to articulate her suspicions to the mute landlady, kept her mouth shut, unlike her earlier indiscretion with Baldassarre.

For months, Tito's visits had been sporadic, seemingly necessitating long journeys. These trips were motivated primarily by a paternalistic desire to verify that Tessa's world remained intact. Though he found the prospect of visiting less pleasant than the reality, he cherished the naive, ignorant trust she placed in him. He was determined to preserve this simplicity, to keep Tessa a genuine country flower, untainted by the complexities of higher society. He would be horrified to see her dressed in finery inappropriate to her station; the zest of her conversation would evaporate if her world expanded, if her pleasures matured beyond the squirrel-like hoarding of nuts. This strategy preserved both Tessa's innocence and Tito's finances, sparing him the escalating costs of maintaining a household that demanded resources he reserved for his own lavish habits.

Previously, the mere knowledge of Tessa's existence up the hill had occasionally lured Tito away from the weariness of caring for the aging Bardo, offering a respite from sharing Romola's burdens. However, the current urgency was different. It stemmed from a visceral hunger for Tessa's unconditional faith in *him*, a craving ignited only recently. This shift was marked as clearly as a sudden blindness or deafness. It occurred the night he returned from buying the armor, crossing the bridge with the steel clinking in his hands. Facing Romola, who waited with innocent eyes, he felt an insurmountable dread. She trusted him blindly, yet behind that frank gaze lay a judgmental intelligence capable of dissecting his motives. Any misplaced trust she held was born not of ignorance, but of a nobility that threatened to expose him. Exhausted by the day's exertions and the tension of self-control, he sought a sanctuary where he could relax his guard, unburdened by public scrutiny. Within reach lay a creature of sweet adoration, safe and unconstraining, who would accept any fabrication without flinching. Thus, that evening, despite Romola's waiting presence, he turned his steps upward toward the hill.

It was inevitable, therefore, that he retraced these exact steps eleven days later, recoiling from Romola's first explosive rebuke. He could not replace Tessa in his wife's position, nor could he wish for reconciliation with Romola, for she belonged to the realm of ambition and high purpose. What he craved was a refuge from a moral rigor he could not dismiss as mere folly; Tessa's small, pliable soul offered exactly that escape hatch.

It was barely past eight o'clock when Tito ascended the stone steps to Tessa's chamber. Usually, she greeted him eagerly upon his entry, but tonight the house was silent. Pushing open the door, he found the cause. A solitary, dim lamp flickered above the dying embers of the hearth, casting long shadows. Tessa knelt beside the bedside where the infant lay. Her head had slumped onto the pillow, her brown rosary slipping from her fingers to rest loosely in her right hand. She had drifted into sleep mid-prayer.

Tito moved quietly across the small room, settling close beside her. She must have sensed his approach in her dreams, for she opened her eyes without a start, remaining perfectly still as if his smiling presence had sealed off any disturbing impulses. When he reached up to stroke her chin and lean in for a kiss, she whispered, "I dreamed it, and then I said it was dreaming—and then I awoke, and it was true."

"Little sinner!" Tito teased, pinching her chin playfully. "You haven't finished your prayers. I'll punish you by refusing to look at your baby; he's ugly."

Tessa's face tightened with pouting distress, though she tried to hide it as she bent anxiously over the sleeping child. "Ah, that's not true! He's prettier than anything. You don't think he's ugly. You'll look at him. He's even prettier than when you saw him before—only he's asleep, so you can't see his eyes or his tongue, and I can't show you his hair. But it grows! Isn't that wonderful? Look at him! His face stays the same when he sleeps; there's less to see than when he's awake. If you kiss him very gently, he won't wake. Do you want to kiss him?"

Satisfied, Tito bestowed a butterfly kiss upon the small bundle, then turned Tessa's face toward him, placing a hand on her shoulder. "You prefer looking at the baby to looking at your husband, you false one!"

Still kneeling, Tessa rested her hands on his knee, gazing up at him with the round-cheeked, adoring expression of an angel from Fra Lippo Lippi's frescoes. "No," she shook her head firmly. "I love you best always. Only I want you to look at the bambino and love him. Before, I only wanted you to love me."

"And did you expect me to return so soon?" Tito asked, indulging her chatter. He still felt the aftershocks of the violent agitation he had endured, shaken like a vessel tossed in a storm. This quiet intimacy was the only relief available to him.

"No," Tessa replied, counting on her fingers. "I've counted the days—I started at my right thumb today—since you wore the beautiful chain-coat that Messer San Michele gave you for your journey. And you're wearing it now," she added, peering through the slit in his tunic. "Perhaps that's why you came back sooner."

"Perhaps it did, Tessa," he conceded. "Forget the coat for now. Tell me what has changed since I was last here. Did you see the tents in the Prato? The soldiers and horsemen crossing the bridges? Did you hear the drums and trumpets?"

"Yes," she admitted, her voice dropping. "I was frightened, thinking the soldiers might come up here. Even Monna Lisa was scared; she said they might take our children away, that it was their business to cause mischief. But the Holy Madonna protected us—we never saw one up here. Something has happened, though. I hardly dare tell you, which is why I've been saying extra *Aves*."

"What do you mean, Tessa?" Tito

Tito saw little of Romola for several days. The next morning, he approached her with gentle precision, instructing her to remove any personal effects from the library before the agents arrived to pack the antiquities. Leaning in to press a kiss to her brow, he advised her to retreat to her chamber, where the small painted tabernacle stood, keeping herself distant from the cacophony of unfamiliar footsteps. Romola assented silently, offering no visible emotional response. The preceding night had been a prolonged state of wakefulness; despite her robust constitution, sensation had dulled into a continuous, throbbing ache, as though she had been struck and bruised by invisible forces. Tito perceived her illness but held his tongue, fearing further distress. Observing that her hand and brow were ice-cold, he fetched a fur-lined mantle and draped it lightly over her shoulders. In every brief interval of his return, the tableau remained unchanged: he attempted to soothe her with subtle acts of tenderness, yet she appeared to have lost the faculty of speech or gaze directed toward him.

"Patience," he murmured to himself. "She will recover, and forgive eventually. The bond between us remains the strongest anchor." When a victim of violence recovers slowly, appearing unaffected, the aggressor often slides effortlessly into the role of the aggrieved party, feeling no sting of their own blow while acutely aware of their benevolent conduct. Yet Tito possessed no natural inclination toward grievance; his mind bent constantly toward reconciliation. He would have endured much to feel Romola's hand rest upon his head once more, as it had that morning when he first recoiled from viewing her. However, waiting patiently for the restoration of domestic harmony proved easier because his existence beyond the walls grew increasingly compelling. While a specific impulse may appear as the sole origin of a life trajectory, it is invariably the fruit of a character's gradual evolution. Since the moment in the Piazza del Duomo, when Tito, seated atop bales, derived sharp satisfaction from flattery that captivated crowds, his imagination had fixated on political engagement within Florence's turbulent public sphere. Conversely, the fresh terror instilled by Baldassarre had previously blocked this path, driving him to sell the library as insurance against exile, coinciding with his growing appreciation for the city. That dread had now subsided significantly. Though he must continue to don his armor and prepare for potential demands requiring cool judgment and ingenuity, he felt no compulsion to abandon Florence for new fortunes. His father's refusal of atonement, forcing a stance of defiance, highlighted the frailty of an aged man with failing memory in a foreign land—a weakness Tito was unwilling to exploit. Tito's latent desires were crystallizing into explicit strategy. As the fervor of youthful passion waned, life assumed for him the guise of a game blending skill and chance. The political arena of Florence promised a rapid, exhilarating contest of revolutionary struggle, rich with covert maneuvers where brilliant intellect could discard inconvenient dogmas in favor of pragmatic wisdom, such as the simple truth that ginger is hot in the mouth.

Immediately upon the departure of the French guests, Florence erupted into agitation akin to a colony of ants reacting to the removal of a looming shadow and the subsequent need to repair their encampment. "How shall we fund the French monarch?" "How do we manage the war with the stubborn Pisan rebels?" "Most critically, how do we restructure our governance to ensure magistrates are chosen and laws voted upon effectively?" Until these queries received satisfactory answers, commerce faced stagnation, and the vast segment of the populace excluded from citizenship—who lacked even a vote to numb their hunger—threatened to grow restless. Action was imperative. First, the great bell tolled, summoning citizens to a parliament in the Piazza de' Signori. When the throng packed tightly, hemmed in by armed guards at every exit, the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and the eight current Priors—emerged and took their stand beside the stone lion on the platform before the Old Palace. They proposed granting dictatorial authority for one year to twenty leading men of the city, empowering them to select all magistrates and reorganize the governmental framework. The populace roared its assent, feeling themselves the electors of the Twenty. This parliamentary format was an ancient Florentine tradition designed to mask the will of the few as the choice of the many. The shouting in the piazza ceased quickly, but the debate within the palace intensified. Was Florence to adopt a Great Council modeled after Venice, where all officials and laws were elected by a broad base of qualified citizens regardless of rank or faction? Or would it follow a narrower, less populist scheme preserving the hereditary dominance of noble families against the dilution of shopkeepers' votes? Legal scholars argued day and night until late into the darkness. Messer Pagolantonio Soderini presented impeccable arguments for the popular model; Messer Guidantonio Vespucci countered with equally formidable reasoning for an aristocratic structure. It was a dispute over whether to boil or roast meat, a preference already decided by the disputants' palates; their eloquence might have continued indefinitely without altering the outcome, merely delaying the meal. The majority holding power within the palace sided with Vespucci, advocating for moderation; the majority outside, acutely aware of their powerlessness and grievances, feared change less. Outside the palace, a rising force began to coalesce the vague aspirations of this outer majority into a determined will: the preaching of Savonarola. Driven partly by a spiritual mandate to guide the people and partly by the pragmatic needs of politicians unable to pass measures without his support, he rapidly shifted his daily sermons from general exhortations to specific directives. He moved from urging the postponement of private passions for the public good to prescribing exactly what form of government would achieve that end—transitioning from "Choose whatever is best for all" to "Choose the Great Council," declaring it the divine will. To Savonarola, these were synonymous truths. The Great Council was the only viable mechanism to express a public will sufficient to counteract the corrupting influence of partisan interests, rendering honest, impartial administration at least possible. As Florence's government purified itself, becoming immune to schemes exploiting the moral degradation of others, the Florentine people would evolve into a pure community, destined to lead the renewal of the Church and the world. Fra Girolamo's mind never halted at intermediate steps; the objectives he pursued retained their sublime magnitude. He harbored no private malice, sought no petty gratification. Even in the final terrible days, when ignominy, torture, and the dread thereof exposed every hidden flaw in his soul, he could tell his relentless judges: "Do not wonder if it seems to you that I have told but few things; for my purposes were few and great."

Three weeks elapsed before the library's contents were finally secured and transported. Instead of retreating into silence, Romola remained vigilant, observing the dismantling of her world. The exhaustion born of violent emotional upheaval often breeds a dreamlike denial of reality's causes; thus, as the workmen departed each evening, she descended upon the chaos of straw and wooden crates with a hand-lamp. She paused before every vacant pedestal, every toppled artifact, driven by a bitter compulsion to verify that her love's absence and the world's sterility possessed a tangible, logical foundation. Yet, as the nights wore on, her visits shifted from verification to necessity. This vivification of pain regarding her father's memory became the sole vitality remaining in her affections. By December 23rd, knowing the final shipments were imminent, she sprinted to the upper loggia. She could not afford to miss the visceral sting of witnessing the slow wheels traverse the bridge and vanish.

The sky was overcast, pressing toward dusk. The Arno ran dark and shivering; the hills stood mournful. Florence, encircled by its stone towers, bore the silent, sepulchral aspect of a city viewed from above under unbroken shadow. Santa Croce, where her father lay interred, was swallowed by the gloom. Crawling slowly across the bridge and disappearing up the narrow street was the white cargo, resembling a cruel, deliberate fate intent on consigning her father's lifelong hopes to an unmarked grave. Romola felt less as the observer than as a participant in a shared hallucination: her father, helpless beneath the imprisoning stones, conscious of this removal, unable to reach out to tell her he was not alone.

She remained motionless even after the load vanished, indifferent to the biting cold, soothed only by the gloom that draped her like a mourning vestment, silencing the discordant joy of the city. Suddenly, the great bell in the palace tower shattered the silence with a mighty peal—not the hammer-stroke of alarm, but an agitated clamor of triumph. One by one, every other bell in every other tower seized the vibration, joining the chorus. As the sound swelled until the air itself seemed composed of noise, little flames, vibrating in sympathy, erupted between the palace turrets and the girdling towers.
