That sudden clang, that leaping light, struck Romola like sharp wounds. These were the triumphs of demons celebrating her husband's treachery and the desolation of her existence. Less than three weeks prior, she had been intoxicated by the very sound of these bells; in the gladness of Florence, she had heard a prophecy of her own joy. Now, the universal celebration appeared cruel. She stood aloof from that common life—the Florence flinging its loud exultation to deafen the ears of sorrow and isolation. She could never again clasp hands with gladness, only with those whom the hard nature of joy inevitably forgot. In her bitterness, she perceived all rejoicing as mockery. Men shouted paeans with souls heavy with grief, then scanned their neighbors' faces to confirm whether joy truly existed. Romola had lost her faith in the happiness she once thirsted for; it was a hateful, smiling, soft-handed construct with a narrow, selfish heart.

Covering her ears, she fled down from the loggia, hurrying across the antechamber when she collided unexpectedly with her husband, who had come seeking her. His step was elastic, radiating a satisfaction unusual for him.

"Was the noise too much for you?" he asked, noting how she pressed her hands tighter against her auditory organs. He took her gently by the wrist, drawing her arm within his, guiding her into the salon adorned with dancing nymphs and fauns. "Florence has gone quite mad with acquiring its Great Council, designed to eradicate all evils under the sun, particularly the vice of merriment. You appear stunned, my Romola, and chilled. Do not linger late in that windy loggia without protection. I am summoned abruptly to Rome for learned business concerning Bernardo Rucellai. I depart immediately to join my party at San Gaggio tonight, ensuring an early start tomorrow. I require no trouble from you; my packages are prepared. I shall return shortly."

He anticipated nothing but her quiet endurance. He dared not kiss her brow, merely pressing her hand to his lips before departing. Tito sensed Romola was a more unforgiving woman than he had imagined; her love lacked that sweet, clinging instinct stronger than judgment, which he now recognized as the essence of a wife's charm. Nevertheless, this petrified coldness proved preferable to passionate, futile opposition. Her pride and her ability to discern where resistance was useless held a distinct utility.

Once the door sealed behind Tito, Romola shed the look of cold immobility that froze her whenever he approached. Internally, however, she was far from tranquil endurance. The days following the rupture with Tito had been consumed by active planning and preparation for the fulfillment of her purpose.

Her first command was directed at old Maso.

"Maso," she stated in a decisive tone, "we depart tomorrow morning. We can now overtake the first convoy of cloth while they await us at San Piero. Prepare the two mules tonight; be ready to set off at dawn and meet me at Trespiano."

She intended to accompany Maso as far as Bologna, then dispatch him with letters to her godfather and Tito, declaring her departure permanent. She engineered her exit for absolute secrecy, intending to hide her broken love and life from vulgar scrutiny. Bernardo del Nero was absent at his villa, escaping political suspicion to tend his lands. She had settled his debts without a personal interview. He remained unaware that the library was sold, left to conjecture that some sudden windfall enabled Tito to raise the necessary funds. Maso knew only that her journey was a secret; performing exactly as instructed was the greatest desire of his withered, wintry age.

Romola did not intend to sleep that night. After securing the door, she lit a taper and moved to the carved, painted chest containing her wedding garments. White silk and gold lay within, along with the long white veil and the pearl circlet. A great sob rose in her throat as she beheld them; they resembled the shroud of her dead happiness. Nestled in a tiny gold loop of the circlet was a sugar-plum—a pink hailstone from a shower of sweets. Tito had discovered it first, decreeing it should remain forever. At intervals like this, Romola was swept by a sudden wave of memory back to the era of perfect trust. She felt again the presence of the husband whose love rendered the world as fresh and wondrous to her as to a child sitting in stillness among sunny flowers. She heard his gentle tones, saw his soft, truthful eyes, and breathed that expansive freedom of the soul granted by faith in a being greater than oneself. In those fleeting moments, tears invariably rose: the woman's lovingness felt akin to a bereaved mother sensing tiny fingers warm on her bosom, yet finding them marble to her lips as she bent over the silent bed.

However, the chest held something else besides the wedding attire: a dark, coarse bundle wrapped tightly. Turning her gaze from the white and gold to the dark mass, her tears subsided as her hands touched the serge. That coarse roughness anchored her firmly in the present, stripping away love and delight. Unfastening the thick white cord, she spread the bundle on the table. It was the grey serge habit of a sister of the Third Order of Saint Francis, a *Pinzochera* living in the world yet devoted to piety. Romola resolved to don this garb as a disguise, putting it on immediately so that if she required rest before dawn, she would awaken perfectly prepared. Removing her black garment, she thrust her soft white arms into the harsh sleeves of the serge mantle. Feeling the hard girdle of rope bite her fingers as she tied it, she welcomed those rude sensations. They aligned with her new scorn for pleasure—the dexterous contrivance for selfish ease, the shrinking from endurance when others bowed beneath burdens too heavy for them, a trait that now united her with her husband. Gathering her long hair, she pulled it tight from her face, binding it in a severe knot at the nape of her neck. Taking a square of black silk, she tied it close across her forehead and under her chin, then drew the cowl over it. Lifting the candle to the mirror, she assessed her transformation. To any stranger, the disguise would be complete. To herself, she resembled her brother Dino: the full oval of her cheek required only wasting; her eyes, already sad, needed only to sink further. Did she resemble him in anything else? Only in understanding now how men could be prompted to flee earthly delights forever, dwelling on images of sorrow rather than beauty and joy.

She did not linger before the mirror. Setting aside vanity, she began collecting and packing the relics of her parents too large for her traveling wallet. All were destined for the chest, to be entrusted to her godfather upon her safe departure. First, the portraits; then, item by item, every object bearing a sacred memory found its place in the wallet or chest.

She paused. Something else remained to be stripped away from her, belonging to a past she intended to turn her back on permanently. She placed her thumb and forefinger on her betrothal ring, but they rested there, failing to draw it off. Romola's mind had rushed impetuously toward this act—the severing of a husband who betrayed all trust, the breaking of an outward tie that no longer mirrored the inward bond of love. Yet, the force of external symbols, which knits our active lives into an inexorable identity resistant to wavering consciousness,

The tabernacle unlocked.

Romola was awakened by a sharp rap at the door. The room was already bathed in the cold, hard light of early morning. Maso had arrived for the traveling wallet. The old man started visibly when she opened the door, expecting the graceful silhouette he was accustomed to seeing, only to find instead the thick folds of a grey mantle, the crown of bright hair hidden beneath a dark cowl, and a pale face obscured by shadow.

"It is well, Maso," Romola said, forcing her voice into a calm register to reassure him. "The wallet is ready. Go on quietly; I shall not be far behind. Once you pass the gates, you may slow your pace, for I hope to join you before you reach Trespiano."

She closed the door behind him. Then, her hand went to the key resting in the casket—the original key of the little painted tabernacle. Tito had failed to drown it in the Arno; it had lodged, as such small things often do, in the corner of the embroidered *scarsella* he wore with his purple tunic. Long after their marriage, Romola had discovered it there and stored it away, unused, yet satisfied that it remained within reach.

The cabinet holding the tabernacle had been shifted to the side of the room, adjacent to a window where the pale morning light struck directly upon it, rendering the painted figures discernible to Romola, who knew them intimately. There was the triumphant Bacchus, adorned with grape clusters and clutching a vine-clad spear, embracing the crowned Ariadne; the Loves scattering roses, the wreathed vessel, the cunning-eyed dolphins, and the rippled sea, all enclosed by a flowery border resembling a paradise bower.

On this chill morning, however, Romola viewed these familiar images with renewed bitterness and repulsion. They appeared as a more pitiable mockery than ever, a lying screen rather than a tomb of sorrow. *Foolish Ariadne!* she thought, her mind sharp with disdain. With her gaze of love, as if that bright face, framed by hyacinthine curls like tendrils among the vines, held the deep secret of her life!

"Ariadne is wonderfully transformed," Romola murmured. "She would look strange among the vines and the roses now."

She picked up the mirror and examined her reflection once more. The sight was startling in the harsh morning light, causing her to lower it again with a sensation of shrinking nearly as acute as her earlier recoil from the joyful painting. The recognition of her own face, veiled by the cowl, triggered a fresh dread: the fear that she might eventually be drawn into fellowship with some wretched superstition, joining the company of howling fanatics and weeping nuns whom she had despised since childhood.

With hurried movements, she inserted the key into the tabernacle, opened it, and extracted the crucifix without looking at it. Trembling slightly, she threaded a cord through the small ring, hung the cross around her neck, and concealed it in the bosom of her mantle.

"For Dino's sake," she whispered to herself.

Still, the letters destined for Maso to carry back to Bologna required attention. They were brief. The first addressed Tito: *"Tito, my love for you is dead; and therefore, so far as I was yours, I too am dead. Do not attempt to enforce any laws to fetch me back; that would bring you no happiness. The Romola you married can never return. I need explain nothing further after the words I uttered to you during our last long conversation. If you believed them to be words of transient anger, you will now understand they marked an irreversible change."*

She continued: *"I think you will fulfill my wish regarding my bridal chest, sending it to my godfather, who gave it to me. It contains my wedding clothes and the portraits and other relics of my father and mother."* She folded the ring inside this letter and wrote Tito's name on the outside.

The second letter was for Bernardo del Nero: *"Dearest Godfather, if I could have been of any benefit to your life by remaining, I would not have departed to such a distance. But now I am gone. Do not ask the reason; and if you love my father, strive to prevent anyone from seeking me. I could not endure life in Florence. I cannot bear to tell anyone why. Help to cover my lot in silence. I have requested that my bridal chest be sent to you; when you open it, you will understand the cause. Please entrust all items belonging to my mother to my cousin Brigida, and ask her to forgive me for failing to offer her words of farewell."*

"Farewell, my second father. The finest possession I retain in life remains the memory of your goodness and my gratitude."

"Romola."

She placed the letters alongside the crucifix within the bosom of her mantle. Everything was done. She was prepared to depart.

The house was silent; she descended the stairs and stepped onto the quiet street almost as silently as a grey phantom. Her heart hammered violently, yet she derived a grim pleasure from the solidity of her tread on the broad flagstones—the swift movement felt like a chained resolution finally released. The urgent drive to execute her act and the terror of encountering obstacles pushed aside all sorrow. As she reached the Ponte Rubaconte, she sensed less the proximity of Santa Croce and more the widening yellow streak of morning light cutting through the grey. Unless she increased her speed, she risked meeting faces.

Her direct route led straight to the Borgo Pinti, then along the walls toward the Porta San Gallo, passing through the Piazza di Santa Croce. Yet she walked with steady, rapid purpose through the square, refusing to glance toward the church. The thought that any eyes might fix upon her with curiosity or recognition, prompting indifferent minds to speculate on her private sorrows, caused her to shrink physically as if from imagined torture. She felt degraded even by the act of her husband, from which she suffered helplessly. However, no signs indicated that eyes watched from windows to note this tall grey sister moving with a firm step and a proud, cowled posture.

Her path skirted the early traffic. Upon reaching the Porta San Gallo, she slipped through easily amidst a dispute over tolls for panniers of eggs and market goods entering the city.

Out!

Once past the houses of the Borgo, she was beyond Florence's outer fringe. The sky stretched broad above her; she had entered a new existence—one of loneliness and endurance, yet of absolute freedom. She possessed the strength to sever the bonds she had once accepted in blind faith. Whatever befell her, she would no longer feel the warm breath of soft, hated lips upon her cheek, nor the stifling pressure of an odious mind crushing her spirit. The bare wintry morning and the chilling air were welcome in their severity; the leafless trees and sombre hills were devoid of the gods of beauty and joy whose worship she had forever renounced.

Suddenly, the light burst forth with violent intensity, casting long shadows across the road. It seemed the sun intended to banish the greyness. Light is rarely felt more profoundly as a divine presence stirring the inarticulate sensibilities of our deepest lives than in these moments when it instantly awakens the shadows. A profound awe, inevitable in such a momentous act, became a conscious element in Romola's feelings as she found herself in the sudden presence of the impalpable golden glory and the long shadow of herself—a shadow that could not be escaped.

Hitherto, she had encountered only occasional peasants with mules, and the winding turns of the level road had prevented her from noticing that Maso was not far ahead. But as she passed Pietra and ascended the rising ground, she lifted the hanging roof of her cowl to look eagerly forward. Immediately, she lowered it again.

She had not seen Maso, but two monks approaching within a few yards. The edge of her cowl formed a penthouse over her brow, blocking anything above eye level; for the last few moments, she had gazed only at the brightness of the path and her own shadow, tall and shrouded like a dread spectre. Regret washed over her; she wished she had not looked up. Her disguise made encounters with monks particularly distasteful—they might demand pious passwords unknown to her. She proceeded with a careful appearance of unconsciousness until the black skirts of their mantles had passed her.

The encounter had made her heart beat unpleasantly. Romola harbored an underlying unease in her religious disguise, a shame at this studied concealment, heightened by a special effort to appear oblivious under actual scrutiny. Yet the black skirts would soon disappear as they descended the hill. Spotting a large flat stone against a cypress tree rising from a projecting green bank, she yielded to the impulse born of the slight shock and sat down to rest.

She turned her back on Florence, intending not to look at it until the monks were entirely out of sight. Raising the edge of her cowl once more as she settled, she discerned Maso and the mules at a distance where overtaking them was still feasible, assuming the old man would linger in expectation. For the present, she might pause. She was free and alone.

The summons to the Rucellai banquet arrived with the sudden, decisive crack of a gavel dropped in a courtroom. Tito Melema had accepted the invitation only the previous evening, his spirits buoyed by the promise of delicate meats, choice wines, and the distinguished company of Bernardo Rucellai. To understand the weight of this host, one must look beyond the marble façade of his palace to the source of his dynasty: the *orcella*, a lichen clinging to the rocks of Greek islands and the Canaries. This humble plant, drinking in sunlight until its stems glowed with a reddish-purple hue, yielded the *oricello* dye—a secret that had transformed a merchant's lineage into the wealthy, prestigious Rucellai family centuries prior. Bernardo himself stood as a monument to this legacy, a man whose resume read like a panegyric carved in stone: brother-in-law to Lorenzo de' Medici, Gonfaloniere of Florence, architect of Santa Maria Novella's twin towers, and patron of the Platonic Academy. He possessed a Latin purity that rivaled the ancients and a mind capable of mapping ancient Rome with scholarly precision. Yet, for all his virtues, the invitation carried an undercurrent of political calculation. Conveyed through Lorenzo Tornabuoni, it hinted at factional maneuvering disguised as gastronomy. Tito, aware that Bernardo sat among the Elect Twenty who currently gripped the reins of the Republic, felt the thrill of entering a game where stakes were high and flavors were potent.

Tito descended the Via della Scala toward the Selva, the Rucellai Gardens, his silhouette cut sharply against the encroaching twilight. Only the intermittent flicker of a lamp suspended before a statue of the Virgin illuminated his path, casting long, dancing shadows that betrayed his tall, graceful frame wrapped in a heavy mantle. Behind him, however, moved a discordant shape: a thickset, elderly man in a coarse serge tunic and felt hat, Baldassarre Calvo. Their conjunction appeared accidental to the casual observer navigating the crowded streets, but the synchronicity was absolute. When Tito halted at the garden gate, Baldassarre stopped instantly. A servant, distracted by a minor query, held the *sportello* ajar. Tito stepped through, offering his name with practiced ease, and vanished into the torch-lit grove of evergreens, their leaves shimmering like polished steel.

"Your name?" the servant demanded, eyeing the intruder.

"Baldassarre Calvo," the old man replied, his voice devoid of hesitation.

"You are not a guest; the list is closed."

"I serve Tito Melema, who has just entered. I await him in the gardens."

The servant hesitated, the tension palpable in the cool night air. "Orders forbid entry to non-guests. Are you a retainer of Messer Tito?"

"No, friend. I am a scholar."

There exists a specific frequency of command, a tone of quiet, unshakeable confidence that compels obedience regardless of rank. To the porter, Baldassarre projected exactly this aura. The man yielded without protest, allowing the older scholar to pass. As Baldassarre stepped into the darkness, the door slammed shut and was chained, sealing him within the green labyrinth alongside the man he hunted.

This transformation was total. Just hours prior, as Tito's shadow had passed the hovel where Baldassarre dwelt, the old man had been paralyzed by the aftershocks of violence, his limbs numb, his hands trembling uncontrollably. He had sat amidst the debris of his own shattered dagger, reliving the bitter sequence of events that led to that moment. In those minutes of post-traumatic lucidity, the fog of dementia had lifted, replaced by a crystalline clarity. He had seen the faces of Monna Lisa and Tessa, recognized his role as the duplicitous husband, and felt the crushing weight of his own impotence. But now, standing in the garden, the paralysis had evaporated. The broken dagger lay forgotten; instead, he clutched a large manuscript of Pausanias, the moonlight striking the Greek letters *ΜΕΣΣΕΝΙΚΑ*.

Where the script had previously been meaningless black marks on parchment, it now ignited a firestorm of memory. The moonbeam seemed to conjure the landscape of Messenia, the dust of battle, and the desperate struggle against Spartan oppression. He visualized the stoning of the traitor Aristocrates, the collective fury of a people rejecting injustice, and the pillars erected to mark the restoration of order. Words surged within him, vibrating with the force of resurrected truth. For a fleeting moment, age ceased to exist; he was young, fierce, and alive. He snatched the book, though the pale light rendered further reading impossible. He recited the chapters from memory, his voice rising in an internal shout of triumph. The cold air bit at his skin, yet he felt no chill, only the radiant heat of regained agency.

He paced the high ground, surveying the sleeping city below. The domes and towers of Florence lay dormant beneath the watch of the mountains, the Arno a silver ribbon winding through the valley. In this moment of mental empire, the labyrinthine complexity of the city dissolved into manageable data. He was no longer a confused wanderer but a master strategist, his mind racing through names, alliances, and betrayals with the velocity of a general reviewing a map. Amidst this flood of intellectual conquest, one dark deity remained fixed in the inner sanctum of his mind: Tito. The viper, the favorite of blind fortune, could no longer evade him. Baldassarre's heart, stripped of all tender sentimentality, retained only the sensitive fibers of selfish anguish, which the sharp point of revenge could easily pierce. The failure of the dagger had not been a defeat but a catalyst, shaking off the stagnation that had suffocated his soul.

Yet, a primal fear lingered—the terror that the light would fade, that the fog would return before dawn. Was this recovery permanent, or merely another flash in the storm? The anxiety burned hotter than the anger. He needed to conserve his strength, to ensure the vigil lasted until the perfect moment struck. Seeking refuge, he curled against a heap of warm garden straw and succumbed to sleep.

When consciousness returned, the world was bathed in the harsh, indifferent light of morning. Bewilderment washed over him initially—a confusion of dual identities—but the memories of the night solidified instantly. The recognition of Pausanias, the surge of facts, the panoramic view of the mountain peak—it was all real. He picked up the book, reading and remembering simultaneously, linking deeds to names with effortless precision. He contemplated the nature of crime and punishment: sanctuaries existed for the swift-footed wicked, and justice often broke against the armor of baseness. But Baldassarre knew the ultimate law: no amount of worldly gain or infernal power could withstand the corrosive acid of hatred once awakened. The greatest torture was not pain, but the submission to the smile of the oppressor.

Now, fueled by an indestructible, motive-less force, his sole purpose was vengeance. From that morning until the evening of the banquet, he had tracked Tito with relentless caution, avoiding anyone connected to the favorite to prevent premature alarms. He had bypassed Nello's shop and turned away from Piero di Cosimo, knowing that any slip could drive Tito to flight or cunning evasion. Hunger and thirst mattered little compared to the exquisite satisfaction of a well-placed wound. Possessing a newly purchased poniard, Baldassarre waited. The possibility of frustration fed his desperation, making the anticipated moment feel both distant and imminent. On this particular evening, feeling a renewed, dizzying clarity, he knew his hour had finally arrived.

The heavy doors of the pavilion swung shut, sealing out the chill of the Tuscan night and admitting a sudden, artificial warmth. As Tito stepped across the threshold, his gaze swept the assembly with the speed of a tactical assessment. The composition of the guests confirmed his hypothesis: this was a political gathering, though masked by the veneer of conviviality. In the Renaissance mindset, the consumption of fine food and vintage wine was not merely hedonistic indulgence but a physiological necessity for political clarity. Only after the stomach was satisfied could the mind truly engage with the weight of statecraft; uninvited hunger rendered one incapable of grasping the nuances of factional loyalty.

The Rucellai household was renowned for its opulence, a stark contrast to the sober frugality typical of Florentines. Here, amidst the splendor of a great table, high philosophical treatises were deemed unnecessary distractions. The presence of Plato's bust, imported from Lorenzo's former villa, was entirely appropriate—a silent overseer of a culture that revered intellect alongside excess. On this specific evening, however, the atmosphere suggested a pragmatic shift away from abstract idealism toward the gritty realities of power consolidation.

Bernardo Rucellai, the host, commanded the room with the poised dignity of a man in his mid-forties. His bearing was haughty yet controlled, radiating the confidence of a man who spoke Latin with such purity that even the sharpest German linguists would struggle to extract a single error. He received Tito with unusual warmth, seating him strategically between Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, two formidable figures of the Medicean faction.

While the silver basin of scented water circulated, allowing the company to cleanse their hands, the ambient noise was a symphony of aristocratic leisure. Rings glinted on manicured fingers under the flickering wax candles, mingling with the perfume of French damask roses. The conversation drifted effortlessly through the standard topics of the era: the absurdity of future generations imagining themselves eating with silver forks, the shock ancestors would feel witnessing modern customs, and the technical admiration of Antonio Pollaiuolo's enamelwork on the serving pieces.

"Antonio grew weary of chasing gold," Niccolò Ridolfi remarked, his tone carrying the effortless arrogance of a man accustomed to command. "He declared that an artist who pours his brain into molten metal is throwing his intellect into the furnace."

Giannozzo Pucci seized upon the sentiment. "A prescient warning. If the war with Pisa continues and the rebellion spreads, our silverware will be melted down. Soon, Antonio's silver saints in San Giovanni will vanish from the altars, replaced by coins in the pockets of the desperate."

"The Friar prepares us for this," Lorenzo Tornabuoni added, his voice smooth. "God demands not silver crucifixes but the gold of brotherly love. He argues that holy gems cannot coexist with starving bellies."

"A doctrine of war finance," Bernardo Rucellai countered, his expression dry. "Practical, if harsh. But politics require liquid courage, Lorenzo. Solid meat cannot carry the weight of debate; wine is the solvent we need."

Niccolò Ridolfi expanded the thread, invoking Luigi Pulci. "Pulci argued that opinion corrupts the palate itself. 'Scepticism,' he claimed, 'is the only philosophy that leaves the tongue clean.' Yet, he was a man of sharp edges, capable of cutting anything he touched."

"And yet a gentle soul," Giannozzo Pucci observed. "His words were soap bubbles, fleeting and harmless, yet he possessed a temperance rare in such a volatile age."

The meal progressed with ritualistic precision. After the initial courses, the main event arrived: the roasted capon and game, culminating in the pièce de résistance—the peacock. Cooked according to Apicius, the bird retained its feathers, arranged on the platter to resemble a living creature resting in its plumage. The task fell to the official carver, a skilled operator who had to rotate the classical bird on its back to reveal the plucked breast, offering slices to the honored guests. Those with independent minds might opt for the simpler capon, but few dared challenge the tradition.

Tito, observing the scene, quoted Horace and distributed his portion in small, respectful fragments. Bernardo Rucellai made a scholarly note on the ancient value of peacock eggs but abstained from eating his share. Niccolò Ridolfi balanced a piece on his fork, using the delay to recount a tale of a Sienese man who boiled a wild goose in its own feathers to deceive guests into thinking it was a pheasant.

Little actual peacock was consumed, but the spectacle provided a shared sense of exclusivity. To dine where such capricious luxuries were served was to align oneself with the elite, a status that made the recent teachings of Fra Girolamo—preaching against the luxury of the rich for the sake of the poor—all the more dangerous.

Outside, in the encroaching shadows, Baldassarre paced in tightening circles. He watched the illuminated windows, seeing Tito gesturing with careless elegance, his voice rising above the din. Baldassarre's mind was a wire pulled taut; he was rehearsing his entry into this fortress of privilege, deriving a savage pleasure from Tito's oblivious joy. It was a dance of preparation, setting the stage for a far more brutal confrontation. Inside, the men bathed in candlelight remained unaware of the pale, fierce visage watching them from the dark. Light could be as impenetrable a shield as shadow.

As the conversation shifted from trivialities to urgent civic matters, the influence of Fra Girolamo became undeniable. Even the most neutral attendees felt the pressure of the Friar's rhetoric. Before the stewed fish was cleared, the name of the Dominican friar dominated the discourse, overriding Bernardo Rucellai's earlier attempts to steer clear of politics. With the servants withdrawn, the discussion hardened.

"They are all knotted together," Niccolò Ridolfi asserted. "The Frate possesses a sharper mind for public affairs than either Soderini or the Piagnones. Soderini is merely his mouthpiece."

"No, Niccolò," Bernardo Rucellai corrected firmly. "Fra Girolamo understands utility; he sees what serves his ends. But Pagolantonio Soderini, with his study of the Venetian Council, carries his own burden. Soderini loads the cannon; the Friar provides the gunpowder and strikes the match. The people are becoming masters of us."

Lorenzo Tornabuoni waited until the room was clear of staff before continuing. "Whether Soderini owes him allegiance or not, we owe the Friar the General Amnesty. Without his intervention, the Medici heads would have been cast aside, and our properties confiscated. Fra Girolamo is a rock on the issue of peace."

A ripple of surprise traveled the table, but Bernardo nodded, signaling Tornabuoni to proceed.

"I interviewed him in his cell," Tornabuoni revealed, his voice dropping to a conspiratorial register. "Valori rarely grants such access. I suspect he favored me precisely because I was not a convert, but someone needing conversion. The path forward for the Medici is clear: we must throw our strength behind the Friar's party. Alone, we are too weak to resist. If the Friar and the popular party fall, the Arrabbiati—Nerli, Alberti, the Pazzi—will rise. They will not offer amnesty; they will hunt us like dogs until half of us are exiled."

The consensus was immediate and vocal. Tornabuoni concluded with a strategic maxim: "Dissimulation is the only weapon for the rational in times of violence. While the Friar preaches a utopia of universal good for an island discovered by Columbus, Florence requires a single dominant family interest. The change is coming; with patience, we shall guide it. Until then, we must keep the Friar's flag flying. Any other banner raised now would be black for us."

"Stop."

The command cut through the morning stillness like a blade, arresting Romola's retreat before she had taken ten paces from the cypress tree.

She froze. The wind died in the leaves above her. Every instinct screamed at her to run, to dissolve into the widening gulf of the countryside that separated her from the suffocating walls of Florence, but her feet remained planted. A voice, familiar and vibrating with a terrifying resonance, had claimed her identity before she could even process the threat.

"You are Romola de' Bardi," the voice stated, devoid of malice yet absolute in its verdict. "The wife of Tito Melema."

Romola did not turn. She knew that cadence; she had heard it echo in her private chambers and in the public squares alike. To acknowledge it would be to grant the speaker the power to define her reality. Instead, she sat rigid on the stone, her spine straightening against the chill, her internal state a war zone of awe and furious resistance.

This was one of the black-robed figures of the Order, a man daring to intrude upon her solitary flight. It was a violation of privacy, yes, but it was also something far more dangerous. It felt as though Destiny itself—a capricious, sceptred deity—had descended from the heavens to grasp her with flesh-and-blood fingers, demanding an accounting she was not prepared to render.

"You are fleeing Florence in disguise," the voice continued, stripping away her pretenses. "I hold a direct command from God to halt you. You are not permitted to leave."

Anger, hot and sharp, flared in Romola's chest. She refused to offer the speaker the satisfaction of seeing her defiance. Sitting perfectly still, she challenged him with a voice that shook with suppressed rage.

"What right do you possess to address me? Or to obstruct my path?"

"The right of a messenger," the voice replied, calm and unyielding. "You have donned religious vestments, yet you harbor no religious intent. You sought the habit solely as camouflage. However, you were not allowed to pass undetected. It has been revealed to me who you are: a woman attempting to escape the station God has ordained. You wish to bury your true name and your true station, to craft a new identity governed solely by your own will. I have orders to recall you. My daughter, you must return to your post."

With every syllable, Romola's resolve hardened. She feared that if she showed even a flicker of submission, she would collapse into paralysis. She spoke with increasing irritation.

"I shall not return. I recognize no jurisdiction in priests or monks to dictate my actions. You wield no power over me."

"I know," the voice conceded, sounding weary rather than defeated. "I know you were raised in contempt for obedience. But it is not this poor monk who seeks to bind you; it is Truth itself that commands you. And Truth cannot be evaded. Either you submit, and it becomes your guide, or you resist, and it becomes a chain you will drag through eternity. But you *will* obey, my daughter. Your former servant returns with the mules shortly; my companion has gone ahead to retrieve him. You are returning to Florence."

Romola surged upward, her eyes blazing with defiance. She faced the speaker directly. It was Fra Girolamo. She recognized him instantly. Their heights were nearly equal, their faces aligned in a confrontation of equals. She had risen to shout words of rejection, but they died in her throat. Fra Girolamo's gaze was not angry; it was unnervingly calm. The impact of that look was alien to her, washing away her anger with a shame that felt entirely irrelevant to her struggle.

There was no ethereal beauty in Savonarola's face. It was rugged, defined by severe features honed by mental rigor and physical discipline. Yet, the reason Romola faltered was not aesthetic. It was the profound sense of genuine interest and care radiating from his stare—a connection based on shared humanity rather than personal affection. It was the first time she had encountered a gaze that expressed simple fellowship as a binding force. Such a look constituted half the vocation of a spiritual leader, and suddenly, questioning his authority felt impossible. She stood silent, staring into his eyes, while he spoke again.

"You claim your freedom proudly, my daughter. But who is so degraded as the debtor who believes himself free?"

A sting struck Romola, and her expression shifted as if a pale lightning bolt had flashed across her features.

"And you are fleeing your debts: the debt of a Florentine woman; the debt of a wife. You are turning your back on the lot assigned to you to forge a different path. But can a man or woman choose their duties? No more than they can select their birthplace or their parents. My daughter, you are running from the presence of God into the wilderness."

As the heat of anger drained from her mind, a strange presentiment took its place. Perhaps there was strength in submission, provided this man possessed a valid law to enforce. But no—he could not possibly understand the forces driving her. Yet, she could not simply refuse guidance anymore. Constrained by a desperate need to remain respectful while resisting, the title she had never previously used rose to her lips without hesitation.

"My father," she whispered, the words foreign yet inevitable. "You cannot comprehend the reasons compelling me to go. Only I can know them. No one else can judge for me. I have been driven by great sorrow. I am resolved to proceed."

"I know enough, my daughter," Savonarola replied, his tone illuminating a truth she had tried to obscure. "My mind has been clarified regarding you. You are unhappy in your marriage; however, I am not a confessor, nor do I seek to pry into matters sealed by the sacrament. I possess a divine warrant to stop you, independent of such secrets. You were warned by a message from heaven, delivered in my presence—warned *before* your marriage, when you could still lawfully choose liberty. But you chose the bond. By deliberately breaking it—and I speak to you as a pagan if the sanctity of matrimony holds no sway for you—you are violating a pledge. Of what grievances will you complain, my daughter, when you yourself commit one of the gravest wrongs a woman and citizen can perpetrate? Withdrawing in secret and disguise from a vow made before God and your fellow men? Of what injustices will you lament when you are shattering the most fundamental law underpinning human trust: faithfulness to the spoken word? Is this the wisdom you have acquired by scorning the Church's mysteries? To fail to perceive the bare duty of integrity, where the Church sees not only integrity but religion?"

Blood rushed to Romola's face, and she recoiled as if struck. "I would not have assumed a disguise," she began, but her voice failed her. She was too shaken by the implication in the friar's words—that her actions mirrored Tito's—to continue.

"And to sever that pledge, you flee Florence: Florence, the only city in the world where you owe the debt of a fellow citizen."

"I should never have left Florence," Romola murmured, her voice trembling, "as long as there was any hope of fulfilling a duty to my father there."

"Do you acknowledge no tie save that of a child to her father in the flesh?" Savonarola countered, his voice rising with intensity. "Your life has been lived in blindness, my daughter. You have dwelled with those perched on a high hill, looking down upon the lives of their neighbors with disdain. I know their hollow discourse. They fill their days with fancied wisdom about the past while scorning God's work in the present. Doubtless you were taught that pagan women once understood living for the Republic, yet you have never felt that you, a Florentine woman, should live *for* Florence. If your own people wear a yoke, will you slip from under it rather than struggle alongside them to lighten it? There is hunger and misery in our streets, yet you declare, 'I care not; I have my own sorrows; I will depart, perhaps to ease them.' The servants of God strive for a law of justice, peace, and charity so that the hundred thousand citizens among whom you were born may be ruled rightly; yet you regard this as trivial, like a bird spreading its wings to fly wherever it pleases in search of food. And yet you have scorned the teaching of the Church, my daughter. As if you, a wilful wanderer following your own blind choices, were inferior to the humblest Florentine woman who stretches out her hands with her own kin, begging blessings for them; who feels a sisterhood with the neighbor kneeling beside her, regardless of bloodline; who contemplates the mighty purpose God has for Florence; and who waits and endures because the promised work is vast, and she feels herself small."

"I was not departing for ease or indulgence," Romola insisted, lifting her head with a desperate urge to defend herself. "I was leaving for hardship. I expect no joy; it has departed from my life."

"You are pursuing your own will, my daughter. You seek some good other than the law you are bound to obey. But how will you find good? It is not a matter of choice: it is a river flowing from the foot of the Invisible Throne, moving along the path of obedience. I say again, man cannot choose his duties. You may choose to abandon your duties and avoid the sorrow they bring. But you will go forth; and what will you discover, my daughter? Sorrow without duty—bitter herbs, and no bread to accompany

"Rise, my daughter," Fra Girolamo commanded finally, his voice cutting through the heavy silence. "Your servant waits with the mules not far off. It is time I press onward toward Florence."

Romola rose from her knees. That posture of absolute stillness had functioned as a sacrament, sealing her entry into a state of yearning passivity. By the single act of renouncing her resolve to flee her husband, her will had been shattered so thoroughly that she required direction even for trivialities. She lifted the edge of her cowl, revealing her face, and spotted Maso and the second Dominican standing with their backs to her on the hill's precipice, roughly ten yards distant. Yet she fixed her gaze upon Savonarola once more, remaining silent, as if the command to recall Maso must originate solely from him, not from her own diminished agency.

"I shall go and summon them," he answered her look of quiet desperation. "And I commend you, my daughter, to the Brother accompanying me. You seek to submit yourself to guidance and acquire that wisdom which has previously appeared as folly to you. A primary gateway to this wisdom is the sacrament of confession. You require a confessor, my daughter, and I intend to place you under the care of Fra Salvestro, one of the brethren of San Marco, in whom I place my utmost confidence."

"I would prefer no guidance save your own, Father," Romola replied, anxiety tightening her features.

"My daughter, I do not act as your confessor. My vocation withdraws me from offices necessitating frequent contact with the laity, interfering with my specific duties."

"Then shall I not be permitted to speak to you privately? Should I falter, should—" Romola broke off, overcome by a sudden surge of agitation. A primal alarm seized her: the fear that the nascent strength born of her renunciation would evaporate the moment Savonarola's direct personal influence receded.

"My daughter, if your soul requires the spoken word from my lips in private, inform me through Fra Salvestro, and I shall meet you in the sacristy or the choir of San Marco. I shall not cease to watch over you. I will instruct my brother regarding you, directing him to lead you onto the path of labor for the suffering and the hungry—a calling to which you are destined as a daughter of Florence in these times of dire necessity. I wish to behold you among the weaker and less learned sisters as the apple tree stands among the forest trees, so that your beauty and all your natural gifts serve merely as a lamp through which the Divine light shines with greater purity. I shall now depart to summon your servant."

Once Maso had been dispatched ahead, Fra Salvestro stepped forward, and Savonarola guided Romola toward him. She had anticipated an internal recoil from a new guide entirely alien to her; however, to resist Savonarola's counsel at this juncture would have been to project an attitude of independence precisely when every ounce of her strength depended on the renunciation of such autonomy. Her entire mental orientation was now directed toward performing what was painful rather than what was easy. She bowed reverently to Fra Salvestro before meeting his gaze directly; yet when she raised her head and beheld him fully, her hesitation crystallized into a palpable doubt.

There exist men whose mere presence instills trust and reverence; then there are those to whom we must transport our trust and reverence, forged and ready-made. This distinction struck Romola as the connection to Savonarola severed and Fra Salvestro Maruffi filled the void. It was not that Fra Salvestro possessed anything manifestly repulsive in his visage or demeanor—no hint of hypocrisy, no tinge of coarseness. His face was actually more handsome than Fra Girolamo's, his stature slightly taller. He served as the long-standing confessor to many of Florence's leading figures, possessing extensive experience as a spiritual director. Yet his countenance bore the vacillating expression of a mind incapable of concentrating intensely within the channel of a single great emotion or belief—an expression fatal to influencing an ardent nature like Romola's.

Such an expression is not necessarily the mark of insincerity; rather, it stamps a shallow soul. One often finds such individuals sincerely striving to fulfill a lofty vocation, earnestly composing their faces to utter sublime formulas, only to find their muscles twitch or relax in defiance of their beliefs, much as prose intrudes where poetry belongs in the mind lacking divine frenzy. Fra Salvestro suffered a peculiar susceptibility to visions, seemingly dependent on a constitution prone to somnambulism. Savonarola accepted the supernatural character of these visions, whereas Fra Salvestro had initially resisted such interpretations, even rebuking Savonarola for his prophetic preaching. Another proof, if evidence were needed, that the relative magnitude of men cannot be gauged by their propensity to dismiss the superstitions of their age. Between these two, there could be no ambiguity regarding which was the great man and which the small. The disparity was measured accurately by the shift in Romola's feelings as Fra Salvestro began addressing her with words of exhortation and encouragement.

After her initial angry resistance to Savonarola had subsided, she had forgotten the ancient dread that any influence might drag her into the circle of fanaticism and sour, monkish piety. But now, once again, the chilling breath of that terror stole over her. It held no decisive power against the momentum her mind had just acquired; it was akin to the gathering of grey clouds over a sunrise, rendering her return path monotonous and sombre. Perhaps of all sombre journeys, the one undertaken after traversing a path with strong resolution is the one that most severely tests the fervor of renunciation.

As they re-entered the city gates, light snowflakes descended around them. As the grey-clad sister hastened homeward from the Piazza di San Marco, crossing the bridge again and turning into the grand doorway of the Via de' Bardi, her footsteps left dark impressions on the thin carpet of snow, and her cowl fell laden and damp across her face. She ascended to her chamber, cast aside her serge habit, destroyed the parting letters, restored all her precious trifles to their places, loosened her hair, and donned her customary black dress. Instead of undertaking a long, exhilarating journey, she was to sit in her accustomed spot. Snow battered against the windows, and she was alone. She felt the dreariness, yet her courage remained high, reminiscent of a seeker who has discovered new signs of gold. She intended to navigate life by a fresh clue. She had poured the entirety of her will's energy into renunciation. The empty tabernacle remained locked, and she placed Dino's crucifix outside it. Nothing disrupted the outward monotony of her solitary existence until night arrived like a white ghost at the windows. Nevertheless, for Romola, this Christmas Eve of 1494 proved to be the most memorable of her life.

October 30th, 1496. The sky offered a deceptive clarity, the air crisp with an autumnal bite that the Florentines ignored. Their attention was fixed elsewhere, on the gales battering the Ligurian coast, a natural phenomenon seemingly conspiring to dismantle Fra' Girolamo's theological assurances that Providence watched over Florence.

Those storms had repelled vessels from Marseille laden with grain and mercenaries. The city faced a dual crisis: starvation and a vacuum of manpower. Famine stalked the streets, and the borders were compromised. The French King, the "new Charlemagne," had withdrawn after his effortless conquest of Naples, reportedly paralyzed by the grief of a lost heir, leaving the region vulnerable. In response, a Holy League had coalesced under Pope Borgia to expel the French, a maneuver that smelled less of liberation and more of wolfish politics—an agreement among predators to eliminate rivals before dividing the carcass. Florence, refusing to join this coalition and clinging to the French alliance, had chosen isolation at a perilous cost.

Now, Pisa, bolstered by Venetian, Milanese, and Imperial forces, besieged Leghorn. If that maritime artery severed, Florence, hemmed in by papal animosity and regional envy, would suffocate. The Signoria responded with vigor, funding armies and fortifications, yet also adhering to the archaic Italian tactic of conciliation. Against precedent, they opened their gates to the starving peasantry and displaced mendicants fleeing other cities.

This humanitarian stance drew sharp criticism from the disaffected. They argued Florence should align with the League to avoid hostility, and that feeding strangers while citizens starved was madness. Yet, the distress intensified daily. Even as Fra' Girolamo remained silent in obedience to Rome, the arrival of news regarding the failed grain shipment forced the Signoria's hand. Two days prior, Savonarola had returned to the Duomo, delivering a sermon of such audacity that he wagered his habit on the city's salvation. He demanded only that Florence fulfill its civic and religious duties; if God did not intervene, he would strip his robes.

By this morning, however, no divine intervention was visible. Hope rested solely on the *Tabernacolo* of the Madonna dell'Impruneta. For generations, this sacred image had delivered Florence from plague, flood, and war. Its patronage, the Buondelmonti family, guarded it with swords; wealth flowed to its shrines. The populace believed the Madonna would bend to their will. Yesterday evening, the tabernacle had departed L'Impruneta, escorted by high dignitaries, destined for a solemn procession through the city gates.

But as dawn broke, the tabernacle remained outside. Pestilence trailed the famine. Hospitals overflowed; private courtyards served as triage centers, yet the streets remained littered with the unsheltered. Early that morning, the confraternities began their grim rounds, carrying the bodies of the dead. Among them moved Romola de' Bardi. Dressed in simple black serge, her head veiled to conceal her hair save for the golden bands framing her brow, she advanced from the Ponte Vecchio toward the Porta Santa Maria.

Her path was blocked by a bier borne by the Company of San Jacopo del Popolo. The brothers paused to inspect a corpse, surrounded by a cluster of idlers whose faces were hollowed by hunger.

"He's taken!" one shouted. "Messer Domeneddio loves him enough to claim him."

"Better stretched out than standing upright with a belly like stone," another muttered. "Death gets a poor bargain for an old fellow."

"No Florentine, ten to one," a third observed. "A beggar expelled from Siena. May San Giovanni protect us from sending soldiers against an army of starving men."

"This one is different," someone corrected. "From the Stinche. Look at the grey patch where the prison mark was."

"Hush! Help them lift him!"

"It's possible he's alive," a brother noted, lifting the body onto the stretcher. "Just collapsed from lack of food."

Romola stepped forward. She loosened a small flask at her belt and, with practiced efficiency, used an ivory implement to pour a few drops of wine into the man's mouth. The liquid worked; the head twitched, eyes fluttering open with a vacant, returning awareness.

Recognition struck Romola instantly. The wild, dark eyes, the sallow, lined face, the long white beard—it was Baldassarre, the escaped prisoner she had glimpsed in the Duomo when Tito first donned armor, the man whose mere presence had terrified the boy. A tremor seized her. Was this the moment she learned the bitter secrets of her husband's past? She felt the urge to flee, yet a stronger compulsion anchored her beside the dying man. Her hands trembled, but muscle memory guided them; her right hand hovered to offer more wine, her left supporting his neck.

Baldassarre looked at her for the first time. Weeks of seclusion since her flight and his arrest had erased the vivid descriptions of the golden-haired wife circulating in the city. All he saw now was the woman before him.

"Shall we carry him to the steps of San Stefano?" Romola asked. "We'll clear the street, and you may proceed."

They moved thirty yards until the church doorway. By then, Baldassarre managed to prop himself against the stone. The confratelli departed, but the crowd of onlookers swelled. The atmosphere shifted; knowing the man was alive bred unease, though Romola's presence imposed a somber respect.

"They fed him daily in the Stinche," a voice grumbled. "No wonder he crumbles without it."

"The Magnificent Eight released prisoners to make room for honest folk," another retorted. "If thieves get wine and bread, we Ciompi might as well drink from the Arno."

Romola sat beside Baldassarre. "Can you eat a crust? I've promised to visit the hospital, but I will return if you wait. I will take you to shelter. Do you understand?"

He stared blankly, repeating the word "back." She opened her basket, filled with soft bread, and placed a piece in his hand.

"Do you hoard bread for those who cannot swallow?" a rough voice cut in. A man in a red nightcap shoved through the crowd.

"If anyone isn't hungry," another interjected, "leave him be. He's luckier than those with gnawing stomachs and no meal."

"Yes, if a man intends to die, don't revive him against his will. Dead men require no trencher."

"Oh, you misunderstand the Friar's charity," a youth in fine cloth countered, his face untouched by want. "The Friar preaches to birds, telling hawks to feed sparrows. Every good citizen feeds six starving beggars from Arezzo or Bologna. Madonna, you're a pious Piagnone; don't waste your bread on honest folks who have the Friar's prophecies to digest."

"Come, Madonna," the red-capped man urged. "The old thief won't eat. Try us. We fast so much we're half-saints."

The circle tightened, the gaunt men crowding Romola until there was no space to breathe. She held out a horn cup, moistening a piece of bread with wine, ignoring their advances. Then she rose, scanning the faces. Instinctively, the nearest men recoiled, as if their proximity was an insult.

Romola extended the basket to the man in the nightcap. Her gaze was direct, devoid of reproach.

"Hunger is a cruel master," she said. "You hold the power to take this bread. It was reserved for the sick and the weak. You are strong; if you choose not to suffer, you can take everything from the powerless. Take the bread from this basket, but know this: I stand guard over this old man. I will resist you taking his sustenance."

Silence fell, heavy and absolute. Romola's pale face bore the deepening sockets of habitual fasting, her hazel eyes burning with intensity. The man in the nightcap looked foolish, backing away and jostling his neighbor with a gesture of moral superiority. The retreat was universal; everyone claimed to have been pushed forward unwillingly. The youth in the fine tunic vanished.

Just then, the armed servitors of the Signoria arrived to clear the obstruction for the approaching procession. The man identified as Cecco retreated up the church steps, bowing respectfully to Romola.

"Madonna, if you must continue your errands, I shall tend to the old man."

Returning from the hospital, Romola altered her course, cutting a wider arc toward the river rather than taking the direct path. She intended to reach the Ponte Vecchio and press onward to San Stefano to find Baldassarre. Dread coiled in her stomach at the prospect of confronting him, yet a stubborn instinct warned her that abandoning him entirely would feel like severing a vital tether to her own existence.

She reached the junction where the Por' Santa Maria curved to her right and the Ponte Vecchio lay to her left. Just as she prepared to cross, the street erupted into sudden, collective prostration. Without hesitation, Romola sank to her knees alongside the throng. The Great Cross of the Duomo, leading the procession, thundered past. Having arrived late, she was forced to remain prone until the massive timber had cleared the intersection.

When she finally rose, the transition from kneeling to standing exposed the full weight of her exhaustion, far more acute than during her walk. Beside her, a shopkeeper stepped forward. "Madonna Romola," he said, his voice thick with deference, "you must be weary. Gian Fantoni offers you a seat in his house; the door is right there. Let me open it. He loves God and our Frate as we do. His home is yours."

Romola had grown accustomed to such fraternal addresses from citizens whose faces she recognized daily within the Duomo. Over the last two years, the concept of 'home' had shifted for her. It was no longer the lonely isolation of the Via de' Bardi, but the entire circumscribed geography of Florence itself—a network of streets where every turn yielded a look of appeal or friendly greeting. Accepting the offer, she passed through the open doorway, guided by the hose-vendor to an upstairs window. There, a stout woman, dressed in the plain garb of the Piagnoni and accompanied by three children, arranged a space for her with profound reverence above bright hanging draperies.

From this vantage point, Romola observed the procession moving in solemn slowness between the lines of houses on the Ponte Vecchio, with the river and the Lung' Arno stretching toward the bridge of Santa Trinita. Silence reigned; not even a wailing chant broke the atmosphere, save for the rhythmic tramp of footsteps and the faint sweep of woollen robes.

First came a long train of Florentine youth, their faces fresh and beardless, bearing high in the midst the white image of the youthful Jesus, crowned with golden glory, standing beside the tall cross where thorns and nails awaited. Following them were the mysterious Companies of Discipline, bound by secret vows of self-chastisement and devout praise. Their garbs completely obscured heads and faces, leaving only the eyes visible. These were citizens of all ranks—merchants, accountants, statesmen—who had shed their individual identities to walk as symbols of a common vow. Each company bore its color and badge, yet the uniform shroud erased all distinction, leaving only the expression of fellowship.

In contrast, the multitude of monks appeared as distinct individuals despite their shared tonsures and frocks. First flowed a white stream of reformed Benedictines, followed by the much longer column of the Frati Minori, or Franciscans. Clad in grey with knotted cords and often wooden *zoccoli* on bare feet, they were arguably the most numerous order in Florence, known for their zeal and hatred of the Dominicans. After the grey came the black of the Augustinians of San Spirito, men who had inherited Boccaccio's library and represented the height of learning in a time when knowledge was rare. Next emerged the white-over-dark habit of the Carmelites, followed by the unmixed black of the Servites, an order founded by seven merchants who renounced wealth to adore the Divine Mother.

Now, the hearts of the onlookers quickened, beating with a mixture of hatred and love. A stream of black mantles over white scapularies crossed the bridge—the Dominicans. Those from Fiesole led the way. Black mantle after black mantle, tonsured head following tonsured head, expectation hung suspended. The mantles were coarse, many threadbare or ragged, evidence of the Prior of San Marco's reduction of the fraternities to strictest poverty. Yet, amidst the worn fabric, one mantle stood slightly less degraded, topped by a tonsured head that would seem unremarkable to a stranger unless they recalled seeing it on bronze medals bearing the sword of God, or surrounded by armed guards en route to the Duomo, or transfigured by the inward flame of an orator addressing a rapt multitude.

As Savonarola's approach became discernible, no one dared break the stillness with a sound rising above the solemn tramp. Nevertheless, a mingled hiss of curses and murmurs of blessing drifted through the air. Perhaps sensing the dominance of the hissing, two or three disciples in the foreground fell to their knees as if divinity itself were passing. This movement of silent homage spread like a subtle shockwave along the street sides, bending some to the knee while leaving others unmoved. Simultaneously, hatred gathered intensity; Romola saw someone at an upper window spit directly upon the passing friar.

Monks returned—the Frati Umiliati, or Humbled Brethren from Ognissanti, heirs to a glorious tradition in the wool trade—and then Vallombrosan and other varieties of Benedictines, their specific forms and colors marking a spiritual shift from earlier abuses. Finally, the shaven crowns ended, giving way to the train of untonsured secular priests.

Then came the twenty-one incorporated Arts of Florence in long array, their banners floating proudly to declare their distinct functions, from bakers to judges and notaries. Followed by the secondary officers of State, ascending from lesser to greater rank, until the line of seculars was broken by the Canons of the Duomo. They carried a sacred relic: the very head of San Zenobio, immortal bishop of Florence, enclosed in silver, believed to have saved the city a millennium prior. This was the nucleus of the procession.

Behind the relic marched the Archbishop in a gorgeous cope, with a canopy held aloft. Following him came the mysterious, hidden Image. First concealed by rich brocade curtains enclosing an outer painted tabernacle, and within that, by an ancient inner tabernacle never opened in living memory. Inside rested the image of the Pitying Mother, discovered ages ago in the soil of L'Impruneta, crying out as the spade struck it. Historically, this unseen Image rarely entered the Duomo without a procession of rich gifts—veils, curtains, mantles. The most prized offering was said to be a mantle of gold brocade woven by a poor abbess and her nuns using only their prayers, embroidered and adorned similarly, then presented in the great Piazza by two youths who spread white wings and vanished into the blue sky.

Today, however, no gifts preceded the tabernacle. No donations were to be made except to the poor. This was the counsel of Fra Girolamo, whose preaching demanded aid for visible needs rather than tribute to invisible powers. Altars had been erected at various church entrances for these oblations. Not even a torch was carried. Surely the hidden Mother cared less for fire and silk than for the wails of the starving populace.

Florence was in extremity; she had done her utmost and could only await a divine intervention beyond her control. The Frate in the torn mantle had promised help would surely come, causing many faint-hearted souls to cling to his word more than to the virtues of the unseen Image. Conversely, the fierce-hearted rejoiced secretly that the Friar's promise might prove false.

Slowly, the tabernacle advanced, and knees bent once more. Profound stillness descended, undisturbed by the train of priests and chaplains from L'Impruneta. The procession neared its conclusion with the Priors and the Gonfaloniere. The long train of symbolic companies, stirring the mind like a chorus, faded from view. A faint, yearning hope struggled against the accustomed despondency.

Romola, whose heart had swelled with a mix of foreboding and enthusiastic fellowship—a feeling habitual to her since the last two years—gave a deep sigh, as if releasing a long-held tension. She remained on her knees, overcome by languor. Suddenly, something brightly colored flashed between the houses onto the distant bridge.

Instantly, Romola sprang upright, stretching her arms and leaning from the window. Her black drapery fell away, revealing the golden gleam of her hair and the flush of her face, illuminated as if by a single burst of light. A shout erupted simultaneously. The last troops of the procession halted, all faces turning toward the bridge. But the bridge was already passed. A horseman pressed along the Arno at full gallop. The sides of his bay horse, streaked with foam, appeared white from the speed; his cap flew loose from his red *becchetto*, and he waved an olive branch in his hand.

It was a messenger—a bearer of good tidings! The olive branch spoke from afar. Yet the impatient crowd could not wait. They surged forward, seizing the horse's reins, pushing and trampling. Romola now saw clearly that the rider was her husband, Tito, sent to Pisa a few days prior on a private embassy. Recognition brought no new flash of joy to her eyes. She had suppressed her initial impulsive expectation, but her governing anxiety remained fixed on whether news of relief had arrived for Florence.

"Good news!" "Best news!" "News to be paid with hose!" (*nov

The crowd surged past, dissolving into the throng, leaving Romola to descend swiftly onto the street. She hurried toward the steps of San Stefano, her movements driven by a desperate need for order amidst the chaos. There, in the shadow of the church portal, stood Baldassarre. Cecco had been swept up with the general flow, but Baldassarre remained solitary, the silver horn-cup clutched firmly in his hand.

A profound transformation marked his visage. The vacant, dreamlike stare of a mind returning from the abyss had vanished, replaced by a ferocity that struck Romola with the intensity of lightning. As she approached and addressed him, the gaze locked onto her—a look of caged fury fixated on its potential prey, seeing her pass safely beyond the invisible bars of his rage. Romola flinched instinctively at the intensity, yet her mind raced instantly to a singular conclusion: he had seen Tito. The hatred radiating from him grated against her nerves, yet it sparked a flickering ember of hope. Perhaps this man *was* the criminal; perhaps Tito had been innocent of the charge that haunted her. If she could force a confrontation, if she could drive Tito face-to-face with this accuser, she might finally find peace.

"If you will come with me," she said, her voice steady despite the turmoil, "I can offer you shelter and sustenance until you regain your strength. Will you come?"

"Yes," Baldassarre replied, his voice rough. "I shall be glad to get my strength." He repeated the phrase, muttering it as if rehearsing a mantra for himself rather than addressing her directly.

"Come!" she commanded, stepping beside him. She guided him away from the main thoroughfare, choosing the route along the Arno toward the Ponte Rubaconte, a quieter passage suited to their current state.

"I suspect you are not a Florentine," she remarked as they crossed onto the bridge.

He scanned the surroundings, silent and wary. The fog of confusion and physical debilitation had thickened around him, clouding his judgment. Yet, Romola watched him intently, her gentle eyes holding a quiet authority that eventually compelled a response.

"No," he answered cautiously. "I am no Florentine. I am a lonely man."

She noted his reticence, fearing that further questioning might trigger a desire to flee. Glancing at him periodically, her mind worked frantically, extinguishing the faint hope that her husband was blameless. If this stranger harbored grievances, where was the source of such dread and secrecy? They walked in silence until they reached the entrance of the Via de' Bardi. Suddenly, Baldassarre halted, turning his head with a jerky motion as if an electric shock had coursed through him. Moments later, Romola stopped at the threshold of the courtyard, facing him.

"Ah!" he exclaimed, cutting her off before she could speak. "You are his wife."

"Whose wife?" Romola asked, her voice trembling slightly.

The name died in Baldassarre's throat. The overwhelming image of Tito seemed to paralyze his tongue, expelling any verbal sign. He offered no reply, staring at her with a fixed, unnerving intensity.

Romola pushed the door wide, revealing a courtyard strewn with straw. Upon it lay four or five sick individuals, while tiny, pale children crawled or sat nearby, gnawing on straws and emitting soft, gurgling sounds.

"If you will come in," Romola pleaded, her voice breaking, "I will find you a comfortable spot and bring you more food."

"No," Baldassarre refused flatly. "I will not come in."

Yet he remained frozen, paralyzed by the crushing weight of conflicting impressions, unable to chart a course through the storm of his mind.

"Is there nothing I can do for you?" Romola insisted. "Let me give you coin so you may purchase food. Supplies will be more abundant shortly."

She reached into her *scarsella*, withdrawing her palm to reveal several *grossi*. Deliberately, she offered more than she would have to any other beggar in his condition.

Baldassarre stared at the coins for a long moment, then nodded. "Yes, I will take them."

She poured the coins into his hand; he gripped them tightly, as if anchoring himself to the earth.

"Tell me," Romola urged, her tone nearly supplicating. "What shall you—"

Baldassarre turned away abruptly, resuming his march toward the bridge. Crossing it, he proceeded straight up the Via del Fosso, stopping abruptly before the shop of Niccolò Caparra. Without hesitation, he stepped inside, as though the weapon rack had been his sole destination.

Niccolò was absent, engaged elsewhere in the armorers' procession, leaving only a single apprentice behind. The shop bristled with weaponry, and Baldassarre's eyes zeroed in on what he craved far more than bread. Though Niccolò would surely refuse to sell arms to a man bearing the marks of imprisonment, the apprentice—unobservant and lacking scruple—sold a sharp hunting-knife for three *grossi* without a second thought.

It was a compact blade, easily concealed within the folds of Baldassarre's tunic. Clutching it, he felt a surge of renewed vigor. That sharp edge could lend lethality to the thrust of an aging arm; at the very least, it was a companion, a power allied with his cause. It might shatter against plate mail, but was the armor guaranteed to remain forever? During those long months of incarceration, baseness had perhaps grown complacent and secure. This knife had been purchased with the traitor's own money—that was justice served.

Before accepting the funds, he had known exactly what he intended: to buy a weapon. Yes, and ideally, food as well. Food to fuel the arm that would wield the steel, food to sustain the body that served as the temple of vengeance. Once he had eaten enough, he would possess the clarity to think and act—to devise a method of concealment, ensuring Tito did not drag him back into custody once more.

With this plan forming in his mind, Baldassarre navigated the narrowest alleys, purchasing meat and bread. He settled beneath the first available loggia to eat. The bells began to toll, their joyful peals swelling louder and louder, vibrating through the air and seemingly against him. To Baldassarre, they represented nothing but the roar of a hostile world arrayed against his quest.

Romola watched him until he vanished around the corner into the Piazza de' Mozzi. Half relieved by his departure, half chastised for failing to pursue the truth with greater resolve, she wrestled with her emotions. Could she have done otherwise if the truth involved a painful secret regarding her husband? Was a wife not permitted to desire ignorance of her spouse's transgressions, especially when she alone bore the duty of remaining silent and non-confrontational? These thoughts tangled too deeply within the intricate fibers of her grief to be untangled now, in her exhaustion.

It was a time for rejoicing, for aid had arrived in Florence. Turning back into the courtyard, she prepared to share the news with her patients lying on their straw beds. Closing the door to muffle the clamor of the bells, she threw back the black drapery covering her head so the women might see her clearly. Standing amidst them, she announced that grain was arriving and that the bells rang in jubilation.

The sick propped themselves up to listen. Children trotted and crawled toward her, tugging at her black skirts with impatient eagerness to be close to her face. Weary as she was, Romola yielded to them, sitting down on the straw. The pale faces peered into her basket, pulling at her hair, while the feeble voices around her chorused, "The Holy Virgin be praised!"

"It was the procession!" someone cried. "The Mother of God has shown pity to us!"

Finally, rising from the heap of straw, too drained to muster a smile, she turned up the stone steps. "I will return by-and-by to bring you your dinner."

"Bless you, Madonna! Bless you!" the faint chorus responded, echoing the praise they had offered moments earlier to the unseen divine presence.

Romola cherished that music profoundly. Unlike some women who found comfort in the mere mechanics of nursing the sick and clothing the destitute, her early training had kept her aloof from such domestic labors. Had she not infused them with the inspiration of her deepest convictions, they would have been tedious burdens. Yet, they had become the only unshakable anchor for her mind, the narrow path where light pierced through the darkness.

Even as the chasm between herself and Tito widened, fueled by her futile attempts to bridge it through submission, doubts gnawed at her. Did the bond she strove to honor prove false? When she withdrew from her confessor, Fra Salvestro, or from the circle of Savonarola's disciples, she often felt a sickness at their narrow-mindedness, reacting with an impulsive resurgence of her old contempt for their superstitions. Yet, in her acts of compassion toward her fellow citizens, she found a solid footing. Whatever else cast doubt, her service to others confirmed Fra Girolamo's wisdom in calling her back.

According to his unforgettable words, her place had not been empty; it had been filled with her love and her labor. Florence needed her. The heavier her personal sorrow pressed down, the greater her gladness in recalling those two long years spent lightening the burdens of others. All the ardor of her nature, no longer expendable on the

"Welcome bearer of the olive branch," the crowd murmured, a tide of eager faces pressing against the path. Tito accepted the accolade with the practiced indifference of a man who knows his value lies elsewhere. This initial triumph, however, was merely the prologue to a series of premeditated commissions. He halted before the Palazzo Vecchio, the weight of the city's governance pressing against the stone facade, and waited for the return of the Ten, the oligarchy managing external affairs and war. Their arrival was necessary to deposit the fruits of his clandestine mission to Pisa—a reconnaissance report designed to pave the way for the formal embassy led by Bernardo Rucellai, aimed at securing a peace with Emperor Maximilian and the League.

Tito's aptitude for diplomacy was undeniable. As he delivered his findings with surgical precision, detailing the intricacies of his interviews, Bernardo del Nero, one of the Ten, suppressed a visible flicker of admiration. Yet, this professional respect was poisoned by a deeper, personal animosity. Since the controversial sale of the library, Bernardo's disdain for Tito had calcified. Romola had remained silent regarding the transaction, interpreting it as a tacit command for Bernardo to avoid the subject, but the old man read her silence as a testament to a profound, blighting grief. His sharp eyes detected further signs of domestic unhappiness, a quiet unraveling in the marriage.

*Ah,* Bernardo thought, the cynicism of age settling over him. *That explains her turn toward Fra Girolamo and the Piagnoni.* In his view, unhappy women without offspring inevitably drift into folly or a fanatical religion that burdens them with the entirety of heaven's work. He viewed Romola's education in Latin and Greek as a cruel error, leaving her intellectually developed but emotionally stunted, akin to a girl who had spent her entire childhood pricking her fingers with needles. Her husband, a mere tool with a smooth handle, was destined to meet a bloody end at the hands of the Tornabuonis and similar factions. *Solco torto, sacco dritto,* he muttered internally. *Many a full sack comes from a crooked furrow; he who commands only honest men pays little in wages.*

Despite this internal monologue, Bernardo maintained a rigid neutrality. With Romola's privacy sacrosanct, he possessed no concrete grounds to oppose Tito, save for the fact that Tito was a Greek and personally disliked. Tito's duplicity—feigning loyalty to the popular government while harboring Medicean sympathies—was a trait shared by half the faction, though Tito executed it with superior skill. Consequently, Bernardo treated him with cold, distant respect, a dynamic Tito mirrored with scrupulous propriety. The prevailing sentiment in Florence held that any service rendered to Romola's husband was, by extension, a tribute to her godfather.

Once the official duties at the Old Palace concluded, Tito felt the need to shed the burden of his thoughts and the contents of his *scarsella*. Exhausted and parched from days of riding, he avoided returning home directly. Opportunities for action must be seized with such subtlety that they appeared accidental. He wandered aimlessly toward the Piazza del Duomo. The religious procession had ended, yet the bells continued to toll, and the populace moved restlessly, craving a tangible outlet for their jubilation. While the new discipline demanded Christian restraint, a latent, violent impulse stirred among the youths shouting "Viva Gesu!"—a desire to throw stones in thanks to the divine.

As he traversed the square, Tito could not evade recognition as the herald of peace.
