He dismissed the intrusive ovation, deflecting eager questions by claiming the true messenger from Leghorn, Meo di Sasso, was arriving at the Porta San Frediano. He spoke truths he did not possess, masking his movements with plausible deniability. Freeing himself from these importunities, he entered the Piazza del Duomo, casting a careless gaze that was actually a tactical scan for a specific opportunity. The termination of the procession meant an unusual density of loiterers near Nello's shop, precisely where he needed to be.

Near the north gates of the Baptistery, a group clustered against the rails. Tito feigned indifference until his eye caught the tallest figure, whom he greeted with a perfunctory nod and a toss of his *becchetto* over his shoulder. The man was Dolfo Spini, a broad-shouldered giant whose attire screamed excess. He wore a richly embroidered tunic and linen in the latest French style, a sword and poniard hanging prominently from his belt. His hat, adorned with a defiant red plume, mocked the somber austerity imposed by the Piagnoni. Youth and coarseness defined him; his eyes held a rude, desecrating stare that grated on the nerves of the refined.

Dolfo was the leader of the *Compagnacci*, the Evil Companions—an armed band of dissolute aristocrats, enemies of the Medici and the popular government, but most vitriolic foes of Savonarola. Organized as champions of indulgence against the reformist pietists who threatened to render the world sterile, Dolfo had recently proclaimed Florence doomed by the Frate's blindness. He argued that salvation lay solely in joining the League and expelling the friar to Rome. Thus, his current laughter at the news of foreign aid was suspect; it rang hollow, a mask for a heart burning with resentment.

Leaning against the doorpost amidst the group was Niccolò Machiavelli, a close-shaven, keen-eyed youth who had already dissected the petty egos of the room.

"Messer Dolfo's head is more of a pumpkin than I anticipated," Machiavelli remarked, measuring the man's stupidity by the delusions he trusted. "Your dullest creature is he who grins after having his shins kicked. If I were slightly less sharp, I would pretend to adore this Melema, who holds a secretaryship perfectly suited to my tastes—as if poorly paid Latin could love better-paid Latin! Melema, you are a pestiferous genius, very much in my camp. I regret hearing of your additional stroke of luck today."

"Questionable luck, Niccolò," Tito replied, placing a friendly hand on Machiavelli's shoulder. "I have gained nothing but being grabbed and suffocated by wool-combers, while I ride as battered as a letter-carrier from Bologna."

"Ah! Then you require a touch of my art, Messer Oratore," Nello interjected, stepping forward as the circle opened. "I see yesterday's stubble on your chin. Come, consign yourself to the priest of the Muses. Sandro, bring the lather!"

"In truth, Nello, that is my sole desire," Tito said, sitting down. "Hence my detour to your shop rather than heading home immediately after the Palazzo."

"It is unfitting to present yourself to Madonna Romola with a rusted chin and tangled hair," Nello chided gently. "Nothing coarse should approach the Florentine lily. Though I see her wandering like a sunbeam among the rags lining our corners—or perhaps a moonbeam now. Yesterday, she looked as pale and worn as the fainting Madonna of Fra Giovanni. You must attend to her, my learned friend; she observes too many fasts and vigils in your absence."

Tito sighed, a gesture of melancholy resignation. "Too true, Nello. She starves herself daily during this famine. But what can a husband do? His influence is powerless against the Frate's fire."

"As are all other influences, including that of the Holy Father," added Domenico Cennini, who had joined them. "Have you gleaned anything from Pisa regarding the winds blowing in Rome, Melema?"

"Secrets of the council chamber, Messer Domenico!" Tito smiled, spreading his palms in mock humility. "An envoy must remain as mute as a confessor."

"Certainly," Cennini agreed. "My belief is that if His Holiness drives Fra Girolamo to desperation, the Frate will mobilize heaven and earth to convene a General Council—and succeed. I would not be displeased, though I am no Piagnone."

"With deference to your wisdom, Messer Domenico," Machiavelli countered, his tone cool and analytical, "I must dissent. Not from your wish for a council to reform the Church, but from your assumption that the Frate will yield to the Pope. The Frate's strategy is impossible. Had he limited himself to preaching against Roman vices and prophesying vague Italian scourges, Pope Alexander would have tolerated him indefinitely. Spiritual blasts do not topple walls. But the Frate seeks to be more than a trumpet; he wishes to be a lever, and he *is* one. He aims to propagate Christ's doctrine by sustaining the popular government in Florence, whereas the Pope, according to reliable sources, holds opposing private views."

"Then Florence stands with the Frate," Cennini declared fervently. "Though I would prefer he cease his prophecies, if our right to govern ourselves is threatened, I shall vote to resist Pope Alexander the Sixth, just as our ancestors resisted Gregory the Eleventh."

"Pardon me, Messer Domenico," Machiavelli interrupted, jamming his thumbs into his belt. He spoke with the effortless dominance of exposition that overrides all debate. "Have you grasped the Frate's leverage? Why is he a threat to an acute mind like the Pope? Because he commands the people's ear. He offers threats and promises believed to be divine decrees concerning hell, purgatory, and the fate of Pisa and the Great Council. However, if events contradict him and erode public faith,

By a Street Lamp.

The evening descended with a brooding heaviness, the sky bruised purple and threatening rain. Romola emerged from the Hospital of San Matteo alongside Maso, the lantern swinging between them like a fragile beacon against the gathering gloom. Just as they reached the threshold of the Via de' Bardi, her husband, Tito, stepped forth from the monastery of San Marco. Tito had departed earlier that afternoon, barely crossing paths with Romola since his return, and now he immediately dismissed Maso, irritated by the servant's short, shuffling gait. Such dismissal was routine; Tito reserved his official attentions strictly for moments when duty demanded it. Between Tito and Romola, there existed no friction, no heated debate. They were hopelessly estranged in their souls, rendering any genuine contest for mutual understanding impossible. Instead, they navigated their shared existence with the rigid precision of diplomats adhering to a treaty. When Tito desired a supper prepared in the old library, now converted into a banquet hall, Romola acquiesced silently, ensuring every detail was flawless. In return, Tito granted her absolute autonomy over her daily rhythms, accepting her assistance with transcription and digesting texts, while simultaneously fulfilling her charitable needs with supplies. Yet, even in this arrangement, intimacy remained a closed book. As he had that morning, Tito avoided eye contact, feigning ignorance of her presence within the house, and playfully attributing to her a permanent preference for solitude over his company.

In the fervent early stages of her internal conquest, after abandoning her resolve to flee, Romola had made tentative attempts to restore an open relationship. However, she realized that reconciliation required a frank confrontation regarding their divergent paths and a moral realignment—a demand Tito could not meet. For Tito, salvation from alienation depended solely on her resuming an effusive tenderness that would necessitate forgetting their fundamental differences. He desired no explanation; he deemed thorough discourse impossible. He wished for Romola to love him again, yet for her, love was an impossibility. She could offer submission and gentleness, suppressing any trace of repulsion, but tenderness could not be fabricated. Helpless before this realization, she acknowledged the inevitable outcome: her husband was irrevocably alienated from her.

This distance provided further justification for her exclusion from his secrets, particularly those concerning his political maneuvers. Tito sought to reassure her that he viewed the Medici cause as lost, arguing that he served the popular government with sincerity, both practically and theoretically—a stance in which she now harbored a passionate interest. Nevertheless, subtle impressions, akin to shifting scents, gnawed at her unease regarding his dealings with San Marco. She was torn between the terror of witnessing proof that would ignite her suspicions and the compulsion to observe closely, fearing that unseen harm might befall someone she could potentially protect.

Walking together under the encroaching twilight, Tito announced, "The day's business is not yet concluded. I will escort you to our door, my Romola, before attending to another commission requiring an hour or so. I require rest urgently." He then shifted to amusing anecdotes of his recent visit to Pisa, guiding her toward a quiet loggia where a single lamp burned before an image of the Virgin. Suddenly, the street erupted with the sound of numerous approaching footsteps and confused voices.

"We shall not reach home dry unless we take shelter," Tito declared, hastening Romola up the steps of the loggia with a start. "Unless we take shelter under this convenient loggia."

"Is it truly necessary to wait for this mere drizzle?" Romola asked, surprised.

"No," Tito replied, his mind sharp with the instinctive caution that defined him. "The rain is intensifying. Wait a moment." Through the flickering lamplight, he had spotted the leader of the approaching group wearing a red feather and a glittering sword-hilt. In all likelihood, this was the last person Tito wished to encounter beside Romola at this hour. Earlier that day, he had met Dolfo Spini; the current mission involved a second, critical interview with the Compagnaccio captain, a sequel to his visit at San Marco. Under a prearranged scheme, Tito had delivered forged letters to Savonarola—one bearing the authentic signature and seal of the Cardinal of Naples, whose influence in Rome favored the friar. These documents claimed the Cardinal was traveling from Pisa, reluctant to enter Florence due to grave concerns, yet eager to consult Savonarola at this precarious juncture. He intended to halt at San Casciano, ten miles distant, and ride out the following morning disguised as a simple priest, meeting Savonarola five miles along the road to Florence, two hours after sunrise. The plot relied on Dolfo Spini and his band of Compagnacci ambushing the friar at a secluded spot near the gates, seizing him along with his Dominican companion, and handing him over to a detachment of Milanese cavalry stationed near San Casciano, destined to carry him into Roman territory.

There was a significant probability that the astute friar would detect the trap and refuse the perilous journey, a risk he had recently avoided. Even during preaching, he was typically guarded by armed men; now, he was invited to traverse a solitary road accompanied only by a fellow monk. Time for deliberation was minimal. The strategy banked on the Friar's zeal for uniting forces within and without the Church to convene a General Council, combined with his expectation of urgent aid from the Cardinal in his struggle against the Pope, overpowering his natural shrewdness and caution within the narrow window of decision-making.

Tito had previously interviewed Savonarola, declining to entrust the letters to anyone else. With consummate skill, he had hinted at their content, leading the Friar to infer a rendezvous outside the gates. Consequently, Tito urged Savonarola to request an armed guard from the Signoria, offering to convey the request discreetly. Savonarola had responded curtly that an armed guard contradicted the necessity of privacy. Speaking with a flashing eye, he signaled his intent to accept the risk.

Tito himself cared little for the outcome. His acumen ensured that whatever eventuality arose would ultimately benefit him. Whether Savonarola fell or rose, Tito secured favor and wealth. This was a crude truth, though Tito dressed it in diplomatic language: his keen intellect, recognizing the hollow foundations of all factions, chose the only logical path—making them subservient to his own ends.

If Savonarola succumbed to the trap, diamonds and papal patronage awaited; if not, Tito's agency would strengthen his standing with both the Friar and Spini, rendering him invaluable to the Medicean faction through the confidences gained.

However, Spini was an unpredictable variable. Cunning enough to enjoy plotting, he lacked the self-control required for such intricate schemes involving secrecy. Prone to drunken excursions—even sober Florence produced its *beoni*, or boisterous drinkers, both lay and clerical—Spini often became loud in taverns and private banquets. Despite agreements with Tito to maintain a cool demeanor in public, the risk remained that an evening encounter might trigger sudden, affectionate bluster. The subtle gesture of casting the *becchetto* over the left shoulder was understood in the morning, but a firm warning might fail to deter a fraternal grasp of the shoulder in the evening.

Tito's primary hope was that Dolfo Spini had not yet spotted him. This hope would have been justified had Spini possessed no clearer vision of Tito than Tito had of Spini. Yet, hidden in the shadows, Tito had seen Spini illuminated momentarily by the direct rays of the lamp. In his own right, Tito was a striking figure, comparable to the captain of the Compagnacci. Romola's black-clad form had escaped notice, and she now stood behind her husband's shoulder in the corner of the loggia. Tito's hopes were not left to linger.

"Hah! My carrier-pigeon!" Spini's harsh voice grated, intended as an aside, as his hand clamped onto Tito's shoulder. "Why hide? You didn't realize it was comrades arriving. Lucky I caught you; it saves time. What of the hunt tomorrow? Will the bald-headed game rise? Are the falcons ready?"

Had Tito possessed a temper prone to rage, it would have flared against this bull-faced accomplice, unfit to lead or follow. His lips whitened, but his agitation stemmed from the urgent need to select a safe response. Attempting to silence Spini would only deepen Romola's suspicion; she was too perceptive to be hoodwinked if alarm struck. Conversely, repelling Spini angrily might provoke the wine-soaked Compagnaccio into savagery, as he was quicker to resent than to divine motives. Tito adopted a third course, demonstrating that Romola retained a specific power over him—the power of dread.

He pressed her hand, signaling a hint to her, and spoke in a genial tone of camaraderie: "Yes, my Dolfo, you may prepare with confidence. But take no trumpets with you."

"Don't worry," Spini retorted, slightly offended. "No need to play the learned scholar with me. I know where the devil hides his tail as well as you. Did he swallow the bait whole? Didn't the prophetic nose smell the hook?" He lowered his voice, attempting a clumsy display of secrecy.

"The brute won't be satisfied until he empties the sack," Tito thought. Aloud, however, he said, "Swallowed it as easily as you drink a cup of Trebbiano. Hah! I see torches. A corpse must be coming. The plague is spreading, I hear."

"Santiddio! I hate the sight of those biers. Good

Tito's meticulous scheming had been rendered obsolete by a trifling accident, a variable too insignificant to factor into the calculus of a mastermind. Yet, fate conspired to place him walking beside Romola on this very evening, rendering her presence an insurmountable obstacle. Life remained a labyrinthine game where the sharpest strategies were routinely dismantled by the chaotic drift of circumstance, unpredictable as falling dandelion seeds. Tito cared little for the collapse of Spini's plot; rather, he faced a tactical dilemma: how to deliver warnings to both Savonarola and Spini without triggering suspicion. Alerting the friar immediately, following the delivery of the forged letters, would invite unfavorable conjecture regarding his motives. Conversely, approaching Spini instantly required confessing the truth—that Savonarola had altered his course—which Spini's sharp intellect would interpret solely as Tito's sudden reversal and sabotage of the operation. Delaying the warning to the friar until morning carried the high probability of losing the chance to stop Spini entirely, leaving the Compagnacci faction to return in a fury of thwarted ambition.

This was the risk Tito elected to accept, banking on his ability to soothe Spini by attributing the failure to the Friar's mere caution. Annoyed, though finding it difficult to suppress such emotion given his usual composure, Tito resolved to avoid further contact with Romola and declined to return home that night.

Inside the house, Romola sat vigil through the darkness, refusing to remove her garments. She listened as the rain intensified, a rhythmic drumming that she found strangely comforting; the tempestuous sky seemed a divine shield against human machinations, forcing men into paralysis. Yet, her own mind was besieged by a dual torment: profound uncertainty regarding her husband's actions and a gnawing doubt concerning her own complicity. What falsehoods might he have woven? What designs remained hidden from her view? Trusting Tito was perilous; to convince herself otherwise was futile. Was she not acting out of selfish pride by shrinking from the duty to warn others against him? *"If her husband was a criminal,"* she reasoned, *"her proper station was within the prison walls beside him."* That sentiment held weight, yet it did not absolve her. Could a wife permit her spouse to commit acts that would brand him a malefactor when she possessed the agency to intervene? Prayer felt impossible; her agitated thoughts precluded the necessary state of expectant passivity. As the night wore on, her imagination spun frantic scenarios of how Tito might evade her threats. Just as dawn approached, the violence of the storm subsided, the wind dispersing the clouds to reveal a clear morning that only exacerbated her unease.

Wrapping her mantle tighter, Romola rushed to the loggia, scanning the vast landscape as if some external detail might dictate her next move, though she suspected only rooftops hid the path toward the Palazzo Vecchio where Savonarola might walk toward treachery. Should she seek her godfather? Might he act preemptively to block Fra Girolamo from crossing the gates without explicit proof? But that might be too late. With fresh distress, Romola realized she had gleaned no concrete intelligence from Tito, and the hour had long passed seven. There was no alternative.

She descended the stairs, ignoring the sick members of her household, and stepped out onto the Via de' Bardi, heading swiftly toward the Ponte Vecchio. She intended to traverse the city's heart via the most direct route; furthermore, the great Piazza offered a slim chance of intercepting her husband. By some lingering hope, he might appear there, clarify the Friar's status, and render a trip to San Marco unnecessary. Upon reaching the courtyard of the Palazzo Vecchio, she peered eagerly into the colonnade, then swept her gaze across the Piazza. The familiar silhouette, once etched by youthful affection and now scarred by suffering, was absent. Undeterred, she pressed onward to the Piazza del Duomo.

The square teemed with activity: worshippers ascending and descending the marble steps, men pausing for conversation, vendors laden with goods. Amidst this throng, Romola spotted her husband. Having exited San Marco, he had diverted into Nello's shop and was now leaning casually against the doorpost. As she drew nearer, she observed him conversing with effortless nonchalance, holding his cap in one hand and tossing back his freshly combed hair. The jarring contrast between this display of ease and the bitter anxieties he had instilled convulsed her with indignation; the renewed vision of his hardness intensified her dread. Standing nearby were Cronaca and two other regulars of San Marco. A sudden resolve struck her: *"I will force him to speak before these witnesses."*

Her light step closed the distance before Tito could retreat, coinciding with Cronaca's announcement, "Here comes Madonna Romola." A subtle shock rippled through Tito's frame as he confronted his wife. She appeared gaunt from sleepless vigilance, yet a flash of intensity beyond mere worry ignited in her eyes.

"Has the Frate crossed the gates?" she demanded.

"No," Tito replied, feeling utterly powerless before her scrutiny. He summoned every ounce of self-command to maintain a facade of simple surprise.

"And are you certain he is not departing?" she pressed.

"I am certain he is not going."

"That suffices," Romola declared, turning up the steps to seek refuge within the Duomo, needing solitude to quell her agitation.

Tito harbored no emotion closer to hatred than the one tracking her ascent. Present among the crowd were not only devoted followers of the Friar but also Ser Ceccone, the notary. At that moment, like Tito, Ceccone operated covertly as an agent of the Medici. Ser Francesco di Ser Barone, universally despised as Ser Ceccone, lacked learning, good looks, success, and generosity. He was a traitor devoid of charisma, and consequently, he held no affection for Tito Melema.

"Counter-check."

Late afternoon light filtered through the narrow window of Romola's chamber, casting long, brittle shadows across the desk where she sat copying documents. Her hand hovered over the quill, ready to cease the labor as the sun dipped lower, when the door opened. Tito entered, not with the casual stride of a husband returning home, but with the focused intent of a commander seizing a strategic position. He sought her specifically, and the shift in his demeanor struck her instantly. Without removing his cap or mantle, he planted himself directly before her, resting one elbow on the cabinet, blocking her path with a silent, immovable presence.

Romola felt the weight of her own error settling in her chest. Throughout the day, she had wrestled with the sting of her public confrontation regarding the Frate's safety—a matter she knew he guarded jealously. A tide of penitence washed over her; she had been driven by distrust and indignation, acting with the impulsiveness of a woman who feared losing control. Now, rationalizing the chaos of her emotions, she convinced herself she had misjudged the situation. The duplicity inherent in Florentine factionalism, a truth even her godfather acknowledged, could easily explain the connection with Spini without implicating Tito in the conspiracy. She intended to atone for her rashness, hoping this confession might finally break the two-year silence that had locked their hearts in separate rooms. That silence was absolute; Tito remained ignorant of her flight and return, unaware of the young love that had shattered under the shock of betrayal. They had never dared to revisit that past, a banquet hall where death had once smashed the feast.

She looked up, her gaze submitting to the gravity of her self-reproach, expecting forgiveness. Instead, she encountered a subtle, terrifying transformation in his face. The crisis had arrived. Beneath the veneer of his usual blandness and beseeching gentleness, Tito's determination to master the situation had surged to the surface. It altered his features, creating a mask of hidden muscular tension, as if a man were secretly throttling something feeble yet dangerous within his own throat.

"Romola," he began, his voice a cool, liquid stream that sent a shiver through her spine. "It is time that we should understand each other."

"That is what I most desire, Tito," she whispered, her pale face stripped of anger, replaced entirely by the timidity of self-doubt. In this vulnerability, his dark strength appeared overwhelming.

"You took a step this morning," Tito continued, "which you must now perceive to have been useless—one that exposes you to remark and may involve me in serious practical difficulties."

"I acknowledge that I was too hasty," Romola said, finding a fuller, firmer tone in her voice. She hoped this expression of regret would soften his glare, clearing the way for the other words she needed to speak. "I wish you once for all to understand," Tito replied, his voice unwavering, "that such collisions are incompatible with our position as husband and wife. Reflect on how you were led to that step, lest the process repeat itself."

"That depends chiefly on you, Tito," Romola retorted, a spark of defiance igniting. It was not what she had planned, but the fog of their mutual misunderstanding had obscured her thoughts.

"You would say," Tito countered smoothly, "that nothing henceforth shall occur to excite your unreasonable suspicions. You were frank enough last night to declare you hold no belief in me. I am not surprised by the exaggerated conclusions drawn from slight premises, but I must warn you of the fruit of making such distortions grounds for interfering in affairs of which you are ignorant. Are you following?"

"Yes," Romola flushed, her resentment boiling at this cold tone of superiority.

"Well, then, it may not be long before some other chance words or incidents set your imagination at work devising crimes for me. Perhaps you will rush to the Palazzo Vecchio to alarm the Signoria, setting the city ablaze with panic. Shall I outline the result? Not merely the disgrace of your husband, to which you look forward with such courage, but the arrest and ruin of many among Florence's chief men, including Messer Bernardo del Nero."

Tito had executed a decisive maneuver, and it had succeeded. The color drained from Romola's face until her lips were pale—a rare sight, for she was seldom subject to fear. Tito perceived his triumph.

"You might flatter yourself," he pressed, "that you are performing a heroic deed of deliverance. You might as well attempt to pick locks with fine words as apply such notions to the politics of Florence. The question is no longer whether you possess belief in me, but whether, now warned, you will dare to rush, like a blind man clutching a torch, into intricate affairs you know nothing of."

Romola felt her mind gripped in a vice; the possibilities he outlined rose before her with terrible clarity.

"I am too rash," she admitted. "I will try not to be rash."

"Remember," Tito insisted, his tone unsparing, "that your act of distrust this morning might, for all you know, have had far more fatal effects than that sacrifice of your husband which you have learned to contemplate without flinching."

"Tito, it is not so!" Romola burst forth, rising and moving closer with a desperate resolve to speak her truth. "It is false that I would willingly sacrifice you. It has been the greatest effort of my life to cling to you. I fled in anger two years ago, and returned because I was bound to you more than to anything on earth. But it is useless. You shut me out from your mind. You treat me as a being too unreasonable to share in your knowledge. You remain closed to me about everything."

She looked like a good angel pleading with a stone, bending her face toward him with dilated eyes, laying her hand upon his arm. Yet, Romola's touch and glance stirred no fiber of tenderness in her husband. The good-natured, tolerant Tito, capable of little hatred or impatience, felt himself growing strangely hard toward this wife who had once been his strongest influence. Despite his soft disposition, he possessed a masculine effectiveness of intellect and purpose, an energy sharp as a blade, working its way forward without needing great momentum. Romola possessed her own energy, one that thwarted his, and no man, unless exceptionally feeble, endures being thwarted by his wife. Marriage demands either sympathy or conquest.

Not a single emotion crossed Tito's face as he heard her mention her departure for the first time. His lips merely tightened slightly as he offered a faint smile.

"My Romola, when certain conditions are ascertained, we must accept them. No amount of wishing will fill the Arno, as your people say, nor turn a plum into an orange. I have observed that prayers have little efficacy in such matters. You are constituted to harbor strong impressions inaccessible to reason; I cannot share those impressions, and you have withdrawn all trust from me in consequence. You have changed toward me; therefore, I have changed toward you. It is useless to dwell on the past. We must simply adapt to altered conditions."

"It would not be useless for us to speak openly," Romola said, her voice trembling with the exasperation of striking living muscle against lifeless, insurmountable rock. "It was the sense of deception in you that changed me, keeping us apart. And it is untrue that I changed first. You changed toward me the night you first wore that chain-armour. You held some secret from me—about that old man—and I saw him again yesterday."

In a tone of agonized entreaty, she pleaded, "Tito, if you would once tell me everything, let it be what it may—I would not mind pain—that there might be no wall between us! Is it not possible that we could begin a new life?"

This time, a flash of emotion crossed Tito's face. He stood perfectly still, but the flash seemed to whiten him. Ignoring her appeal, he paused briefly before speaking quietly.

"Your impetuosity about trifles, Romola, possesses a freezing influence that would cool the baths of Nero."

At these cutting words, Romola shrank, drawing herself up into her usual self-sustaining posture. Tito continued.

"If by 'that old man' you mean the mad Jacopo di Nola who attempted my life and made a strange accusation against me, of which I told you nothing because it would have alarmed you to no purpose, he, poor wretch, has died in prison. I saw his name listed among the dead."

"I know nothing of his accusation," Romola stated flatly. "But I know he is the man I saw with a rope around his neck in the Duomo—the man whose portrait Piero di Cosimo painted, grasping your arm as he saw him grasp it the day the French entered, the day you first donned the armor."

"And where is he now, pray?" Tito asked, still pale but governing himself.

"He was lying lifeless in the street from starvation," Romola replied. "I revived him with bread and wine. I brought him to our door, but he refused entry. I gave him money, and he departed without telling me anything. But he had discovered I was your wife. Who is he?"

"A man, half mad, half imbecile, who was once my father's servant in Greece and harbors a rancorous hatred because I dismissed him for theft. Now you have the whole mystery, along with the satisfaction of knowing I am once again in danger of assassination. The fact of my wearing the armor, which seems to have occupied your thoughts so greatly, must have led you to

The winter days drifted past Romola with the silent, relentless monotony of white ships passing a lone watcher on the shore—each vessel identical in appearance, yet carrying a hidden cargo of impending transformation. Tito's whispered warnings had infused her curiosity regarding public affairs with a paralyzing dread, driving her to court ignorance rather than seek truth. The menacing figure of the German Emperor had vanished once more; Florence's political posture had softened in other respects. Yet, the underlying distress persisted, leaving Romola's active duties undiminished. As always, she sought refuge within the complexity of these tasks, shielding her mind from its gnawing doubts. She refrained from celebrating the relief that had arrived in extremity—a reprieve that seemingly validated the strategy of the Frate's faction. Indeed, Francesco Valori, the fiery leader of the Piagnoni, had been elected Gonfaloniere at the start of the year, eager to imprint his brand of radical liberty upon the city during his brief tenure. Superficially, this appeared to fortify the party most devoted to freedom and to bolster Savonarola's protective shield. However, Romola perceived every nuance suggesting that this triumph was merely an unconscious incubation of mixed seeds of change, destined to sprout tragedy at any moment. By Carnival time, shortly after mid-February, her intuition crystallized into certainty. The Medici had abandoned their passivity, mobilizing openly to secure the election of Bernardo del Nero as the new Gonfaloniere.

On the final day of the Carnival, between ten and eleven in the morning, Romola departed toward the Corso degli Albizzi, fulfilling her promise to retrieve her cousin Brigida. Their shared goal was to depart from the Via de' Bardi early in the afternoon, securing positions at a window Tito had reserved in the Piazza della Signoria. A spectacle of unprecedented novelty awaited them there, demanding the gaze of every Florentine eye. The Piagnoni had seized total control of the Carnival's observance. Despite the desperate efforts of Dolfo Spini and his allies to revive traditional masques and practical jokes seasoned with indecency, such frivolity was deemed incompatible with a city where Christ had been proclaimed King. Romola ventured forth in a languid mental state, typical of those embarking on a long day of sightseeing solely to gratify a child or a cherished friend. While the day marked an epoch in Carnival reform, this specific phase failed to ignite her enthusiasm. Unaware of it, she stood at the precipice of a personal epoch wherein another destiny would become visibly, irrevocably entwined with her own.

She chose to traverse the vast Piazza first, conducting a solitary reconnaissance of the unparalleled sight unfolding below. Entering from the south, her vision was arrested by a monstrous, multi-colored edifice shaped like a pyramid—or, more accurately, a colossal fir-tree towering sixty feet high. Its shelves widened progressively toward the base until reaching a circumference of eighty yards. The Piazza teemed with life: slender youths clad in white garments, olive wreaths adorning their heads, moved to and fro around the pyramid's base, carrying baskets brimming with vibrant hues. Maturer figures, some in monastic robes, others in the loose tunics and dark-red caps of artists, assisted, examined, or retreated to distant vantage points to survey the wondrous totality. Among a significant group standing on the marble steps of Orgagna's Loggia, Romola recognized Piero di Cosimo, whose demeanor radiated discontent and scorn.

Approaching closer, she paused to inspect the multifarious objects arranged in gradation from the base to the summit. There were tapestries and brocades of immodest design; paintings and sculptures deemed too likely to incite vice. Boards and tables hosted every variety of game, alongside playing cards, printing blocks, dice, and other gambling apparatus. Worldly music books lined up with instruments of all varieties—lutes, drums, cymbals, and trumpets. Masks and costumes from the old Carnival shows cluttered the space, joined by handsome copies of Ovid, Boccaccio, Petrarch, Pulci, and other texts dedicated to vanity or impurity. Implements of feminine vanity abounded: rouge pots, false hair, mirrors, perfumes, powders, and transparent veils designed to provoke inquisitive glances. Finally, at the very apex, sat the unflattering effigy of a presumably mythical Venetian merchant, rumored to have offered a heavy sum for this collection of marketable abominations. Soaring above him in surpassing ugliness was the symbolic figure of the old, debauched Carnival.

This assembly served as the preparation for a novel bonfire—the Burning of Vanities. Concealed within the pyramid's interior was a plentiful stockpile of dry fuel and gunpowder. On this final evening, the pile was to be ignited to the sound of trumpets, sending the grotesque relic of the old Carnival tumbling into the flames amidst songs of reforming triumph. Such a crowning act required a peculiar organization initiated by Savonarola two years prior. The mass of Florentine boyhood and youth was no longer left to their natural inclinations toward street mischief and crude dissoluteness. Under the tutelage of Fra Domenico, Savonarola's lieutenant, lads and striplings—the hope of Florence—were trained to utter only pure words, cultivating a zeal for the Unseen Good that would shame the lukewarmness of their elders. Their sole pleasures were of an angelic sort: singing divine praises and walking in white robes.

For these troops, rows of seats had been erected high against the Duomo's walls, accustomed to hearing Savonarola address them as the future glory of a city divinely appointed to execute God's work. These fresh-cheeked soldiers were the primary agents of the regenerated merriment of the new Carnival, a sacred parody of the old. Had there been bonfires in ancient times? Now, a bonfire consumed impurity from the earth. Had there been symbolic processions? Now, symbols consisted of white robes, red crosses, and olive wreaths—emblems of peace and innocent gladness—with banners proclaiming the triumphs of goodness. Had there been dancing in rings under the open sky to the sound of choral voices chanting loose songs? Now, monks and laity danced in fraternal love and divine joy, accompanied by the music of hymns. Collections from street passengers exceeded previous records, not for gross suppers, but for the benefit of the hungry and needy. Furthermore, there was the collection of the *Anathema*, the Vanities destined for the great pyramidal bonfire.

Troops of young inquisitors patrolled the streets, urging households to surrender their *Anathema*. Perhaps, after yielding the obvious vanities, the Madonna of the home still possessed small reddened balls imported from the Levant, intended to force a sudden, insincere bloom onto a sallow cheek? If so, she was to deposit them into the basket of doom. Or perhaps she retained ringlets and coils of "dead hair"? Then she was to bring them to the street door, holding them in her hands rather than wearing them, publicly renouncing the *Anathema* that disguised the respectable signs of age with a ghastly mockery of youth. In reward, she would hear fresh, youthful voices pronounce a blessing upon her and her house.

These beardless inquisitors, organized into miniature regiments, undoubtedly embraced their duties with vigor. Coercing people through shame or spiritual pressure into relinquishing items they would otherwise cherish is a form of piety to which the boyish mind converts with remarkable ease. Even when obstinate wicked men erupted in threats of whips or cudgels, the excitement mounted. Savonarola himself evidently wrestled with the difficulty inherent in training these boys—noble yearnings for great ends clashing with an imperfect grasp of means, forcing reliance on supernatural constraint as the only sure hope. The Florentine youth had harbored vile habits and foul tongues; initially, it seemed an unmixed blessing when they shouted "*Viva Gesù!*". Yet, Savonarola was eventually compelled to declare from the pulpit that there was too much shouting. Constant repetition of sacred words bred contempt. He ordered a cessation of the clamor until the next Festa.

Nevertheless, as the long stream of white-robed youthfulness, adorned with little red crosses and olive wreaths, proceeded to the Duomo at dawn to receive communion from Savonarola's hands, it constituted a sight of profound beauty. Doubtless, many of these young souls stored memories of hope and awe that would protect them from viewing their civic duty through a merely vulgar lens. No conscious obedience represents a regression from lawlessness; these boys became the generation of men who fought and endured greatly in the Republic's final struggle.

In the interim hours between early communion and dinner, they conducted their final perambulations to collect alms and vanities, explaining Romola's observation of the slim white figures circling the pyramid's base.

"What think you of this folly, Madonna Romola?" a brusque voice intoned close to her ear.

"Your Piagnoni will make *l'inferno* a pleasant prospect for us if they continue to carry things their own way on earth," the speaker continued. "It takes a cudgel fetched from the mountains to witness painters like Lorenzo di Credi and young Baccio assisting in the burning of color out of life in this manner."

"My good Piero," Romola replied, lifting her gaze to smile at the grim man, "even you must find solace in seeing some of these things destroyed. Observe those gewgaws, wigs, and rouge pots. I have heard you speak with as much indignation against them as Fra Girolamo himself."

"What then?" Piero snapped,

"Good morning, Mistress Tessa," the peddler announced, his voice cutting through the thin February air with the practiced cadence of a man who knows exactly how to extract value from innocence. "You're dressed for a walk, yet you've forgotten the most essential talisman of our season. Without a red cross, you're walking unarmed into a sea of demons."

Tessa paused, clutching her small basket against her hip. She had descended from the quiet heights of the Porta Santa Croce, a district chosen by Tito for its airy isolation, far removed from the shadow of the San Giorgio hill where her past had burned so brightly. Here, amidst the broad gardens and the relative peace of the city's northern edge, she hoped to raise her children free from the memory of Baldassarre's malice. But the Carnival was drawing near, and the festive hum of Florence was beginning to vibrate through the cobblestones.

"You wish to sell me a cross?" Tessa asked, her voice soft, betraying neither fear nor greed.

"I wish to save your soul," the man corrected, stepping closer. "Four white quattrini. A pittance for eternal security. Prices are rising in Purgatory, I assure you."

Tessa hesitated. Her purse contained coins saved for confetti, for Ninna's laughter, for Lillo's delight. She glanced toward the direction of her home, where Tito sat in his carved armchair, radiating a calm that had replaced the tension of the previous evening. He had allowed her this brief excursion, a rare indulgence granted only because he perceived her weariness melting away in the presence of the children. To spend her savings on a trinket felt like a betrayal of their simple domestic joy.

"I... I cannot spare four quattrini," she admitted, lowering her gaze. "My husband provides for us, but we must be careful."

The peddler's expression shifted instantly from commercial opportunism to feigned concern. "Ah, the humble wife! Married, you say? Then you've found your footing. Nofri used to call you 'vermin,' but an ass may bray loudly before he shakes the stars from the sky. Running away was the wisest move you ever made."

"Nofri is ill now," Tessa murmured, recalling the news that had spread through the neighborhood. "He walks with crutches. He is dangerous when angered."

"Dangerous indeed," the man agreed, nodding sagely. "But you seem to have forgotten the old ways. With the famine and the new religion choking the streets, ordinary goods are scarce. Only holy wares remain valuable. This cross is blessed by a Padre. It wards off the Devil's tooth. Have you seen him biting the man in San Giovanni? You'd do well to keep such protection close."

Tessa felt a prickle of unease. The carnival was supposed to be a time of celebration, of roses and kings and queens, as she remembered from years ago. Yet the atmosphere felt thick with superstition and anxiety. The man's words, while seemingly charitable, carried an underlying pressure, a subtle attempt to isolate her from the safety of her family's routine.

"But I have so much to buy for the children," she protested, her voice tightening slightly. "Confetti for Ninna, toys for Lillo. Nice colored sweets cost a great deal. They would prefer the candy to a wooden cross, however holy it may be."

"Since you are an old acquaintance of the Mercato," the peddler said, his tone suddenly dropping to a conspiratorial whisper, "I shall offer you a gift. Two quattrini instead of four. Consider it a blessing I bestow upon you personally."

Tessa reached into her pouch, her fingers trembling as she counted out the coins. She was relieved to escape the transaction, eager to return to the safety of her home before Naldo grew suspicious of her lingering among strangers.

"Wait," the man said, placing a hand on her arm. "Before you depart, tell me: where do you reside?"

"A long way off," Tessa answered quickly, her mind already racing to formulate an excuse. "Beyond San Ambrogio, in the Via Piccola. At the top of the house where the wood is stacked below."

"Inside the gates, then," the man noted, his eyes narrowing slightly as he processed the location. "Well, well. I'll mark this down. Perhaps I'll drop by later to collect your payment. Or perhaps I'll check if you have any rags to trade for future favors."

"No!" Tessa pulled her arm away, her heart pounding. "I can pay now. Please, take the money and leave me."

"Not so fast," the man insisted, holding up the small red cross. "I'm not a hard-hearted peddler. Let me see your house. We can make a proper bargain. Go ahead, fill your basket at Pippo's shop nearby. I must attend to my own affairs."

With a final, ambiguous smile, he turned and walked away, disappearing into the throng of the early holiday crowd. Tessa stood frozen for a moment, the weight of the two quattrini heavy in her palm. She hurried toward Pippo's shop, her steps frantic, driven by a sudden, irrational terror. She had spoken too freely. She had revealed the location of her home to a man who had once plotted murder against her husband.

As she entered the shop, the scent of sugar and spice filling the air, she tried to convince herself that it was merely a coincidence, a harmless interaction born of the carnival spirit. But deep down, she knew the truth. The man with the burden of yarn on his back—the one who had followed Tito in the shadows, who had waited in vain for a chance to strike—had been watching. He had seen her descend. He had heard her answer.

Outside, the sun warmed the stone pavement, but Tessa felt a chill that no amount of confetti could dispel. The quiet safety of the Via Piccola was no longer safe. The threads of fate, once carefully woven by Tito's protective love, had been snagged by a hook of vengeance, and the pull was already beginning.

Monna Brigida's conversion was precipitated not by a sudden epiphany, but by the crushing weight of her own reflection. When Romola spoke of expecting someone else, she referred to her cousin, yet Brigida remained oblivious to the depth of her kin's necessity. As they walked toward the Piazza, the two women encountered a cluster of youths halting before Tessa. Upon seeing the simple peasant girl's distress, Romola stepped forward, intending to intervene. "Wait for me a moment, cousin," she called out.

Brigida, however, recoiled instantly. "Ah, I will not go on: come for me to Boni's shop—I shall go back there."

Her retreat was driven by a fractured psyche. On one hand, she clung to the "vanities" pinned to her person; on the other, a paralyzing dread seized her that the Piagnoni were correct—that rouge, false hair, and pearl embroidery were instruments of damnation. The air itself seemed thick with their dogma, distorting the flavor of reality until nothing appeared as it once had. Here stood Romola, youthful and radiant, living a life that demanded rigorous asceticism. For a widow of fifty-five whose identity was constructed entirely upon the adoration of her own image, maintaining her spirit required immense imaginative fortitude. Brigida found herself battling her morning routine. Was it truly worth the effort to apply makeup if her soul required purification? Yet, when she lifted her hand-mirror, the illusion shattered. She saw a sallow visage, cheeks sagging, lined with crows' feet that no amount of lip-smoothing could conceal. When she parted her grey hair to arrange it in the plain style of the Piagnoni, her courage evaporated.

Monna Berta, though only fifty-two, possessed a sharp tongue and a superior bearing that would reduce Brigida to ridicule, calling her an old hag. Any woman outside the sect would shrug at her appearance; any man would address her as if she were his grandmother. At fifty-five, she reasoned, she was not ancient—merely requiring a touch of artifice. Thus, the rouge, the braids, and the embroidered *berretta* remained. Today, however, as she prepared for the Piagnone Carnival, the internal conflict became unbearable. Fearing the collectors of the Anathema without Romola's protection, she attempted to flee to the druggist's shop. But her retreat was too slow.

Before she could turn, she was spotted by the white-robed boys trailing behind the group approaching Tessa. Their eagerness to claim her was fueled by the knowledge that part of their troop had already cornered a figure bearing the visible mark of the Anathema. Several of these children were in their earliest years of strict training, their imaginations inflamed by a terrifying reverence for divine consequence mixed with a mischievous desire for dominance. A small boy of ten, wearing an olive wreath over cherubic cheeks and wide brown eyes, was the first to intercept her. His gaze held a solemn awe, convinced that every action carried eternal weight, yet his instincts ran toward mischief.

He blocked her path. Brigida felt a surge of anger, searching frantically for an exit, but none existed. It was not the small boy who spoke first, but a youth of fifteen gripping the handles of a wide basket.

"Venerable mother!" he declared. "The blessed Jesus commands you to surrender the Anathema you carry upon you. That cap embroidered with pearls, those jewels securing your false hair—give them up and sell them for the poor. Cast the hair itself away, for it is a lie fit only for the fire. Doubtless, beneath your silk mantle, you harbor other treasures."

"Yes, lady," chimed in the youth at the opposite handle, reciting Fra Girolamo's sermons with practiced fluency. "They are too heavy for you. Heavier than a millstone, weighing you down toward perdition. Will you adorn yourself with the hunger of the poor and proudly bear God's curse?"

"In truth, you are old, *buona madre*," the small boy added in a sweet soprano. "You appear frightfully ugly with the red on your cheeks and that black, glistening hair, along with those fine things. Only Satan delights in such a sight. Your Angel feels sorrow. He wishes you to wipe away the red."

Snatching a soft silk scarf from the basket, he held it out for her to cleanse her face as her guardian angel commanded. Brigida's anger and humiliation rapidly curdled into spiritual terror. Monna Berta and the crowd of fashionable onlookers had vanished from her awareness. Instead, she was encircled by young monitors in white robes, adorned with wreaths and red crosses, radiating a dreadful, unnatural candor. Her Franciscan confessor, Fra Cristoforo, was absent to bolster her skepticism of Dominican teachings. Helpless, gripped by the sensation of a supreme warning, she accepted the scarf. Trembling with submission, she wiped her cheeks.

"It is well, madonna," the second youth affirmed. "This is a holy beginning. Once you remove these vanities from your head, the dew of heavenly grace will descend."

A wave of mischief surged through the group. Reaching for one of the jeweled pins anchoring her braids to the *berretta*, the older boy pulled it free. The heavy black plait fell forward, dragging the entire headgear down over Brigida's face. This provided the final catalyst. She raised her hands hastily, undoing the remaining fastenings, and tossed her beloved crimson-velvet *berretta*, with its intricate embroidery of seed-pearls, into the basket of doom. She stood revealed as an unrouged woman, grey hair pushed back from a face where the deep lines of age had decisively conquered her former plumpness.

Yet the *berretta* was not permitted to rest. With impish fervor, the youngsters lifted it, holding it aloft pitilessly, the false hair dangling limply.

"See, venerable mother," the taller youth proclaimed, "what ugly lies you have liberated yourselves from! And now you resemble the blessed Saint Anna, mother of the Holy Virgin."

Thoughts of immediate entry into a convent, of vanishing from the world forever, raced through Brigida's mind. Nothing remained but the salvation of her soul. Of course, spectators laughed; she needed no confirmation. Perhaps being forced to contemplate Paradise was preferable. But the realization that her accustomed world was no longer within her grasp brought hard tears to her elderly eyes. Through the mist of emotion, she saw a large, rough hand thrusting a red cross before her, obscuring the boys' shoulders. A strong, guttural voice boomed:

"Only four *quattrini*, madonna, blessing included! Buy it. You'll find comfort in it now that your wig is gone. *Deh!* What are we sinners doing all our lives? Making soup in a basket and receiving only the dregs for our stomachs. Better buy a blessing, madonna! Only four *quattrini*; the profit is less than the scent of a single coin, and it goes to the poor."

Blinded by confusion, Brigida moved toward the final act of submission, reaching for money hidden in her embroidered pouch beneath her silk mantle. Suddenly, the circle around her parted. A figure emerged, as welcome as an angel breaking prison bars.

"Romola, look at me!" Brigida cried in a piteous tone, extending both hands.

The white-robed troop began to drift away, sensing their zeal regarding the headgear had exceeded bounds and warranting a reprieve from demanding penitential offerings.

"Dear cousin, do not be distressed," Romola said, struck with pity yet unable to suppress a smile at the sudden transformation of her kinswoman into a figure of genuine, natural simplicity, sharply contrasting with all her previous memories. She removed the black drapery from her own head and draped it over Brigida's shoulders.

"There," she soothed. "No one will notice you now. Let us turn down the Via del Palagio and proceed directly home."

They hurried away, Brigida clutching Romola's hand tightly, seeking tangible proof of her presence. "Ah, my Romola, my dear child!" the short, stout woman exclaimed, hurrying with frequent steps to match the majestic stride of her relative. "What an old scarecrow I am! I must be good—I mean to be good!"

"Yes, yes; buy a cross!" the guttural voice insisted. The rough hand thrust the red cross forward once more. Bratti, undeterred by Romola's presence and unwilling to lose a potential customer, had quietly trailed their retreat.

"Only four *quattrini*, blessing and all—and if there is any profit, it will all go to the poor."

Brigida would have paused even in a less submissive state. She raised one hand deprecatingly to stop Romola's protest, then extended a *grosso*, worth many white *quattrini*, in an entreating tone.

"Take it, good man, and begone."

"You're in the right, madonna," Bratti replied, snatching the coin and shoving the cross into her palm. "I shall not offer you change, for I might as well rob you of a mass. What! We must all be scorched a little, but you'll emerge the easier. Better to fall from the window than the roof. A good Easter and a good year to you!"

"Well, Romola," Brigida wailed pathetically as Bratti departed, "if I am

The carnivals of Florence had faded into the gray dust of Lent, leaving Romola with a singular, grinding task: the support of her cousin Brigida, whose failing health mirrored the city's own decay. Yet, beneath this domestic duty, a fever of anticipation burned within her. Bernardo del Nero, now Gonfaloniere, had secured the Medicean victory through sheer force of will, but Romola sensed the trap closing. Every morning, as the pale dawn breached her curtains, the same dread returned—the certainty that her godfather's two-month tenure was merely the incubation period for a catastrophe, whether a glorious restoration or a bloody betrayal. This fear accompanied her through the crowded streets toward the Duomo, only to dissolve into the roar of the crowd, much like the terror of death vanishes amidst the chaos of battle.

Inside the cathedral, the atmosphere shifted from dread to a terrifying, electric intensity. Savonarola preached his final Lenten sermons, aware that excommunication loomed like a storm cloud. He spoke without euphemisms, holding up the rotting body of the Church in a mirror of unflinching truth. He promised a coming revolution, a resurrection of the faithful akin to Lazarus rising from the tomb, though he also foresaw his own martyrdom. To Romola, whose soul resonated with his fervor, this crisis felt personal. His words became the fuel for her daily labors among the starving and the sick, transforming simple pity into a resolve to serve a higher, albeit distant, heroic end.

Yet, the harmony of this mission was fractured by discordant notes. The initial unity of the reform movement had shattered; the dream of French salvation had evaporated, replaced by political intrigue and renewed hostility. Savonarola's sermons grew sharper, laced with defiance and a desperate craving for divine validation. He moved from denying miracles to demanding them, threatening vengeance upon tyrants with a voice thick with personal rage. While Romola recoiled at the fanaticism bleeding into his rhetoric, others embraced it. Baldassarre, driven by a thirst for justice, attended these services specifically to hear the Prophet's threats. He watched Romola closely, timing his approach for a moment when fate—or desperation—would compel her to listen. Twice their gazes locked in the nave; Romola wished to break the connection, yet she lingered, waiting for a sign that never came.

As April drew to a close, the feared coup failed to materialize. Rumors of Piero de' Medici's machinations died with Bernardo's impending departure. Just as Romola began to dismiss her anxieties as futile, a messenger from Camilla Rucellai intercepted her. Camilla, one of Florence's most notorious female prophets, demanded an immediate audience regarding matters of supreme urgency. Though Romola harbored a visceral aversion to the shrill, hysterical nature of these "seers," especially given Camilla's recent attacks on Bernardo, she suppressed her instinct to flee. Habit and a gnawing conscience overrode her impulse to remain silent; she complied, stepping into the trap of her own making.

Camilla awaited her in a chamber stripped bare, resembling a monk's cell. The prophetess clutched Romola's arm with a grip of iron, her voice trembling with manic energy. She claimed a vision from Romola's own Angel, revealing that Romola possessed secret knowledge that could save the Republic. As Camilla escalated her tirade, claiming Christ and the Blessed Infant had commanded her to expel Bernardo del Nero from the Palazzo Vecchio window—a directive allegedly endorsed by Savonarola himself—Romola's composure snapped. With a surge of righteous fury, she tore her arm free, screaming that Camilla was either mad or wicked.

Driven from the room, Romola fled until she reached the quiet sanctuary of the Badia. Collapsing onto the steps before Filippino Lippi's serene depiction of the Virgin, she sought refuge from the clamor of the street and the storm within her mind. Her greatest fear was not the accusation itself, but the implication that Savonarola had sanctioned such barbarity. She knew his teachings were rooted in law and order; he could not condone murder disguised as prophecy. The answer haunted her: Savonarola was paralyzed. Internally, he feared admitting that such visions stemmed from sources other than his own; externally, he dreaded alienating his followers by condemning the very spiritual phenomena that bolstered his authority.

Kneeling there, Romola faced a crushing contradiction. The intellectual allure of Savonarola's grand design clashed violently with the emotional horror of his methods. She loved her godfather, remembered her father, and believed in the necessity of resisting tyranny, yet she could not reconcile these truths with the fanaticism that now defined their cause. The struggle between reason and feeling tore at her, leaving her unable to find solace even in the holy images surrounding her. Rising abruptly, she resolved to return to her suffering neighbors, seeking strength in tangible acts of kindness rather than abstract dogma. But as she turned to leave, the sanctuary doors seemed to part, and she found herself confronting the man she had been avoiding: Baldassarre.

"I would speak with you," Baldassarre said, his voice cutting through the quiet as Romola regarded him in silent expectation. It was evident he had trailed her, waiting for this exact moment. She was finally poised to uncover the secret that haunted him. "Yes," she replied, offering the same hollow submission one might display under an imposed penance. "But you wish to go where no ears can catch us?" "Where *he* will not intercept us," Baldassarre corrected, turning to glance nervously over his shoulder. "Out—in the open air—far from the streets." "I occasionally visit San Miniato at this hour," Romola stated. "If you prefer, I shall lead the way now; you may follow. The distance ensures solitude." He nodded, and Romola moved out. To lesser minds, venturing to a secluded spot with a man bearing the outward marks of the madness Tito had diagnosed would seem reckless. Yet Romola possessed no such personal timidity; she welcomed the buffer of distance, delaying the inevitable impact of whatever blow awaited her.

The afternoon had waned, the sun dipping low in the western sky, casting long shadows when Romola halted on rough terrain beneath the cypress trunks, scanning for Baldassarre. He was close, yet upon reaching her, he collapsed onto the stony ledge. The robust vigor that had defined him since his initial appearance with the rope in the Duomo was gone. Even the transient tremor of climbing the hill had drained his eyes, rendering them vacant and helpless. "The ascent is steep," Romola observed, her tone soft with compassion as she sat beside him. "And I suspect you have been ravaged by want?" He turned, fixing his gaze upon her in silence, paralyzed by the inability to formulate the words required to express his truth. She remained rigid, terrified that any movement might signal impatience. In this moment, he resembled nothing more than a discarded, common-born elder. Yet Romola was accustomed to proximity with such figures, acutely aware of their burdens. Slowly, his stare coalesced into focus. "Ah!" he burst out with sudden intensity. "You would have been my daughter!"

A crimson flash swept across Romola's face, receding instantly to leave her lips parted in a pale, marble-like horror. The revelation struck her mind with the force of a physical blow. She deduced the facts underlying that singular phrase, and in that first heartbeat, the impulsive belief born of her intimate knowledge of Tito's nature surged unchecked. Her visceral reaction served as fuel for Baldassarre; for the first time, his words found purchase. He seized her arm, his grip tightening with gathering desperation. "You possess noble blood—is it not true? You attend the preacher; you despise baseness—the kind that smiles and triumphs. Do you hate your husband?" "Oh God! Were you truly his father?" Romola whispered, her voice lost in the ghost-images of the past, oblivious to his query. "Or did he lie? Did you claim him when he was small?" "Ah, you believe me! You understand his essence!" Baldassarre cried, exultant, pressing his hand harder against her arm as if tactile contact could anchor his fading sanity. "Will you aid me?" "Yes," Romola answered, misinterpreting his intent. She placed her palm gently over his rough fingers, tears welling as she gazed at him. "Oh, it is pitiful! Speak—were you a great scholar? Did you teach him? What became of it?" She faltered. Tito's accusation of insanity echoed in her mind, and where were the traces of former refinement? Still, she maintained her composure, refusing to withdraw her hand. She sat perfectly still, bracing herself to listen with heightened caution.

"It is gone! All of it!" Baldassarre declared. "They refused to believe me because he lied, branding me mad, and had me dragged to prison. I am old; my mind will not return. The world conspires against me." He paused, his eyes sinking under a wave of despair, before lifting them again with renewed fervor. "But *you* stand with me. He manipulated you into loving him, yet he betrayed you; you hate him. Yes, he manipulated *me*: he was beautiful and gentle, while I was a lonely man. I claimed him when they beat him. He slept in my bosom as an infant; I watched his growth, imparting all my knowledge, intending to pass everything I owned to him. I possessed wealth, books, jewels. He took my jewels and sold them; he abandoned me to slavery. He never sought me out. When I returned, destitute and broken, he denied me. He called me a lunatic." "He told us his father was drowned," Romola murmured faintly. "He must have believed it then. Oh! He could not have been so vile *then*!" A vision rose of Tito in those early days, innocent in her eyes, regarding no evil greater than poison in flowers. The yearning regret of that memory offered a fleeting respite from the horror. With a great sob, tears flooded her eyes. "Ah, you are young, and tears flow easily," Baldassarre remarked, with a hint of impatience. "But tears extinguish the inner fire; only the fire accomplishes anything. Tears will hinder us. Listen." Romola shifted slightly. The specter of his madness flickered through her mind, checking the tide of belief. Was this merely a deranged assassin? Yet her deep conviction in his story outweighed her fear; she avoided risking pain to him by displaying doubt. "Tell me," she asked gently, "how did you lose your memory, your scholarship?" "I fell ill. I cannot measure the duration—it was a void. I recall nothing until I sat in the sun among the stones, surrounded by darkness. Slowly, gradually, I felt a longing for something undefined that never arrived. Upon the ship, amidst the waters, I realized what I craved: the return of the Boy, the restoration of my thoughts. I was locked outside them all. And I remain outside now. I perceive only walls and darkness." Baldassarre drifted into reverie, burying his head in his hands, and Romola's faith in him submerged all prudent hesitation. The pity in her voice resonated like an old wound reopening. Did she not witness daily how her own father mourned Dino and the future he had envisioned?

"It all returned once," Baldassarre continued. "I mastered everything. I saw the world anew, my gems, my books; I believed I held him in thrall. I went to expose him where the lights burned and the trees stood tall. He lied again, calling me mad, and they dragged me to prison... Wickedness is formidable; he wears armor." Fierceness flared within him once more. He spoke with his former intensity, seizing her arm again. "But you will assist me? He has betrayed you too. He keeps another wife, and she bears children. He convinces her he is her husband; she is a foolish, helpless creature. I will reveal her dwelling." The first shock rippling through Romola was pure indignation. The woman's sense of insult surged to the forefront. Baldassarre instinctively sensed her solidarity. "You hate him," he pressed. "Is it not true? No love exists between you. I know women can hate; you possess proud blood. You detest falseness, and you crave revenge." Romola sat paralyzed by the collision of emotions, unaware of the bruising grip on her tender arm. "You shall devise it," Baldassarre whispered urgently. "I have memorized that you are his rightful wife. You are a noble woman. You frequent the preacher of vengeance; you will champion justice. But you must think for me. My mind fails—everything slips away save the fire. The fire is God; it is justice; it cannot die. You believe this, do you not? If they refuse to hang him for stealing from me, you must strip him of his armor. You will make him defenseless, and I shall strike him. I carry a knife, and my arm retains strength." He slid his hand beneath his tunic, tracing the hidden blade absently, needing the sensation to sustain his resolve. To Romola, every passing hour promised increasing difficulty. Her judgment was too sharp, too rapid, to waste breath on futile deprecation toward a man in Baldassarre's state. She chose silence, seeking to buy time for his agitation to subside by pivoting to a question of genuine interest. Her voice trembled slightly. "You say she is foolish and helpless—that other wife—who believes him to be her true husband. Perhaps he is: perhaps he wed her before marrying me." "I cannot say," Baldassarre replied, pausing in his ritual of stroking the knife, his expression bewildered. "My memories end there. I only know her location. You shall see her. I will escort you; but not today," he added hastily, "*he* may be present. Night falls." "It is true," Romola started, suddenly aware that sunset had swallowed the hills in gloom. "But you will come and fetch me—when?" "Tomorrow morning," Baldassarre dreamt, assuming she shared his urgency for vengeance. "Then come to me where you met me today, in the church. I shall await you at ten. Should you not appear, I will return toward midday. Can you recall?" "Mid-day," Baldassarre insisted—"Only mid-day. The same place, and mid-day. And thereafter," he added, rising and clutching her arm with his left hand while brandishing the knife in his right, "we

The evening and the morning arrived with the velocity of a collapsing siege. Romola possessed a singular, driving purpose that had coalesced from the afternoon's anxieties, swelling like a tributary merging into a flood. It was less a decision than a physiological imperative. Ignoring the encroaching gloom of the streets and discarding the thought of Maso's cautious escort, she sprinted toward the bridge. Below, the Arno churned black against the fading crimson of the horizon. She chose the shortest route to the Old Palace, accepting the risk of encountering her husband as a variable she could not calculate; she needed only to purge her heart.

She navigated the pillared court and ascended the broad stairs in a trance, her destination defined solely by the urgent need to speak. At the threshold of the audience chamber, she demanded an usher for the Gonfaloniere, citing her name and insisting on privacy. She was not kept waiting long amidst the silent vigil of frescoed saints and flickering tapers. The door swung open, and Bernardo del Nero entered, his white hair stiff and upright within the folds of his silk *lucco*.

"Romola, my child, what is this?" he asked, his voice tight with alarm as he shut the door against the corridor.

She removed her veil and moved toward him, speechless. He placed a firm hand on her shoulder, pulling her slightly back to inspect her features. Her face was gaunt, carved by exhaustion and prolonged tension; her hair had fallen in chaotic disarray. Yet, her eyes burned with a feverish intensity that seemed to transcend her physical collapse.

"What has he done?" Bernardo demanded abruptly. "Speak, child. Discard your pride. I am your father."

"It concerns not myself," Romola replied quickly. "Nothing concerns me personally. Dearest godfather, it concerns you. I have gathered intelligence—some of which I dare not articulate. But you are under threat within these walls, and everywhere. Fanatics seek your blood, and traitors lurk in the shadows. Trust no one. If you place faith in others, you will be betrayed."

Bernardo offered a weary smile. "Have you driven yourself to this hysterical state, my poor child," he said, lifting a hand to stroke her hair, "to recite such ancient platitudes to an old man like me?"

"Oh, no! These are not old truths," she insisted, clutching her hands together until her knuckles whitened, as if the gesture could contain the words she dared not speak. "These are fresh revelations I possess but cannot vocalize. Dearest godfather, you know I am not foolish. Why did I come to you without cause? Is it too late to warn you against every single individual currently operating in your favor? Is it too late to command, 'Retreat to your villa and remain hidden in the countryside once these final three days of office conclude'?"

Her voice cracked. "Oh God! Perhaps it is too late! And if harm befalls you, it will feel as though I have delivered the blow!"

The confession erupted involuntarily, a spasmodic release of a long-suppressed dread. She recoiled, startled by her own vehemence.

"I mean," she stammered, regaining composure, "I hold no concrete evidence. Only a terror that consumes me."

"Poor child!" Bernardo observed, regarding her with a gaze of quiet penetration for several moments. Then he spoke softly. "Return home, Romola. Rest. These fears may merely be monstrous shadows cast by something trivial and harmless. Even traitors require motive; they follow the scent of profit like rats to cheese. We do not yet know the direction of the wind."

He paused, averting his eyes with an air of abstract resignation, then shrugged slowly. "Warnings serve no purpose for me, child. I engage in no conspiracies, nor do I abandon my banner. Should I march alongside stubborn men charging with pikes and muskets, I must share the consequence. Let us cease this discussion. I have few years remaining in my sack for them to plunder. Go, child; go home and rest."

He touched her head again, affectionately. She could not resist leaning into his arm, pressing her forehead against his shoulder. This caress represented the final remnant of that youthful filial bond, which now appeared radiant even amid its tribulations, untouched by malice.

"Is silence the best path, my Romola?" the elder asked.

"Yes, for now," she answered hesitantly, lifting her head with a pleading expression. "But I cannot foresee if it will endure."

"Well, you possess a father's ear while I draw breath," he declared, lifting the heavy black drapery of his cloak to wrap it securely around her head. "And a father's home. Remember that."

Opening the door, he gestured urgently. "Hasten away. You resemble a specter of darkness; you will find safety elsewhere."

That night, Romola sank into a profound sleep. Her agitation had reached its zenith; she required reserves of strength before facing further suffering. Against her usual discipline, she remained unconscious well past sunrise.

When consciousness returned, it was shattered by the roar of artillery. Piero de' Medici, backed by thirteen hundred men, stood before the gate facing Rome. This was the report Maso delivered, embellished with numerous circumstantial details of questionable reliability. A peasant had rushed into the Signoria before dawn, preventing a surprise attack. His master had already departed, summoned to the Palazzo earlier.

Romola dispatched the old servant to gather further intelligence while she climbed to the loggia, scanning the skyline for signs of the dreaded entry or its repulsion. Maso confirmed that the great Piazza teemed with armed troops and that many prominent citizens suspected of Medician sympathies had been detained within the palace. Public sentiment was fractured; some appeared indifferent to Piero's arrival, whispering that the Signoria itself had invited him, while others noted the hostile reception from Pisan soldiers.

In the swirling chaos of those morning hours, few external events registered clearly in Romola's mind, obscured by the tumult of retrospection and anticipation. She recalled walking to the Badia precisely as scheduled, despite street alarms, and waiting in vain. However, the scene unfolding outside the church doors as they were barred for the afternoon recess remained vividly etched in her memory.

A palpable shift had occurred in the expressions and cadences of the crowd, both armed and unarmed. The gunfire resumed, yet this time it elicited laughter rather than fear. She soon understood the cause. Piero de' Medici and his cavalry had abandoned Florence, galloping furiously down the road to Siena.

This confirmation came from a sturdy shopkeeper among the *Piagnoni*, who had not yet lowered his pike.

"It is true," he concluded, his tone bitter. "Piero has fled, but those who conspired with his arrival remain. All know their identities; if the new Signoria fulfills its duty, we shall soon expose them."

The words struck Romola like a sudden, sharp convulsion. Yet, the impending evil they hinted at was not yet immediate. As she stepped back into her home, her primary anxiety shifted to the terrifying possibility that she had lost sight of Baldassarre for an extended period.

Waiting. The sun-drenched days stretched on, offering neither the reunion Romola craved nor the confirmation of her deepest fears. Baldassarre remained silent; despite the government's intensified surveillance, no evidence surfaced regarding the suspected conspiracy. Yet, the season delivered other calamities that pierced the veil of her anxiety, forcing her into the crucible of immediate action. The Black Death spread across Florence, and Savonarola faced formal excommunication. These twin disasters severed her drifting detachment from the Friar, anchoring her once again to the man who had ignited her vocation. She could not navigate the labyrinth of plague-ridden misery without acknowledging the debt she owed to Fra Girolamo for the moral fortitude to endure it. Nor could she stand idly by as a man distinguished solely by his fierce commitment to Christian reality was silenced, without feeling the magnetic pull toward his cause.

In mid-June, the bull of excommunication, delayed for weeks from Rome, was read aloud within the Duomo. Romola attended, seeking to bolster her resolve through the spectacle of resistance. The scene stood in stark, chilling contrast to the vibrant mornings she had known. Gone were the crowds of citizens, their faces upturned in hopeful anticipation, forming a living amphitheater around the aging friar. Gone was the thunderous voice that once animated the masses with visions of tangible and spiritual grandeur. Instead, the vast nave echoed with the somber flicker of taper-light against bare stone walls. A black-and-grey congregation of monks and secular priests sat with bowed heads, their expressions expectant yet subdued. Only the intermittent chiming of small bells broke the monotony of a voice reciting a sentence already suspended in church tradition. As the candles were extinguished, the heavy, shuffling retreat of monastic footsteps faded into a dim silence.

Romola's fervor for the Frar was doubled by the cruel irony she observed: the gleeful triumph etched onto the coarse features of his enemies, juxtaposed against the terrified confusion in the eyes of his staunchest allies.
