The boundary between the duty of obedience and the right of resistance was inherently precarious, but the stakes were elevated to cosmic proportions by the conviction that the Church was not a mere political compromise, but a living organism imbued with divine power to bless or curse. For most devout Florentines, this belief was an immutable truth, as undeniable as the curvature of the sky. Consequently, Savonarola's logical rebuttals—that the excommunication was unjust and therefore void—only served to heighten their terror, appearing as a reckless challenge to a mystical force against which humanity possessed no defense.

Romola, however, viewed the institution of the Church exclusively through the lens of Savonarola. Having grown up outside the traditional communal fabric of her father's secluded household, she recognized no authority above his moral force. To her, the excommunication represented not theological nuance, but the naked aggression of vice. On one side stood a man dedicated to public virtue and spiritual purity; on the other, the predatory coalition of alarmist selfishness led by Rodrigo Borgia, now crowned as the infamous Pope Alexander VI. While neutral observers might perceive the finer shades of truth softening the edges of such binary oppositions, Romola required a clarity that neutrality could not provide. This excommunication stripped away the petty local antagonisms that plagued Savonarola in the heat of the pulpit, elevating his struggle to a universal appeal against the corruption of ecclesiastical power. He became a standard-bearer leaping into the breach, and in witnessing this generous self-sacrifice, Romola found her doubts dissolving. She felt the exhilaration of certainty, convinced of the soul's highest prize and her own capacity to achieve it.

This renewed enthusiasm carried her through the arduous summer months. She held back from Tito any mention of her clandestine meeting with Baldassarre or the disturbing revelations he had imparted. Such confessions would yield nothing but cold sarcasm regarding her sympathy for his alleged assassin. Baldassarre appeared paralyzed; the greater threat was not his retaliation against Tito, but the possibility that Tito, driven by paranoia, would devise a new scheme to eliminate the man who haunted his conscience. Romola knew she could take no decisive action until she saw Baldassarre again and uncovered the full truth concerning the "other wife"—determining if she was the woman to whom Tito was originally bound. The implications of this mystery, striking at the core of her inherited pride, poisoned her lingering memories of her husband. Her absolute faith in Baldassarre's testimony bred a visceral revulsion toward Tito, a horror so profound it might have provoked a desperate confrontation had he not been conspicuously absent from the city. Like many affluent citizens fleeing the plague, Tito spent his leisure in the countryside, where the villa of Melema awaited. Since Romola had refused to abandon the city, he had no need to establish a separate rural retreat.

Finally, in late July, the crushing weight of public crisis began to lift. The plague receded, and Savonarola's fortunes improved as a sympathetic magisterate penned urgent appeals to Rome for the annulment of the excommunication. Yet, Romola's robust physique succumbed to the inevitable lethargy following prolonged exertion. Her restless mind, however, demanded constant engagement, compelling her to walk the cooling streets of Florence every morning and evening, altering her route with the faint hope of intercepting Baldassarre. Perhaps a sudden illness had induced a paralysis of memory, causing him to forget her address—or her very existence. This was her most optimistic hypothesis for his silence. The probability she clung to with trembling certainty was far darker: that he had succumbed to the plague.

The morning sun had already begun to press heavily upon Romola, the heat becoming an oppressive weight as she navigated the intersecting streets beyond the gate of Santa Croce. The silence of the Borgo La Croce was absolute, broken only by the rhythmic tap of her own footsteps on the pavement. As she rounded a bend, a small figure emerged from the dust—a toddler, no older than three, clad solely in a white shirt. He halted mid-waddle, turning to regard her.

At first, she saw only the back of a sturdy, square-shouldered boy crowned with a cloud of reddish-brown curls. Then he turned, revealing dark eyes swollen with tears and a lower lip protruding in a silent tremor. His fat brown fists gripped the fabric of his shirt with desperate, useless force. A passing shadow cast by a taller figure seemed to trigger a climax of terror, erupting into a loud, wailing sob that sent great beads of moisture rolling down his cheeks.

Instinctively, driven by a maternal compassion that was a hidden wellspring of her own passion, Romola removed her hood. She dropped to her knees on the hot stones, wrapping her arms around the child and pressing her cheek against his. Initially, his cries intensified, but he made no attempt to flee. Soon, the outburst ceased with the abrupt, illogical finality characteristic of childhood emotion. His distorted features smoothed out, replaced by a wide-eyed, open-mouthed stare fixed upon her.

"You have wandered off, little one," she said softly, kissing his forehead. "Fear not; we shall locate your home. Perhaps your mother awaits us."

She deduced he had slipped away during a moment when his guardian's attention had been diverted, and surmised he would soon be pursued.

"Oh, what a heavy, heavy boy!" she murmured, attempting to hoist him. Finding him immovable, she gestured. "Come, then. You must toddle alongside me."

His parted lips remained sealed in awed silence, one fist still clutching the shirt with tenacious grip, while the other yielded readily to her surprisingly strong yet gentle hand.

"Do you have a mother?" Romola asked, gazing down at the boy with a mixture of curiosity and longing. He offered no verbal response. Under different circumstances, a girl might have chattered endlessly; this square-shouldered miniature man, however, possessed a solemn reserve.

Yet, he was acutely aware of his destination. At the turn near San Ambrogio, he dragged Romola toward it, lifting his head to signal recognition.

"Ah, that is the path home, is it?" she smiled. He merely thrust his head forward and pulled, a silent command to hasten their pace.

Another corner awaited his scrutiny before Romola arrived at a short lane opening onto a garden. Here, the boy stopped, tugging her toward a flight of stone stairs. He showed no inclination to release her hand, and she, unwilling to part with him without ensuring safe delivery to his guardians, ascended. The sudden withdrawal from sunlight rendered visibility dim, but at the final landing, a beam of light spilled from an open doorway. Passing through a narrow antechamber, they entered the main room, where Romola paused. No sound greeted her arrival.

In the far corner, bathed in the light, Tessa sat on a low chair beside a cradle, her head lolling to one side, fast asleep. By a window, with her back turned to the entrance, Monna Lisa worked silently, preparing a salad in a state of deafened obliviousness. Romola allowed herself a fleeting second to register the stillness before Lillo snatched his hand away and bolted to his mother's side. He leaned his head back against her arm, regarding Romola with a serious, assessing gaze, making no overt move to disturb her rest.

When Lillo nudged her, Tessa stirred, blinking up in confusion. Her glance landed on the stranger at the threshold, and she jolted upright, flushing deeply and trembling, unable to speak or advance.

"Ah! We have met before," Romola said, stepping forward with a reassuring smile. "I am delighted it was your son. He was weeping in the street; I presumed he had fled. So we walked together briefly until he recognized his surroundings and led me here. You had not noticed his absence? Good, otherwise you would have been terrified."

The shock of discovering Lillo missing momentarily eclipsed all other sensations for Tessa. Her complexion drained, then flushed again. Seizing her son's arm, she rushed toward Monna Lisa, whispering loudly in the old woman's ear with a sob caught in her throat:

"Oh, Lisa, you are wicked! Why stand with your back to the door? Lillo ran far into the street."

"Holy Mother!" Monna Lisa exclaimed in her meek, thick voice, dropping the spoon from her grasp. "Where were *you*, then? I believed you were present, watching him."

"But you *know* I drift off to sleep while rocking," Tessa retorted pettishly.

"Well, well, we must keep the outer door barred or bind him tight," Monna Lisa advised, "for before long he'll be as cunning as Satan, and that is the holy truth. But how did he return, then?"

This query snapped Tessa back to the awareness of Romola's presence. Without answering, she turned, blushing and shrinking once more, while Monna Lisa's eyes tracked her movement. The elder woman performed a low reverence.

"Doubtless the most noble lady brought him back." Advancing slightly, she added, "It is my shame that he was found in only his shirt. He kicked and refused his garments this morning, and the mother, poor soul, will never permit him to be beaten. But what can an old woman do without a stick when the lad's legs grow so strong? Let your nobility inspect his legs."

Conscious of the scrutiny, Lillo hitched his shirt higher, examining his olive-toned calves with a dispassionate curiosity. Romola laughed, bending to give him a affectionate shake and a kiss. This gesture bolstered the reassurance Tessa had gleaned from Monna Lisa's deference. Earlier, when Naldo recounted the Carnival incident and Tessa asked who the celestial lady—who appeared precisely when needed and vanished swiftly—might be, whether perhaps the Holy Madonna herself, Naldo had replied, "Not exactly, my Tessa; only one of the saints," refusing further elaboration. Thus, within the dreamlike synthesis of Tessa's limited experience, Romola remained vaguely associated with church iconography. Upon her reappearance, the memory of her protection was tinged with religious awe, albeit lightly, for Tessa's primary dread concerned ugly and malevolent entities. It seemed improbable that benevolent forces would anger or punish her, as Nofri and the Devil were wont to do. With Monna Lisa speaking freely of Lillo's legs and Romola laughing, Tessa relaxed considerably.

"Ninna sleeps in the cradle," she noted. "_She_ is lovely too."

Romola moved to observe the sleeping infant, while Monna Lisa, one of the exceptionally meek deaf who rarely anticipated speech directed at them, resumed her salad preparation.

"Ah! She is waking; her blue eyes have opened," Romola observed. "You must lift her, and I shall occupy this chair—may I?—to nurse Lillo. Come, Lillo!"

She settled into Tito's chair, extending her arms toward the boy, whose gaze had followed her. He hesitated, pointing small fingers at her with a mix of puzzlement and indignation. "That is Babbo's chair," he declared, seemingly trapped by the dilemma of what to do if Babbo returned to find Romola occupying his seat.

"But Babbo is absent, and I depart shortly. Come, allow me to nurse you as he does," Romola said, wondering internally for the first time the nature of this father whose wife dressed in peasant garb yet possessed a certain daintiness indicative of idleness and abundance. Lillo consented to be lifted, finding the lap remarkably comfortable, and began exploring her dress and hands, searching for ornaments beyond the rosary.

Tessa, previously engaged in soothing Ninna's waking irritability, now seated herself on a low stool near Romola's knee, arranging the infant with jealous precision. She resented that the strange lady seemed to favor the boy as much as Naldo did.

"Lillo intended to be angry with me for sitting in Babbo's chair," Romola remarked, bending to kiss Ninna's tiny foot. "Will he return soon and claim it?"

"Ah, no!" Tessa assured her. "You may sit therein for a long while. I shall regret your departure. When you first arrived to care for me at the Carnival, I deemed it marvelous; you came and departed so quickly. Naldo suggested you might be a saint, which caused me to tremble somewhat, though I know saints are very good. You were kind to me, and now you have tended Lillo. Perhaps you will always visit to care for me. That is how Naldo behaved long ago; he came to protect me when I was frightened, one San Giovanni. I could not discern his origin—he was so beautiful and good. And so are you," Tessa concluded, gazing upward at Romola with devout admiration.

"Naldo is your husband. His eyes mirror Lillo's," Romola stated, observing the boy's darkly penciled eyebrows, unusually prominent for his age. She spoke not as an interrogator, but with a quiet certainty of inference that remained enigmatic to Tessa.

"Ah

Tito possessed excellent reasons for asserting his safety. Over the preceding quarter, anticipating the inevitable exposure of the Medicean conspirators, he had meticulously fortified his position. He had expanded his influence across Rome and Milan, positioning himself as the conduit for clandestine intelligence and indirect pressure against both the Frate and the popular faction. Simultaneously, he had cultivated the popular party with unprecedented diligence, projecting a facade of unwavering political alignment. Yet, paradoxically, he had intensified his active employment and exclusive reliance by the Mediceans. Maintaining this triple deception was effortless; the Mediceans' own admission of duplicity stripped them of any metric to judge a colleague lacking their hereditary bonds, entrenched alliances, and visceral prejudices. To the Medici, deceiving the opposition was a legitimate stratagem, while deceiving their own kin was a baseness they felt no urge to commit. In leveraging Tito's pliable talents, they remained oblivious to the fact that his lack of traditional attachments rendered him a convenient tool but removed the primary guarantee of mutual honor found among themselves. Furthermore, the *Arrabbiati* of Rome and Milan, the aristocratic party's bitterest foes of Savonarola, operated a network of espionage grounded in profound hypocrisy, demanding exorbitant fees for their services. Suspecting an agent solely because he performed a role convincingly would have been a logical fallacy. Conversely, the *Piagnoni*, driven by the directness of energetic conviction, were prone to crediting Tito with sincere adherence due to his feigned indifference toward religious sentiment. Under these conditions, the last three months constituted a period of flattering triumph for Tito. His paramount objective was securing a future station in Rome or Milan; he harbored a growing resolve to abandon Florence for a capital where life was less arduous and the rewards for talent and learning more magnificent. Currently, the scales tipped toward Milan; rendering specific services to Duke Ludovico Sforza within the year promised a court appointment surpassing the allure of Rome. The revelation of the Medicean conspiracy, however, was an event Tito had foreseen but not anticipated in its modus operandi. The arrest of Lamberto dell'Antella, clutching a damning letter and fueled by rancorous hatred of the Medici, was an incalculable variable. Despite the careful pretexts guarding his agency, Tito could not evade implication in a broad discovery. Yet, his sharp intellect rapidly charted the path to his own preservation with minimal collateral damage. While preserving one's integrity is pleasant, the skin remains an organ sensitive to atmospheric shifts. His calculations proved accurate. That evening, prior to returning home, he secured the three outcomes he coveted: exemption from prosecution regarding Medicean complicity, retention of his secretaryship for another annum absent resignation, and the strict confidentiality of the price exacted for these guarantees. A prudent man might deem the cost excessive, and Tito himself would prefer otherwise, yet necessity dictates sacrifice.

His initial maneuver targeted Francesco Valori, head of the *Piagnoni* and one of the Ten under whom Tito served. Valori, though possessing noble qualities, was plagued by an arrogant temperament that estranged allies and bred bitter animosity, particularly toward Bernardo del Nero. Securing a pledge of secrecy, Tito confessed his agency for the Mediceans, citing motives he described with frank candor. He declared his belief in the futility of their efforts and his genuine preference for the popular government. He further feigned confidence in Valori, revealing his personal disdain for Bernardo del Nero. This preamble led to the critical proposition: there existed another Medicean plot, and provided certain conditions were met, Tito could travel to Siena and Romagna, where Piero de' Medici was amassing forces, to procure documentary evidence for the council. Crucially, his reputation as a Medicean agent must remain unblemished in the eyes of the Medici; thus, his status as a source of information to the authorities required absolute obscurity. Nevertheless, precautions could fail. Before facing the repercussions of turning against his associates, he needed assurance of immunity from prosecution and deprivation of office for the coming year. These terms did not resonate with Valori as they might today. Valori's mind was not fixated on evaluating Tito's conduct; rather, it was consumed by the imperative to secure an extreme sentence against the five prisoners. With immense efforts surely mounting to save them, the evidence required to alarm the well-disposed to the dangers of clemency had to be irrefutable. Legal proceedings of the era posited that evidence was a commodity attainable only through foul means. Torturing suspects until they confessed universal guilt was a step toward justice, especially if a traitor emerged. Lamberto dell'Antella had been tortured to extract knowledge beyond his grasp, yet stronger facts were needed, particularly against Bernardo del Nero, who had thus far refrained from betrayal despite earlier attempts to dissuade the plot. Florence's welfare demanded Bernardo's guilt be illuminated in the starkest light. Zealous in this belief, perhaps blinded by his own hatred, Valori deemed Tito's proposal acceptable. He presented it to his colleagues anonymously, winning their assent.

Late that afternoon, Tito was granted an audience by the Special Council. He produced a profound sensation by unveiling a second plot designed to ensure Piero de' Medici's mastery of Florence, scheduled for mid-August. Documentary proof on this matter would clarify the righteous course more effectively than any other action. He received a commission to depart for Siena at dawn, accompanied by a written guarantee of his immunity and tenure. Among the twenty Florentines casting grave gazes upon Tito as he stood gracefully before them, articulating startling truths with easy circumlocution and an apparent lack of higher motive—a mask often worn by the most intense affectation—they formed silent verdicts. Several concluded that this ingenious and serviceable Greek was henceforth to be utilized for public exigencies rather than private intimacy. Unprincipled men were assets, allowing those with scruples to keep their hands relatively clean amidst necessary filth. Indeed, it remained obscure to respectable Florentine minds, barring those holding the Frate's extravagant faith in earthly purity, how any department functioned without human instruments worthy of being kicked or spat upon while receiving their wages. Those very men passing tacit judgment on Tito were soon to engage in a memorable transaction impossible without such resolute unscrupulousness. As the Florentine poet Pulci noted, "Betrayal pleases many, but no one loves the traitor." Society maintained gibbets for murderers and martyrs alike, offering execrable hisses for both dastardly acts and generous truths—a mixed state signaling not chaos, but struggling order.

For Tito, he was acutely aware of his slight descent in the esteem of those accepting his services. Possessing the self-reflection inherent to habitual rational calculation, he would have declined disapproval from worldly men. He had never intended to be judged poorly, aiming instead to conduct affairs so ably that opposition to societal standards went unnoticed. The barrier separating him from Romola had risen precisely because such concealment was impossible with her. He recoiled from condemnatory judgments as one recoils from an inhospitable climate. Yet, circumstances were not as malleable to cleverness as desired; events had turned inconveniently. He harbored no rancor against Messer Bernardo del Nero; he held personal affection for Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci. Having served them ably, he would have deserved high reward had their party prevailed. Was he to forfeit all life's agreeable fruits because their cause failed? His offer of additional proof against them would likely alter their fate little; he was convinced they would escape extreme consequences. Had he withheld it, however, his promising prospects would have been utterly destroyed. What motive drives a man but self-interest? Florentines engrossed in petty political schemes might conflate self-interest with family pride and ancient hatreds. A modern simpleton swallowing philosophical systems might mistake self-conceit for divine reward. Fanatics believing in the Coming Scourge might view their interest in a future palm-branch and white robe. But no man of clear intellect allows his course to be steered by such puerile impulses or dubious internal vapors. Consider Pontanus, poet and philosopher of unrivaled Latinity, who delivered a magnificent oration in Naples welcoming the French king—the very monarch destined to dethrone his royal friend and patron. Yet Pontanus held his head high and prospered. Men rarely concern themselves with such matters unless their personal spleen is provoked. Weakness alone is despised; power commands immunity. No man ascends high without incurring unpleasant necessities exposing him to enmity and occasional hissing when a pretext is needed. It was a faint prognostic of this hissing, gathered by Tito from the council chamber, that lent his current conduct the character of an epoch, prompting him to dwell upon it with argumentative vindication.

This was not a deeper plunge into wrongdoing, for no bond to the Medici could supersede his tie to his father. Yet, his conduct toward his father had been shielded by successful lies; his present act admitted no total concealment—it was inherently a revelation. Tito winced under this new liability to disesteem. Very well; a little patience, and within six months or a year, he might turn his back on these hard, eager Florentines with their futile quarrels and sinking fortunes. His brilliant success in Florence bore ugly flaws: his infatuation with the wrong woman and Baldassarre's return under incalculable circumstances. But as Tito galloped with a loose rein toward Siena, he envisioned a future free from these haunting errors. He held substantial funds safely outside Florence, stood in the prime vigor of twenty-eight, and possessed well-tested skill. Could he

The dust of Siena settled upon Tito's coat as he departed, leaving Romola to endure a fortnight of agonizing suspension. The city held its breath; the accused remained incarcerated, their fate a pendulum swinging between the precipice of execution and the fragile hope of reprieve. Rumors circulated like smoke in a drafty hall: some whispered that the delay itself was a shield, the inertia of power refusing to crush five distinguished names until the political winds shifted. Others screamed that lenity would invite the Medici wolves to devour the Republic whole. The weight of judgment rested with the Eight, yet they recoiled from the burden of six votes, deferring to the Signoria, who in turn shrunk from the responsibility, pushing the matter toward the Great Council. Yet, the law of Savonarola—the *set fave*, the six beans that pulsed as the political heartbeat of Florence—demanded a path of appeal. The conspirators' allies blocked this legal recourse, fearing that admitting the sentence would seal their doom, forcing a convoluted middle ground where a massive assembly of magistrates and citizens would convene on the seventeenth of August to decide the prisoners' lives.

On that day, Romola, her spirit frayed by the terror of imminent loss, secured permission to visit her godfather, Tito, though only under the cold gaze of witnesses. Returning home via the Via de' Bardi alongside her cousin Brigida, she carried no news, only the hollow ache of uncertainty. Brigida vanished quickly to intercept official dispatches elsewhere, leaving Romola collapsed upon a bench in the sun-drenched salon, her head bowed in her hands, oblivious to the vibrant chaos surrounding her. Time stretched thin. When the door finally creaked open, it was not Brigida who entered, but the man whose betrayal haunted her dreams.

Since their separation that night, Romola had clung to the illusion that Tito remained loyal, a pawn of the Medici executing errands in Romagna. But the shadow of his cruelty toward Baldassarre loomed larger than any diplomatic mission; it felt as though his hands were permanently stained with fresh blood. As his footsteps echoed on the stone floor, a primal chill seized her. She could not turn, could not rise. He moved to sit opposite her, calm and unreadable.

"This meeting mirrors our parting, Romola," he said, his voice cool, devoid of the heat she expected. "Yet I perceive a terrible suspense hangs over you. I come to offer you the relief of hope."

She flinched, shifting her posture, her eyes narrowing with suspicion. "Hope?"

"The council has been prorogued until the twenty-first," he announced smoothly. "While the Eight have finally succumbed to fear and pronounced a sentence of condemnation, the plea for an Appeal to the Great Council has been lodged. It awaits ratification. The Special Council reconvenes on the twenty-first to deliberate. Until then, three days remain—a window where fortune may smile upon the prisoners, where influence can be wielded."

Romola surged from her seat, color flooding her cheeks, her hands trembling violently. In that surge, her resentment evaporated, replaced by desperate utility. "Possibly," Tito continued, rising to match her intensity, "your own thoughts anticipated mine. You are considering the Frate."

"I am," she admitted, surprised by her own candor. "Has he acted? Tell me."

"Francesco Valori's venom drove today's proceedings. Half the council yielded to terror, but strong voices oppose the death sentence within these walls and without: Piero Guicciardini of the Signoria, and Giovan Battista Ridolfi, who will not forgive the killing of his brother Niccolò."

"But how can the Appeal be denied?" Romola cried, indignant. "When it is the law! One of the glories of our people!"

"They label this an exception. Loud bluster masks the danger of the Republic. However, a powerful influence applied correctly over these three days could sway the wavering and check Valori's fury. I learned that the Frate has already sent a message to Valori in support of Lorenzo Tornabuoni. You possess access to him, Romola. Use it."

"It is true," she murmured, staring at the floor. "I cannot believe the Frate would sanction denying the Appeal."

"Tito leaned forward, his tone dropping to a conspiratorial whisper. 'Rumors swirl in the Palazzo that it would be a stain on Fra Girolamo's honor to allow a government of his own making to block the Appeal without protest, especially given his sermons on the necessity of killing tyrants.' He paused, his cynicism sharp. 'Between ourselves, with all due respect to your saint, he has grown accustomed to preaching human sacrifice. Some of his followers find this inconsistent with leniency.'"

Romola winced, the pain evident in her gaze. "He is driven to excess. It was not always so. I *will* seek an audience. I cannot rest until I have spoken to him. I trust in the greatness of his heart." Her eyes were fixed on the ground, disconnected from the man speaking to her.

"Do not waste time," Tito urged, twirling his cap with practiced ease. "And consider this, Romola: can you not see, past your prejudices, that my interests align with yours? Do not mistake my survival for an offense."

An electric jolt struck Romola. The fog lifted; she saw him clearly for the first time in weeks. "At least," he added, his voice hardening, "do not base our future solely on the premise that because an evil act is possible, *I* committed it. Am I the sole outlier beyond the reach of your charity?"

The dam broke. The suppressed rage, held back for two weeks, crashed forth like a tidal wave. "It is too late, Tito," she declared, her voice ringing with absolute resolve. "Deceit, once born, cannot be killed. And now I know the truth. That old man was your father—the man to whom you owe everything, more than if he were your biological sire. Compared to that, breaking my trust and my father's is a trivial transgression. As long as you deny the truth regarding that man, a horror festers between us. The law meant to bind us remains unobeyed. I am a human being with a soul that abhors your actions. Our union is a pretense; a perpetual lie cannot constitute a sacred marriage."

Tito fell silent, his composure cracking under the weight of her accusation. When he spoke, it was with calculated caution, fueled by alarm. "So, you intend to exercise this independence by abandoning me?"

"I desire to leave you," Romola snapped.

"And suppose I refuse to relinquish what the law grants me?" Tito countered, his voice dripping with mockery. "Then I shall proclaim my reasons to all of Florence. I shall produce my mad assassin, who is eager to obey, and claim his testimony is valid precisely because he is so rational as to wish to kill you. First, I will inform the Signoria you are a Medicean conspirator; then, I will tell the Medici you have betrayed them. In either case, I will offer the irrefutable proof that you are capable of every vice. It will be a striking role for a wife. And if, based on such evidence, you are dragged through the mud, you will surpass all the heroines of Greek tragedy."

He paused, letting the threat hang in the air, but she stood motionless. He resumed, his tone condescending. "I suspect your only grievance is that I failed to meet some lofty, undefined conditions of your affection, reducing me to the status of a mere pious donor, a generous Piagnone. Your victory in hanging me is not guaranteed. Perhaps you will start by courting Messer Bernardo del Nero?"

"Why do I speak of anything?" Romola shrieked, collapsing back onto her chair, overwhelmed by the sheer ugliness of the thought. "It is hateful to me to dwell on myself."

She did not register Tito's departure nor the passage of time until the door opened again, admitting Monna Brigida. In that split second, she leaped up, her mind clearing of all hesitation.

"Cousin," she commanded, her voice steady. "We must go to San Marco immediately. I must see my confessor, Fra Salvestro."

The morning light cut sharp across the Piazza della Signoria, illuminating a city gripped in the feverish throes of political purgatory. Romola moved through the throng toward San Marco, her destination secured only yesterday through the intervention of Fra Salvestro. Interviews with Savonarola were rare commodities, guarded with the rigid precision of a fortress; when granted, they were stripped of all theatrical mystery to avoid the pretexts of calumny. Thus, she arrived during the peak hour of the outer cloisters, ensuring her audience occurred amidst the noise of the crowd rather than in the sterile isolation of the chapter-house.

She walked with purpose, scanning the urban landscape for the pulse of public sentiment. Every loggia, every shaded corner of the piazza, every shopfront serving as a gossip station buzzed with the agitation of gratuitous debate—a languishing trade that paradoxically fueled the vigor of political discourse. The factions supporting and opposing the death penalty for the conspirators were engaged in a frantic race to gauge the popular mood during the three-day lull. Handbills plastered the walls like invasive weeds, shouting their messages in massive, bold typefaces: *JUSTICE* versus *RUIN*, *OBSERVANCE OF LAW* versus *THE APPEAL*. These islands of black capital letters floated above seas of smaller, explanatory text, which the populace largely ignored. At that moment, in the first flush of triumph over the invention of printing, the prevailing belief held that no argument could match the persuasive weight of a question-begging phrase rendered in giant font.

Romola, however, possessed a different agenda. She craved the substance hidden in the smaller print, the nuanced reasoning that mattered. Though driven by urgency, she scanned her surroundings anxiously, desperate to intercept any stray pamphlets before the *sbirri* tore them from the walls or before eager readers consumed them whole. Her search led her behind San Giovanni, where she spotted Bratti, a familiar face from the humble quarters of Florence. He was currently bartering his stock of flyers for small coins with passersby.

"Bratti," she called out, her voice cutting through the din. "Do you have both editions? Give them to me quickly."

"Two sorts," Bratti replied, his movements slow and deliberate as he separated the damp sheets. "'Law' and 'Justice.'"

"Which sells faster?"

"'Justice,' by far. So I raised the price to two danari. Then I realized 'Law' was equally valuable merchandise; people despise cheapness, and selling it for one danaro would be a wrong to the product. I'm a fair trader. Both cost me nothing; both yield a fair profit. Do you need more than one of each?"

"No, no. Here is a white quattrino for the pair." Romola folded the bills tightly and melted away before he could elaborate further.

She reached the outer cloisters of San Marco, where Fra Salvestro waited beneath the arcade. He did not register her approach, engrossed in conversation with lay visitors. Under the current administration, which favored the Frate, the post-excommunication timidity regarding visits to San Marco had begun to recede. Among the listeners sat Andrea Cambini, a known satellite of Francesco Valori. Cambini gestured emphatically, expounding on some topic with theatrical flair, while Fra Salvestro listened with an air of trivial curiosity—the look of someone who values the mere existence of news far more than its truth or merit.

Romola recognized the subject instantly from the disjointed fragments reaching her ears: the fate of the conspirators. She halted her approach, allowing the distraction to play out. When she finally caught Fra Salvestro's gaze, she turned sharply toward the chapter-house door. He nodded in silent approval and vanished into the inner cloister. A lay brother stepped forward to open the heavy doors, closing them firmly behind her as she entered.

Inside, the air was thick with the scent of incense and old stone. The frescoes lining the walls depicted scenes of sorrow, figures that seemed to mourn alongside her for the death of her brother, Dino. The memory surged, not as a clear flashback, but as a chaotic blend of impressions merging with her current terror. It felt as though her present anxiety was a resurrection of a previous longing, one that had previously struck against the immovable rigidity of marble. She suppressed the thought, forcing her focus onto the task at hand. Unfolding the handbills, she hoped to read them before Fra Girolamo's arrival. By the time she finished the tract advocating strict legal observance, the great wooden doors creaked open. She doubled the papers and stood expectant.

Fra Girolamo entered. According to custom, Romola knelt, but as he offered his benedictory greeting, she rose and stood opposite him at a respectful distance. Time had altered him profoundly. Since his excommunication, years of seclusion had etched deeper lines into his face, marking the toll of overtaxed intellect and bodily austerity. Yet, Romola sensed a shift more profound than mere aging. The serene elevation and pure human fellowship that had once captivated her seemed diminished. Was it the fading of his former spirit, or the awakening of a dormant alienation born from her godfather's estrangement from the Frate? Perhaps both currents converged. Human relationships often fracture not from a single cruel act, but from the gradual erosion of affection under the strain of repeated compromises.

Savonarola's gaze met hers with a hardness born of egotistic preoccupation. He intuitively grasped that her visit concerned the conspirators, a subject requiring him to silence internal voices that threatened to resurface under external pressure. Seated earlier in his cell, correcting proofs for his *Triumph of the Cross*, he had found solace in a posture of neutrality.

"It is a matter of grave importance, undoubtedly, that brings you to me, my daughter," he began, his tone gentle, a mask of self-control rather than genuine inclination. "I know you do not typically dwell on trifles."

"Father, you know the gravity before I speak," Romola interrupted, pouring forth her plea with urgent intensity. "I seek the life of the old man I love most in the world. The thought of him has accompanied the image of my father since I can remember daylight. That is my warrant for coming, even if my presence seems superfluous. Perhaps you have already decided that your power over human hearts should be used to deny Florentines a right you yourselves helped secure."

"I do not interfere with the functions of the State, my daughter," Savonarola replied, clearly disinclined to reignite a debate he had already wrestled with internally. "I have preached and labored for a good government, for it is essential to the perfection of the Christian life; but I keep my hands from particular affairs entrusted to experienced citizens."

"Surely, father—" Romola started, then stopped. The impulse to remonstrate with the man who had once been her guiding star felt like a violation of her own reverence.

Savonarola, perceptive and noble, detected the conflict arresting her. Too wise to fall into the self-justifying evasions he sometimes employed in public crowds, he assumed a stance of calm expectation.

"Speak what is in your heart, continue, my daughter," he commanded, arms crossed, watching her intently.

"I was about to say, father, that this matter transcends the fervor of many sermons you have delivered. If it belongs to you to urge that men condemned for offenses against the state retain the right to appeal to the Great Council—and if counting that victory is a glory to you—can it not also belong to you to oppose the denial of that right to those who need it most? Surely this touches the Christian life more intimately than predicting the death of the Dauphin or the conquest of Pisa."

A subtle movement, akin to suppressed pain, tightened the corners of Savonarola's mouth before he responded.

"My daughter, I speak only as permitted. I am not master of the timing when I may become a vessel for knowledge beyond common understanding. In this instance, I possess no illumination beyond what wisdom dictates for those charged with the safety of the State. Regarding the Law of Appeal against the Six Votes, I labored tirelessly for its passage to ensure no Florentine suffered loss of life or goods due to the private hatred of a few in power. However, these five men sought to overthrow a free government and restore a corrupt tyrant. They were condemned with the assent of a vast assembly of their peers. They initially refused to bring their cause before the Great Council. Consequently, they forfeited the right to appeal."

"How can they have lost it?" Romola countered, her voice rising. "It is the inherent right to appeal against condemnation, and they have never been condemned until now. Forgive me, father, but it *is* private hatred that denies them the appeal; it *is* the violence of the few that terrifies the many. Else, why did the assembly fracture immediately after appearing united? If anything weighs against the law, let this weigh heavily: you once preached more earnestly than any other doctrine that there should be no place for hatred and bloodshed arising from party strife, lest private ill-will find opportunity in public acts. Father, you know private hatred is at work here. Will it not dishonor you not to intervene on the side of mercy, when so many view it as synonymous with law and justice?"

"My daughter," Savonarola said, his voice carrying a visible tremor of emotion, "there exists a mercy that is weakness, and even treason against the common good. The safety of Florence—which encompasses more than the mere welfare of its inhabitants—now demands severity, as it

Midnight had barely claimed the sky above the Piazza della Signoria when the moon, cresting the rooftops of the Old Palace, failed to cast its customary chiaroscuro upon the cobblestones. Instead of dappled light and shadow, the ground was swallowed by a suffocating density of humanity. No single paving stone was visible, obscured entirely by the heads of a frenzied, struggling multitude. The usual nocturnal symphony of Florence—the rhythmic patter of footsteps, the murmur of voices, the distant strum of a lute, the skittering of night-wanderers—had been obliterated. In its place roared a chaotic cacophony of shouted curses, trampling feet, jostling bodies, and the metallic clash of weapons, creating a sonic wall through which nothing penetrated save a piercing shriek or the heavy, tolling boom of a bell. Every citizen capable of rising was awake, either trapped within the piazza's boundaries or fighting desperately to breach them.

Inside the palace, the Council Chamber remained a fortress of tension. The chief magistracies, the eighty senators, and the select citizens who had debated through the burning daylight and torchlit nights sat in suspended animation. Their deliberation had been a ferocious quarrel regarding the Appeal: whether to grant clemency or execute the prisoners immediately to preempt the dangers of delay. The debate had grown so heated that the clamor leaked through the walls to the mob outside. Only in the final hour had the verdict fallen. The Signoria had fractured; four members had stubbornly voted for the Appeal, defying the pragmatic warning that refusal meant the sack of their homes. Yet, Francesco Valori, in a brief, furious address, had rendered his faction's resolve ominous, declaring that if the Signoria failed to defend the liberties of the Florentine people by executing the five perfidious citizens, others would seize the cause at the peril of all opponents. The Florentine Cato had triumphed. Upon recounting the votes, the four obstinate white beans vanished; the entire panel of nine cast the fatal affirmative black, sentencing the five prisoners to immediate death—a decision that implicitly, though with greater delay, sentenced Francesco Valori as well.

Now, while the Judicial Eight departed for the Bargello to prepare the instruments of execution, the five condemned men were dragged barefoot and shackled through the council chamber. This spectacle was orchestrated by their friends, hoping that the visible humiliation of such cruelty alongside two venerable elders, Bernardo del Nero and Niccolò Ridolfi, and two brilliant young leaders, Tornabuoni and Pucci, would stir the populace. It was futile. Any pity awakened was of a hopeless variety, leading not to rescue but to the slower, darker fuel of revenge.

Upstairs, amidst the chaos, Romola stood motionless against a massive pillar in the palace courtyard, awaiting the arrival of her godfather on his way to execution. Through powerful intervention, she had secured permission to spend the evening with him, remaining until the council's outcome was sealed. Now, she waited beside his confessor, prepared to follow the guard leading him to the Bargello. Her heart was set on clinging to the childless old man until the absolute final moment, mirroring the devotion of her own father; she had silenced all objections. Giovan Battista Ridolfi, a devoted disciple of Savonarola who faced the bitter sight of his elder brother Niccolò's death, had pledged to ensure her safety and stood faithfully by her side. Tito was also present in the palace, though unseen by her. Since the seventeenth, they had deliberately avoided one another. Tito knew only by inference from the friar's neutral report that her pleas had failed. Surrounded by officials and dignitaries, both Florentine and foreign, he maintained the grave, subdued silence of a man torn between public duty and private agony. When alluded to regarding his wife, he implied that her violent mental state, coupled with her status as the spouse of a man holding office under a government condemning her godfather, bred a diseased hostility toward him. Thus, for her own sake, he deemed it prudent not to approach her.

"Ah, the old Bardi blood!" Cennini muttered, shaking his head. "I shall not be surprised if this affair shakes *her* loose from the Frate, as surely as it loosens others I could name."

"It is excusable in a woman," interjected a young French envoy, bowing smoothly to Tito, "who is doubtless beautiful, since she is the wife of Messer Tito. One assumes her affections must supersede the good of the State, and that no one is to be beheaded who is anyone's cousin. But such sentimentality is not to be encouraged in the male population. Your Florentine polity seems significantly weakened by it."

"That is true," Niccolò Machiavelli replied calmly. "Where personal ties are strong, the hostilities they ignite must be accounted for. Many of these half-measures are merely hot-headed blundering. The only safe blows inflicted on men and parties are those too heavy to be avenged."

"Niccolò," Cennini warned, "there is a clever wickedness in thy talk sometimes that makes me mistrust thy pleasant young face as if it were a mask of Satan."

"Not at all, my good Domenico," Machiavelli smiled, placing a hand on the elder's shoulder. "Satan was a blunderer, an introducer of novelty who met with a stupendous failure. Had he succeeded, we would all be worshipping him, and his portrait would be far more flattering."

"Well, well," Cennini conceded. "I say not thy doctrine is not too clever for Satan; I only say it is wicked enough for him."

"I tell you," Machiavelli continued, "my doctrine belongs to all men who seek an end slightly beyond their own noses. Ask our Frate, our prophet, how his universal renovation is to be achieved. He will tell you: first, establish a free and pure government. Since this cannot be accomplished by making all Florentines love one another, it must be done by severing every head that stands obstinately in the way. Only if a man incurs odium by sanctioning a severity insufficient to be final does he commit a blunder. And I suspect the Frate has committed precisely that blunder. On this occasion, he might have won lustre by exerting himself to maintain the Appeal. Instead, he has lost lustre and gained no strength."

Before another word could be spoken, the announcement came that the prisoners were leaving the council chamber. The majority surged toward the door, racing to secure the clearest path to the Bargello behind the prisoner's guard, drawn equally by deep passion and cold curiosity. Tito remained behind. He possessed a natural repugnance to sights of death and pain; five days prior, he had hoped the execution would not occur, settling for exile as the maximum penalty. His own safety required no more. Yet now, he felt a grim assurance: learning that Bernardo del Nero's head had rolled would be a welcome guarantee of his security. The new knowledge and attitude revealed by Romola on the day of his return had instilled in him a fresh dread of the power she held to render his position precarious. If any act of hers succeeded in making him an object of suspicion and hatred, he foresaw not only frustration but frustration under unpleasant circumstances. Her belief in Baldassarre had clearly hardened her wavering feelings against further submission; if her godfather lived, she would easily win him to share her faith. Romola seemed an increasingly unmanageable variable in his destiny. However, if Bernardo del Nero were dead, the difficulties besetting her in opposing her husband would likely prove insurmountable to her shrinking pride. Consequently, Tito felt relieved knowing the Eight had gone to the Bargello to order the instant erection of the scaffold. Four other men—his intimates and confederates—were to die alongside Bernardo. A man's own safety is a god that sometimes makes very grim demands. Tito felt them acutely: even in pursuing what was agreeable, this paradoxical life forced upon him the desire for what was disagreeable. Hearing the shuffle of many feet and the clanking of metal on the stairs through the open doorway, he answered the French envoy's questions with a display of sad resignation to state necessity.

Those sounds struck Romola as if her auditory faculties had been exalted alongside every other sense. She required no arm to support her; she shed no tear. She felt an intensity of life transcending both grief and joy, wherein the mind aligns itself with elder forces that wrought existence before the birth of pleasure and pain. Since her godfather's fate was decided, her internal struggle had yielded to an identification with him in these supreme moments. She asserted inwardly that if he suffered the punishment of treason, he did not deserve the name of traitor; he was the victim of a collision between two forms of faithfulness. It was not given him to die for the noblest cause, yet he died because of his nobility. He might have been a meaner man and found it easier to avoid this guilt. Romola felt the full force of that sympathy with the individual lot that continually opposes the formulas by which actions and parties are judged. She walked the path with her second father to the scaffold, steeling herself to defy ignominy through the consciousness that it was undeserved.

The route was barricaded by three hundred armed men, ordered by Francesco Valori. Among the apparent contradictions of this event, none was more striking than the dual alarms: the fear of popular rage against the conspirators and the fear of a rescue attempt by a hostile crowd. Upon reaching the courtyard of the Bargello, Romola was permitted to approach Bernardo

On the eighth day following that catastrophic night, Romola stood upon the precipice of the Mediterranean, gazing at the gentle, rhythmic pulse of the summer sea just beyond the modest fishing hamlet of Viareggio. She had fled Florence once more, and this time, no authoritative voice halted her retreat. Clad in the grey habit of a religious sister, she no longer viewed the garb as a mask; rather, it served as armor against a heart-sickness that rendered the distinction between identity and disguise irrelevant. A fresh rebellion had ignited within her, fueled by a profound despair. Why cling to one label over another, or to the hollow echo of her own name, when she despairs of finding any coherent duty attached to it? What force remains capable of forging that sacred motive men term "duty," unless it is anchored in a specific, believing love? Every bond of strong affection had shattered. In her marriage—the highest covenant of all—she had ceased to perceive the mystical union that guarantees indissolubility, nor even the obligation of a voluntary pledge. Had she not proven that the objects of her vows were inherently impossible? The impulse to liberate herself surged with overwhelming force, yet this freedom promised only an exchange of calamities. There is no recompense for a woman who realizes her life's primary relationship was merely a mistake. She had lost her crown. The deepest secret of human blessedness had whispered its truth to her, only to slip past her forever. Now, her greatest support in this supreme sorrow had vanished. The vision of any grand purpose, any end to existence capable of ennobling endurance and elevating the mundane toil of daily life with divine ardor, was utterly eclipsed by a sense of cosmic confusion. All effort felt like dragging at tangled threads; every fellowship, whether for resistance or advocacy, appeared as mere unfairness and exclusion.

Who, after all, was the man who had embodied for her the highest heroism—not the stoic endurance of the isolated warrior, but the willing, self-offering love of the martyr? Who was the cause he struggled to defend? Romola had lost her trust in Savonarola, extinguishing the fervent admiration that had previously blinded her to his aberrations, allowing her to focus solely on the grand trajectory of his ambition. Now that her devotion to her godfather had cast her into direct antagonism with the Friar, she perceived the repulsive and contradictory details of his teachings with a painful, hyper-lucid clarity that distorted their scale. In the bitterness of her disillusionment, she declared his striving to renovate the Church and the world to be a pursuit of a mere name—a title that signified nothing more than the measures designed to fortify his own position in Florence. Indeed, these were often questionable deeds and words, undertaken solely to shield his influence from the consequences of his own errors. The political reform that once sparked a new passion in her life now reduced itself to narrow stratagems for the safety of Florence, a contemptible contradiction to his alternating professions of blind trust in Divine providence.

It was inevitable that she should judge the Friar unjustly regarding questions of individual suffering, viewing them through the lens of personal tenderness, while he viewed them through the rigid eyes of theoretical conviction. In his assertion that his party's cause was synonymous with the Kingdom of God, she heard only the ring of egoism. Perhaps such words are rarely spoken without that baser undertone; yet, they constitute the implicit formula of all energetic belief. If such belief, pursuing a grand and distant goal, risks descending into demon-worship—where the votary sends his children through the fire with a readiness that hardly resembles sacrifice—then tender fellow-feeling for the near and dear possesses its own dangers, tending toward timidity and skepticism regarding the larger aims without which life cannot ascend to religion. Thus, poor Romola was being blinded by her tears. No one who has ever experienced the loss of faith in a fellow-man whom they have profoundly loved and revered will lightly claim that such a shock leaves the faith in the Invisible Goodness unshaken. As the trust in high humanity sank, so too did the dignity of life; we cease to believe in our own better selves, for that too is part of the common nature degraded by our thoughts. All the finer impulses of the soul grew dull. Romola felt even the springs of her once-active pity drying up, leaving her to barren, egoistic complaint. Had not *she* suffered her sorrows? And few had cared for her while she had cared for many. She had done enough; she had striven for the impossible and was weary of this stifling, crowded existence. She longed for that repose in pure sensation she had sometimes dreamed of during the sultry afternoons of her youth, when she imagined herself floating naively in the waters. The clear waves seemed to beckon; she wished she could lie down upon them and drift from sleep into death. Yet Romola could not directly seek death; the fullness of young life within her forbade such a direct act. She could only wish that death would arrive.

At the point where she paused, the shoreline curved sharply inward, forming a deep bay where a small sailing boat was moored. In her longing to glide across the waters turning golden under the level rays of the setting sun, she recalled a tale from Boccaccio, one she had loved to linger over when her father slept and she moved from her stool to the floor to read the *Decameron*. It was the story of Gostanza, a woman in lovesick despair who desired no further life but lacked the courage to end it. She had placed herself in a boat, pushed off to sea, and lain down, wrapping her mantle around her head, hoping to be wrecked so that her fear would be powerless to flee from death. This memory had remained a mere thought in Romola's mind until now. As she paced back and forth, she observed another vessel gliding black against the red-gold horizon, carrying a single man toward the bend where the first boat lay. Continuing her walk, she eventually saw the man land, haul his craft onto the sand, and begin unloading cargo. He was likely the owner of the smaller boat as well; he would depart soon, and her opportunity would vanish with him—her chance to purchase that smaller vessel. She had not yet consciously admitted her intent to use it, but she felt a sudden, sharp eagerness to secure the possibility of its use, signaling the unconscious growth of a thought into a desire.

"Is that little boat yours also?" she asked the fisherman, who had looked up, startled by the tall grey figure, and offered a reverence to this holy sister wandering mysteriously in the evening solitude. It was indeed his boat—an old craft, barely seaworthy, yet valuable to any man willing to repair it. By the blessing of Saint Anthony, whose chapel stood in the village nearby, his fishing had prospered, and he now possessed a better vessel, formerly owned by Gianni, who had died. However, he had not yet sold the older one. Romola inquired of its value, and while the man was occupied, she thrust the sum into a small satchel lying on the ground, containing the remnants of his dinner. Afterwards, she watched him furled his sail and asked him how to set it for a voyage to sea. Pacing up and down again, she waited for his departure. The image of herself gliding away in that boat upon the darkening waters expanded into a consuming longing, much like the thought of a cool stream in oppressive heat becomes a painful thirst. To be freed from the burden of choice when all motives were bruised, to commit herself, in a state of sleep, to a destiny that would either bring death or new necessities capable of awakening a new life—it was a thought that beckoned her the more because the soft evening air made her crave rest in the still solitude, rather than returning to the noise and heat of the village.

Finally, the slow-moving fisherman gathered his belongings and walked away. Soon, the gold of the sunset faded into dusk, and there was no living creature in sight, no sound but the lulling monotony of lapping waves. In this sea, there was no tide strong enough to carry her away if she waited for its ebb; however, Romola sensed the land breeze rising slightly. She entered the boat, unfurled the sail, and secured it as she had learned in that first brief lesson. She noted that it caught the light breeze, and that was all she required. Loosening the boat from its moorings, she attempted to urge it forward with an oar until she was far from the land, until the sea turned dark even to the west, and the stars began to disclose themselves like a palpitating life over the vast heavens.

Resting finally, she threw back her cowl and removed the kerchief beneath, which had confined her hair. She folded both under her head for a pillow against one of the boat's ribs. Her fair head was still very young and could endure a hard cushion. Thus she lay, letting the soft night air breathe upon her as she drifted on the water, watching the deepening quiet of the sky. She was alone now; she had freed herself from all claims, freeing herself even from that crushing burden of choice which grows heavier as claims loosen their guiding hold. Had she found anything resembling the dream of her girlhood? No. Memories clung to her like the weight of broken wings that could never be lifted—memories of human sympathy which, even in its pain, leaves a thirst that the Great Mother has no milk to still. Romola felt orphaned amidst those wide expanses of sea and sky. She read no message of love for her in that distant, symbolic writing of the heavens. "Ten o'clock. The tide turns toward San Marco."

The morning of the twenty-seventh of February moved with the mechanical inevitability of a clockwork mechanism, driven by the collective desperation of a populace seeking salvation. It was the final day of the Carnival, yet the festive masks had been discarded for the grim reality of the season ahead. Everyone knew the preparations for the second Bonfire of Vanities were underway before the Old Palace, but the gravitational pull of the city's attention had shifted decisively to the Piazza.

The square was a cauldron of humanity. Newcomers surged from every alleyway, a chaotic river pressing against the banks of the established crowd. The front ranks, tightly packed and immovable, formed a living wall around the semicircular barrier guarding the church entrance. Within this enclosure, the Dominican Brethren of San Marco assembled with military precision. Yet, the wooden pulpit erected above the doorway remained vacant—a hollow throne awaiting the occupant who held the city in a state of suspended animation.

This man was the Pope's pariah, the ecclesiastical outcast forbidden to consort with the clergy of Florence, and the heretic whose mere presence warranted excommunication for anyone daring to listen. He had declared the Pontiff a usurper, a man who bought the papal chair with bribes, wielding a hilt without a blade. He had proclaimed that the Pope's curses were broken swords and that obedience to such a ruler was not merely unlawful—it was a crime against the Christian life. Despite the Vatican's terrifying threats of interdict and the promise of sending him to Rome to be "converted," the masses continued to flock to San Marco. They accepted communion from his excommunicated hands, driven by a desperate calculus: *If this Frate truly commanded divine lightning, why should we fear?*

For the common citizen, the theological debate was abstract. But the stakes had become brutally concrete. The belief in the Prophet now threatened their commerce, destabilized their position among the Italian states, and risked plunging the entire city into spiritual and economic ruin. The question had metastasized in the public consciousness: *"What miracle showest thou?"*

Slowly, then with accelerating intensity, this fatal demand had grown in Savonarola's ear. He had responded outwardly by promising the miracle would arrive at the fitting time, and inwardly by clinging to a faith that was far from unwavering. What faith remains solid when the weight of a potential interdict presses down? His conviction wavered, yet his rhetoric hardened. It is the burden of the demagogue to speak in the currency of yesterday's faith, banking on its resurrection tomorrow.

The wooden pulpit had been constructed specifically to stage the spectacle the impatient crowd demanded—a supernatural guarantee of his mission. As the ordinary friars in black mantles filed in, chanting their psalms, the multitude's gaze flickered between the monks and the empty platform. Interest lingered on the faces of the brethren; several belonged to high Florentine families, others to the artisan class that comprised the bulk of the crowd. Only when the roster of monks was complete, and the chanting reached a rhythmic crescendo, did a murmur ripple through the square: *"Fra Girolamo must be coming now."*

It was this anticipation, sharper than the familiar drone of psalmody, that cast a pallid, solemn light over the sea of upturned faces. All eyes locked onto the void where the preacher should stand.

In an instant, the emptiness vanished. A figure draped entirely in black cowl and mantle stepped into the pulpit, kneeling with head bowed, face obscured. To the eager throng, the silence stretched agonizingly long. The black silhouette knelt while the monks chanted, creating a tension thick enough to cut. The stillness was absolute, charged with an electric awe that paralyzed even those ready to stone him; their arms felt unnerved, their aggression dissolving into reverence.

Then, a vibration passed through the crowd, a wave of shared recognition moving from neighbor to neighbor like a silent handshake. The Frate had risen. He turned toward the people and partially pushed back his hood. The monotonous wail of the choir ceased abruptly. To those nearest the platform, the fading sounds of the chant seemed to merge instantly into the force of Savonarola's flashing glance as he surveyed the silence.

He stretched out his hands. In their exquisite delicacy, they appeared transformed—from crude instruments of grasping into vessels of hypersensitive perception, capable of receiving truth without gross contact. These hands spoke a language of pure appeal from the soul masked by his fierce, passionate visage, etched now with the deep lines of forty-four years of struggle.

At the extension of his arms, a reaction rippled through the front ranks. Some collapsed to their knees, and scattered devout disciples further back followed suit. However, the majority remained standing. Some resisted the impulse to kneel before an excommunicated man, fearing that such an act of blessing might invite immediate divine judgment. Others scoffed, viewing the scene as a new comedy staged by an ambitious deceiver, though they admitted a strange impotence in opposing the rising tide of fashion.

Then came the voice. Clear, low, and cutting through the air.

More fell to their knees. As the intonation rose, becoming clearer and more resonant, the number of upright heads dwindled rapidly. By the time he uttered *"Benedicat vos omnipotens Deus,"* the phrase erupted into a masculine cry, a roar of protest against the demonish forces attempting to stifle the blessing. It rang like a trumpet across the extremities of the Piazza. Underneath the sound, every single head bowed.

Following the utterance of the benediction, Savonarola himself sank to his knees, hiding his face in temporary exhaustion. These eruptions of emotion were the fuel of his existence; he had told the people long ago, *"Without preaching I cannot live."* But it was a life that consumed him, shattering him piece by piece.

Minutes later, as some began to rise, a larger number remained prostrate, all faces fixed intently on him. He held a crystal vessel containing the consecrated Host, preparing to address the assembly.

"You remember, my children," he began, his voice carrying clearly in the vast stillness, "three days ago I besought you. When I held this Sacrament in my hand before you all, I asked you to pray fervently to the Most High: if this work of mine does not come from Him, let Him send a fire to consume me, that I may vanish into eternal darkness, away from His light which I have hidden with my falsity."

He paused, the breathless moment hanging heavy in the air. Perhaps no one truly prayed, save for those acting in devout obedience. Instead, every consciousness was seized by the overwhelming sensation that Savonarola himself was praying. His voice was not loud, yet it pierced the wide silence.

*"Lord, if I have not wrought in sincerity of soul, if my word cometh not from Thee, strike me in this moment with Thy thunder, and let the fires of Thy wrath enclose me."*

He ceased speaking. He stood motionless, holding the sacred mystery in his hand, eyes uplifted, his entire aspect trembling with excitation. The rest of the square mirrored his stillness. Sunlight, previously struggling through the grey atmosphere, began to pierce the gloom, casting fitful streaks across the convent wall. Some awe-stricken spectators started timidly.

Soon, however, the sky parted. With a gentle quickness, like a sudden smile, a stream of brightness poured itself onto the crystal vase. The light spread over Savonarola's face, bathing him in mild glorification.

An instantaneous shout tore through the Piazza. *"Behold the answer!"*

The warm radiance thrilled through Savonarola's frame, syncing perfectly with the roar of the crowd. It was his last moment of untroubled triumph. In his rapturous confidence, he felt himself transported to a grander scene—an audience representing all Christendom, where he would be sealed once more as the messenger of supreme righteousness, charged with divine strength.

But the vision lasted only a heartbeat. Before the shout faded completely, he turned away within the church, unable to bear the strain any longer.

When the Frate disappeared and the sunlight returned to its impartial, mundane illumination, washing both clean and unclean alike, the silence shattered. Alongside the general movement of the crowd, a cacophony of voices erupted. Strong discords and varying scales of laughter revealed that the previous universal kneeling had been a submission born of compulsion, not conviction. Hostility and scorn had merely retreated behind a temporary spell.

"It seems to me the plaudits are giving way to criticism," observed Tito, watching the scene from an upper loggia of a house opposite the church.

"Nevertheless, it was a striking moment, eh, Messer Pietro?" Tito continued. "Fra Girolamo is a man to make one understand that there was a time when the monk's frock was a symbol of power over men's minds rather than over the keys of women's cupboards."

"Assuredly," replied Pietro Cennini. "And until I have seen proof that Fra Girolamo possesses less faith in God's judgments than the common run of men—and certainly more—I shall not believe he would brave Heaven in this manner if his soul were laden with a conscious lie."

March brought a chill that cut deeper than the winter frost, settling into the marrow of Florence as the Carnival faded into memory. One morning, near the end of the month, Tito descended the marble stairs of the Old Palace, driven by a mission of critical weight toward San Marco. Yet, he eschewed the direct route—a simple, slightly curved line connecting the palace to the basilica. Instead, he chose a circuitous path leading through the Piazza di Santa Croce, anticipating a surge of humanity spilling forth from the great church following the early Lenten sermon.

Inside Santa Croce, the atmosphere had grown electric. For weeks, the daily sermons had drawn the largest crowds the city had seen in months. Even within the confines of San Marco, where Savonarola preached, the air had grown thick with hostility; the Signoria, obeying a stern papal directive threatening immediate interdict, had silenced the friar, branding him a "wretched worm" and a "monstrous idol," demanding he seek pardon in Rome. Consequently, the most thrilling Lenten spectacle was no longer the friar's own words, but the vitriolic rebuttals hurled against him. This fervor peaked at Santa Croce, where the Franciscan assigned to deliver the Quaresimal sermons had proposed a terrifying gambit: to validate his arguments by walking through fire alongside Fra Girolamo. The schismatical Dominican had boldly claimed his prophetic doctrine would be proven by a miracle at the opportune moment. Now, that moment had arrived. If Savonarola emerged unscathed, the divine origin of his teachings would be undeniable; if the flames consumed him, his falsehood would be exposed. To ensure the logical premise held, the Franciscan had declared himself willing to share the pyre, offering his own flesh as the necessary minor premise for this high-stakes logic.

Savonarola, true to his nature, remained oblivious to these pulpit assaults. However, the zealot presiding at Santa Croce was none other than Fra Francesco di Puglia. The previous year, at Prato, he had faced a similar challenge from Savonarola's devoted follower, Fra Domenico, but had been recalled by superiors before the rhetoric boiled over into violence. Upon hearing of this renewed threat, honest Fra Domenico—who was currently preaching to women in the Via del Cocomero—immediately seized the gauntlet for his master, declaring his readiness to walk through the fire with Fra Francesco. The populace, initially captivated by what appeared to be a swift and conclusive method of debate, began to lose interest once Savonarola intervened. Keenly aware that mere discussion posed a tangible danger, he commanded Fra Domenico to retract his acceptance and sever ties with the affair. The Franciscan accepted this withdrawal gracefully, noting he had challenged the Friar directly, not a subordinate.

Following this resolution, public enthusiasm for the Lenten sermons waned. Yet, that morning, as Tito threaded his way through the Piazza di Santa Croce, he found exactly what he anticipated: a dense throng exiting the church. Rather than dispersing, the crowd coalesced near the entrance of the Franciscan monastery. Tito moved with a careless, leisurely gait, yet his eyes scanned the monastic doors with predatory precision, expecting a significant development.

The crowd's focus, however, rested elsewhere. Dominating the foreground was a massive placard, affixed by order of the Signoria and covered in legible official script. Curiosity was momentarily stifled by the fact that the document was primarily in Latin. While nearly everyone knew the gist of its contents, a gnawing desire for exactitude drove them to scrutinize the text, creating an irritating sense of intellectual superiority over their neighbors' inability to decipher the learned tongue. Mere familiarity with Latin phrases picked up from sermons offered little aid when confronted with written syntax, especially given the chaotic state of orthography among the literate masses. Furthermore, those closest to the board often lacked the requisite education entirely.

"It is the Frate's doctrines that he must prove by being burned," observed Goro, a prominent local figure standing among the foremost observers. "The Signoria has taken matters into its own hands; the writing confirms it. It aligns perfectly with what the Padre has been preaching."

"Nay, Goro," corrected a sleek shopkeeper with a tone of pity. "Thou hast twisted thy legs into hose. The Frate must prove his doctrines by *not* being burned. He is to walk through the flame and emerge sound and whole on the other side."

"Yes, yes," chimed in a young sculptor, wearing his white-streaked cap and tunic with jaunty confidence. "But Fra Girolamo objects to the ordeal. Being sound and whole already, he sees no necessity to walk through fire to remain in that condition. He leaves such trivialities to Fra Domenico."

"Then I say he flinches like a coward!" Goro retorted in a wheezy treble. "Suffocation! That was his trick at the Carnival. He had us gathered in the Piazza to witness lightning strike him, and nothing happened."

"Cease that bleating," interrupted a tall shoemaker, stepping forward with bunches of slippers draped over his shoulders. "It appears to me, friend, that you possess the wisdom of a calf with water on its brain. The Frate will flinch from nothing. Perhaps he says nothing beforehand, but when the hour strikes, he will walk through the fire without requesting a grey-frock to accompany him. Though, I would give a shoestring to understand what this Latin actually states."

"There is far too much of it," the shopkeeper sighed. "Unless I'm mistaken, I can guess the gist. Is there no scholar present?" he added, his voice tinged with disdain.

A collective shift of heads revealed Tito approaching from behind.

"Here is one," the young sculptor smiled, lifting his cap.

"He is the secretary of the Ten," the shopkeeper announced, bowing respectfully—a gesture rarely afforded Florentines save to the highest dignitaries. "He is undoubtedly heading to the convent. Make way." The exceptional reverence was owed to the splendor of Tito's appearance; his black mantle, fastened with a golden fibula, resembled a royal robe, and his velvet cap seemed an entirely unique headgear. The hardening of his cheeks and mouth, the primary alteration to his visage since arriving in Florence, lent his beauty a sharper, more masculine edge. Tito acknowledged the greeting with a nod and a lift of his own cap.

"Thank you, my friend. I merely wished, as you did, to inspect the text at the base of this placard," Tito said smoothly. "Ah, as I suspected. I was informed that the government permits anyone wishing to subscribe their name as a candidate to enter the fire—an act of liberality worthy of the magnificent Signoria—while reserving the right to select the participants. Doubtless, many believers will be eager to sign. What is it to enter the fire for one whose faith is firm? A man fears fire because he believes it will consume him; but if he believes otherwise?" Tito lifted his shoulders, pausing for rhetorical effect. "For this reason, I have never hesitated to trust the Frate when he stated he would enter the fire to prove his doctrine. In his place, if you believed the fire would not burn you, which of you, my friends, would not step into it as readily as you would walk along the dry bed of the Mugnone?"

As Tito surveyed his audience, a transformation rippled through them, akin to an eager dog sensing a pungent scent. With the prospect of burning becoming practical, few rushed to commit to a broad philosophical stance on faith and fire. The scene might have overwhelmed someone lacking Tito's command of gravity.

"Then, Messer Segretario," the young sculptor ventured, "it seems to me Fra Francesco is the greater hero, for he offers to enter the fire for the truth, certain that the fire will burn him."

"I do not deny it," Tito replied blandly. "Yet, if it transpires that Fra Francesco is mistaken, he will have been burned for the wrong cause, and the Church has never counted such victims among martyrs. We must suspend judgment until the trial truly commences."

"It is true, Messer Segretario," the shopkeeper murmured with subdued impatience. "Will you favor us by interpreting the Latin?"

"Assuredly," Tito smiled. "It expresses the specific conclusions or doctrines the Frate teaches, which the trial by fire aims to verify as true or false. They are doubtless familiar to you. First, 'Ecclesia Dei indiget renovatione': the Church of God requires purification or regeneration."

"It is true," several voices chorused.

"That implies priests ought to lead better lives; no miracle is needed to prove that. That is precisely what the Frate has always proclaimed," the shoemaker noted.

"_Flagellabitur_," Tito continued. "It will be scourged. _Renovabitur_: it will be purified. _Florentia quoque post flagellam renovabitur et prosperabitur_: Florence also, after the scourging, shall be purified and shall prosper."

"That means we regain Pisa," the shopkeeper declared.

"And recover the wool from England as we once did, I should hope," added an elderly man in a traditional beretta, breaking his silence. "There has been sufficient scourging with the collapse of our trade."
