At that precise moment, a towering figure adorned with a red feather emerged from the convent door, exchanging a neutral glance with Tito. The secretary tossed his *becchetto* casually over his left shoulder, returning his attention to the text, while the bystand

The announcement of Tito's arrival preceded him, and Fra Niccolò, Savonarola's secretary, ushered him up the spiraling stone stairs into the monastery's labyrinthine corridors. These passages, lined with cells, bore witness to Fra Angelico's frescoes—delicate as rainbows suspended on melting clouds—which occasionally startled the unaccustomed eye. Here, amidst the gloom, the painted Madonnas seemed to sit crowned in radiant glory, their divine infants gazing forth with perpetual promise, casting sudden reflections from an ethereal world onto the bare, whitewashed walls.

It was an hour of relative quiet in the monastery; most cells stood empty, illuminated only by the pale, filtered light of narrow windows falling upon nothing but stark plaster, a hard pallet, and a crucifix. Even beyond the heavy door at the end of the corridor—the inner sanctum where the Prior typically sat at his desk or received private visitors—a shaft of high light revealed only one additional object: the broad back of a figure clad in the long white Dominican tunic and scapulary. A man knelt there, head bowed before a crucifix. To the casual observer, he appeared indistinguishable from any ordinary Fra Girolamo, a soul burdened only by minor sins: fleeting distractions during choir practice or a petty resentment when a fellow friar spilled ink upon a miniature. Such a man sought only the narrow ladder of prayer, fasting, and obedience to climb safely into Paradise. Yet, beneath this pristine white garment beat a heart pulsing with a consciousness alien to the average monk, perhaps incomprehensible to any man who had not forged self-knowledge through a tumultuous inner storm. Within this vessel, irrevocable errors and lapses from truth were inextricably entwined with noble purposes and sincere convictions. Self-justifying expediency was woven so tightly into the fabric of a grand design that the entire being seemed incapable of abandoning it, just as the body cannot cease trembling before the objects of hope and fear. Consequently, regardless of the path chosen, the conscience found no perfect repose.

Savonarola was not merely in the posture of prayer; Latin supplications rolled from his lips. Yet, he prayed in name only. Having collapsed to his knees, he had erupted into words of beseeching, seeking a surge of calmness that would serve as divine warrant against the resolutions urged upon him by crowding thoughts and passions. However, the premonitions and impulses working within him for the past hour remained imperious. As he pressed his hands against his face and muttered aloud, *"Cor mundum crea in me,"* his mind remained cluttered with the imagery of the trap his enemies had set, busy constructing arguments to justify himself against their taunts.

His defense extended beyond his adversaries. That morning, he had received fresh evidence that his own followers were as eager to push for the Trial by Fire as his detractors. They desired, tacitly expecting, that he finally accept the challenge to evoke the long-awaited miracle destined to dispel doubt and crush malignity. Had he not proclaimed that God would reveal Himself at the fitting time? To the pragmatic Florentines, eager to settle partisan disputes, no moment seemed more fitting than this. Indeed, if Fra Domenico emerged from the flames unscathed, *that* would be a miracle, and the fervent faith of that good brother served as a cheering omen. Yet, Savonarola was acutely aware that his explanations had failed to quell the secret longing of his followers to see him step forward.

It was impossible for him to satisfy them. With bitter distress, he realized he could no longer resist the prosecution of the trial regarding Fra Domenico. He had not lied when declaring his belief in a future supernatural attestation; however, his constitution rendered such belief conditional. While it was easy for him to envision a distant, undefined miracle shielded by logical necessity, it was equally easy to trust in inward miracles—his own prophetic inspiration and divinely wrought intuitions. Conversely, believing in the probability of a specific, tangible miracle—one requiring him to walk through fire, pressing every detail upon his imagination and demanding exceptional action—was insurmountably difficult.

Savonarola's nature harbored opposing tendencies in nearly equal measure: a passionate sensibility, impatient of rigid thought, flooding every concept with emotion and drifting toward contemplative ecstasy, alternating with a keen perception of external facts and a vigorous, practical judgment of men and things. In this instance, the latter traits were activated by an acute physical sensitivity that amplified the conception of pain and destruction as an inevitable consequence of prior suffering. The speed with which men agree to touch red-hot iron with a wet finger cannot be measured by their theoretical acceptance of the danger; practical belief hinges on what is most vividly represented in the mind at that moment. For the Frate, when the Trial by Fire was presented as an immediate demand, it became impossible for him to believe that he—or anyone—could traverse the flames unharmed. Impossible, furthermore, to believe that even if he resolved to offer himself, he would not recoil at the final threshold.

The Florentines, however, were unlikely to make such fine distinctions. To the common mind, finding moral questions easy and judging conduct via concise alternatives constitutes proof of mental vigor. Nothing appeared plainer than that a man who once declared God would not leave him without a miraculous guarantee, yet retreated when the test was proposed, had spoken falsely. Were not Fra Domenico and Fra Mariano, along with countless *Piagnoni*, ready to enter the fire? What drove their superior courage if not superior faith? Savonarola could not adequately explain his conduct to his friends, nor to himself. Naked emotions rush to clothe themselves in convenient propositions; articulating the true state of one's interior requires more than mere sincerity, even when unmixed. In these very moments, as Savonarola knelt in audible prayer, he had ceased to hear the words. They were drowned by internal argumentative voices shaping reasons for an outward audience.

"To appeal to heaven for a miracle by rashly accepting a challenge, a mere snare prepared by ignoble foes, would be tempting God, and the appeal would go unanswered. Let the Pope's legate arrive, let the ambassadors of all great powers come promising that the convocation of a General Council and the reform of the Church depend upon this miracle, and I will enter the flames, trusting God will not withhold His seal from that great work. Until then, I reserve myself for higher duties directly laid upon me: it is not permitted to leap from the chariot to wrestle with every loud vaunter. Yet, Fra Domenico's invincible zeal to enter the trial may signal a Divine vocation, a pledge that the miracle—"

"No!" When Savonarola focused his mind on the threatened scene in the Piazza, imagining a human body entering the fire, his belief recoiled violently. It was not an event his imagination could simply visualize; he felt it with shuddering vibrations traveling to the extremities of his sensitive fingers. The miracle could not occur. Nay, the trial itself was not meant to happen; he was warranted in using all his power to prevent it. Fuel might be gathered in the Piazza, crowds assembled, formalities completed—all this might now be inevitable, and he could no longer resist without bringing dishonor upon himself, yes, and thereby upon the cause of God. But it was not truly intended that the Franciscan should enter the fire; while *he* hesitated, there existed means to block Fra Domenico's entry. At the absolute worst, if Fra Domenico were compelled to proceed, he should carry the consecrated Host, and with that Mystery in hand, there might be a warrant to expect the ordinary effects of fire to cease; or, more likely, this demand would be resisted, becoming a final obstacle to the trial.

These intentions could not be avowed. He must appear frankly to await the trial and trust in its outcome. This divergence between inward reality and outward appearance was not the Christian simplicity he had strived for through his youth and prime, preaching it as a chief fruit of the Divine life. In the stress and heat of the day, with cheeks burning and shouts ringing in the ears, who remains blessed enough to recall the yearnings of the cool, silent morning and realize they have betrayed them?

"O God, it is for the sake of the people—for they are blind—for their faith depends on me. If I don sackcloth and cast myself among the ashes, who will take up the standard and lead the battle? Have I not been led by a path unknown to the work lying before me?"

The conflict was endless. In the effort of prayerful pleading, his uneasy mind constantly washed its wounds in thoughts of the magnitude of that task, unique to a man inspired with the vision and daring of a sacred rebel. Even the words of prayer had faded. He continued to kneel, but his mind was filled with visions of repercussions rippling across Europe, the sense of immediate difficulties lost in the glow of that grandeur, until the knock at the door announced the anticipated visitor.

Savonarola drew on his mantle before exiting his cell, adhering to custom when receiving guests. With that immediate responsiveness to external appeals characteristic of a power-loving nature accustomed to making its influence felt through speech, he met Tito with a gaze as self-possessed and strong as if he had risen from resolution rather than conflict.

Tito did not kneel, offering instead a greeting of profound deference, which Savonarola received quietly, devoid of sacerdotal formulae. Desiring Tito to sit, he asked immediately:

"Your business carries some weight, my son, unsuitable for transmission through intermediaries?"

"Assuredly, Father, otherwise I should not presume to request it. I shall not waste your time with preliminaries. From a remark of Messer Domenico Mazzinghi,

The Piazza della Signoria, seven days later on the seventh of April, staged a spectacle that dwarfed the infamous Bonfire of the Vanities in sheer audacity. A crowd larger than any previous gathering, even surpassing the throngs of San Giovanni, squeezed into every available inch of the vast square. By midday, the space was a chaotic mosaic of desperate humanity clinging to the city's architecture: men wedged themselves onto iron rods jutting sharply from walls, draped their limbs over slender pillars, balanced precariously atop the necks of rough-hewn statues guarding noble entrances, and found purchase on mere palm-widths of architrave or rusticated stone. They gripped iron rings and staples driven into the masonry, their bodies contorted, their flesh abraded, yet seemingly oblivious to the pain. They had come to witness a Miracle. While mankind generally hears of miracles with skeptical detachment, the Florentines were prepared to see one unfold before their eyes. At minimum, they would observe the entry; if the monk emerged unscathed, the proof would be absolute. If he failed, the inference would be equally damning: he had been consumed in the middle.

There was no ambiguity regarding the mechanics of the event. Before them lay a long platform, eight feet wide and twenty yards long, constructed against the marble terrace of the Old Palace near the stone lion, the Marzocco. Its gaze seemed to frown upon the terrifying grove of fuel stacked diagonally across the square: a foundation of dry oak branches, topped with crackling thorns, and finally, the well-anointed tow and rags renowned for producing fierce flames in Florentine illuminations. Flanking this stage were three distinct bodies of armed force. Five hundred mercenaries of the Signoria stood guard before the palace; five hundred *Compagnacci* commanded by Dolfo Spini occupied the opposite side of the square; and three hundred citizen-soldiers led by Marco Salviati, a trusted ally of Savonarola, held position before Orgagna's Loggia, where the Franciscans and Dominicans awaited placement alongside their champions.

Significant capital and labor had been expended to orchestrate this drama, involving high dignitaries. The stakes were clear: if the two monks were simply incinerated, God would have spoken plainly, declaring Fra Girolamo not His prophet. Time was short. Half the monks were already in position, filling the section of the Loggia facing the palace with grey habits, while the opposing half remained empty save for a small altar. The Franciscans had entered in silence. From the other side of the piazza, however, a deafening chant rose from two hundred voices, signaling the arrival of the Dominicans. The repetitive invocation—"Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered"—was grating to the impartial ear, sounding less like piety and more like a calculated attempt to project confidence and induce panic. Leading this procession was Fra Domenico, clad in a blaze of flame-colored velvet, holding the cross. His simple mind was genuinely exalted by faith, intent on entering the flames for the glory of God and Fra Girolamo. Following him walked Savonarola in white priestly vestments, carrying a vessel containing the consecrated Host. Both men chanted loudly, projecting an image of unshakeable firmness. As the crowd's gaze shifted anxiously, curiously, or malignantly toward them from the moment of entry until Savonarola ascended the Loggia steps to deposit the Sacrament, a flash of intensified energy lit his countenance, responding to the scrutiny.

Human nature dictates that the false pretense we dread in private solitude often becomes our instinctive posture under the pressure of expectant eyes. The stimulus of the multitude transforms hesitation into performance. For Savonarola, accustomed to wielding immense influence, this need to dominate had become imperative, especially as life's complexities fused his selfhood with a non-selfish purpose. On the day of the Trial by Fire, the duplicity inherent in public careers—whether priest, orator, or statesman—reached its zenith in his consciousness. He was not merely struggling against impending martyrdom; he was fighting against impending ruin.

Consequently, he projected total confidence while a leaden weight of foreboding crushed his heart. This anxiety stemmed not only from the probable outcome of the trial but from a separate catastrophe already unfolding. Two evenings prior, news had reached Florence that the courier of the Ten had been arrested and stripped of his dispatches. Savonarola's letter was now in the hands of the Duke of Milan and imminent delivery to the Pope—a development that would heighten rage and justify extreme measures. Watching this turn of events from a window of the Old Palace was Tito Melema. His satisfaction was devoid of malice; it was the quiet gratification of a strategist who had won a game requiring hypothetical skill rather than brute force. To Tito, the bundle of desires and contrivances known as human nature, molded into the form of a plain-faced friar and a man of absurd superstition, was not a pathetic object but a puzzle solved. Yet, Fra Girolamo was an immensely clever operator, blending fabrication with remarkably acute notions of governance. Tito harbored no spite for him; rather, Fra Girolamo's existence had been highly convenient, providing Tito with a rung on the ladder from which he intended to leap to a smoother, more advantageous footing. With preparations complete, Tito awaited the setting sun to execute his departure from Florence. He had been so industrious that he now possessed the leisure to enjoy today's comedy, a feat the thick-headed Dolfo Spini could never have achieved without his intervention.

The chanting swelled to a roar, amplified by the *Piagnoni*, who carried their red crosses as badges of office. Most chanted the prayer for the confusion of God's enemies, expecting an answer mediated through a figure more signal than Fra Domenico. The good friar, in his flamboyant cope, knelt before the altar where the Sacrament rested, awaiting his summons. Across the Loggia, the atmosphere was starkly different: silence and grey. The Franciscans possessed two champions: Fra Giuliano paired with Fra Domenico, while the original champion, Fra Francesco, reserved his challenge solely for Savonarola.

"Surely," thought the men perched uneasily on rods and pillars, "all must be ready now. This chanting must cease, and we shall see the Frati move toward the platform."

Yet the monks did not stir. Pale Franciscan faces peered over the boarding at the flame-colored cope with suspicion. It bore an evil aspect; perhaps it was enchanted, capable of working a false miracle by magic. One could emerge whole from the fire, and yet it might be the work of the Devil.

Movement began between the Loggia and the marble terrace of the Palazzo. The roaring chanting softened slightly as distant observers watched more intently. However, this movement proved to be an obstacle rather than a commencement. Dignitaries appointed to moderate the affair entered and exited the Palace, engaging in heated debate with the Franciscans. Eventually, it became clear that Fra Domenico, conspicuous in his bright robes, was being summoned toward the Palace. Presumably, the fire had been kindled, though visibility was poor, and the miracle was about to commence.

This was a deception. The flame-colored cope vanished inside the Palace, followed shortly by another Dominican being withdrawn. For a prolonged interval, the scene reverted to its former state: the tedious chanting, devoid of the miraculous, and Fra Girolamo in his white vestment standing motionless. Finally, a shift occurred. Fra Domenico emerged from the Palace, returned to his brethren, and had completely exchanged garments with a fellow monk. He was flanked on each side by a Franciscan guard, lest proximity to Savonarola allow him to be enchanted anew.

"Aha," reasoned the distant spectators, their discomfort momentarily forgotten, "Fra Domenico is not entering the fire. It is Fra Girolamo who offers himself after all. We shall see him move presently, and if he emerges unburnt, we shall have a magnificent view!"

But Fra Girolamo did not move beyond the ordinary gestures accompanying speech. His words were bold, firm, and perhaps ironically admonitory, reminiscent of Elijah confronting the priests of Baal, demanding the cessation of trivial delays. Yet speech is the most irritating form of argument for those who are out of hearing range, cramped in their limbs, and hollow in their stomachs. Why argue? If the miracle did not proceed, the blame fell squarely on Fra Girolamo, who could resolve all difficulties by offering himself to the fire now that it was ready, just as he had been eager to do when no fuel was visible.

Further movement, further debate. The afternoon slipped away, accelerated by gathering clouds that altered the light and sent a chilling wind through the spectators, who were starving both physically and spiritually.

Now, the focus shifted. Fra Domenico insisted on carrying a crucifix into the fire, an act forbidden by the Franciscans who deemed it a profanation. After minor resistance, Savonarola conceded, gaining the strategic advantage of appearing compliant. Immediately, however, he placed the vessel containing the consecrated Host into Fra Domenico's hands. The notion that the presence of the sacred mystery might avert the ordinary effects of fire in the worst-case scenario lingered in his mind, but his primary calculation was more pragmatic. Accepting the Host, he spoke quietly, as if fulfilling a predetermined script: "Since they are unwilling that you should enter with the crucifix, my brother, enter simply with the Sacrament."

A surge of horror rippled through the Franciscans; Savonarola's demeanor hardened into steel. "It was impious presumption to carry the Sacrament into the fire: if it were burned, the scandal among the

Palm Sunday, or as the olive-growers of the Valdarno knew it, Olive Sunday, arrived with a sky washed clean by the previous night's storm. The sun burned with a terrifying clarity, illuminating the dust of a city that had barely slept. Inside San Marco, Savonarola climbed the pulpit once more, greeted by a congregation whose faith remained unbroken, yet their eyes held a hollow reverence. With a calm that bordered on the tragic, he proclaimed his readiness to die, seeing his own execution in every shadow cast by the candlelight. He delivered the benediction, watching the sea of upturned faces, and then descended the steps, turning his back on the adoration forever. Before the sun dipped below the horizon, Florence erupted.

The tensions that had simmered since the previous day finally boiled over, fueled by incitement and tacit approval from the highest offices. The violence began at the Duomo, where Compagnacci attempted to disrupt the evening sermon intended for the Piagnoni. As blood rose in the throats of the mob and the disturbance escalated into a riot, the cry rang out: "To San Marco! Burn San Marco!" Long before darkness fell completely, the church and convent were besieged by a swelling tide of rage. Resistance was fierce; the monks, aware of the encroaching threat, wielded weapons within their walls, fighting with the vigor of Knights Templar in their long white robes. Even Savonarola's commands could not quell the instinct for self-defense among men whose muscles were still strong beneath their Dominican habits. Laymen who had refused to flee joined the fray, hurling stones and burning embers from the roof while swords clashed violently in the cloisters. Despite the overwhelming numbers of the attackers, the assault dragged on until the deepest hour of the night.

Throughout the conflict, the government's stance remained singularly hostile toward the convent. Early in the chaos, guards were dispatched not to disperse the rioters, but to order everyone inside to surrender their arms, command all laymen to depart, and demand that Savonarola leave Florentine soil within twelve hours. Had he complied then, he likely would have been torn apart; he was willing to go, but his friends obstructed him. Many high-ranking laymen deemed it too dangerous to exit via the garden wall, yet Francesco Valori chose to leave, hoping to rally reinforcements from outside. By the time the struggle reached its nadir—when the Compagnacci might have breached the library where Savonarola prayed with unarmed brethren—a second contingent of guards arrived, commissioned by the Signoria to arrest Fra Girolamo and his two coadjutors, Fra Domenico and Fra Salvestro.

When the lantern-light revealed the Friar emerging from the convent doors, escorted by a guard offering no protection beyond the prison walls, a roar of triumphant hatred tore through the crowd. The battle shifted instantly to the narrow streets leading to the Old Palace. Every man scrambled to position himself for the ultimate humiliation: to reach the Prophet's ear with insults, or worse, to strike and kick him. Armed Compagnacci, unimpeded by protocol, surged forward. As Savonarola was dragged and shoved through the jeering throng, illuminated by lanterns reflecting derisive faces, spat upon, and beaten with coarse epithets, he felt the bitterest sting of life pass. If judgment had fallen, death was the only remaining sentence. Yet the deepest despair lies not in martyrdom, but in the silent realization that one is unworthy of the title, that truth may prosper, but not through one's hand. This brief, imperfect victory of the mob ended as quickly as it began; the Friar vanished beneath the archway of the palace.

For the Compagnacci, however, the satisfaction was fleeting, akin to a tiger tasting blood. Their attention turned to the houses of the hypocrites' allies. Half the armed multitude, previously kept at bay during the siege, now moved with purposeful efficiency to sack wealthy residences. This was not random looting; it was a targeted operation against the homes of chief Piagnoni, proving the riot was orchestrated and led by sword-wielding officers. Next on the list was Francesco Valori, suspected of plotting with the Friar to establish a lifelong doge or gonfalonier. Then came the grey-haired man who had demanded justice eight months prior, only to escape San Marco to witness a different kind of retribution: his home surrounded by a greedy horde, his wife struck down by an arrow, and himself murdered en route to the Palazzo by the blades of the Ridolfi and Tornabuoni families. Thus, the Masque of the Furies played out across Florence through the night and into the dawn.

Yet the mastermind remained invisible, issuing orders from a secluded retreat, his reputation too lofty to allow his red plume to wave amidst the carnage. The location was a quiet house between Santa Croce and San Marco, the same chamber where Tito had recently visited Dolfo Spini. Here, the Captain of the Compagnacci spent the memorable night, receiving a revolving door of visitors—some disguised as armed brothers, others cloaked in obscurity. Wine flowed freely, cups raised for opportunists, though Spini, wary of excess, drank sparingly to fuel the adrenaline of the incoming reports. Among the shadowed guests, Ser Ceccone was a constant presence, accompanied late into the twilight by Francesco Cei, who hid in Florence awaiting the revocation of his exile upon the Friar's fall.

Candles burned down to ragged, shapeless stubs, and holes in the shutters glowed with a somber external light. Suddenly, Spini leaped from his chair, pacing with a flushed, angry countenance, reacting to the latest gossip from his two unmilitary associates. "The devil spit him! He shall pay," he roared, a manic laughter escaping him. "Ha, ha! The claws will descend when he least expects it. So *he* was to be the great man after all! Pretending to throw everything at my feet as if I were a blind beggar, while he winked and filled his own purse. I'd hang skins about him and set my hounds loose! And he stole that fine ruby of mine—I was a fool to gift it yesterday. Malediction! He laughed at me in his sleeve two years ago, ruining the finest plan ever devised. I trusted a rascal whose twisted schemes remain invisible to anyone but himself."

"A Greek, too," interjected Francesco Cei, a faint smile playing on his lips, "who dropped into Florence with gems packed about him."

"You did not choose your confidant wisely, my Dolfo," Cei remarked.

"He is cunning beyond you, Francesco, and handsome as well," Spini retorted, turning on his associate with a predatory glare.

"I humbly conceive," Ser Ceccone chimed in, "that Messer Francesco's poetic genius will outweigh—"

"Yes, yes, rub your hands! I detest that notary's trick," Spini snapped, impatient with the delay. "But here comes Taddeo, or someone else: now is the moment! What news?" Two Compagnacci entered, their faces heated.

"Bad," one reported. "The populace has decided they want to sack Soderini's house. We've blocked them, but if we don't act, they'll turn on us. I suspect Medicean agitators are buzzing among them; they may attack your palace over the bridge shortly unless we provide another bait."

"I have it!" Spini shouted, seizing Taddeo by the belt and dragging him aside for instructions while the other continued briefing Cei on the Signoria's interference regarding Soderini.

"Ecco!" Spini commanded moments later, pushing Taddeo toward the door. "Go, and make quick work."

The grey twilight of the Arno offered no comfort, only a suffocating blanket of indifference. On the opposite bank, where the city walls retreated to leave a desolate expanse of warehouses and granaries, a solitary figure sought refuge in the damp. This was Baldassarre, driven not by commerce or crime, but by a hollow, gnawing desperation. He sat amidst the long, waterlogged grass, bending over the swift, channelled flow of the river, his eyes fixed on the water as if it held the answers to a prayer he could no longer articulate.

His presence there was born of a singular, irrational hope. Years prior, a kind-hearted runlet—a small boatman—had occasionally tossed him scraps of bread, raw carrots, and apple parings. These fleeting acts of charity had created a habit of vigilance; Baldassarre returned daily to this secluded spot, hoping for another miracle. It was preferable to the silent, humiliating begging on the church-steps beyond the Porta San Frediano. Baldassarre despised the act of begging with a visceral hatred; he would have welcomed death a thousand times over than to lower his head and plead for alms. Yet, he lived. Why? Because a vague, formless possibility had taken root in his mind, clinging to him like a parasite. It was the same futile hope that drives a woman to stare at a horizon, waiting for a ship known to be lost for decades.

After his release from the convent hospital of San Miniato, where the monks had tended him during his period of total incapacity, Baldassarre had searched for the wife who was meant to be his anchor. She had vanished, swallowed by the pestilence that seemed to rot the very air of Florence. Without her, and stripped of his knife, the instrument of his intended vengeance, he was reduced to a shattered, bewildered relic of a man. He was too physically broken to earn a living, too mentally fractured to devise a plan, even if he possessed the blade. Yet, he wanted to live. He waited for something undefined, a phantom stimulus that caused his heart to hammer against his ribs with the intensity of waking from a nightmare.

While Baldassarre sat in the mud, plotting a future that existed only in his fevered imagination, the Compagnacci executed their own grim agenda. Left largely unchecked by a paralyzed Signoria, these bands of armed mercenaries split into two converging streams. One mass headed for the Ponte Rubaconte, the other for the Ponte Vecchio, united by a single, brutal directive: to sack the house on the Via de' Bardi. The common rabble required no explanation; the thrill of destruction was sufficient fuel. The leaders, however, understood the tactical precision of their mission. They knew exactly where to converge, anticipating finding the master of the house asleep in his bed, vulnerable and unsuspecting.

Tito, the master, was not sleeping. The events of the previous day had ignited a panic in him that sleep could not quell. Investigations loomed, delays threatened, and a pervasive dread of danger permeated his thoughts. While falsehoods flourished in Florence, fear had grown alongside them, evolving from a specific terror of Baldassarre into an undying, habitual paralysis. Tito felt unsafe within the city's confines. He was preparing to flee. His household was in motion: Maso was handing over the keys to the new occupant, horses awaited in San Gallo, and his family was being readied in the Borgo outside the gates.

Dressed in his finest attire, nearly as resplendent as the day he had first walked into this house and made the fatal error of loving Romola, Tito descended the stone steps. The mistake was corrected now; the old life was discarded. He turned toward the Piazza dei Mozzi, intending to cross the Ponte Rubaconte, but a sudden shift in the ambient noise halted him. Sounds of commotion drifted from the Oltrarno district. Annoyed by the loss of privacy, he altered course, opting for the safer route via the Ponte Vecchio. Unpleasant sensations prickled his skin as he pulled his mantle tight, accelerating his pace.

In the grey gloom, unseen by the few, the sounds grew louder, more chaotic. A mob was approaching. Tito spun around, fleeing in the opposite direction, convinced the rioters were crossing the bridge. He hesitated briefly to verify, only to confirm the terrifying reality: the crowd was pouring onto the Via de' Bardi from the opposite end. Panic seized him. He abandoned the thought of returning home; they wouldn't attack *him* specifically, surely? But he carried valuables, and mobs operated on chaos, not reason. Necessity forged a temporary courage. He marched toward the Ponte Vecchio, the roar of trampling feet and confused shouts drowning out his own thoughts.

Reaching the end of the street, he collided with the advancing tide. Before he could process the sudden shout rising around him, he was surrounded—not by unarmed thugs, but by the armed Compagnacci. His hat was knocked from his head, and he was shoved violently into the throng, pushed along the narrow passage of the bridge. The cacophony erupted: *"Piagnone! Medicean! Piagnone! Throw him over the bridge!"* Strong hands tore at his mantle, threatening to choke him, until the fibula gave way. Someone lunged for his *scarsella* (purse), but the grab missed; it still hung at his side.

Shouts, yells, and motiveless curses hammered into his ears, spreading like wildfire among those who hadn't yet identified him. Tito's worst nightmare crystallized: he feared being struck down or trampled before reaching the open arches of the bridge. His only salvation lay in being thrown overboard before his strength was broken. Every fiber of his being focused on this slim chance.

Yes, they were at the arches. In that critical moment, Tito experienced a surge of primal instinct. With a desperate, tearing effort, he unclasped his belt and hurled it, along with the *scarsella*, toward a patch of clear space against the parapet. *"There are diamonds! There is gold!"* he cried in a ringing voice.

The hold on him loosened instantly as the mob surged for the purse. Seizing the opening, Tito threw himself onto the parapet and launched himself into the void. He plunged with a great splash into the dark river below. It was his chance. He had survived drowning before; his powerful strokes propelled him upward. Rising to the surface, gasping, he felt a profound sense of deliverance. The current aided him. If he could swim past the Ponte alla Carrara, he might reach a remote section of the city, perhaps even San Gallo. Life remained possible. Above, the idiot mob continued to shout and bellow, convinced he was already dead. Peering over the parapet, they saw only the drifting blackness of floating hair and velvet sleeves. Only from a distance could a pale olive face be seen, white against the dark water—a face with a square forehead, long arched brows, and lustrous, agate-like eyes.

Onward he drifted, passing the bridge of Santa Trinita. Should he risk landing now? No. Yells pursued him, and the terror of human malice outweighed the uncertainty of the river. He swam on, panting and straining, fueled by a fear of his fellow men rather than the abstract dangers of the water. Exhaustion began to cloud his edges, pulling him toward unconsciousness, but he pushed through. The final bridge passed. He felt the shift, a vague certainty that he was safe, though the current carried him blindly. Where was he? The dreamy haze of exhaustion took hold.

Then, eyes caught sight of him. Aged, sharp eyes scanning the distance. Baldassarre, staring blankly from his post among the rushes, saw a white object emerging from the broader stream. Could this be fortune? He strained, watching until the shape resolved. A start of recognition jolted him. He leaned forward, his eyes filling with a new, fierce light. Yet, he remained motionless. Something was being delivered to him.

Suddenly, a body was cast violently onto the grass mere yards from him. Baldassarre sprang forward like a panther, grabbing the velvet tunic as he collapsed onto the stranger. He flashed a look into the man's face. Dead? The eyes were rigid. But no—death was rarely so absolute. Men feigned death; life could return. Baldassarre did not feel feeble. He knew exactly what he could do. He wedged his large fingers into the neck of the tunic, kneeling beside the body, watching the face intently.

A fierce hope ignited in his heart, mingled with trembling. All the slow-burning embers of his remaining vitality leaped into flame. Rigid. Still rigid. Those eyes, half-lidded, were locked against vengeance. *Could* he truly be dead? Time was meaningless; it felt long enough for hope to freeze into despair. Surely, the eyelids were quivering now. The rigidity was breaking. A vibrating light flickered in them; they opened wide.

Tito recognized him, but confusion warred with memory. Did he recognize life or death? It might be death, and death meant the eternal chill of the hideous past looming over him. But now, Baldassarre's sole dread was that the young limbs would slip away. He pressed his knuckles against the round throat, kneeling on the chest with the full force of his aged frame. *Let death come now!*

He kept his watch on the face. When the eyes stiffened

"Full up. No room."

The door slammed with the brutal finality of a garrison lock, sealing out the last shred of dignity.

I stood in the biting chill of the morning mist, calculating the caloric deficit that had turned my legs to lead. Scouring the coastlines of Italy for passage was a known hazard; seeking refuge in a hospice was a tactical necessity. To be rejected by both was a failure of logistics, a cruel reminder that institutions collapse under the weight of their own inefficiency.

A figure detached itself from the gloom of the stone wall.

"No space?" the man asked, his voice raspy, betraying a cynicism honed by years of navigating the margins of society.

"The keeper insists on capacity," I replied, keeping my voice level despite the tremor seizing my fingers.

"That's the standard defense mechanism of the coastal towns," he muttered, adjusting the ragged collar of his tunic. "They claim fullness to filter the weak. Keep the numbers down, keep the trouble away."

"You suggest it's a fabrication?"

"I'm telling you the gatekeeper is bluffing. If there is a cot, he is bound to provide it. If I were you, I'd press the issue until the bureaucracy breaks."

There was a pragmatic logic to challenging the status quo. I stepped forward, forcing the door open with renewed vigor. The same gaunt sentinel emerged, his expression shifting from smug authority to confused irritation. Even a parish priest, draped in the vestments of his office, could not wear a heavier cloak of incompetence.

"Again?" he sneered. "What is your strategic objective? Do you expect me to serve as a permanent concierge for the destitute?"

***

Inside the boat, the transition from dream to wakefulness was abrupt, yet the landscape outside remained suspended in a state of serene suspension.

My eyes opened to the pale gold of dawn. The vessel drifted motionless in a secluded cove, anchored by the sheer geometry of the coastline. To starboard stretched the unblemished azure of the Mediterranean; to port, the terrain unfolded in a rhythmic repetition of nature's design—a deep curve of mountains framing a verdant expanse, draped in the dappled shade of ancient oaks.

Sunlight, sharp and oblique, struck the western rim of this crescent-shaped bay, illuminating a cluster of buildings that seemed to have grown organically from the rock face, crowned by a solitary belfry reaching toward the sky. Below, the village slept in clusters of terracotta roofs. The air was still, the water glass-smooth, holding its breath in anticipation of the day.

The warmth of the sun penetrated my skin, a physical caress that dissolved the tension of the previous night. In the early hours of consciousness, mere existence becomes a form of anesthesia; the exquisite, undefined sensation of peace creates a bliss devoid of memory or desire. As the heat settled into my limbs, the chaotic turbulence of the past receded like the tide, leaving behind the tranquil aftermath of childhood reveries. For a fleeting moment, the oblivion was absolute. I did not calculate the duration of my stay, nor the implications of my arrival; I simply *was*.

Then, the illusion fractured.

Reality returned with the jarring clarity of a ship hitting a reef. I was not in a sanctuary of eternal rest, but in a drifting skiff, adrift on the edge of the known world. The boat had not delivered me to death, as I had feared, but served as a cradle for a new, uncertain existence. Despite the despair that had gripped me at dusk, the morning brought a grim relief: I was alive, bathed in familiar sunlight, rather than consigned to the void.

*Could* I remain here?

The silence of Florence, miles away, was a comforting fiction. Already, the golden haze of the morning gave way to the silhouette of domes and towers, bisected by the Arno and hemmed in by green hills. The isolation was temporary.

I rose, pushing myself upright in the hull. The urge to analyze my position was overwhelming, but I suppressed it. This was a sheltered inlet, inhabited by simple folk unlikely to harbor malice. For now, I would conserve energy, allowing my mind to settle before addressing the immediate needs of sustenance. Bread and milk awaited; thereafter, I would nestle into this green quietude and accept the pause in my journey.

I turned my gaze back to the valley, seeking to reclaim the serenity of my awakening.

Less than a minute passed before the tranquility was shattered by a sound that cut through the stillness like a knife. It was not a cry of joy or warning, but a continuous, rising wail of pure distress.

Instinct took command. I assumed the posture of a rescuer, planting one foot on the gunwale, ready to launch onto the shore. But I hesitated, listening. A mother must be nearby; the cry should subside. It did not. Instead, the sound intensified, drawing me with an irresistible magnetic pull, amplified by the profound contrast with the preceding peace.

I leapt onto the beach, moving blindly until the source of the noise revealed itself. It emanated from a dilapidated hovel nestled among rough vegetation, yards to my right. I scrambled over a crumbling stone fence, traversing patches of weedy greens and neglected grain stalks. The cry grew distinct, a piercing shriek that vibrated in my chest.

As I approached the hovel, I sensed a shift in the atmosphere—a subtle corruption hanging over the lush greenery, a taint that promised danger. Through the gap in the roof, I saw the interior. The cry was a physical lance, driving me forward.

I crossed the threshold.

The dim light revealed a tableau of horror. Three bodies lay strewn across the straw-strewn floor. One was a tall man, rigid and pale; another, a girl of approximately eight years, equally desiccated. The third, a young woman, lay clutching a living child. The infant, the source of the scream, writhed violently, its tiny hands tearing at the long black hair of the dying mother.

My training in the theaters of plague and death activated instantly. Thought and action merged into a single reflex. I scooped the child from the chaos, pressing it to my breast to soothe its terror, while simultaneously scanning the corpses to confirm their status.

The features were unmistakable: the high cheekbones, the specific cast of the eyes, the garb. These were Sephardic Jews, victims of the Inquisition's relentless purge. Greed had driven them from their ships; superstition had cast them back to the rocks. Their property stripped, their lives forfeited, they had been abandoned to die.

*"Surely,"* I reasoned, *"there is a woman in this village whose maternal instinct will compel her to aid this orphan."*

Yet, doubt lingered. The man and the girl appeared emaciated, long since departed. The woman, however, retained a stubborn vitality, her frame still robust. I knelt, preparing to check for a pulse, lifting the coarse yellow woolen cloth covering her chest. Beneath it, the skin was marred by purplish blotches—the signature of the pestilence.

If the villagers knew of this contagion, my mission would be fraught with peril. They might recoil from me, terrified of the child I carried. But I possessed coin. Gold was a universal language, and they would not refuse goat's milk in exchange for it.

I moved toward the settlement, my mind focused entirely on calming the dark-skinned infant and devising a strategy to recruit a guardian. I hoped that the aura of sanctity radiating from my religious habit would disarm suspicion. As I crossed a field of cultivation, I noted with alarm that sections of the harvest had been left to rot, golden apples and figs decaying on the soil. No livestock grazed the visible pastures. The stillness had transformed from peaceful to ominous.

I hurried toward the densest cluster of dwellings, seeking the pulse of life. Clutching two figs, I tore off small pieces of the sweet pulp to quiet the child's cries, using the distraction to mask my own growing anxiety.

Between two rows of houses, I found the village. By all rights, the inhabitants should have been stirring, but the streets were deserted. The air grew increasingly oppressive, thick with an invisible, foul impurity. I peered into an open door, finding only grim emptiness. Another door yielded a similar view: a man collapsed mid-stride, his head resting awkwardly across the handle of a spade, an earthenware jug clutched in a stiffened hand.

Horror seized me. Was this a graveyard of the unburied? I sought to listen for any sign of life, but the child's fresh burst of crying drowned out my efforts.

Finally, a figure crawled from a doorway, collapsing against the wall in a fetal position. I rushed to her side. She was a young woman, racked by fever, holding a pitcher. As I approached, she did not flinch; her focus was singular, consumed by agony.

"Water!" she gasped, her voice a broken whisper.

I lowered myself beside her, placing the pitcher in her grasp. "You shall have water. Can you indicate the location of the well?"

Her hand pointed vaguely toward the far end of the narrow street. I adjusted my load, balancing the pitcher against the child, and set off. The infant, fed intermittently with the fig pulp, grew quieter, allowing me to observe my surroundings with greater clarity.

The well lay beyond the street's termin

In the silent, biting hours of winter, Romola lay resting from her exhaustion, her mind traversing the landscape of the past and peering across the fog-shrouded horizon of the future. Her perspective had shifted irrevocably. Since awakening in the boat, the weight of her recent ordeal had struck her with the force of a fresh seal pressing onto dissolving wax. Previously, she had drifted in a state of unmoored despair, convinced that existence offered no sustenance, whispering to the void, "I am weary of life; I wish for death." That fatalistic thought had choked her sleep, yet upon waking, the cry of necessity had severed her lethargy. Unlike in Florence, where the intricate lattices of marriage, civic duty, and religious dogma had complicated her humanity until their betrayal left her alienated and numb, she now found herself stripped bare. She possessed no pretenses, only the raw imperative to act.

She had ceased to argue for the value of living; instead, she simply existed with a kinetic urgency to share the burden of the world, answering every call of need and performing the labor that screamed to be done. This transformation was akin to a violent rebirth. In Tuscany, the simple connections between humans had been obscured by the heavy machinery of the State and the Church; when these structures failed her, the resulting shock had paralyzed her empathy. Now, she declared internally, "It was sheer baseness to crave death. If all else is uncertain, this suffering I can alleviate is absolute truth; if the glory of the cross is a phantom, then sorrow remains the only certainty." As long as strength remained in her limbs, she would extend them to the collapsing; as long as vision remained in her eyes, she would seek out the abandoned.

With this resolve solidified, the past surged forward with renewed intensity. Her mission in this verdant valley concluded, and the emotional currents previously held captive by her local community flooded back into the deep reservoirs of her former attachments. The solitude of her departure granted her a rare opportunity for rigorous self-audit, a judgment she had never before permitted herself. Compunction, inherent to a sensitive soul attuned to the potential agony of others, stirred within her with mounting force. She scrutinized her own conduct: she had been reckless, arrogant, perpetually dissatisfied with the inadequacy of others while failing to honor the highest ideals her soul recognized. She condemned her flight as a cowardly retreat born of self-preservation. The principles that had guided Savonarola in reclaiming her were far truer and deeper than the flimsy justifications for her second escape. How could she claim to understand the plight of strangers while ignoring the desperate needs of those closest to her?

Yet, a counter-current of defense rose against this self-flagellation. The memory of her life with Tito—the insurmountable conditions that rendered their spiritual union impossible, contrasted with the false obligations of their public marriage which demanded she conceal and validate the very thoughts her mind rejected—confirmed that flight had been her sole viable option. Every intellect, save those numbed by apathy, must endure this cyclical conflict where life's tangled complexities forbid the fulfillment of sacred bonds. Strictly speaking, relationships cannot be replaced; the arrival of a new connection does not erase the rupture of the old. Life is imperfect, maimed, and scarred. Until those wounds heal completely, conscience relentlessly casts backward, casting doubt.

Romola recoiled in terror at the prospect of renewing her proximity to Tito, yet unease gnawed at her regarding her absence. She feared the moment might arrive when he suffered and required her aid. A persistent thread of pain testified to Fra Girolamo's warning: she could never truly cease to be a wife. Could anything ever extinguish what had once merged with the very current of her blood? Florence, and the entirety of her existence there, returned to her like a sudden, ravenous hunger. Her emotions could not wander aimlessly toward the abstract and the vague; their vital fiber was nourished by the memory of specific, tangible realities. The notion that she had severed herself permanently from them grew increasingly urgent during these idle hours.

*What if Fra Girolamo was mistaken?* *What if the life of Florence is a tapestry woven from contradictions?* *Am I, therefore, superior, destined to shake the dust from my feet and declare, 'This world is unworthy of me'?* If she were truly elevated, she would not have so readily lost her faith. Her righteous grief for her godfather no longer held total dominion; her sense of indebtedness to Savonarola was regaining supremacy. No event, past or future, could negate the profound inspiration he had ignited within her—a spark that awakened a dormant vitality. Who in her entire experience could command such gratitude? Yet, his errors... could they not precipitate catastrophe? She could find no peace. She struggled to discern whether her renewed vigor stemmed from the spring thaw or an ardent longing to approach Florence. She did not dare envision entering the city, but the drive to remain close enough to witness its developments surged with a power that eclipsed all other intentions.

On a crisp March morning, the villagers assembled in the valley to bid farewell to the Blessed Lady. Jacopo had procured a sturdy mule and prepared to accompany her over the mountain passes. The Padre also joined the procession, intending to guide her to the nearest town and ensure she discovered the safest route to Pistoja. Her hoard of trinkets and coin, untouched throughout her stay in the valley, provided ample resources for her journey. Had she resisted the magnetic pull drawing her away, the final walk through the village street would have been agonizing. The Padre and Jacopo waited near the well with the animal, but Romola's progress was impeded by the wailing crowd. People knelt, kissing her hands, clutching her skirts, and pressing their lips to the grey fabric of her habit, pleading, "Ah, why must you depart when the good season begins and the crops promise abundance?"

"Do not mourn," Romola replied, her voice steady. "You are well now, and I shall carry your memory. I must go to see if my kin require me."

"Ah, yes—if they are stricken by the pestilence!" someone cried.

"Look upon us again, Madonna!"

"Yes, yes, we shall be virtuous for little Benedetto!"

Finally, Romola mounted the mule. However, a vigorous shriek from Benedetto, who saw her turning away in this new role, served as a pretext for the entire assembly to pursue her, insisting that the boy must ride on the mule's neck to the base of the slope. The separation was inevitable, yet as Romola paused repeatedly before disappearing from view, she watched the small flock linger, striving to catch the final wave of her hand.

On the fourteenth of April, Romola returned to Florence, driven by a singular, overriding imperative. Having failed to find rest in Pistoia due to conflicting rumors regarding the Trial by Fire, she had pushed onward to Prato, intending to cross the threshold of the city regardless of her dread. Her resolve was tested immediately upon encountering the Augustinian monk from San Spirito, former confessor to her late father-in-law. From him, she extracted the brutal facts of Savonarola's arrest and the fate of her husband. The monk recounted how Tito had fled an enraged mob by plunging into the Arno, only to be slain on the riverbank by a lifelong enemy. Yet, Romola perceived the catastrophe with a clarity that eluded others. When the monk spoke of Savonarola's fall, adopting the typical bias of the Black Brothers toward a brother who had shown weakness under duress, claiming he had confessed to deceiving the people, Romola remained unmoved by such partisan rhetoric.

That evening, she sat in a state of agitated silence within Florence, surrounded by the cacophony of Monna Brigida's hysterical joy and wailing. The older woman, having recently reverted to wearing false hair, now pulled it off, declaring her willingness to embrace gray locks if Romola would remain with her. Romola, however, was too consumed by the weight of Tito's death and Fra Girolamo's coerced confession to absorb Brigida's trivial gossip. Her entire mental faculty, strained by awe-struck grief, focused exclusively on two objectives: locating Savonarola and discovering the whereabouts of Tessa and the children.

"Tell me, cousin," Romola interrupted Brigida's rambling proposals for cohabitation, "has anything been seen or heard of a young woman with two small children since Tito's passing?"

Brigida recoiled, her eyes widening in horror. "Cristo! No! Was he so terrible, my poor child? Ah, that explains your departure. You left me only a vague note of your own volition. Had I known... well, I suppose I blamed your father's temper. 'She's got her father's fire,' I muttered to myself. But if you had spoken the truth about the woman and the babes..."

"No, dear cousin," Romola insisted, cutting her off firmly. "Cease speaking thus. Above all, I must find that woman and her children. They are utterly helpless. Do not speak against it; securing their safety is my first commandment."

"If that makes me a Piagnone," Brigida muttered, lowering her voice with confused discomfort, "then I've been counting peas instead of prayers. Fra Girolamo preached that widows should not remarry. Stepping through the door is a sin; crawling through the chimney is grace. Two children—Santiddio!"

"Cousin, the poor soul committed no conscious wrong. She is ignorant of everything. I will explain later—not now."

Early the following morning, Romola proceeded to the house beyond San Ambrogio where she had previously located Tessa. As feared, the young woman was gone. Romola deduced that Tito had dispatched her ahead to a rendezvous point, unwilling to separate her from the children. The prospect was agonizing; if Tessa had fled Florence, recovery seemed impossible, leaving the child to wander in helpless misery at some roadside halt. Neighbors offered only that the elderly deaf Lisa had departed a week prior with her belongings, providing no lead on Tessa. With no tangible starting point for inquiry, and restrained by both natural reserve and a noble sensitivity that forbade exposing Tessa's connection to Tito, Romola fell into days of anxious inaction.

Even under the pressure of other concerns, her heart raced at the sight of round brown legs or a peasant woman in the traditional *contadina* dress. She never rationalized this vigilance as heroic charity; rather, she possessed a specific, binding obligation to protect these lives. She yearned to embrace the children and earn their affection, a sweet redemption for her own sorrows. While legal avenues existed to claim Tito's property, Romola distrusted the purity of such funds. She resolved to surrender all assets to the State, retaining only enough to purchase her father's library—a sum sufficient for Tessa and the children's modest sustenance.

Monna Brigida, however, drowned these plans in clamorous insistence that Romola reside with her until she reached Paradise, questioning why she had encouraged the conversion to the Piagnoni order otherwise. Brigida argued that if Romola wished to raise other people's children, she must do so alongside her. Romola recognized the subtext: a demand for submission. Yet, strong emotion fueled a superstition of hope. Somehow, she would find the mother and the children.

A new avenue of investigation emerged. She learned that Tito had arranged horses and mules at San Gallo, indicating his intention to flee via that gate. Determined, though lacking absolute confidence, she decided to query the gatekeepers regarding anyone matching Tessa's description who had passed before the morning of the ninth of April.

Walking along the Via San Gallo, her gaze sharp and watchful beneath her long widow's veil, she spotted Bratti haggling with a customer. This itinerant man might prove useful; she would not hesitate to discuss Tessa with him. As she removed her veil and stepped across the street, a glint of gold hanging from the corner of his basket caused her heart to leap.

"Bratti, my friend," she called out abruptly, "where did you acquire that necklace?"

"Your servant, madonna," Bratti replied, scanning the street with practiced deliberation, his demeanor immune to surprise. "It is a piece worth money, yet I shall gain little from selling it. My heart is too tender for trade; I have pledged it."

"Please, tell me where you obtained it—from a young woman named Tessa, is it not true?"

"Oh! If you know her," Bratti said, "and would redeem it for a small profit to return it to her, you'd be performing a great charity. She wept bitterly when parting with it—as if diving into a brook. A trifling fee, I'll grant you. One florin, for I dislike being hard-hearted."

"Where is she?" Romola asked, handing over the coin and retrieving the necklace from the basket with trembling hands.

"Just outside the gate, at the far end of the Borgo, at old Sibilla Manetti's. Anybody can show you the house."

With wings of purpose, Romola hastened toward the destination, grateful for the Carnival memory that allowed her to recognize the distinctive necklace instantly. Upon arrival, she found the young woman and the children confined to an inner room. They had been displaced weeks ago, possessing only their clothes to barter for food and lodging with a destitute widow. Since Romola knew them, the transaction ceased to matter. She entered without delay.

Tessa sat on a low bed, her earlier screaming replaced by silent, tearless sobs. Her eyes were fixed with a hollow sadness on the two children playing in the corner. Lillo covered his head with his skirt, roaring at Ninna to frighten her, then peeking out cautiously to gauge her reaction. The door stood slightly behind Tessa, and she did not turn, assuming it was the old woman; hope had long since extinguished itself.

Romola discarded her veil, holding the necklace aloft for a brief moment. Then, in the pure, resonant voice that once cheered her father, she spoke: "Tessa!"

Tessa sprang to her feet, turning in alarm. "See," Romola said, fastening the beads around Tessa's neck. "God has sent me to you again."

The poor woman screamed and sobbed, clutching the arms that secured the necklace, unable to form words. The children rushed from their corner, seizing their mother's skirts and gazing upward with wide, startled eyes at the stranger.

That afternoon, the group returned to Monna Brigida's residence in the Borgo degli Albizzi. Romola gently informed Tessa that Naldo could never return—not out of cruelty, but because he was dead. "Be comforted, my Tessa," Romola assured her. "I have come to care for you forever. We have Lillo and Ninna."

Monna Brigida's face twisted in a struggle between reverence for Romola and the urge to interject unseasonably. *Let it be for now,* she thought. *It feels like a millennium until I reveal to this simple country girl, who cannot count her fingers, exactly who Romola is. I shall tell her eventually, or she will never understand her station. It suits Romola perfectly; no one owns their soul when she is present. But if this cat-faced minx is to live in my house, she must submit to me.*

Nevertheless, Brigida's maternal instincts surfaced during supper, offering the children excessive sweets. Before retiring, she confessed to Romola that it would be shameful not to care for such cherubs. "However, you must yield to me somewhat regarding their feeding and such matters," she added, noting Romola's lack of infant experience. "I had twins, though they died shortly after birth."

April was nearing its close when Romola returned home with Tessa and the children, carrying the weight of a city teetering on the brink of civil war. Her mind, already frayed by domestic anxieties, was now seized by the publication of Fra Girolamo's trial—a document drafted by sixteen Florentine citizens commissioned by the Signoria. Issued officially, it had ignited a firestorm of public suspicion and discontent so severe that the government rushed to recall the printing press. Yet, as often happens when truth is suppressed, copies leaked into the hands of eager readers, and a second, unauthorized edition circulated rapidly.

Romola, having abandoned hope of ever speaking directly with the friar, devoured the text repeatedly, desperate to pierce through the fog of partisan distortion. Among the devout followers of Savonarola, the friar's collapse under torture and his retraction of prophetic claims caused a consternation that eventually hardened into a specific theory: that every contradictory word attributed to the prophet was actually the fabrication of Ser Ceccone, the notorious notary tasked with compiling the interrogation. This narrative painted the event not as a judicial proceeding, but as a slow-formality murder orchestrated by the Old Palace. The Signoria, Romola reasoned, had predetermined the friar's doom to secure a political bargain with the Pope and the Duke of Milan, extinguishing a threat to both foreign tyrants and a corrupt clergy. The choice presented to the Republic was stark: surrender the friar alive to the Papacy in exchange for taxing rights, or assert their sovereignty by executing him themselves. Under such circumstances, how could anyone accept the authenticity of the confession? If the friar had denied his gift, it was only due to the agony of the rope and pulley, a torment that would inevitably drive a sensitive man to ravings. Furthermore, the report claimed he had confessed only once, under no coercion, while the reality suggested far more brutal treatment designed to break the hearts of those who loved him.

Those less ardent in their devotion acknowledged the probable falsification by the notary but insisted the core substance remained genuine. They argued indignantly that no crime warranted death, let alone burning. To impartial observers, the case rested solely on political inconvenience: the friar had become a liability to the Pope, a nuisance to rapacious Italian states eyeing Tuscany, and a thorn to ambitious citizens. No shadow of political crime existed; no stain marred his private conduct. Even his former secretary, a man of superior intellect among his peers, testified to an unimpeachable purity of life that had commanded veneration until the shock of the retraction. The Pope himself could levy charges only of disobedience and ignoring excommunication—a breach of discipline that, while indefensible logically, sparked a moral insurgency in the minds of serious men who blurred the line between the institution of the Church and its flawed agents. To Soderini and Piero Guicciardini, the severity of the crackdown revealed a grotesque hypocrisy: a system willing to bypass all law to hang a man whose public career had merely warped his strict veracity. They noted bitterly that if the friar had mixed his fraud with less inconvenient zeal, it would have served as excellent lubricant for the ecclesiastical and political machines. Yet, even these shrewd men admitted the necessity of the Pope's defensive measures; leaving a rebel capable of stirring a General Council to depose him would have been sheer ineptitude added to iniquity.

Florence had heard Savonarola declare he would not obey the devil, making their conflict a life-and-death struggle. However, it was less inevitable that Florence should serve as the Pope's executioner. Romola found herself trapped between two conflicting visions: the ardent faith of her youth and the cold discernment of worldly politics. Neither satisfied her. Her meditations on the printed document were haunted by painful observations gathered during years of discipleship, whispering that the retraction was not merely a spasmodic escape from pain, but something deeper. Conversely, her soul screamed for an explanation that preserved the purity of his life's striving. The recent memory of her own disillusionment, feeling betrayed by a man who had incarnated her highest motives, had triggered a reaction akin to faith born of despair. She told herself that the negative thoughts drying her soul could not reflect the truth of things; the spirit that had once breathed through the friar's words was real, not a hollow pretense. Any falsehood in him was a tragic fall, a gradual entanglement he struggled against, not a calculated scheme nurtured by success.

Scanning the text, Romola identified sentences stamped with the clumsiness of fabrication—the repetitive, emphatic self-accusations typical of low-level hypocrites. Yet, these glaring lies stood in sharp opposition to the overall tone of the document, reinforcing the belief that the remainder represented the actual words of the friar. Hardly anything was dishonorable except his claims to special inspiration. Regarding his actions for Florence, the Church, and the world, he remained consistent. Apart from the false claim to divine inspiration used to capture minds, he admitted no use of unworthy means. Even within this compromised text, Fra Girolamo appeared as a man seeking glory, but a glory derived from laboring for the moral welfare of humanity, striving to transform beliefs into tangible energies for daily life.

One memorable passage, though possibly altered, resonated with her: *"Everything that I have done,"* he stated, *"I have done with the design of being for ever famous in the present and in future ages; and that I might win credit in Florence; and that nothing of great import should be done without my sanction."* He envisioned establishing his position locally before expanding to Italy and beyond, rousing Christian princes to subdue the infidels. He did not seek the papacy primarily, believing that leading such a work required excellence of virtue, whereas a man without virtue could still occupy the throne. This blend of ambition and belief in supreme goodness was familiar to Romola, echoing the voice that had rung through the Duomo. Savonarola conceived great things and believed he was the vessel to execute them. Iniquity must fall; justice, purity, and love must triumph through his voice, his work, and potentially his blood. While moments of ecstasy might have dissolved his ego into the unspeakable divine, in the presence of men, pre-eminence seemed essential. Perhaps this confession, describing a conscious doubleness, reflected only the natural wavering of human judgment under extreme duress. A life so tumultuous as his offered endless possibilities for shifting self-assessment. Instead of kneeling crowds and a flourishing project, he faced hoots, spittle, curses, and the hard faces of judges. Then came the torture, triggering the irrepressible cry: *"It is true, what you would have me say: let me go: do not torture me again: yes, yes, I am guilty. O God! Thy stroke has reached me!"*

As Romola contemplated the anguish following this confession—whether conscience retracted or confirmed the words in solitary prison—it pressed upon her own heart, drawing slow, bitter tears. Vulgar Florentines glibly pronounced his demerits, unaware of the depth of sorrow known only to a soul that has loved perfection and beheld its own fall. She had not yet witnessed the evidence of his mental state post-interrogation. Days passed; reports of new examinations ceased, yielding no changes. Savonarola was left alone in his prison, granted pen and ink to write with his bruised right arm. What emerged was no legal defense or protest against the proceedings, but a continuous colloquy with divine purity, an outpouring of self-abasement, a single long cry for inward renovation. The old, vehement assertion—"Look at my work, for it is good, and those who set their faces against it are the children of the devil!"—had vanished.

In its place, the Voice of Sadness spoke: *"God placed thee in the midst of the people even as if thou hadst been one of the excellent. In this way thou hast taught others, and hast failed to learn thyself. Thou hast cured others: and thou thyself hast been still diseased. Thy heart was lifted up at the beauty of thy own deeds, and through this thou hast lost thy wisdom..."* Hope, arguing that divine love remained, offered no promise of future works, only the comfort that his bowed heart was itself a gift. There was no evidence that from imprisonment to his final hour, Savonarola viewed himself as a martyr. Martyrdom had been a passion dividing his dreams of future triumph from the joy of completed work. Now, replaced by both, came a resignation he named with no glorifying title. *But therefore he may the more fitly be called a martyr by his fellow-men to all time.* Power rose against him not because of his sins, but because of his greatness—not because he sought to deceive, but because he sought to elevate the world. Through that greatness, he endured a double agony: the reviling, the torture, the death-throes, and the crushing pain of sinking from a vision of glorious achievement into a deep shadow where he could only whisper, *"I count as nothing: darkness encompasses me: yet the light I saw was the true light."*

The final silence that descended upon the Piazza della Signoria was not merely an absence of noise; it was the cessation of a specific frequency of human suffering, a sound Romola had carried in her marrow since the day Savonarola signed his confession. She remembered the words echoing through the corridors of San Marco, repeated by a hundred desperate lips: *"Pray for me, for God has withdrawn from me the spirit of prophecy."* Those words had once shaken her faith, introducing a terrifying doubt into her understanding of the friar's retrospective clarity. But the doubts were quickly eclipsed by the brutal reality of the nineteenth of May, when the Papal Commissaries marched into Florence under the sun, hailed by a mob screaming for the executioner. The popular cry was absolute: *"It is the Frate's deception that has brought on all our misfortunes; let him be burned, and all things right will be done."*

The subsequent days were a catalog of systematic dismantling. Torture, applied with clinical precision to a frame already shattered by previous agony, yielded a new confession. Confronted with the horrific instruments of the rack, Savonarola, agitated by convulsive spasms, fell to his knees. He retracted his earlier denial of prophecy, declaring passionately that he had lied only to deny his gift, and that if he suffered, it would be for the truth—"The things that I have spoken, I had them from God." Yet, the interrogators demanded consistency. When pressed on why he had recanted, the answer was pragmatic, devoid of divine inspiration: *"I said it that I might seem good; tear me no more, I will tell you the truth."* This double retraction spread through the Florentine assessors like a contagion, filling Romola with a dismayed uncertainty. A single thought flashed through her mind, cutting through the fog of despair: *There will come a moment when he may speak.* A moment when the dread of falsehood vanishes, replaced only by the inevitability of death. When he is lifted above the people, looking down for the last time, no force can stop him from uttering a final, decisive word. *I will be there.*

Three days later, on the twenty-third of May 1498, the stage was set. A long, narrow platform stretched from the Palazzo Vecchio toward the Tetta de' Pisani, terminating in a vast circular area dominated by a single, immense heap of fuel. Rising from this pyre was a gibbet with three halters, its two arms creating a grotesque parody of a cross—one arm deliberately truncated to avoid the religious resemblance, leaving behind a symbol of pure execution. Upon the marble terrace of the Palazzo stood three distinct tribunals. One, near the main door, awaited the Bishop to perform the ritual degradation on Fra Girolamo and his two companions. Another housed the Papal Commissaries, tasked with pronouncing them heretics and schismatics before handing them over to the secular arm. The third, positioned near the Marzocco statue at the platform's edge, belonged to the Gonfaloniere and the Eight, who would deliver the sentence of death.

The Piazza was thronged. The crowd pressing around the gibbet was a sea of ferocious hatred and morbid curiosity, eager to witness a barbarous spectacle. Yet, scattered across the wide pavements, perched on rooftops, and peering from high windows, were others. These spectators, enduring insults alongside the hypocritical Piagnoni, clung to a lingering, fragile hope. Even at this eleventh hour, they prayed that God might interpose, manifesting their beloved prophet as His servant through some miraculous sign. Among them were those who, like Romola, trembled with eagerness for that final moment when Savonarola might declare, *"O people, I was innocent of deceit."*

Romola stood at a window on the north side of the Piazza, far removed from the marble terrace where the tribunals stood. Beside her watched Jacopo Nardi, a young Florentine of twenty-two, destined to earn honor as one of the few historians to write of Fra Girolamo with simple veracity. Seeing the war raging within Romola—a conflict between her shuddering horror at the cruelty of the scene and her desperate yearning to witness the end—Jacopo spoke gently. *"Madonna, there is no need for you to look at these cruel things. I will tell you when he comes out of the Palazzo. Trust to me; I know what you would see."* Romola covered her face, but the hooting of the mob seemed to penetrate the stone, making the hideous scene visible regardless. Finally, a hand touched her shoulder. *"He comes."*

She turned toward the Palace. Through the haze of anticipation, she saw Savonarola emerge in his Dominican garb. Before the Bishop, he was stripped of the black mantle, the white scapulary, and the long tunic until he stood exposed in a coarse woolen under-tunic that revealed no sacred office, no rank. He had been degraded, severed from the Church Militant. The baser elements of the multitude delighted in such humiliations; it was the satire they understood best. A fresh hoot of triumph erupted as the three degraded brothers moved to the tribunal of the Papal Commissaries. Could the prophet now appear as a schismatic and a heretic? It was easy to believe in the damnable state of a man standing stripped and humiliated. Passing the third tribunal, the Florentine officials including the odious Dolfo Spini in his grave black lucco, the three figures in their close white raiment walked the length of the platform amidst yells and grating insults. *"Cover your eyes, Madonna,"* Jacopo warned. *"Fra Girolamo will be the last."*

Before she could obey, she was forced to uncover her eyes. Savonarola was there. He had mounted the steps, and for a fleeting second, she saw him look out upon the multitude. But in that same instant, expectation died. All she saw was what he saw: torches waving to ignite the fuel beneath his corpse, faces glowing with a light darker than hate. All she heard was what he heard: gross jests, taunts, and curses. The moment had passed. She covered her face again, knowing with absolute certainty that Savonarola's voice had passed into eternal silence.

***

On the evening of the twenty-second of May 1509, five individuals sat in a handsome upper room opening onto a loggia. From its right-hand corner, the view extended along the Borgo Pinti, over the city gate, and toward Fiesole and the solemn heights beyond. At one end of the room, an archway led to a narrow recess where light filtered from above onto a small altar draped in fine white linen. Above it hung a picture, discernible only as the small, full-length portrait of a Dominican Brother, shaded by overhanging branches and wreaths of flowers. Fresh tapers stood unlit before it. The decoration was incomplete; the floor was strewn with a chaotic abundance of flowers and green boughs.

Among them sat a delicate, blue-eyed girl of thirteen, tossing her long, light-brown hair from her face as she selected blooms for the wreath she wove. Occasionally, she looked up at her mother's work with an air of instruction, offering guidance. The mother, however, was not particularly adept at floral arrangements or any other craft. Tessa's fingers, once adroit, had grown clumsy with age, swollen and fat. She worked slowly, turning her head frequently to ask Ninna's opinion with deferential humility. Tessa remained perpetually astonished by the wisdom of her children. She still wore her contadina gown, now broader than in her youth, adorned with a silver pin in her rough, curly brown hair and the memorable necklace with its red cord resting mysteriously against her bosom. Her rounded face bore a look of childish contentment that had only deepened with the years. *Everybody is so good in the world,* Tessa thought. *Even Monna Brigida never finds fault with her now; she does little else but sleep, an amiable practice shared by everyone, one that Tessa enjoys for herself.*

Monna Brigida slept in a straight-backed armchair a couple of yards away. Her hair, parted backward under her black hood, possessed a soft whiteness unlike snow—it was simply the lovely whiteness of aged hair. Her chin rested on her bosom, her hands folded on the chair's elbow. She had not woven a flower or performed any task; she had simply gazed outward, as she always did, until exhaustion claimed her.

Further away, at the wide doorway opening to the loggia, sat the other two figures. Lillo lounged on the ground, his back against the door-post angle, his long legs stretched out. He held a large book open on his knee, occasionally swatting an inquisitive fly with a hand movement more animated than the finely printed copy of Petrarch resting nearby, as if he were committing lines to memory. Romola sat nearly opposite him, yet she observed neither him nor the room. Her hands were crossed in her lap, her eyes fixed absently on the distant mountains. An eager life had left its indelible marks upon her: the finely molded cheek had sunk slightly, the golden crown of her hair less massive. Yet, there was a profound placidity in Romola's face that had never existed in her youth. It is but once that we truly know our worst sorrows, and Romola had experienced them while life was still new.

Absorbed in her reverie, she was initially unaware that Lillo had stopped reading. He watched her
